# Majmu' al-Fatawa - Tome 3

## Page 878

Part Three
A Summary of the Creed of the Early Muslim Generations

In the name of Allah, the Most Compassionate, the Most Merciful. All praise belongs to Allah alone, and blessings and peace be upon him after whom there is no prophet.

The shaykh and imam, the learned and erudite scholar, Shaykh al-Islām Taqī al-Dīn Abū al-‘Abbās Ahmad ibn ‘Abd al-Halīm ibn ‘Abd al-Salām Ibn Taymiyyah al-Harrānī—may Allah be pleased with him and grant him contentment—said:

All praise belongs to Allah. We praise Him, seek His help and ask His forgiveness. We seek refuge in Him from the evil within ourselves and from our evil deeds. Whoever Allah guides, none can lead astray; and whoever He leads astray, none can guide.

I bear witness that there is no deity worthy of worship except Allah alone, without partner, and I bear witness that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger. May Allah’s blessings and peace be upon him, his family and his Companions.

To proceed:

Those whom I was obliged to answer have asked me to write down for them the substance of what they heard me say at certain gatherings concerning Allah’s oneness and His attributes, and concerning revealed law and divine decree, given the pressing need to establish a sound understanding of these two fundamental principles and the widespread confusion

---

## Page 879

concerning them.

For everyone needs these two principles, and those devoted to reflection, learning and spiritual aspiration, as well as worshippers, inevitably encounter thoughts and statements concerning them that require a clear distinction between guidance and error. This is especially so given the many who have ventured into these matters, sometimes speaking truth but often speaking falsehood, and the doubts that beset hearts concerning them and lead them into various forms of misguidance.

Discussion of Allah’s oneness and His attributes belongs to the category of declarative speech, which involves negation and affirmation. Discussion of revealed law and divine decree belongs to the category of demands and volition, which concerns willing and loving, as opposed to aversion and hatred, whether these are negated or affirmed.

A person recognizes within himself the distinction between negation and affirmation, between accepting something as true and rejecting it as false, and between love and hatred, encouragement and restraint. Indeed, the distinction between these two categories is familiar to ordinary people and specialists alike, and to the various groups who discuss scholarly matters. Jurists have mentioned it in the Book of Faith, as have those who classify speech among scholars of reasoning, grammar and rhetoric. They explain that speech is of two kinds: declarative and non-declarative. Declarative speech involves negation and affirmation, whereas non-declarative speech takes the form of a command, a prohibition or permission.

Since this is so, the servant must affirm for Allah those attributes of perfection that must be affirmed for Him, and deny of Him whatever must be denied of Him because it is incompatible with this perfection. And with regard to His rulings, the servant must

---

## Page 880

affirm His creation and His command, believing in His creation, which entails the perfection of His power and the all-encompassing scope of His will, and affirming His command, which sets forth the words and deeds He loves and is pleased with. He must believe in His revealed law and His decree with a faith free from error. This entails (affirming His oneness by worshipping Him alone, with no partner): that is, affirming His oneness in intention, will and action. The first entails (affirming His oneness in knowledge and speech), as indicated by the surah:

“Say, ‘He is Allah, the One.’”

The other is indicated by the surah:

“Say, ‘O disbelievers!’”

These are the two surahs of sincerity. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to recite them after al-Fātihah in the two units of prayer at dawn, the two units of prayer associated with circumambulation, and on other occasions.

As for the first—affirming Allah’s oneness with regard to His attributes—the fundamental principle in this matter is to describe Allah as He has described Himself and as His messengers have described Him, both in negation and affirmation. Thus, one affirms for Allah what He has affirmed for Himself and denies of Him what He has denied of Himself.

It is well known that the way of the early generations of this community and its leading scholars is to affirm the attributes He has affirmed, without specifying their manner or likening them to those of created beings, and without distortion or denial.

Likewise, they deny of Him what He has denied of Himself, while affirming the attributes He has affirmed, without deviation concerning either His names or His signs. For Allah Most High has condemned those who deviate concerning His names and His signs, as He says:

“To Allah belong the most beautiful names, so call upon Him by them, and leave those who deviate concerning His names. They will be recompensed for what they used to do.”

And He says:

“Indeed, those who deviate

---

## Page 881

concerning Our signs are not hidden from Us. Is one who is cast into the Fire better, or one who comes secure on the Day of Resurrection? Do whatever you will”—to the end of the verse.

Their way thus entails affirming Allah’s names and attributes while denying any likeness to created beings: affirming His attributes without likening Him to creation, and declaring Him free of imperfections without denying His attributes. As Allah Most High says:

“There is nothing like Him, and He is the All-Hearing, the All-Seeing.”

His words, “There is nothing like Him,” refute likening Him to creation or equating Him with it; and His words, “He is the All-Hearing, the All-Seeing,” refute deviation and the denial of His attributes.

Allah, glorified be He, sent His messengers (with detailed affirmation and negation in general terms). They affirmed Allah’s attributes in detail and denied of Him what is unbefitting, such as resemblance or equivalence to created beings. As Allah Most High says:

“So worship Him and remain steadfast in His worship. Do you know of any equal to Him?”

The linguists explain “Do you know of any equal to Him?” as meaning a counterpart deserving a name like His. It is also explained as a rival who matches Him. This is the meaning of what is reported from Ibn ‘Abbās (“Do you know of any equal to Him?”—that is, any counterpart or likeness).

Allah Most High says:

“He neither begets nor is born, and there is none comparable to Him.”

And Allah Most High says:

“So do not set up rivals to Allah while you know.”

And Allah Most High says:

“Among people are those who take others besides Allah as equals, loving them as they love Allah. But those who believe are stronger in their love for Allah.”

And Allah Most High says:

“They have made the jinn partners with Allah, though He created them, and have falsely attributed sons and daughters to Him without knowledge. Glory be to Him, and exalted is He above what

---

## Page 882

they attribute to Him.”

“Originator of the heavens and the earth! How could He have a child when He has no consort? He created all things, and He has knowledge of all things.”

Allah Most High says:

“Blessed is He who sent down the Criterion upon His servant, so that he may be a warner to all worlds—He to whom belongs the dominion of the heavens and the earth, who has taken no child and has no partner in His dominion.”

Allah Most High says:

“Ask them: Does your Lord have daughters, while they have sons? Or did We create the angels as females while they were witnesses? Indeed, out of their falsehood they say, ‘Allah has begotten,’ and they are surely liars. Has He chosen daughters over sons? What is wrong with you? How do you judge? Will you not take heed? Or do you have clear authority? Then bring your scripture, if you are truthful. They have claimed a kinship between Him and the jinn, but the jinn know that they will surely be brought forth. Glory be to Allah, far above what they attribute to Him—except Allah’s chosen servants.”

Up to His words:

“Glory be to your Lord, the Lord of Might, far above what they attribute to Him. Peace be upon the messengers. And all praise is due to Allah, Lord of all worlds.”

Thus He declares Himself far above what the fabricators who associate partners with Him attribute to Him. He pronounces peace upon the messengers because their words are free of falsehood and the association of partners with Allah. And He praises Himself, for He, glorified be He, deserves all praise for His names and attributes and the wonders of His creation.

As for (detailed affirmation: He has mentioned His names and attributes in the clear verses He has revealed, as in His words:

“Allah—there is no deity except Him, the Ever-Living, the Sustainer of all”—the verse in its entirety.

And His words:

“Say: He is Allah, the One. Allah, the Eternal Refuge”—the surah in its entirety.

“And He is the All-Knowing, the All-Wise.”

“And He is the All-Knowing, the All-Powerful.”

“And He is the All-Hearing, the All-Seeing.”

“And He is the Almighty, the All-Wise.”

“And He is the All-Forgiving, the Most Merciful.”

## Page 884

—exalted is Allah above whatever they associate with Him.”

“He is Allah, the Creator, the Originator, the Fashioner. To Him belong the most beautiful names. Whatever is in the heavens and the earth glorifies Him, and He is the Almighty, the All-Wise.”

There are many other such verses and authentic hadiths from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, concerning the names and attributes of the Lord, Exalted is He. They affirm His Being and attributes in detail and establish His oneness by denying that anything is like Him. Through this, Allah has guided His servants to the straight path. This is the way of the messengers, may Allah’s blessings and peace be upon them all.

As for those who have deviated and turned away from their path—among the disbelievers, the polytheists, those who were given the Scripture, and those included among them, such as the Sabians, the philosophers, the Jahmiyyah, the Qarmatians, the Bātiniyyah, and others like them—their approach is the opposite. They describe Him through detailed negations and affirm only an absolute existence that, upon examination, has no reality. It amounts merely to an existence in the mind that cannot be realized in the external world. Their position entails the utmost denial of His attributes and the utmost likening of Him to other things: they liken Him to impossibilities, nonexistent things, and inanimate objects, and deny His names and attributes in a manner that entails denying His very Being.

The extremists among them deny both sides of a contradiction with regard to Him, saying: “He is neither existent nor nonexistent, neither living nor dead, neither knowing nor ignorant.” For they claim that describing Him in affirmative terms would liken Him to existing things, while describing Him in negative terms would liken Him to nonexistent things.

---

## Page 885

and thus they negated both sides of the contradiction, which is impossible according to the self-evident principles of reason. They distorted the Scripture Allah revealed and what the Messenger brought, thereby falling into something worse than what they sought to escape. For they likened Him to impossibilities: denying both sides of a contradiction is just as impossible as affirming both together. It is necessarily known that existence requires an originator whose existence is necessary in itself, who is independent of everything else, beginningless and eternal, and for whom neither coming into being nor nonexistence is possible. Yet they described Him as that which cannot possibly exist, let alone be necessary, existent, or beginningless.

A group of philosophers and their followers approached this position. They described Him through negations and relations, without affirming attributes, and identified Him with absolute existence subject to the condition of absoluteness. Clear reason establishes that this exists only in the mind, not among things existing outside it. They identified the attribute with the one possessing it, making knowledge identical to the knower in defiance of self-evident propositions. They also identified one attribute with another, making no distinction between knowledge, power, and will, thereby denying truths known by necessity.

A third group approached their position: the speculative theologians among the Muʿtazilah and their followers. They affirmed Allah’s names without affirming the attributes those names entail. Some treated the All-Knowing, the All-Powerful, the All-Hearing, and the All-Seeing as merely synonymous proper names. Others said: “All-Knowing without knowledge, All-Powerful without power, All-Hearing and All-Seeing without hearing or sight.” Thus they affirmed the name without affirming the attributes it entails.

---

## Page 886

The falsity of their position and its incompatibility with clear reason—which accords with authentic transmitted revelation—have been discussed elsewhere, beyond these remarks. All these groups flee from something only to fall into its like and into something worse, besides the distortion and denial of divine attributes that their positions entail. Had they examined the matter closely, they would have treated like things alike and distinguished between unlike things, as reason requires. They would have been among those given knowledge, who see that what has been revealed to the Messenger is the truth from his Lord and guides to the path of the Almighty, the Praiseworthy. Instead, they embrace obscurities disguised as rational truths: they practice sophistry in rational matters and treat revelation in the manner of the Qarmatians.

For it is self-evident to reason that there must be a beginningless existent, independent of everything else. We observe things coming into being, such as animals, minerals, and plants. Whatever comes into being is contingent: its existence is neither necessary nor impossible. It is necessarily known that whatever comes into being must have an originator, and whatever is contingent must have one who brings it into existence. As Allah Most High says:

“Or were they created by nothing, or are they the creators?”

Since they were neither created without a creator nor the creators of themselves, it necessarily follows that they have a Creator who created them.

Since it is necessarily known that among existing things there is that which is beginningless and necessarily existent in itself, and that which comes into being and is contingent, admitting both existence and nonexistence, it is known that the former exists, and the latter

---

## Page 887

exists. Their sharing in what is designated by “existence” does not entail that the existence of one is like the existence of the other. Rather, each has an existence peculiar to it. Their sharing a general name does not imply that they are alike in what that name designates when attributed to a particular subject, specified, and qualified, nor in any other respect.

No rational person, upon being told that the Throne is an existing thing and that a mosquito is an existing thing, would say that one is like the other merely because both are designated by “thing” and “existence.” There is no independently existing thing outside the mind, distinct from the two, in which they both share. Rather, the mind abstracts a shared, universal meaning, which is what the unrestricted name designates. When we say that this exists and that exists, the existence of each is peculiar to it, and no other shares in that existence, even though the name genuinely applies to each.

For this reason, Allah has given Himself names and has given His attributes names. When attributed to Him, those names are specific to Him, and no other shares in them. He has also given some of His creatures names which, when attributed to them, are specific to them. These correspond to the divine names when detached from attribution and specification. Yet the correspondence between the two names, and the likeness and identity of what they designate when used without restriction and stripped of attribution and specification, do not entail that they correspond or designate like realities when attributed to particular subjects and applied specifically—still less that what they then designate is one and the same.

Thus Allah has called Himself living, saying:

“Allah—there is no deity except Him, the Ever-Living, the Sustainer of all existence.”

He has also called some of His servants living, saying:

“He brings the living out of the dead and brings the dead out of the living.”

Yet the living one in the first passage is not like the living one in the second, because His expression “the Ever-Living” is a name for Allah, specific to Him, whereas His words:

---

## Page 888

“He brings the living out of the dead” contain a name for the created living being, specific to it. The two names correspond only when used without restriction and stripped of specification. Yet the unrestricted term has no independently existing referent outside the mind. Rather, the mind understands from it a meaning shared by the two named beings. When the term is applied specifically, the mind qualifies that meaning by what distinguishes the Creator from the creature and the creature from the Creator. This distinction is necessary for all of Allah’s names and attributes: one must understand both the shared, common meaning conveyed by the name and the meaning conveyed when it is attributed to a particular subject and made specific to it. Such specificity precludes any creature from sharing in anything peculiar to the Creator—glorified and exalted is He.

Likewise, Allah has called Himself All-Knowing and Forbearing, and He has called some of His servants knowing, saying:

“And they gave him good tidings of a learned boy,”

meaning Isaac. He called another forbearing, saying:

“So We gave him good tidings of a forbearing boy,”

meaning Ishmael. Yet the All-Knowing is not like the knowing servant, nor the Forbearing like the forbearing servant.

He has called Himself All-Hearing and All-Seeing, saying:

“Indeed, Allah commands you to render trusts to those entitled to them, and when you judge between people, to judge with justice. Excellent indeed is what Allah instructs you to do. Indeed, Allah is ever All-Hearing, All-Seeing.”

He has also described some of His servants as hearing and seeing, saying:

“Indeed, We created man from a mixed drop of fluid to test him, and We made him hearing and seeing.”

Yet the All-Hearing is not like the hearing servant, nor the All-Seeing like the seeing servant.

He has called Himself Compassionate and Merciful, saying:

“Indeed, Allah is truly Compassionate and Merciful to people.”

He has also described some of His servants as compassionate and merciful, saying:

“There has certainly come to you a Messenger from among yourselves; grievous

---

Yet the All-Mighty is not like the human dignitary.

Yet the Compeller is not like the domineering creature, nor the Supreme like the arrogant creature. There are numerous other examples of this.

And:

“Above every possessor of knowledge is one more knowledgeable.”

And:

“They rejoiced in the knowledge they possessed.”

And:

“Allah is the One who

created you in weakness, then after weakness gave you strength, then after strength gave you weakness and gray hair.”

He also says:

That is, endowed with power. Yet Allah’s knowledge is not like a creature’s knowledge, nor His strength like a creature’s strength.

“You desire the fleeting gains of this world, while Allah desires the Hereafter. And Allah is All-Mighty, All-Wise.”

“Allah will bring forth a people whom He loves and who love Him.”

“Say, ‘If you love Allah, then follow me; Allah will love you.’”

“Allah is pleased with them, and they are pleased with Him.”

It is well known that Allah’s will is not like His servant’s will, nor His intention like His servant’s intention, nor His love like His servant’s love, nor His pleasure like His servant’s pleasure.

Likewise, He has described Himself as abhorring the disbelievers and has attributed abhorrence to them, saying:

“Those who disbelieved will be called out to: ‘Allah’s abhorrence of you was greater than your abhorrence of yourselves, when you were called to faith but disbelieved.’”

Yet Allah’s abhorrence is not like theirs.

“Do not keep your hand shackled to your neck, nor extend it completely.”

Yet Allah’s hand is not like His creatures’ hands, nor is His extending His hands like theirs. And if extending the hands is intended to mean giving and generosity, then Allah’s giving is not like His creatures’ giving, nor is His generosity like theirs. There are many similar examples.

---

## Page 893

Thus, we must affirm what Allah has affirmed for Himself and deny that He resembles His creation. Whoever says, “Allah has no knowledge, strength, mercy, or speech; He neither loves nor is pleased, nor has He called out, spoken privately, or risen over the Throne,” denies and rejects His attributes, likening Allah to nonexistent things and inanimate objects. Whoever says, “He has knowledge like my knowledge, strength like my strength, love like my love, pleasure like my pleasure, two hands like my two hands, or a rising over the Throne like my own settling,” likens Allah to His creation, equating Him with living creatures.

Rather, we must affirm His attributes without likening Him to His creation, and declare Him free of imperfection without denying His attributes. This will be made clear (through two noble principles), two illustrative analogies—and to Allah belongs the highest description—and (through a comprehensive conclusion).

---

## Page 894

Section

As for the two principles, the first is this:

What is said of some attributes applies equally to others.

If the person being addressed holds that Allah is living by virtue of life, knowing by virtue of knowledge, powerful by virtue of power, hearing by virtue of hearing, seeing by virtue of sight, speaking by virtue of speech, and willing by virtue of will, and regards all of this as literal, yet disputes His love, pleasure, anger, and aversion, treating these as figurative and interpreting them either as will or as certain created things, such as blessings and punishments, then he is told: “There is no difference between what you have denied and what you have affirmed. Rather, what is said of one applies equally to the other.”

If you say, “His will is like the will of created beings,” then the same applies to His love, pleasure, and anger—and this is precisely likening Allah to His creation. But if you say, “He has a will befitting Him, just as a created being has a will befitting it,” you are told: “Likewise, He has love befitting Him, and a created being has love befitting it. He has pleasure and anger befitting Him, and a created being has pleasure and anger befitting it.”

If you say, “Anger is the boiling of the blood in the heart in pursuit of vengeance,” the reply is: “And will is

---

## Page 895

the soul’s inclination to obtain benefit or avert harm.” If you say, “That is the will of a created being,” you are told: “And this is the anger of a created being.” The same reasoning necessarily applies to His speech, hearing, sight, knowledge, and power. If what is denied of Him in anger, love, pleasure, and the like is that which is peculiar to created beings, then this is likewise excluded from hearing, sight, speech, and all the attributes. If he says, “These have no reality other than what is peculiar to created beings, so they must be denied of Him,” he is told: “The same would apply to hearing, sight, speech, knowledge, and power.”

Thus, the person who distinguishes between some attributes and others is answered concerning what he denies with the very argument he uses against his opponent concerning what he affirms. When a Muʿtazilī says, “He has neither will nor speech subsisting in Him, because these attributes subsist only in created beings,” this person explains to the Muʿtazilī that the Eternal possesses these attributes, yet they are not like the attributes of things brought into existence. Those who affirm the remaining attributes, such as love, pleasure, and the like, make the same argument to him.

If he says, “I have established those attributes through reason, because an act that comes into existence indicates power, specification indicates will, and precision indicates knowledge. These attributes necessarily imply life, and a living being cannot be without hearing, sight, and speech, or their opposites,”

---

## Page 896

the others who affirm the divine attributes say to him:

“There are two replies to your argument.

The first is that the absence of a particular proof does not entail the absence of what it would prove. Suppose that the rational argument you have followed does not establish these attributes: it does not negate them either. You have no right to deny them without evidence, for the burden of proof rests on the one who denies just as it does on the one who affirms. Revelation has established them, and no rational or revealed evidence contradicts this. We must therefore affirm what is established by evidence unopposed by counter-evidence of comparable force.

The second is that these attributes can be established through rational arguments analogous to those used to establish the others. Thus, His benefiting His servants through kindness to them indicates mercy, just as specification indicates will. His honoring the obedient indicates His love for them, and His punishment of the disbelievers indicates His hatred of them—as His honoring of His allies and punishment of His enemies have been established through witnessed evidence and reports. The praiseworthy ends of His works and commands—that is, the commendable outcomes to which His works and commands ultimately lead—indicate His consummate wisdom, just as specification indicates will, and indeed more strongly, because of the strength of final causation.

This is why the Quran explains the blessings and wisdom in His creation more extensively than it explains how His creation indicates will alone.”

---

## Page 897

If, however, the person being addressed denies the attributes but acknowledges the names—as does the Muʿtazilī who says that He is Living, All-Knowing and All-Powerful, yet denies that He possesses life, knowledge and power—he should be told:

“There is no difference between affirming the names and affirming the attributes. If you say, ‘Affirming life, knowledge and power entails likening Him to creation or attributing corporeality to Him, because in the observable world we find nothing possessing these attributes except bodies,’ you are told, ‘Likewise, in the observable world we find nothing called living, knowing or powerful except bodies.’ If you deny these attributes because you have observed them only in bodies, then deny the names too—indeed, deny everything—for you observe it only in bodies. Every argument used by someone who denies the attributes can also be used by someone who denies the Most Beautiful Names. Whatever answer you give to the latter is also available to those who affirm the attributes.”

If the person being addressed is among the extremists who deny both the names and the attributes, and says:

“I do not say that He exists, or that He is living, knowing or powerful. Rather, these names belong to His creatures, for they are figurative: affirming them would entail likening Him to that which exists, lives and knows,”

he should be told:

“Likewise, if you say that He does not exist and is neither living, knowing nor powerful, you would be likening Him to nonexistent things, which is even worse than likening Him to existing things.”

If he says, “I deny both negation and affirmation,” he should be told:

“Then you must liken Him to impossible things in which two contradictories coincide, for it is impossible for a thing to be both existent and nonexistent

---

## Page 898

or neither existent nor nonexistent. It is impossible for a thing to be described as combining existence and nonexistence, life and death, or knowledge and ignorance; likewise, it is impossible to describe it as having neither existence nor nonexistence, neither life nor death, and neither knowledge nor ignorance.”

If you say, “Denying both contradictories is impossible only where the subject is capable of either. These pairs are opposed as privation and possession, not as negation and affirmation. A wall, for example, is called neither blind nor sighted, neither living nor dead, because it is not capable of either,” you are told:

“First, this does not hold for existence and nonexistence. All rational people agree that these are opposed as negation and affirmation: denying either necessarily establishes the other. As for what you have mentioned concerning life and death, and knowledge and ignorance, this is a terminological convention adopted by the Peripatetic philosophers. Verbal conventions are no grounds for denying rational truths. Allah Most High has said:

‘And those they invoke other than Allah create nothing, and they themselves are created.’

‘They are dead, not alive, and they do not perceive when they will be resurrected.’

“He thus called inanimate things dead. This usage is well known in Arabic and other languages.”

Second, you are told:

“That which cannot be characterized by life or death, blindness or sight, or similar opposing attributes is more deficient than that which can. A blind person who is capable of sight is more perfect than an inanimate thing incapable of either. You have therefore fled from likening Him to living creatures capable of possessing attributes of perfection, only to describe Him with the attributes of inanimate things incapable of possessing them.

---

## Page 899

Furthermore, what is incapable of either existence or nonexistence is a greater impossibility than what is capable of either—indeed, even greater than combining existence and nonexistence or denying both together. What you declare incapable of either existence or nonexistence is therefore more impossible than what you describe as neither existent nor nonexistent. If the latter is impossible according to the clear dictates of reason, the former is still more impossible. You have thus made necessary existence, which cannot admit nonexistence, the greatest of all impossibilities. This is the utmost contradiction and invalidity.

Among these Bātiniyyah (esotericists), some explicitly deny both contradictories: existence and nonexistence. Denying both is like affirming both together. As for someone who says, ‘I affirm neither,’ his refusal to affirm either as an actual reality does not prevent one of them from being an actual reality. It is merely like the ignorance of an ignorant person, or the silence of someone who says nothing and does not express the facts.

If what is incapable of either existence or nonexistence is a greater impossibility than what is supposed capable of either—even while both are denied of it—then what is supposed incapable of life or death, knowledge or ignorance, power or incapacity, speech or muteness, blindness or sight, hearing or deafness is closer to what is nonexistent and impossible than what is supposed capable of either member of these pairs—even while both are denied of it. Accordingly, denying both while allowing the capacity for either is closer to existence and possibility.

Whatever is possible for the Necessary Existent—given His capacity for it—is necessary for Him, because His attributes do not depend on anything other than Him. Thus, if that capacity is possible, it is necessary; and if the existence of that capacity is possible, it is necessary. This has been explained at length elsewhere, and it has been shown that He must possess attributes of perfection that involve no deficiency whatsoever.”

He is also told:

“Two named entities sharing some names and attributes does not constitute

---

## Page 900

the kind of resemblance and equivalence ruled out by revealed and rational evidence. Rather, that evidence rules out anything entailing that the two share what is exclusive to the Creator—what is uniquely necessary, possible, or impossible for Him. No creature can share any of this with Him, nor does any creature share any of His exclusive characteristics—glorified and exalted is He. What you have denied, however, is established by revelation and reason. Your calling it resemblance and corporealism merely deceives the ignorant, who suppose that any meaning someone labels in this way must be denied.

Were this acceptable, every proponent of falsehood could give the truth names that repel some people, so that people would reject the truth known through revelation and reason. By this method, heretics have corrupted the reason and religion of groups of people, leading them into the gravest unbelief and ignorance and the deepest error and misguidance.”

If those who deny the attributes say, “Affirming knowledge, power, and will entails a multiplicity of attributes, and this is an impossible composition,” they are told:

“When you say, ‘He is existent, necessary, an intellect, a subject of intellection, an object of intellection, a lover, a beloved, pleasurable, one who experiences pleasure, and pleasure itself,’ is not the meaning understood from this the same as the meaning understood from that?

“These are multiple meanings, distinct from one another in the mind. According to you, this is composition, yet you affirm it and call it monotheism.”

If they say, “This is monotheism in reality, not an impossible composition,” they are told: “The essence’s possession of attributes inseparable from it is likewise monotheism in reality, not an impossible composition.”

---

## Page 901

and this is because it is evident to sound reason that a thing’s possessing knowledge does not mean the same as its possessing power, nor is its very essence identical to its possessing knowledge and power.

Anyone who allows that an attribute may be identical to the entity possessing it is among the greatest sophists. Moreover, he contradicts himself: if he allows this, he must also allow the existence of one thing to be identical to the existence of another. Existence would then be one and the same in numerical identity, not merely in kind. Thus, if the existence of a contingent being were identical to the existence of the Necessary Being, the existence of every creature—which ceases to be when its existence ends and comes into being after having been nonexistent—would be the very existence of the True One, who is beginningless, everlasting, and enduring, and cannot cease to exist. On this supposition, necessary existence would be characterized by every kind of resemblance and corporeality, every deficiency and every flaw. This is precisely what the proponents of the unity of existence openly declare, having carried this corrupt principle through to its conclusions.

The claims of those who deny the attributes are therefore false on every supposition. This is a principle that applies consistently: whenever anyone denies an attribute conveyed by the Messenger in order to escape something objectionable, he has already affirmed something that entails the counterpart of what he sought to escape. Ultimately, he must affirm a necessary, beginningless existent possessing attributes that distinguish Him from all else, without resembling His creation in those attributes. He is therefore told: the same reasoning applies to all the attributes. Every name and attribute you affirm must indicate some meaning shared by the things so designated; otherwise, the discourse could not be understood. Yet we know that what is exclusive to Allah and distinguishes Him from His creation is greater than anything that comes to mind or can be conceived in the imagination.

---

## Page 902

This becomes clear through the second principle, which states:

What is said of the attributes applies equally to the essence.

For there is nothing like Allah, whether in His essence, His attributes, or His actions. If He has a real essence unlike all other essences, then that essence possesses real attributes unlike all other attributes.

If someone asks, “How did He rise over the Throne?” he is given the answer expressed by Rabī'a, Mālik, and others—may Allah be pleased with them both:

“Rising over the Throne is known; its manner is unknown. Belief in it is obligatory, and asking about its manner is an innovation.”

This is because the question concerns something human beings do not know and cannot answer.

Likewise, if he asks, “How does our Lord descend to the lowest heaven?” he is asked, “What is the manner of His being?” If he replies, “I do not know the manner of His being,” he is told that we likewise do not know the manner of His descent. Knowing the manner of an attribute necessarily requires knowing the manner of the one who possesses it; the former derives from and depends upon the latter. How, then, can you demand that I know the manner of His hearing, His sight, His speaking, His rising over the Throne, and His descent, when you do not know the manner of His essence?

And if you acknowledge that He has an objectively existing reality that necessarily entails the attributes of perfection

---

## Page 903

which nothing resembles, then His hearing, sight, speech, descent, and rising over the Throne likewise have an objective reality. He possesses attributes of perfection, and the hearing, sight, speech, descent, and rising of created beings do not resemble His. This argument is binding upon them both in matters of reason and in their interpretation of revelation.

For if someone affirms one thing and denies another on rational grounds, he faces the same implications regarding the attributes conveyed by the Quran and Sunnah that he denies as he does regarding those he affirms. If asked to distinguish between what he considers objectionable in either case, he would find no difference between them. Thus, those who deny some attributes but not others—requiring, for those they deny, either consigning the matter to Allah or interpreting the wording contrary to what it entails—have no consistent rule.

If they are asked, “Why have you reinterpreted this but acknowledged that, when the same question applies to both?” they have no sound answer. Such is their inconsistency in denial; likewise, they are inconsistent in affirmation.

For those who interpret the texts as conveying one of the meanings they affirm, when they divert a text from the meaning it entails to another meaning, the meaning to which they divert it carries the same implications as the meaning from which they diverted it. Thus, if someone says, “His love, pleasure, anger, and displeasure mean His will to reward and punish,” the implications he faces concerning will are analogous to those he faces concerning love, hatred, pleasure, and displeasure.

---

## Page 904

And if he were to interpret these as His created effects—namely, the reward and punishment He creates—he would face the same implications he sought to escape. For an act must first subsist in its agent, and reward and punishment, as effects produced, are only in response to deeds that the One who rewards and punishes loves and is pleased with, or is displeased with and hates. Thus, if they affirm His action in the same manner as a human servant’s action is understood in the observable world, they liken Him to creation; but if they affirm it in a manner unlike that, the same applies to His attributes.

---

## Page 905

Section

As for the two illustrative examples:

Allah—glorified and exalted is He—has told us of the created things in Paradise: its various foods, garments, marital companionship, and dwellings. He has told us that it contains milk, honey, wine, water, meat, silk, gold, silver, fruit, maidens with beautiful eyes, and palaces. Ibn ‘Abbās, may Allah be pleased with them both, said: “Nothing of what is in Paradise exists in this world except its names.”

If those realities of which Allah has told us share their names with realities found in this world, yet are not like them—rather, the differences between them are known only to Allah Most High—then the Creator—glorified and exalted is He—differs far more from created beings than one created being differs from another. His difference from His creation is greater than the difference between what exists in the Hereafter and what exists in this world. For a created being is closer to another created being with which it shares a name than the Creator is to a created being. This is plain and clear.

Accordingly, people have divided into three groups on this matter:

The early generations, the leading scholars, and their followers believed what Allah has told us about Himself and about the Day

---

## Page 906

of Judgment, while recognizing the difference between what exists in this world and what exists in the Hereafter, and that Allah’s difference from His creation is greater still.

The second group affirmed what Allah has told us about reward and punishment in the Hereafter, but denied much of what He has told us about His attributes. These include certain groups of speculative theologians.

The third group denied both, such as the Qarmatians, the Bātiniyyah (esotericists), philosophers who followed the Peripatetic school, and similar heretics who deny the realities of what Allah has told us about Himself and the Day of Judgment.

Many of them also treat commands and prohibitions in the same way. They claim that prescribed religious duties and forbidden acts have hidden interpretations contrary to what Muslims understand by them. Thus they reinterpret the five daily prayers, fasting during the month of Ramadan, and pilgrimage to the House. They say that the five daily prayers mean knowing their secrets, that fasting during Ramadan means concealing their secrets, and that pilgrimage to the House means travelling to their shaykhs.

These and similar interpretations are self-evidently lies and fabrications against the Messengers—may Allah’s blessings be upon them—and distortions of the words of Allah and His Messenger from their proper meanings, as well as deviations from the truth concerning Allah’s signs.

They may also say that religious laws are binding upon ordinary people but not the elite. Thus, when a man becomes

---

## Page 907

one of their gnostics, accomplished masters, and monotheists, they release him from religious obligations and permit him to commit forbidden acts. Some who identify with Sufism and spiritual wayfaring may adopt certain aspects of these doctrines. These Bātiniyyah are the heretics whom Muslims unanimously regard as greater disbelievers than Jews and Christians.

The arguments that those who believe and affirm Allah’s attributes use against these heretics can also be used against anyone who shares in some of their heresy. Thus, if one affirms Allah’s attributes while denying that He resembles created beings—as the clear verses indicate—this is the truth that accords with both reason and revelation and demolishes the foundations of heresy and misguidance.

Allah, glorified is He, must not be the subject of comparisons that liken Him to His creation, for Allah has no equal. Rather, His is “the highest attribute.” Therefore, He and created beings must not be placed together in an analogy, nor in a syllogism whose members are treated as equal. Rather, “the highest attribute” applies to Him: whatever perfection a created being possesses, the Creator is more entitled to it; and whatever deficiency a created being is held to be free of, the Creator is more entitled to be declared free of it.

Thus, if a created being is unlike another created being despite sharing the same name, the Creator is all the more entitled to be declared unlike created beings, even when the same name is used. The same reasoning applies to the second illustration,

---

## Page 908

namely, the spirit within us, which has been described in terms of affirmative and negative attributes. The scriptural texts tell us that it ascends and rises from one heaven to another, and that it is taken from the body and drawn out of it as a hair is drawn out of dough. People hold conflicting views about it.

Some groups of speculative theologians regard it as a part of the body or one of its attributes. Some say that it is the soul or the air that circulates within the body; others say that it is life, temperament, or the body itself.

Some groups of philosophers describe it in the same terms they use to describe what they regard as the Necessary Existent—descriptions that could apply only to something whose existence is impossible. They say that it is neither inside the body nor outside it, neither separate from it nor interpenetrating it, neither moving nor at rest; that it neither ascends nor descends, and is neither a body nor an accident. They may also say that it does not apprehend particular things or realities existing outside the mind, but apprehends only abstract universals.

They may say that it is neither inside the world nor outside it, neither separate from it nor interpenetrating it. Sometimes they say that it is neither inside the bodies of the world nor outside them, while defining a body as that which cannot be pointed to by the senses. Thus, they describe it as something that cannot be pointed to, and assign it similar negative attributes that place it alongside the nonexistent and the impossible.

---

## Page 909

And when they are told, “To affirm the existence of such a thing is impossible according to the self-evident dictates of reason,” they reply, “Rather, it is possible, as shown by the fact that universals are possible and do exist, yet cannot be pointed to.” They overlook the fact that universals exist as universals only in the mind, not in external reality. Thus, their claims about origination and the final return rest on fantasies whose falsity is evident even to most ignorant people.

There is much inconsistency concerning the spirit among both those who negate and those who affirm. The reason is that the spirit—which the philosophers call the rational soul—is not of the same kind as this body, nor of the same kind as the elements or the things generated from them. Rather, it belongs to another kind, distinct from all these. Consequently, some know it only through negative descriptions that distinguish it from observable bodies, while others classify it as belonging to the same kind as observable bodies. Both views are mistaken.

An unqualified assertion that the spirit is a body, or that it is not a body, requires clarification. People assign the word “body” various technical meanings that differ from its linguistic meaning. According to the authorities on language, it means the physical body. In this sense, the spirit is not a body, which is why people speak of “the spirit and the body.” As Allah Most High says:

“When you see them, their bodies please you, and if they speak, you listen to their speech.”

And He Most High says:

“And has increased him abundantly in knowledge and stature.”

---

## Page 910

As for the speculative theologians, some define a body as anything that exists; others, as that which subsists in itself; others, as that which is composed of atoms; and others, as that which is composed of matter and form. All these maintain that it can be pointed to by a sensory gesture. Others say that it is composed of neither, but is something that can be pointed to and described as being here or there.

Accordingly, if the spirit can be pointed to and the deceased’s gaze follows it—as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“When the spirit departs, the gaze follows it, and it is taken and raised to heaven”

—then the spirit is a body according to this technical usage.

The point is that if the spirit exists, is alive, possesses knowledge, power, hearing and sight, ascends and descends, comes and goes, and has other such attributes, human minds nevertheless cannot determine its precise nature or define it, because they have never observed anything comparable to it. A thing’s reality can only be apprehended by observing it or observing something comparable to it.

Thus, if the spirit possesses these attributes without resembling the creatures that we observe, then the Creator is all the more distinct from His creatures, while possessing the names and attributes that rightfully belong to Him. Those endowed with reason are even less capable of defining Him or determining the manner of His being than they are of defining the spirit or determining the manner of its being.

---

## Page 911

If, then, whoever denies the spirit’s attributes is a denier who divests it of its attributes, and whoever likens it to the creatures he observes is ignorant, likening it to something unlike it—while the spirit nevertheless truly exists and rightfully possesses its attributes—then this applies with even greater force to the Creator, glorified and exalted is He: whoever denies His attributes is a denier who divests Him of His attributes, and whoever compares Him to His creation is ignorant of Him and likens Him to His creatures.” He, glorified and exalted is He, truly exists and rightfully possesses His names and attributes.

---

## Page 912

Section

As for the comprehensive conclusion, it contains useful principles.

The First Principle

Allah, glorified is He, is described through affirmation and negation. Affirmation includes His declaration that He knows all things, has power over all things, and is All-Hearing, All-Seeing, and other such descriptions. Negation includes His saying: “Neither drowsiness nor sleep overtakes Him.”

It should be understood that negation conveys neither praise nor perfection unless it entails affirmation. Mere negation, otherwise, conveys neither praise nor perfection, for pure negation is pure nonexistence. Pure nonexistence is nothing, and what is nothing is, as has been said, nothing—let alone a source of praise or perfection. Moreover, pure negation can describe both the nonexistent and the impossible, neither of which can be described as possessing anything praiseworthy or perfect.

---

## Page 913

For this reason, the descriptions by which Allah characterizes Himself through negation generally entail the affirmation of something praiseworthy, as in His saying:

“Allah—there is no deity except Him, the Ever-Living, the Self-Subsisting Sustainer of all. Neither drowsiness nor sleep overtakes Him,”

through to His saying:

“And preserving them both does not weary Him.”

Negating drowsiness and sleep entails the perfection of His life and His self-subsistence and sustaining of all. It thus makes clear the perfection of His being the Ever-Living, the Self-Subsisting Sustainer of all. Likewise, His saying:

“And preserving them both does not weary Him”

means that it causes Him neither hardship nor burden. This entails the perfection and completeness of His power, unlike a created being who possesses power but can accomplish something only with effort and difficulty: this is a deficiency in his power and a defect in his strength. Likewise, His saying:

“Not an atom’s weight in the heavens or on earth escapes Him.”

Negating anything’s escaping Him entails His knowledge of every atom in the heavens and on earth. Likewise, His saying:

“We certainly created the heavens and the earth and everything between them in six days, and no weariness touched Us.”

Negating any touch of weariness—which means fatigue and exhaustion—indicates perfect power and utmost strength, unlike a created being, who is subject to fatigue and exhaustion. Likewise, His saying:

“Vision does not encompass Him.”

He negated only apprehension in the sense of encompassing Him, as most scholars have explained; He did not negate seeing Him as such. For what does not exist cannot be seen, and there is nothing praiseworthy in its not being seen. Were that so, what does not exist would be praiseworthy. Rather, what is praiseworthy is that He cannot be encompassed even when He is seen, just as He cannot be encompassed even when He is known. Thus, just as knowing Him does not mean encompassing Him in knowledge, seeing Him does not mean encompassing Him in vision.

---

## Page 914

Thus, negating encompassing Him affirms His greatness in a way that constitutes praise and an attribute of perfection. This is evidence affirming that He is seen, not denying it; rather, it affirms that He is seen without being encompassed. This is the truth upon which the early generations of the Muslim community and its leading scholars agreed. If you reflect on this, you will find that every negation that entails no affirmation is something with which Allah has not described Himself.

Those who describe Him only through negations have not, in reality, affirmed a praiseworthy deity—or even an existing one. The same applies to those who share some of their views, such as those who say that He does not speak, does not see, is not above the world, or did not rise over the Throne. They say that He is neither within the world nor outside it, neither separate from the world nor occupying the same place as it. These descriptions can apply to what does not exist, and they entail no affirmative attribute. For this reason, Mahmūd ibn Sabuktakīn said to someone who made such claims about the Creator: “Distinguish for us between this Lord whom you affirm and what does not exist.” Likewise, saying that He does not speak or does not descend contains no attribute of praise or perfection.

Rather, these descriptions liken Him to deficient or nonexistent things. Some of them apply only to what does not exist, while others apply only to inanimate or deficient things. Whoever says, “He is neither separate from the world nor within it,” is like someone who says, “He neither subsists by Himself nor through another; He is neither beginningless nor originated; and He neither precedes the world nor is contemporaneous with it.”

---

## Page 915

And whoever says, “He is neither living nor dead, nor does He hear, see, or speak,” must consequently regard Him as dead, deaf, blind, and mute.

If he says, “Blindness is the absence of sight in something capable by its nature of possessing sight; something incapable of sight, such as a wall, is called neither blind nor seeing,” he is answered: “This is a convention you have adopted. Otherwise, whatever is described as lacking life, hearing, sight, and speech can be described as possessing death, blindness, muteness, and inability to articulate speech. Moreover, everything that exists can possess these attributes and their opposites, for Allah is able to make inanimate things living, just as He turned Moses’ staff into a serpent that swallowed the ropes and staffs. Furthermore, that which cannot possess these attributes is more deficient than that which cannot possess them while possessing their opposites.”

An inanimate thing described as neither seeing nor blind, neither speaking nor mute, is more deficient than a living being that is blind and mute. Thus, to say that the Creator cannot possess these attributes is to ascribe a greater deficiency to Him than to describe Him as mute, blind, deaf, or the like. Moreover, to regard Him as incapable of possessing them is to liken Him to an inanimate thing incapable of possessing any of them. This likens Him to inanimate things, not to living creatures. How, then, can someone who says this [object to] others for what he claims amounts to likening Him to a living being?

---

## Page 916

Moreover, the very negation of these attributes is a deficiency, just as their affirmation is a perfection. Life, considered in itself and without regard to the particular being possessing it, is an attribute of perfection. The same is true of knowledge, power, hearing, sight, speech, action, and similar attributes. Whatever constitutes an attribute of perfection, He—glory be to Him—is more deserving of possessing it than created beings. If He did not possess it while a created being did, that created being would be more perfect than He.

Know that the thoroughgoing Jahmiyyah, such as the Qarmatians and those who resemble them, deny that He, the Exalted, possesses either of two contradictory attributes. They even say, “He is neither existent nor non-existent, neither living nor not living.” Yet it is self-evident to reason that possessing neither of two contradictories is impossible, just as possessing both is impossible.

Others describe Him solely in terms of negation, saying, “He is neither living, nor hearing, nor seeing.” These are greater in disbelief than the former in one respect, while the former are greater in disbelief than these in another. When these people are told, “This entails describing Him with the contradictory attributes, such as death, deafness, and muteness,” they reply, “That would follow only if He were capable of possessing such attributes.” This defense only makes their position more unsound.

The same applies to those who resemble them—namely, those who say, “He is neither within the world nor outside it.” When they are told, “This is self-evidently impossible to reason, just as it would be to say that He is neither eternal nor originated, neither necessary nor contingent, neither self-subsisting nor subsisting in another,” they reply, “This holds only if He is capable of such attributes. Such capability belongs only to what has spatial location. Thus, if spatial location is absent, so too is the capacity to possess either of these two contradictories.”

---

## Page 917

They are therefore told: Created beings’ knowledge that it is impossible for anything to lack both of these contradictories is universal; no existing being is exempt from it. As for the “spatial location” mentioned: if it means that existing regions of space encompass Him, then this means being within the world. If it means that He is separate from created beings—that is, distinct from them and apart from them—then this means being outside the world.

Thus, “what has spatial location” sometimes refers to what is within the world and sometimes to what is outside it. To say, “He has no spatial location,” therefore means that He is neither within the world nor outside it. They have changed the wording to mislead those who do not understand the true meaning of their statement into thinking that this is a different meaning. Yet it is the very meaning whose invalidity is self-evident to reason, just as those others did when they said, “He is neither living nor dead, neither existent nor non-existent, neither knowing nor ignorant.”

---

## Page 918

The Second Principle:

Whatever the Messenger has reported about his Lord must be believed—whether we understand its meaning or not—for he is truthful and confirmed in his truthfulness.

Every believer must therefore believe whatever is stated in the Quran and Sunnah, even if he does not understand its meaning. The same applies to what has been established by the agreement of the early generations of the Muslim community and its leading scholars. Indeed, most matters in this subject are explicitly stated in the Quran and Sunnah and agreed upon by the early generations of the Muslim community.

As for expressions whose affirmation or negation has been disputed by later scholars, no one is obliged—or even permitted—to agree with anyone in affirming or denying his expression until he understands what that person intends. If he intends something true, it is accepted; if he intends something false, it is rejected. If his statement contains both truth and falsehood, it should neither be accepted without qualification nor have its entire meaning rejected. Rather, judgment on the expression should be suspended and its meaning clarified. This applies, for example, to people’s disputes over direction, spatial location, and similar matters.

The word “direction” may mean an existing entity other than Allah, in which case it is created—as when “direction” means the Throne itself or the heavens themselves. It may also mean what is not an existing entity other than Allah Most High—as when “direction” means what is above the world.

It is well known that the revealed texts neither affirm nor deny the word “direction,” whereas they do affirm His being on high, His rising over the Throne, His being above, ascent to Him, and the like. It is also known that there is no existing being

---

## Page 919

except the Creator and created beings. The Creator—glorified and exalted is He—is distinct from His creation. Nothing of His essence is within His creation, and nothing of His creation is within His essence.

Someone who denies direction should therefore be asked: Do you mean by “direction” an existing, created thing? Allah is not within created things. Or do you mean by “direction” what lies beyond the world? There is no doubt that Allah is above the world, distinct from created beings.

Likewise, someone who says that Allah is in a direction should be asked: Do you mean that Allah is above the world, or that Allah is within some created thing? If you mean the former, it is true; if you mean the latter, it is false.

The same applies to the expression “spatial location.” If someone means by it that created things contain Allah, then Allah is far greater and mightier than that. Indeed, His Kursī encompasses the heavens and the earth. Allah Most High has said:

“They have not esteemed Allah as He deserves. The whole earth will be in His grasp on the Day of Resurrection, and the heavens will be rolled up in His right hand.”

It is established in the collections of authentic hadith that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah will grasp the earth and roll up the heavens in His right hand. Then He will say: ‘I am the King! Where are the kings of the earth?’”

In another hadith:

“Indeed, He tosses it as children toss a ball.”

And in the hadith of Ibn ‘Abbās:

“The seven heavens, the seven earths, and all that they contain are, in the Hand of the Most Merciful, no more than a mustard seed in the hand of one of you.”

If, however, someone means by it that He is separate from created beings—that is, distinct from them, apart from them, and not dwelling within them—then He, glorified is He, is as the imams of the Sunnah have said: above His heavens, upon His Throne, distinct from His creation.

---

## Page 920

The Third Principle

If someone says, “The apparent meaning of the texts is intended,” or, “Their apparent meaning is not intended,” the response is: The term “apparent meaning” is ambiguous and has more than one sense. If the speaker believes that their apparent meaning likens Allah’s attributes to those of created beings, or ascribes to Him qualities peculiar to them, then there is no doubt that this is not intended.

However, the early generations and the imams did not call this their apparent meaning, nor would they accept that the apparent meaning of the Quran and hadith is disbelief and falsehood. Allah, glorified and exalted is He, is far too knowing and wise for His words describing Himself to have no apparent meaning other than disbelief or misguidance. Those who regard this as their apparent meaning err in two ways:

Sometimes they make an unsound meaning the apparent meaning of the wording, thereby treating it as requiring an interpretation contrary to its apparent meaning, when that is not the case. At other times, they reject the true meaning—which is the apparent meaning of the wording—because they believe it to be false.

(The first error is exemplified by what they say concerning His words:

“My servant, I was hungry, yet you did not feed Me,”

and the rest of the hadith; and concerning the other report:

“The Black Stone is Allah’s right hand on earth. Whoever shakes hands with it or kisses it is as though he had shaken hands with Allah and kissed His right hand.”

And His words:

“The hearts of the servants are between two fingers of the Most Merciful.”

They say: “It is known that the fingers of the True One are not within our hearts.”

---

## Page 921

So they should be told:

If you gave due consideration to what the texts signify, you would know that they indicate nothing but the truth. As for (the first example, the statement:

“The Black Stone is Allah’s right hand on earth. Whoever shakes hands with it and kisses it is as though he had shaken hands with Allah and kissed His right hand.”

This explicitly indicates that the Black Stone is neither an attribute of Allah nor His actual right hand, for it says:

“Allah’s right hand on earth,”

and:

“Whoever kisses it and shakes hands with it is as though he had shaken hands with Allah and kissed His right hand.”

It is well known that the thing being compared is not the thing to which it is compared. Thus, the report itself makes clear that whoever touches the Stone is not shaking hands with Allah, and that the Stone is not His actual right hand. How, then, can its apparent meaning be deemed disbelief on the grounds that it requires reinterpretation—especially when this report is known only from Ibn Abbās?

As for the other hadith, it appears in the Sahīh with its meaning explained:

“Allah says, ‘My servant, I was hungry, yet you did not feed Me.’ The servant replies, ‘My Lord, how could I feed You when You are the Lord of all worlds?’ He says, ‘Did you not know that My servant so-and-so was hungry? Had you fed him, you would have found that with Me. My servant, I was ill, yet you did not visit Me.’ The servant replies, ‘My Lord, how could I visit You when You are the Lord of all worlds?’ He says, ‘Did you not know that My servant so-and-so was ill? Had you visited him, you would have found Me with him.’”

This explicitly indicates that Allah, glorified is He, was neither ill nor hungry; rather, His servant was ill and His servant was hungry. He described His servant’s hunger as His own hunger and his illness as His own illness, explaining this with the words, “Had you fed him, you would have found that with Me,” and, “Had you visited him, you would have found Me with him.” Thus, no expression in the hadith remains that requires reinterpretation.

---

## Page 922

As for his statement:

“The hearts of the servants are between two fingers of the Most Merciful,”

its apparent meaning does not indicate that the heart is connected to the fingers, touches them, or contains them within it. Nor does someone’s saying, “This is between my two hands,” imply that it is in direct contact with his hands. Likewise, saying, “The clouds held in service between heaven and earth,” does not imply that they touch heaven and earth. There are many similar examples.

A similar kind of claim is to treat an expression as equivalent to another that is not equivalent to it. Thus, concerning His words:

“What prevented you from prostrating to what I created with My two hands?”

it has been said that they are equivalent to His words:

“Have they not seen that We created for them livestock from what Our hands have made?”

Yet these two expressions are not equivalent. In the latter, He attributes the action to the hands, making it similar to His words:

“Because of what your hands have earned.”

In the former, however, He attributes the action to Himself, saying:

“What I created,”

and then:

“With My two hands.”

Furthermore, in this passage He refers to His holy Self in the singular, while using the dual form for the two hands, as in His words:

“Rather, both His hands are outstretched.”

In the other passage, He attributes the hands to Himself using the plural pronoun, making it comparable to His words:

“Sailing under Our eyes.”

This use of the (plural is analogous to His words:

“In His hand is the dominion,”

and “In His hand is all good,” in the (singular. Allah, glorified and exalted is He, sometimes refers to Himself in the singular, whether by an explicit noun or a pronoun, and sometimes in the plural, as in His words:

“Indeed, We have granted you a clear victory,”

and other similar passages. He never refers to Himself in the dual, for the plural form conveys the magnification that He deserves, and may also signify the meanings of His names.

---

## Page 923

As for the dual form, it indicates a limited number, and He is exalted above that. Thus, if He said:

“What prevented you from prostrating to what I created with My two hands?”

this would not be equivalent to His words:

“From what Our hands have made.”

This is analogous to His words:

“In His hand is the dominion,”

and “In His hand is all good.” Even if He said, “I created,” using the singular form, it would differ from the other expression; how much more so when He says, “I created with My two hands,” using the dual form?

Alongside this is the evidence of the widely transmitted—indeed, mass-transmitted—hadiths, and the consensus of the early generations on what the Quran indicates, as explained at length elsewhere. One example is his statement:

“Those who act justly will be with Allah on pulpits of light, to the right of the Most Merciful—and both His hands are right hands: those who are just in their judgments, with their families, and in whatever is entrusted to them.”

There are other similar examples.

If the speaker believes that the apparent meaning of the texts whose meaning is disputed is of the same kind as the apparent meaning of those whose meaning is agreed upon—with the apparent meaning being intended in all of them—then, when Allah declared that He knows all things and has power over all things, and Ahl al-Sunnah and the leading scholars of the Muslims agreed that these statements are to be understood according to their apparent meaning and that this meaning is intended, it was understood that they did not mean that His knowledge is like our knowledge or His power like our power.

Likewise, when they agreed that He is truly living, truly knowing, and truly powerful, they did not mean that He is like a created being who possesses life, knowledge, and power. Similarly, when they say that His words, exalted is He:

“He loves them, and they love Him,”

“Allah is pleased with them, and they are pleased with Him,”

and:

“Then He rose over the Throne,”

are to be understood according to their apparent meaning, this does not imply that their apparent meaning entails a rising like that of a created being, love like its love, or pleasure like its pleasure.

---

## Page 924

If the listener supposes that the apparent meaning of the attributes is that they resemble the attributes of created beings, he must conclude that none of that apparent meaning is intended. But if he believes that their apparent meaning is what befits the Creator and is proper to Him, he cannot deny this apparent meaning or deny that it is intended without evidence supporting that denial.

Neither reason nor revelation provides any basis for denying this except grounds of the same kind as those used to deny the other attributes. Thus, the same argument applies to them all. To explain: some of our attributes are concrete entities and bodies that are parts of us, such as the face and hand. Others are qualities and accidents that inhere in us, such as hearing, sight, speech, knowledge, and power.

It is well known that when the Lord described Himself as living, knowing, and powerful, the Muslims did not declare this apparent meaning unintended on the grounds that what it signifies in His case is the same as what it signifies in ours. Likewise, when He described Himself as having created Adam with His two hands, this did not require declaring its apparent meaning unintended on the grounds that what it signifies in His case is like what it signifies in ours. Rather, an attribute befits the one who possesses it. Since His holy essence is not like the essences of created beings, His attributes, like His essence, are not like their attributes. The relation of a created being’s attribute to that being corresponds to the relation of the Creator’s attribute to Him; yet the attribute in one case is not like the attribute in the other, nor is the one to whom it belongs like the other.

As the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“You will see your Lord as you see the sun and the moon.”

He thus likened the act of seeing to the act of seeing; he did not liken the One seen to the things seen.

---

## Page 925

This is clarified by:

The Fourth Principle

Many people imagine that some, many, most, or all of Allah’s attributes resemble the attributes of created beings. They then seek to deny what they have understood, thereby falling into four pitfalls:

First: They have likened what they understood from the texts to the attributes of created beings and assumed that the texts signify such a likeness.

Second: If they take this to be the meaning of the texts and reject it, the texts are left stripped of what they signify: the affirmation of attributes befitting Allah. Thus, besides their offense against the texts and their ill opinion of Allah and His Messenger—since they suppose that their words convey this false likeness—they have negated what Allah and His Messenger placed in their words: the affirmation of Allah’s attributes and the divine meanings befitting the majesty of Allah Most High.

Third: They deny these attributes of Allah—Mighty and Majestic—without knowledge, thereby denying the Lord what rightfully belongs to Him.

---

## Page 926

Fourth: He describes the Lord by the opposites of these attributes: the attributes of the dead and of inanimate things, or those of nonexistent things. He thereby denies the attributes of perfection that rightfully belong to the Lord, likens Him to deficient and nonexistent things, strips the texts of the attributes they signify, and makes their meaning out to be a likeness to created beings. Thus, in his treatment of Allah’s words and of Allah Himself, he combines denial of the divine attributes with likening Allah to creation, deviating concerning Allah’s names and signs.

An example is that all the texts indicate that Allah is exalted above created beings and that He rises over the Throne. His being above and distinct from created beings is known through reason in agreement with revelation; His rising over the Throne, however, is known through revelation.

Neither the Quran nor the Sunnah describes Him as neither within the world nor outside it, neither distinct from it nor intermingled with it. Yet someone under this misconception supposes that, if Allah is described as rising over the Throne, His doing so must resemble a person’s mounting ships and livestock, as in His words:

“and has made for you ships and livestock that you ride”

“so that you may settle upon their backs”.

He therefore imagines that, if Allah is over the Throne, He must need it just as someone riding a ship or an animal needs it: if the ship sank, its rider would fall, and if the animal stumbled, its rider would tumble down.

By this analogy, if the Throne ceased to exist, the Lord—glorified and exalted is He—would fall. Then, imagining that he is refuting this, he says: “His rising over the Throne is neither sitting nor settling.”

---

## Page 927

and it is not recognized that what is said of the meaning of rising over also applies to the meanings of sitting and settling. If dependence is included in those meanings, then there is no difference between rising over, sitting, and settling: in this sense, He is neither rising over, nor settled, nor sitting. If, however, their meanings include nothing beyond what is included in the meaning of rising over, then affirming one while denying the other is arbitrary. It is known that there are familiar distinctions between the meanings of rising over, settling, and sitting. The point here, however, is to recognize the error of someone who denies something while affirming its counterpart.

This error appears to stem from his mistaken understanding of Allah’s rising over the Throne: he supposes that it resembles a person’s mounting the backs of livestock or boarding ships. Yet nothing in this wording indicates that, for Allah attributes rising over to His noble Self, just as He attributes all His other acts and attributes to Himself. Thus, He states that He created and then rose over the Throne, just as He states that He determined and then guided, that He built the heaven with might, and that He is with Moses and Aaron, hearing and seeing, and other such things.

He does not speak of rising over in an unrestricted sense applicable to a created being, nor in a general sense encompassing created beings, just as He does not speak in such terms concerning His other attributes. Rather, He speaks of rising over as something He attributes to His noble Self. If it were supposed—on the basis of an impossible hypothesis—that He was like His creation—exalted is He above that—then His rising over would resemble that of His creation. But if He is not like His creation—indeed, it is known that He has no need of creation, that He is the Creator of the Throne and everything else, and that everything other than Him depends upon Him,

---

## Page 928

and He has no need of anything other than Himself, and He has spoken only of a rising over that is particular to Him, not one that applies to others and is unbefitting Him—just as, regarding His knowledge, power, sight, hearing, and creation, He speaks only of what is particular to Him—then how can anyone imagine that, if He is risen over the Throne, He needs it, and that, if the Throne were to fall, the One above it would fall down? Glorified and exalted is He, far above what the wrongdoers and deniers say! Is this anything but sheer ignorance and error on the part of anyone who understands or imagines such a thing, supposes it to be the apparent meaning and import of the wording, or considers it possible for the Lord of all worlds, who has no need of creation? Rather, even if an ignorant person were supposed to understand or imagine something like this, it should be explained to him that this is impossible and that the wording does not indicate it at all, just as it does not indicate comparable notions in any of the other attributes with which the Lord describes Himself.

When He, glorified and exalted, says:

“And the heaven We constructed with strength,”

can anyone imagine that His building resembles that of a human being in need, who requires a basket, shovels, the making of bricks, the preparation of clay, and helpers?

Moreover, it is known that Allah Most High created the world with some of its parts above others, without making its higher parts dependent upon its lower ones. The air is above the earth, yet does not need the earth to support it. The clouds, too, are above the earth, yet do not need it to support them. The heavens are above the earth, yet do not need the earth to support them. Thus, the Most High, the Highest, the Lord of all things

---

## Page 929

and their Sovereign—if He is above all His creation, how could it follow that He needs His creation or His Throne? How could His being above His creation entail such dependence when it does not entail this even among created things?
