It is known that whatever independence from others is established for a created being, the Creator, glorified and exalted, has a greater and more rightful claim to it. The same applies to His words:

“Do you feel secure that He who is in heaven will not cause the earth to swallow you, whereupon it would quake?”

Anyone who imagines that this verse implies that Allah is inside the heavens is, by agreement, ignorant and astray—even though saying that the sun and moon are in the sky does imply that they are within it. For the preposition “in” is connected to what precedes and follows it, and its meaning varies according to what it is related to. Thus, a distinction is made between a thing being in a place, a body being in a region of space, an accident being in a body, a face being in a mirror, and speech being on paper. Each of these kinds has a distinctive feature that sets it apart from the others, although the preposition “in” is used in all of them.

If someone were to ask, “Is the Throne in heaven or on earth?” the answer would be, “In heaven.” Likewise, if someone asked, “Is Paradise in heaven or on earth?” the answer would be, “Paradise is in heaven.” Yet this does not entail that the Throne is inside the heavens, nor even that Paradise is. It is established in the Sahīh that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“When you ask Allah for Paradise, ask Him for al-Firdaws, for it is the highest and central part of Paradise, and its roof is the Throne of the Most Merciful.”

Thus, the roof of this Paradise—the Throne—is above the celestial spheres, even though Paradise is in

---

## Page 930

heaven, where the term denotes what is above, whether Paradise is above the celestial spheres or below them. Allah Most High says:

“Let him stretch a rope up to the ceiling.”

And He says:

“And We sent down from the sky purifying water.”

Since it was firmly established in the minds of those addressed that Allah is the Most High, the Highest, and that He is above everything, His saying that He is in heaven was understood to mean that He is above, and above everything. Likewise, when he asked the slave girl, “Where is Allah?” and she replied, “In heaven,” she meant only that He is above, not that He is confined to created bodies or dwells within them.

The expression “above” includes what is above all created things. Thus, whatever is above them all is “in heaven.” This does not imply that an actually existing enclosure surrounds Him, for nothing exists above the world except Allah. Similarly, saying that the Throne is in heaven does not imply that the Throne is inside some other existing, created thing.

If, however, “heaven” is taken to mean the celestial spheres, the meaning would be that He is above them, just as He says:

“And I will surely crucify you on the trunks of palm trees.”

And:

“Travel through the land.”

And:

“Journey through the land.”

Likewise, one may say that someone is “in the mountain” or “on the roof,” even when he is on its highest part.

---

## Page 931

The Fifth Principle:
We know what we have been told in one respect, but not in another.

For Allah says:

“Do they not reflect upon the Quran? Had it been from anyone other than Allah, they would have found in it much inconsistency.”

And He says:

“Have they not reflected upon the Word?”

And He says:

“This is a blessed Book which We have sent down to you, so that they may reflect upon its verses and those of understanding may take heed.”

And He says:

“Do they not reflect upon the Quran, or are there locks upon their hearts?”

Thus, He commands reflection upon the entire Book. Allah Most High also says:

“He is the One who has sent down to you the Book. In it are clear verses, which are the foundation of the Book, and others that are not entirely clear. Those whose hearts are inclined to deviation follow what is not entirely clear in it, seeking discord and seeking its interpretation. But none knows its interpretation except Allah. Those firmly grounded in knowledge say, ‘We believe in it; all of it is from our Lord.’ And none take heed except those of understanding.”

The majority of the early and later generations of the Muslim community hold that the pause comes at His words:

“But none knows its interpretation except Allah.”

This is what has been transmitted from Ubayy ibn Kaʿb, Ibn Masʿūd, Ibn ʿAbbās, and others.

---

## Page 932

and it is reported that Ibn ‘Abbās said:

“Explanation of the Quran is of four kinds: an explanation that the Arabs know from their language; an explanation that no one is excused for not knowing; an explanation known to scholars; and an explanation known only to Allah. Whoever claims to know the last is a liar.”

It has also been reported from Mujāhid and others that those firmly grounded in knowledge know its interpretation. Mujāhid said:

“I went through the Quran with Ibn ‘Abbās from beginning to end, stopping him at every verse and asking him about its explanation.”

On careful examination, there is no contradiction between the two views, for differing terminological conventions have resulted in the term ta’wīl (interpretation) being used in three senses:

The first:

In the terminology of many later scholars discussing jurisprudence and its principles, ta’wīl means taking an expression in its less likely sense rather than its more likely sense, on the basis of accompanying evidence. This is what most later scholars meant when discussing whether to apply ta’wīl to the texts concerning Allah’s attributes or refrain from doing so, and whether this is praiseworthy or blameworthy, true or false.

The second:

Ta’wīl means explanation. This is its predominant usage among Quranic commentators, as when Ibn Jarīr and other authors of Quranic commentaries say, “The scholars of interpretation differed.” Mujāhid is a leading authority among Quranic commentators. Al-Thawrī said:

“If an explanation comes to you from Mujāhid, that is sufficient for you.”

Al-Shāfi‘ī, Ahmad, al-Bukhārī, and others rely upon his explanations. Thus, when he states that he knows the ta’wīl of what is not entirely clear, he means knowing its explanation.

---

## Page 933

The third meaning of ta’wīl:

It is the reality to which the words ultimately refer, as Allah Most High says:

“Do they await anything but its fulfillment? On the Day its fulfillment comes, those who had forgotten it before will say, ‘The messengers of our Lord certainly brought the truth.’”

Thus, the ta’wīl of the Quran’s accounts of the Hereafter is what Allah has foretold in it will come to pass: the Resurrection, the reckoning, recompense, Paradise, Hell, and the like. Likewise, Allah Most High relates in the account of Joseph that, when his parents and brothers prostrated before him, he said:

“O my father, this is the fulfillment of my earlier dream.”

He thus identified the very event that actually took place as the ta’wīl of the dream. The second meaning is the explanation of speech: the words used to explain an expression so that its meaning may be understood, or its rationale or supporting evidence known. This third meaning of ta’wīl, however, is the very thing that exists in reality.

An example is the statement of ‘Ā’ishah:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to say while bowing and prostrating: ‘Glory and praise be to You, O Allah, our Lord. O Allah, forgive me.’”

He was putting the Quran into practice, meaning His words:

“So glorify your Lord with praise and seek His forgiveness.”

Another example is the statement of Sufyān ibn ‘Uyaynah: “The Sunnah is the ta’wīl of commands and prohibitions.” The commanded act itself is the ta’wīl of the command to perform it, and the actual thing about which a statement is made is the ta’wīl of that statement. Speech consists of statements and commands. This is why Abū ‘Ubayd and others say, “Jurists are more knowledgeable about ta’wīl than language scholars,” as

---

## Page 934

they mention when explaining ishtimāl al-sammā’ (a particular manner of wrapping oneself in a garment). For jurists understand what the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, commanded and prohibited because they know his purposes, just as the followers of Hippocrates, Sībawayh, and others like them understand aspects of their purposes that cannot be known through language alone. Yet the ta’wīl of commands and prohibitions must be known, unlike the ta’wīl of statements.

Once this is understood, the ta’wīl of what Allah Most High has related about His holy Self, characterized by the realities of His names and attributes, is the actual reality of His holy Self, characterized by the realities of His attributes. The ta’wīl of what Allah Most High has related concerning promises and threats is the very fulfillment of those promises and threats. Hence the words found in the hadith:

“We act upon its clear passages and believe in its equivocal passages.”

For what Allah has related about Himself and the Last Day includes expressions whose meanings resemble what we know in this world. He has told us, for example, that Paradise contains meat, milk, honey, wine, and other such things. These resemble what exists in this world in wording and meaning, but they are neither identical to them nor the same in their actual reality. This applies all the more to Allah’s names and attributes: although they bear some resemblance to the names and attributes of His servants, that resemblance does not make the Creator like the created being, nor His reality like its reality.

An account of what is unseen cannot be understood unless it is expressed using names whose meanings are known in the observable world. Through these names, what is unseen is understood by means of our knowledge of what is observable, while recognizing the distinction between them and knowing that what Allah has related of the unseen is greater than what is known in the observable world. And in the unseen there is

---

## Page 935

what no eye has seen, no ear has heard, and no human heart has ever conceived. Thus, when Allah tells us about matters of the unseen whose knowledge He has reserved to Himself—Paradise and Hell—we understand their meaning, grasp what we are intended to understand from that address, and explain it. But the actual reality described—such as that which has not yet come to pass and will come to pass only on the Day of Resurrection—belongs to the ta’wīl known only to Allah.

Hence, when Mālik and others among the early generations were asked about the Most High’s words:

“The Most Merciful rose over the Throne,”

they said: “Istiwā’ (rising over the Throne) is known; its manner is unknown. Belief in it is obligatory, and asking about it is an innovation.”

Likewise, Rabī‘ah, Mālik’s teacher, had said before him: “Istiwā’ is known; its manner is unknown. Explanation comes from Allah, conveying the message is the Messenger’s duty, and belief is our duty.” He thus made clear that istiwā’ is known, while its manner is unknown. Statements like this occur frequently in the words of the early generations. The imams deny that Allah’s servants know the manner of His attributes: none but Allah knows how He is, and none but He knows His true reality.

The Prophet said:

“I cannot fully praise You; You are as You have praised Yourself.”

This is recorded in Sahīh Muslim and elsewhere. In another hadith, he said:

“O Allah, I ask You by every name belonging to You with which You have named Yourself, or which You have revealed in Your Book, or taught to any of Your creation, or reserved to Yourself in Your knowledge of the unseen.”

This hadith is recorded in al-Musnad and Sahīh Abī Hātim. In it, he stated that Allah has names which He has reserved to Himself in His knowledge of the unseen.

---

## Page 936

Thus, no one but He knows the meanings of those names which He has reserved to Himself in His knowledge of the unseen. Allah, glorified is He, has informed us that He is All-Knowing, All-Powerful, All-Hearing, All-Seeing, All-Forgiving, and Merciful, along with His other names and attributes. We understand what these mean and distinguish knowledge from power, and mercy from hearing and sight. We know that all these names refer to Allah’s essence, despite their different meanings. They agree and coincide in referring to His essence, while differing in the attributes they signify.

The same applies to the names of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, such as Muhammad, Ahmad, al-Māhī, al-Hāshir, and al-ʿĀqib. Likewise, the names of the Quran include the Quran, the Criterion, the Guidance, the Light, the Revelation, the Healing, and others. People have disputed whether names of this kind are synonymous because they refer to a single entity, or distinct because they signify different attributes.

An example is the use of al-sayf (the sword), al-sārim (the cutting blade), and al-muhannad (the Indian blade), when al-sārim is intended to convey the meaning of cutting, and al-muhannad a connection to India. The precise conclusion is that they are synonymous with respect to the entity they name, but distinct with respect to its attributes.

What further clarifies this is that Allah has described the entire Quran as perfectly formed and as mutually consistent, while elsewhere He has designated some of it as clear and some as ambiguous. One must therefore distinguish the perfection and consistency that apply to the whole Quran from the clarity and ambiguity that apply to particular parts of it. He says:

---

## Page 937

Allah, Most High:

“Alif, Lām, Rā. A Book whose verses have been perfected and then explained in detail.”

He thus states that He has perfected all its verses. He also says:

“Allah has sent down the best discourse: a Book, mutually consistent, with recurring passages.”

He thus states that the whole of it is mutually consistent. Judgment means distinguishing between two things; hence a judge settles the dispute between two litigants. Judgment distinguishes between things that resemble one another, in both knowledge and action, by discerning truth from falsehood, truthfulness from lying, and benefit from harm. This entails doing what is beneficial and refraining from what is harmful.

Thus, the expressions hakamtu al-safīha and ahkamtuhu mean “I restrained the foolish person,” when one prevents him from acting. Likewise, hakamtu al-dābbata and ahkamtuhā mean “I fitted the animal with a bridle restraint,” when one equips it with a hakama—the part of the bridle that surrounds its jaw. Perfecting something means making it sound and precise. Perfecting speech therefore means distinguishing truth from falsehood in its statements, and right guidance from error in its commands.

The entire Quran is perfectly formed in the sense of being flawlessly composed. Allah has called it “wise,” saying:

“Alif, Lām, Rā. These are the verses of the wise Book.”

Here, hakīm (“wise”) means hākim (“one that judges”). Likewise, He describes it as recounting, saying:

“Indeed, this Quran recounts to the Children of Israel most of that over which they differ.”

He also describes it as giving a ruling, saying:

“Say, ‘Allah gives you a ruling concerning them, as does what is recited to you in the Book.’”

That is, what is recited to you gives you a ruling concerning them. He also describes it as guiding and bringing glad tidings, saying:

“Indeed, this Quran guides to what is most upright and gives glad tidings to the believers who do righteous deeds.”

As for the mutual consistency that characterizes the whole Quran, it is the opposite of the discrepancy denied of it in His words: “And had it been

---

## Page 938

from other than Allah, they would have found within it much contradiction.” This is the discrepancy mentioned in His words:

“Indeed, your speech is conflicting.”

“Turned away from it is whoever is turned away.”

Mutual consistency here means that the discourse is harmonious and coherent, with its various parts confirming one another. If He commands something, He does not command its opposite elsewhere; rather, He commands that same thing, something comparable to it, or something that entails it. If He forbids something, He does not command it elsewhere; rather, He forbids that same thing, something comparable to it, or something that entails it—provided there has been no abrogation.

Likewise, if He affirms something, He does not state its opposite; rather, He affirms it or affirms something that entails it. If He denies something, He does not affirm it; rather, He denies it or denies its necessary consequences. This contrasts with inconsistent discourse, whose parts contradict one another: it affirms something on one occasion and denies it on another, or commands and forbids it at the same time. It also treats equivalent things differently, praising one and condemning the other.

Thus, inconsistent statements here are those that contradict one another, whereas mutually consistent statements are those that agree. This consistency concerns meanings, even when the wording differs. When the meanings agree with, reinforce, harmonize with, bear witness to, and entail one another, the discourse is mutually consistent. This contrasts with contradictory discourse, whose parts oppose one another.

Mutual consistency in this general sense does not conflict with perfection of composition in its general sense; rather, it confirms it. For speech

---

## Page 939

that is sound and finely composed has parts that confirm rather than contradict one another. This differs from clear differentiation in the specific sense, which is the opposite of resemblance in the specific sense. Resemblance in the specific sense occurs when something resembles another thing in one respect but differs from it in another, so that some people mistakenly think it is that very thing, or is equivalent to it, when it is not. Clear differentiation means distinguishing the two so that neither is confused with the other.

Such resemblance arises only through a feature shared by the two things, while a distinction between them remains. Some people fail to recognize that distinction and thus find the matter confusing, whereas others recognize it. Resemblance that prevents a person from distinguishing between things may therefore be relative to the observer: it confuses some people but not others. Scholars possess knowledge of such matters that dispels this confusion for them. For example, some people confuse what they have been promised in the Hereafter with what they witness in this world, imagining that they are alike. Scholars, however, know that they are not alike, even though they resemble one another in certain respects.

The misleading ambiguities through which some people go astray belong to this category: truth and falsehood resemble one another within them, leaving some people confused. Those who have been granted knowledge of how to distinguish between the two do not confuse truth with falsehood.

Faulty analogy belongs to this category of misleading ambiguities, for it likens one thing, in certain respects, to something that does not resemble it in those respects. Whoever knows how to distinguish between the two things recognizes the difference that dispels the confusion

---

## Page 940

and the faulty analogy.

Any two things share something in common and differ in something else: they resemble one another in one respect and differ in another. Thus, the ways in which human beings go astray through resemblance and faulty analogy are beyond enumeration. As Imam Ahmad said:

“Most human error arises from interpretation and analogy.”

Interpretation concerns revealed evidence, while analogy concerns rational evidence. It is indeed as he said. Erroneous interpretation occurs only where expressions resemble one another, and erroneous analogy occurs only where meanings resemble one another. Human beings have fallen into most of the kinds of error encompassed by this statement. Matters have gone so far that some who claim true understanding, affirmation of Allah’s oneness, and spiritual knowledge have confused the Lord’s existence with the existence of every existing thing, imagining them to be identical. They have thus made the existence of created things identical to the existence of the Creator. Yet nothing could be further from resembling another thing, being identical to it, being united with it, or indwelling it than the Creator in relation to creation. Those who confuse the Creator’s existence with the existence of all created things, imagining their existence to be His existence, are therefore the most profoundly misguided through such confusion.

This is because existing things share the meaning denoted by the term “existence.” They therefore regarded existence as one, without distinguishing between being one and the same individual entity and being one in kind. Others mistakenly supposed that, if it is said that existing things share the meaning denoted by the term “existence,” this necessarily entails

---

## Page 941

likening the Creator to creation and attributing composition to Him. They therefore said: “The term ‘existence’ is used homonymously.” Thus they contradicted the agreement of rational people of every persuasion that existence is divided into eternal and originated existence, and other such categories of existing things.

Another group supposed that, if existing things share the meaning denoted by “existence,” there must be something existing outside the mind that they all share. They claimed that unqualified universals exist outside the mind—existence in the abstract, animal in the abstract, body in the abstract, and the like. They thereby contradicted sensory perception, reason, and revelation, and treated what exists in the mind as existing in concrete reality. All of this is a form of confusion arising from resemblance.

Those whom Allah guides distinguish between things even when they share certain aspects, and recognize what unites and distinguishes them, and how they resemble and differ from one another. Such people are not led astray by ambiguous speech, because they consider it together with clear, decisive speech that establishes the distinctions and explains the differences.

For example, the expressions “Indeed, We” and “We,” and other plural forms, may be used by a single person who has partners in an action. They may also be used by a single person of great stature who possesses attributes, each of which stands in the place of an individual, and who has helpers subordinate to him, not partners.

Thus, if a Christian appeals to the words of the Most High:

“Indeed, it is We who sent down the Reminder,”

and similar passages as evidence for a plurality of gods, then clear, decisive passages, such as His words:

“And your God is one God,”

and other statements admitting only one meaning, dispel whatever

---

## Page 942

ambiguity there may be. The plural form He uses thus makes clear the greatness, names and attributes that are His due, and the obedience owed to Him by His creatures, angels and others. As for the actual realities to which this points—the realities of His names and attributes, and the hosts He employs in His acts—none but He knows them:

“And none knows the hosts of your Lord except Him.”

This belongs to the ultimate reality of the ambiguous passages, which Allah alone knows. It is otherwise with a human king: when he says, “We have ordered that you be given a grant,” it is understood that he and his assistants—such as his secretary, chamberlain, servant and others—have been instructed accordingly. One may also know the beliefs, intentions and the like from which that action proceeded.

Allah—glorified and exalted is He—does not disclose to His servants the actual realities of His attributes and the characteristics of the Last Day about which He has informed them. Nor do they know the actual realities of the wisdom He intends through His creation and command, or of the will and power from which these proceed.

This makes clear that ambiguity occurs in univocal terms just as it occurs in homonymous terms that are not univocal, even when the ambiguity is removed by a qualification or identification that distinguishes one of the two kinds. For example, when it is said:

“Therein are rivers of water,”

the water is specifically associated with Paradise, so the distinction between it and the water of this world becomes clear. Yet the actual reality of what distinguishes that water remains unknown to us. Together with what Allah has prepared for His righteous servants—what no eye has seen, no ear has heard, and no human heart has conceived—it belongs to the ultimate reality that Allah alone knows.

---

## Page 943

and likewise what His names and attributes uniquely denote—a reality known only to Him.

For this reason, the imams, including Imam Ahmad and others, censured the Jahmiyyah and those like them—who distort words from their proper places—for interpreting passages of the Quran that they found ambiguous in ways other than their correct interpretation. Ahmad said this in the book he wrote to refute the heretics and the Jahmiyyah concerning the ambiguous passages of the Quran about which they harboured doubts and which they interpreted incorrectly.

He censured them precisely because they interpreted these passages incorrectly. In that work, he discussed passages whose meanings were unclear to them, even though they were not unclear to others, and censured them for interpreting those passages incorrectly. He did not reject the term ta’wīl altogether. As explained earlier, ta’wīl may mean an explanation that clarifies what Allah intends; this is not blameworthy but praiseworthy. It may also mean the actual reality whose knowledge Allah has reserved to Himself; that is known only to Him. We have discussed this at length elsewhere.

Those who fail to understand this become inconsistent in their statements. For example, some say that ta’wīl is invalid and that the wording must be understood according to its apparent meaning. They cite the words of the Most High:

“And no one knows its interpretation except Allah.”

They invoke this verse to invalidate ta’wīl, but this is a contradiction on their part: the verse establishes that there is a ta’wīl known only to Allah, whereas they reject ta’wīl altogether. Their error lies in failing to recognise that the ta’wīl whose knowledge Allah has reserved to Himself is the actual reality known only to Him.

---

## Page 944

As for blameworthy and invalid ta’wīl, it is the interpretation offered by those who distort the texts and introduce religious innovations. They interpret them incorrectly and claim to divert the wording from what it signifies to something else, without evidence requiring this. They claim that its apparent meaning entails an objectionable consequence comparable to what necessarily follows from what they themselves affirm through reason. They then redirect it to meanings comparable to those they have denied it conveys. Thus, what they deny is of the same kind as what they affirm. If what they affirm is true and possible, then so is what they deny; and if what they deny is false and impossible, then so is what they affirm.

Those who reject ta’wīl altogether and cite the words of the Most High:

“And no one knows its interpretation except Allah.”

may suppose that we have been addressed in the Quran with words that no one understands, that have no meaning, or from which nothing can be understood. Besides being false, this is self-contradictory. If we understand nothing from it, we cannot say that it has an interpretation that either contradicts or accords with its apparent meaning; for it may have a valid meaning. That valid meaning does not contradict any apparent meaning known to us, since, according to their claim, it has no apparent meaning. Its signification of that meaning would therefore not contradict an apparent meaning, and so would not constitute ta’wīl.

Nor, on this assumption, may we deny that it signifies meanings unknown to us, for we may not know the meanings it signifies. Moreover, if we do not understand the wording and what it signifies, we are even less likely to know the meanings it does not signify. This is because wording indicates what is intended by it more strongly than it indicates what is not intended by it. Thus, if the wording gives no indication of a meaning

---

## Page 945

of any kind, and no meaning whatsoever can be understood from it, then it does not indicate what is intended by it; still less can it indicate what is not intended by it. Thus, one cannot say that this wording has been subjected to ta’wīl in the sense of being diverted from its more likely meaning to a less likely meaning, let alone say that Allah alone knows this interpretation. The only exception would be if ta’wīl meant an interpretation contrary to an apparent meaning peculiar to created beings. If someone understands “apparent meaning” in this way, then the wording must undoubtedly have an interpretation contrary to that apparent meaning.

Yet if these people say that these texts have no interpretation contrary to their apparent meaning, or that they are to be taken according to their apparent meanings, they contradict themselves. If they intend one sense of “apparent meaning” here and another there within the same discussion, without explaining the distinction, this is misleading. And if by “apparent meaning” they mean merely the wording—that is, that the texts are taken according to the wording as it appears, without understanding its meaning—then their rejection or affirmation of ta’wīl is contradictory, since anyone who affirms or denies an interpretation has already understood some meaning. These distinctions expose the contradictions of many people in this matter, both those who deny the divine attributes and those who affirm them.

---

## Page 946

The Sixth Principle

Someone may say:

In this matter, there must be a criterion by which to distinguish what is possible with respect to Allah from what is impossible, both in negation and in affirmation. Merely denying likeness, or affirming without qualification while denying likeness, is not a sound basis. For any two things have something in common and something that distinguishes them.

If someone denies an attribute on the grounds that affirming it entails likeness, he should be told: “If you mean that one is identical to the other in every respect, this is false. But if you mean that one resembles the other in some respects but not others, or shares the same name, then this also applies to everything else you affirm. You have only established evidence against likeness and equivalence as you yourselves have defined them: whatever is possible for one is possible for the other, whatever is impossible for one is impossible for the other, and whatever is necessary for one is necessary for the other. Clearly, no rational person who understands what he is saying would affirm likeness in this sense, for its impossibility is self-evident to reason. Denying this, however, does not entail denying resemblance in certain respects, as with names and attributes used in a shared sense.”

Yet some people define likeness in a particular sense, then say of anyone who affirms that meaning: “He likens Allah to His creation.” Their opponent, however, says: “That meaning does not constitute likeness.”

---

## Page 947

A distinction may also be drawn between the terms “likening” and “asserting equivalence.” The Muʿtazilah and others who deny the divine attributes say:

“Anyone who affirms a beginningless attribute for Allah likens Him to another and asserts equivalence between them.”

Thus, anyone who says that Allah has beginningless knowledge or beginningless power is, in their view, guilty of such likening and equivalence. This is because most of them regard beginninglessness as the most distinctive characteristic of God. Whoever affirms a beginningless attribute for Him has therefore, in their view, affirmed a beginningless counterpart to Allah, and on this basis they describe him as someone who asserts equivalence.

Those who affirm the divine attributes do not agree with them. Rather, they say: “His most distinctive characteristics are those that belong to no one else, such as His being the Lord of all worlds, His knowing all things, His having power over all things, His being the One God, and the like.” An attribute cannot itself be described by any of these characteristics.

Among those who affirm the attributes, some do not say that the attributes are beginningless. Instead, they say: “The Lord, with His attributes, is beginningless.” Others say: “He is beginningless, and His attribute is beginningless,” but do not say: “He and His attributes are both beginningless.”

Still others say: “He and His attributes are both beginningless.” However, they say: “This does not entail that an attribute shares any of His distinctive characteristics. Beginninglessness is not a distinctive characteristic of the essence stripped of attributes, but of the essence possessing attributes. Indeed, in their view, an essence stripped of attributes does not exist at all, let alone possess beginninglessness as its distinctive characteristic.”

They may also say: “The essence is characterized by beginninglessness, and the attributes are characterized by beginninglessness; yet the attributes are neither a god nor a lord, just as the Prophet came into existence and his attributes came into existence, yet his attributes are not a prophet.”

---

## Page 948

Thus, when these people describe those who affirm the divine attributes as guilty of “likening” and “asserting equivalence,” they do so on the basis of beliefs that the latter dispute. Those who affirm the attributes then reply:

“Granted that some people may call this meaning ‘likening’ in their terminology, neither reason nor revelation has ruled it out. What must be denied is only what scriptural and rational proofs negate. The Quran has denied that Allah has any likeness, equal, rival, or the like.”

However, those who affirm the attributes say that, in Arabic usage, an attribute is neither the likeness, equal, nor rival of the one it describes, and therefore does not fall within the scope of the scriptural text. As for reason, it has not ruled out what the Muʿtazilah call “likening” in their terminology.

They also say:

“Attributes can subsist only in a spatially situated body, and all bodies are alike. If attributes subsisted in Him, it would necessarily follow that He was like all other bodies. This is what constitutes likening.”

This is also asserted by many of those who affirm the divine attributes but deny His being above the Throne, deny that acts of His choosing subsist in Him, and deny other such matters. They say:

“Attributes may subsist in what is not a body. Being above the world, however, is possible only if He is a body. If we affirmed His being above it, this would necessarily entail that He is a body; and since all bodies are alike, likening would necessarily follow.”

For this reason, you find that they describe anyone who affirms His being above the world and similar matters as one who likens Him to others, but do not describe anyone who affirms His hearing, sight, speech, and similar attributes in this way. This is the position expressed by the author of al-Irshād and others like him.

---

## Page 949

Likewise, al-Qādī Abū Ya‘lā and others who affirm the divine attributes and His being above the world agree with them that all bodies are alike. However, they treat His being above the world as an attribute known through revelation, as in the earlier of al-Qādī Abū Ya‘lā’s two positions. Discussion of it thus follows the same approach as discussion of His Face. They may also say that what they affirm is not incompatible with corporeality, just as they say concerning the other attributes. Anyone who reflects rationally will find that what they deny stands on the same footing as what they affirm: there is no difference.

The arguments of all these groups rest on the premises that affirming the attributes necessarily entails ascribing corporeality to Allah, and that all bodies are alike. Those who affirm the attributes respond sometimes by rejecting the first premise, sometimes by rejecting the second, sometimes by rejecting both, and sometimes by asking for clarification of what is meant.

There is no doubt that their assertion that all bodies are alike is false, whether they define a body as something that can be pointed to, something that subsists in itself, something that exists, something composed of matter and form, or something similar. If, however, they define it as something composed of indivisible atoms and assume that these atoms are all alike, their conclusion depends on the validity of that account, on establishing the existence of indivisible atoms, and on establishing that they are all alike. Most rational thinkers disagree with them on these matters.

(The point here is that they apply the term “likening” to what they believe constitutes ascribing corporeality to Allah, on the premise that all bodies are alike. Those who affirm the attributes dispute this belief, just as the Rāfidah level the charge of hostility to Alī against

---

## Page 950

those who are loyal to Abū Bakr and ‘Umar, may Allah be pleased with them both, on the premise that whoever loves them hates ‘Alī, may Allah be pleased with him, and that whoever hates him is a Nāsibī (one hostile to ‘Alī). Ahl al-Sunnah dispute the first premise.

For this reason, these people say that two things do not resemble one another in one respect while differing in another. Most rational thinkers disagree. We have discussed this at length elsewhere, setting out the arguments of those who maintain that all bodies are alike and those who deny this, and demonstrating the falsity of the former position.

Furthermore, relying on this approach to deny likeness is invalid. For if it is established that all bodies are alike, they deny such likeness only through the argument by which they deny that He is a body. If it is established that this entails corporeality, and that corporeality is impossible for Him, this alone suffices to deny it; there is no need to deny what is termed “likening.” However, the denial of corporeality is based on denying this likeness, by saying: “If such-and-such were affirmed of Him, He would be a body.” Then it is said: “All bodies are alike, so they must share what is necessary, possible, and impossible for them; yet this is impossible for Him.”

But in that case, whoever follows this approach relies on denying corporeality in order to deny likeness. Thus, the foundation of his denial is the denial that He is a body. This is another approach, which we shall discuss, if Allah wills.

---

## Page 951

The point here, however, is simply this: relying solely on the denial of likeness to deny whatever one seeks to deny serves no purpose, since any two things resemble one another in some respect and differ in another. This is unlike relying on the denial of deficiency, defect, and similar imperfections from which He—glorified be He—is free, for this is a sound approach. Likewise, affirming His attributes of perfection while denying that anyone else is His equal in them denies equality in what rightfully belongs to Him. This is the essence of monotheism: nothing shares with Him anything that is exclusively His. He possesses every attribute of perfection in a manner in which no one is His equal. Hence, the position of the early generations of the Muslim community and its leading scholars was to affirm the attributes with which He described Himself and to deny that He is like any created being.

If it is said:
“When one thing resembles another in some respect, whatever is possible for the latter in that respect is possible for the former; whatever is necessary for the latter is necessary for the former, and whatever is impossible for the latter is impossible for the former.”

The reply is:
“Granted that this is so. But if that shared aspect entails neither affirming what is impossible for the Lord—glorified be He—nor denying what rightfully belongs to Him, then it is not impossible. For example, it is said that He exists and is living, knowing, hearing, and seeing; some created beings have also been described as living, sam‘ī (pertaining to hearing), knowing, and seeing. If it is then said, ‘It follows that whatever is possible for such a created being insofar as it exists and is living, knowing, hearing, and seeing must also be possible for Him,’ the reply is: ‘What this shared aspect entails is not impossible for the Lord Most High, for it entails neither coming into existence, nor contingency, nor deficiency, nor anything incompatible with the attributes of lordship.’”

---

## Page 952

For the shared aspect is what is denoted by “existence,” “an existent being,” “life,” “living,” “knowledge,” “knowing,” “hearing,” “sight,” “one who hears,” “one who sees,” “power,” or “powerful.” This shared aspect is an unrestricted universal, not exclusive to either of the two. Thus, they share neither what is exclusive to the contingent being that has come into existence nor what is exclusive to the necessarily existent, eternal Being. Whatever is exclusive to either cannot be shared by both.

If the aspect they share is an attribute of perfection, such as existence, life, knowledge, or power, and contains nothing indicating the distinctive characteristics of created beings—just as it indicates none of the distinctive characteristics of the Creator—then there is nothing objectionable whatsoever in affirming it. Indeed, affirming this is a necessary consequence of existence: any two existent beings must share something of this kind. Whoever denies this must consequently deny the existence of every existent being. Hence, when the leading scholars recognized that this was the true implication of the Jahmiyyah’s position, they called them “negators.” Jahm denied that Allah could be called a “thing,” though the Jahmiyyah sometimes said, “He is a thing unlike other things.” Thus, denying any shared aspect whatsoever entails universal negation.

The meanings conveyed by descriptions of the Lord Most High—such as life, knowledge, and power, and indeed existence, actuality, reality, and the like—have implications that must necessarily hold. For whenever something holds, its necessary implications also hold. The distinctive characteristics of created beings, from which the Lord must be declared free, are not among the necessary implications of these meanings at all. Rather, they are necessary implications of the existence, life, knowledge, and similar qualities peculiar to created beings.

---

## Page 953

And Allah, glorified be He, is free of the distinctive characteristics of created beings and of anything that necessarily entails those characteristics. Whoever understands this point well and reflects upon it will find most doubts dispelled and recognize the errors of many intelligent people in this matter.

This has been discussed at length in many places, where it has been explained that the universal shared aspect exists in external reality only in a particular, qualified form. For existent beings to share something means that they resemble one another in that respect, and that the same general meaning applies to each. In external reality, however, neither shares with the other anything actually existing within it; rather, every existent being is distinct from others in its essence, attributes, and actions.

Such being the case, many people are inconsistent in this matter. At times, a person supposes that affirming the shared aspect necessarily entails the false assimilation of Allah to created beings. He therefore uses this as an argument for denying those attributes he thinks should be denied, seeking to avoid anything that would entail such assimilation. At other times, he realizes that this shared aspect must be affirmed under a given assumption, and uses that recognition to defend the attributes he does affirm against those negators who invoke the same argument.

The considerable confusion surrounding this matter has given rise to doubts over whether the Lord’s existence is identical with His essence or additional to it, and whether the word “existence” is predicated equivocally, univocally, or with gradation in its meaning. Similar confusion has arisen over the affirmation and denial of states

---

## Page 954

and over whether the nonexistent is a thing, and whether the existence of existing things is additional to their essences. Leading speculative thinkers have displayed much inconsistency and contradiction on these questions. At times, one of them adopts two mutually contradictory positions and attributes to others views they never expressed; at other times, he remains in doubt and bewilderment.

We have discussed these questions at length, along with the confusion, error, and bewilderment they have occasioned among leading theologians and philosophers, in greater detail than these brief statements can accommodate. We have explained that the correct position is that the existence of each thing in external reality is its essence as it exists in external reality. This differs from its essence as conceived in the mind, which is distinct from what exists externally.

We have also explained that the terms “self,” “thing,” “essence,” “reality,” and the like are all univocal. If they are said to be graded because their meanings admit of differing degrees, then graded predication is a type of univocal predication in its broader sense: what matters is that the term signifies a shared meaning, whether that meaning varies in degree across its instances or is equal in them.

We have explained that the nonexistent is also a thing in knowledge and in the mind, but not in external reality. Thus, there is no distinction between subsistence and existence; rather, the distinction is between existence in knowledge and existence in external reality. Nevertheless, what is present in knowledge is not the existent reality itself, but the knowledge that belongs to the knower and inheres in him. Likewise, the states in which existent beings resemble one another and differ have an existence in

---

## Page 955

minds, while in external reality there are only existing entities and their particular attributes that inhere in them, through which they resemble one another and differ.

As for this brief account, its purpose is to draw attention to concise, comprehensive points. Whoever understands them will appreciate their value; the door to guidance will open for him, and it will become possible for him to close the door to misguidance. Their detailed exposition and explanation belong to another occasion, for each occasion calls for its own discourse.

“The point” here is that relying on an argument of this kind to determine what is to be denied of the Lord and what He is to be declared free of—as many authors do—is a mistake, as anyone who reflects on it will recognize. This is one of the invalid methods of negation.

---

## Page 956

Section

Even more unsound is the approach taken by those who deny the attributes, or some of them, when they seek to declare Him free of things from which He must indeed be declared free, and whose attribution to Him constitutes some of the gravest forms of disbelief. For example, they seek to declare Him free of sorrow, weeping, and the like, and to refute those Jews who say that He wept over the Flood until His eyes became inflamed and the angels visited Him in His illness, as well as those who ascribe divinity to a human being and claim that he is Allah.

Many people argue against these claims by denying corporeality, spatial location, and the like. They say: “If He possessed these deficiencies and afflictions, He would be a body or have spatial location, and that is impossible.” By adopting such an approach, they have allowed these heretics who deny the names and attributes to gain the upper hand over them. This method does not achieve its intended purpose, for several reasons.

First:

Attributing these deficiencies and afflictions to Allah Most High is more evidently invalid, both rationally and religiously, than denying spatial location and corporeality. The latter involves ambiguity, dispute, and obscurity that are absent from the former. That someone who attributes such deficiencies to Him has committed disbelief is necessarily known in the religion of Islam.

A proof serves to make known and clarify what it establishes. It is therefore inadmissible to establish what is more evident and clearer by means of what is less evident, just as this is not done when formulating definitions.

---

## Page 957

Second:

Those who ascribe these attributes to Him could say, “We do not affirm corporeality or spatial location,” just as those who affirm the attributes while denying corporeality say. Their dispute would thus become like the dispute involving those who affirm speech and the attributes of perfection. The arguments of those who ascribe attributes of perfection to Allah and those who ascribe attributes of deficiency to Him would then be the same, and those who deny the attributes would refute both groups by the same method. This is utterly unsound.

Third:

These people deny the attributes of perfection by this same method, whereas His possessing the attributes of perfection is necessary and established by both reason and revelation. This therefore demonstrates the unsoundness of this method.

Fourth:

Those who follow this method contradict themselves. Whenever one of them affirms something, another holds him to the implications of what they both affirm; likewise, whenever one of them denies something, another holds him to the implications of what they both deny.

Thus, when those who deny the attributes, such as the Muʿtazila, say to those who affirm attributes—such as life, knowledge, power, speech, hearing, and sight—“This is corporealism, because these attributes are accidents, and an accident can subsist only in a body; or because we know of nothing possessing attributes except a body,” those who affirm the attributes reply:

“Yet you yourselves have said that He is living, knowing, and powerful, and that He is not a body. You know of no existing being that is living, knowing, and powerful except a body. You have thus affirmed Him in a manner unlike what you have known; we do likewise.”

They also say to them:

“You have affirmed one who is living, knowing, and powerful, yet without life, knowledge, or power. This is a contradiction that reason necessarily recognizes.”

---

## Page 958

Then, when these affirmers say to those who affirm that He is pleased, becomes angry, loves, and hates—or who describe Him as rising above, descending, coming, and arriving, or as having a face, a hand, and the like—“This entails corporealism, because we know of nothing described in this way except a body,” those who affirm these attributes reply:

“Yet you yourselves have described Him as possessing life, knowledge, power, hearing, sight, and speech, and the same reasoning applies to these. If only a body can be described by these attributes, the same holds for the others. If something that is not a body can be described by one set of attributes, the same holds for the other. To distinguish between them is therefore to distinguish between equivalent cases.”

Thus, since this method of refuting those who ascribe deficiencies to Allah Most High is unsound, none of the early generations or imams adopted it. None of them spoke of Allah in terms of a body, whether to deny or affirm it, nor in terms of substance, spatial location, or the like. These are ambiguous expressions that neither establish any truth nor refute any falsehood. Accordingly, nothing of this kind appears among the things Allah condemned in the Jews and other disbelievers in His Book. Rather, this belongs to the innovated discourse that the early generations and imams condemned.

---

## Page 959

Section

As for methods of affirmation, it is likewise clear that merely denying resemblance is not enough to establish what is being affirmed. If it were enough, it would be permissible to ascribe to Him—glory be to Him—almost innumerable bodily parts and actions that are impossible for Him, while denying resemblance. It would also be permissible to ascribe to Him deficiencies that cannot be attributed to Him, while denying resemblance—as when someone fabricates claims about Him by ascribing to Him weeping, sorrow, hunger, and thirst while denying resemblance.

Such a fabricator might say:

“He eats, but not as His servants eat; He drinks, but not as they drink; He weeps and grieves, but not as they weep and grieve—just as it is said, ‘He laughs, but not as they laugh; He rejoices, but not as they rejoice; and He speaks, but not as they speak.’”

It would likewise be permissible to say:

“He has many bodily parts, but not like theirs—just as it is said, ‘He has a face, but not like their faces, and two hands, but not like their hands.’”

This could extend even to mentioning a stomach, intestines, a male organ, and other things far beneath the majesty of Allah, Mighty and Majestic. Glory be to Him! Exalted is He far above what the wrongdoers say.

For someone who denies these things while affirming the attributes known through revelation and other attributes would be asked:

“What is the difference between these things and what you have affirmed, if you deny resemblance and consider that denial alone sufficient for affirmation?”

There must, therefore, be a distinction between them in reality.

---

## Page 960

If, then, he says:

“The basis for this distinction is revelation: whatever revelation conveys is affirmed, whereas whatever it does not convey is not affirmed,”

he is told, first: Revelation is the truthful messenger’s report of things as they actually are. Whatever the truthful messenger reports, whether by way of negation or affirmation, is true. A report is evidence for what it reports, but evidence does not entail the converse: its absence does not necessarily entail the absence of what it indicates. Thus, something not mentioned in revelation may nevertheless be established in reality, provided revelation has not denied it.

It is known that revelation has not denied these things by their particular names. One must therefore identify what in revelation rules them out; otherwise, it would not then be permissible to deny them, just as it would not be permissible to affirm them.

Furthermore, there must be a distinction in reality between what is affirmed of Him and what is denied of Him. Things alike with respect to possibility, necessity, and impossibility cannot be assigned different statuses in these respects. What is denied must therefore differ from what is affirmed in some respect that warrants its denial; likewise, what is affirmed must differ from what is denied in some respect that warrants its affirmation.

This may also be expressed as follows: There must be a reason requiring the denial of whatever must be denied of Allah, just as there must be a reason establishing whatever is affirmed of Him. If revelation is sufficient, then it reports things as they actually are. What, then, is the distinction in reality between the two?

The reply is:

Whatever negates the attributes of perfection established for Allah is something He is exalted above, for the affirmation of one of

---

## Page 961

two opposites entails the negation of the other. Thus, when it is known that He exists necessarily in Himself and is necessarily without beginning, it is known that nonexistence and coming into being are impossible for Him. It is also known that He is independent of everything other than Himself. For whatever depends on something else for any of its own needs does not exist by itself alone, but by itself together with that other which supplies what it needs; it therefore cannot exist without it.

He—glorified is He—is independent of everything other than Himself, and so He is exalted above whatever contradicts His independence. He—glorified is He—is powerful and strong, and so He is exalted above whatever contradicts His power and strength. He—glorified is He—is the Ever-Living, Self-Subsisting Sustainer of all, and so He is exalted above whatever contradicts His life, His self-subsistence, or His sustaining of all things.

In short, revelation has affirmed for Him the most beautiful names and attributes of perfection that have been transmitted. Revelation therefore denies whatever contradicts them, just as it denies that He has any likeness or equal. For affirming something negates its opposite and whatever entails that opposite. Reason recognizes this negation just as it recognizes the affirmation of the opposite: affirming either of two opposites negates the other and whatever entails it.

Thus, the ways of knowing that whatever the Lord is exalted above must be denied of Him are extensive. There is no need to confine them merely to denying resemblance and corporeality, as those deficient in understanding and negligent in their efforts have done. They have contradicted themselves in this regard and distinguished between things that are alike, to the point that whenever anyone affirms something, whoever denies it argues against him that it entails resemblance. Likewise, the Qarmatians argued for denying all things, until they even denied negation itself, saying:

---

## Page 962

“One must not say that He exists or that He does not exist, nor that He is living or that He is not living, for that would liken Him to what exists or to what does not exist.” This entails denying both sides of a contradiction—something whose impossibility is utterly plain. Moreover, these people necessarily liken Him to nonexistent things, impossibilities, and inanimate objects to a far greater degree than the resemblance to perfect living beings from which they sought to flee. The ways of declaring Him exalted and holy above all that He is free from are extensive; there is no need for this approach.

It has already been explained that whatever is denied of Him—glorified is He—is denied in a manner that entails affirmation, since mere negation contains neither praise nor perfection. Nonexistent things can be described through negation, and what does not exist does not resemble existing things; yet this is no praise of it. Resembling a deficient being in its deficiencies is itself a deficiency in every case, just as being identical to a created being in any attribute constitutes a likeness and resemblance above which the Lord—blessed and exalted is He—is far exalted. Deficiency is the opposite of perfection.

For example, it is known that He is living, and death is the opposite of life, so He is exalted above it. Likewise, sleep and drowsiness are contrary to the perfection of life, for sleep is the brother of death. Fatigue, too, is a deficiency in power and strength. Eating, drinking, and similar things involve dependence upon something other than oneself, just as seeking another’s assistance, relying on another’s support, and similar things entail dependence upon and need for that other. Anyone who needs someone to bear him or help sustain his being and his actions is dependent upon him

---

## Page 963

and is not self-sufficient without him. How much more so one who eats and drinks! One who eats and drinks has a hollow interior, whereas one who is solid, without a hollow interior, is more perfect than one who eats and drinks. This is why the angels have no hollow interiors and neither eat nor drink. It has already been explained that the Creator is more entitled to every perfection established for a created being, and more entitled to be free of every deficiency from which a created being is free. Revelation has denied these things of Him in several places, as in His statement, exalted is He:

“Allah, the Eternal Refuge.”

Al-Samad is He who has no hollow interior and neither eats nor drinks. This surah describes the Most Merciful, or is the foundation of this subject. He also says concerning the Messiah and his mother:

“The Messiah, son of Mary, was not but a messenger; other messengers have passed on before him. And his mother was a woman of truth. They both used to eat food.”

He thus made their eating food evidence that they were not divine. This demonstrates, all the more certainly, that He is exalted above such things. The liver, spleen, and similar organs serve the functions of eating and drinking. The Self-Sufficient One, who is exalted above these functions, is therefore exalted above the organs that serve them. The hand is different, for it serves work and action, and He—glorified is He—is described as working and acting, since these are attributes of perfection. One who can act is more perfect than one who cannot.

He—glorified is He—is exalted above having a consort or offspring, and above the organs and means associated with that. Likewise, weeping and sorrow entail weakness and incapacity, above which He—glorified is He—is exalted. Joy and anger, by contrast, are attributes of perfection. Thus, just as He is described as possessing power rather than

---

## Page 964

incapacity, knowledge rather than ignorance, life rather than death, hearing rather than deafness, sight rather than blindness, and speech rather than muteness, so too He is described as possessing joy rather than sorrow, laughter rather than weeping, and so forth.

Moreover, reason has established what revelation affirms: that He—glorified is He—has no equal or peer, and nothing is like Him. His reality therefore cannot be like the reality of any created being, nor can the reality of any of His attributes be like that of any attribute of created beings. It is thus known with certainty that He does not belong to any category of created beings: neither angels, nor the heavens, nor stars, nor air, nor water, nor earth, nor human beings, nor their bodies or souls, nor anything else. Indeed, it is known that His reality is further removed from resemblance to anything in existence than all other realities are, and that any resemblance between Him and any of them is more remote than the resemblance between the reality of one created being and that of another. For if two realities are alike, whatever is possible for either is possible for the other, and whatever is necessary for either is necessary for the other.

It would then follow that nonexistence and need, which are possible for a created being brought into existence, would also be possible for the beginningless Creator, whose existence is intrinsically necessary. What is affirmed of the one would also have to be affirmed of the other, including necessary existence and passing away. Thus, the same thing would be both intrinsically necessary and not intrinsically necessary, both existent and nonexistent. This combines two contradictories.

This demonstrates the falsehood of the position of those who liken Allah to His creation, saying, “Sight like my sight,” or “A hand like my hand,” and similar things. Exalted is Allah far above what they say!

---

## Page 965

The purpose here is not to give an exhaustive account of what is affirmed of Him, what He is declared free of, or the ways of establishing these matters, for this is discussed at length elsewhere. Rather, the purpose here is to draw attention to the general principles governing these matters and the ways of establishing them.

Whatever revelation neither affirms nor denies, and for which reason provides no grounds for either affirmation or negation, we leave unaddressed, neither affirming nor denying it. Thus, we affirm what we know to be established, deny what we know to be negated, and remain silent about what we do not know to be either negated or established. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 981

Section

As for the second principle—devoting worship to Allah alone, which encompasses belief in both divine law and divine decree—we say:

It is essential to believe in Allah’s creation and command. We must believe that Allah is the Creator, Lord, and Sovereign of all things; that He has power over all things; that whatever He wills comes to pass, and whatever He does not will does not come to pass; and that there is no might or power except through Allah. He knew what would happen before it happened, ordained all decrees, and recorded them wherever He willed. As the Most High says:

“Do you not know that Allah knows what is in heaven and earth? Indeed, that is in a Record. Indeed, that is easy for Allah.”

It is reported in the authentic collection that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah ordained the destinies of all creatures fifty thousand years before He created the heavens and the earth, and His Throne was upon the water.”

We must also believe that Allah commanded that He alone be worshipped, without any partner, just as He created the jinn and humankind to worship Him. For this purpose He sent His messengers and revealed His books. Worship of Him entails

---

## Page 982

utter humility before Him and perfect love for Him. This entails complete obedience to Him:

“Whoever obeys the Messenger has obeyed Allah.”

The Most High has said:

“We did not send any messenger except to be obeyed by Allah’s permission.”

And the Most High has said:

“If you love Allah, then follow me; Allah will love you and forgive you your sins.”

And the Most High has said:

“Ask those of Our messengers whom We sent before you: Did We appoint any gods besides the Most Merciful to be worshipped?”

“We did not send any messenger before you without revealing to him: There is no deity except Me, so worship Me.”

And the Most High has said:

“He has prescribed for you as religion what He enjoined upon Noah, that which We have revealed to you, and what We enjoined upon Abraham, Moses, and Jesus: ‘Establish the religion and do not be divided in it.’ What you call them to is hard for those who associate partners with Allah.”

And the Most High has said:

“O messengers, eat of the good things and do righteous deeds. Indeed, I know all that you do.”

“And indeed, this community of yours is one community, and I am your Lord, so fear Me.”

Thus, He commanded the messengers to establish the religion and not to be divided in it. Hence the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said in an authentic hadith:

“We prophets share one religion. The prophets are brothers with the same father and different mothers. Indeed, I am the closest of all people to the son of Mary; there is no prophet between him and me.”

This religion is Islam, and Allah accepts no other religion, whether from earlier or later generations, for all the prophets followed the religion of Islam. Allah Most High said concerning Noah:

“Relate to them the account of Noah, when he said to his people: ‘O my people, if you find my presence among you burdensome, along with my reminders

---

## Page 983

of Allah’s signs—then in Allah I put my trust. So resolve upon your course, together with your partners.’” Up to His saying:

“And I have been commanded to be among the Muslims.”

He said concerning Abraham:

“And who would turn away from the religion of Abraham except one who makes a fool of himself?”

Up to His saying:

“When his Lord said to him, ‘Submit,’ he said, ‘I have submitted to the Lord of the worlds.’”

Up to His saying:

“So do not die except as Muslims.”

He said concerning Moses:

“O my people, if you have believed in Allah, then put your trust in Him, if you are Muslims.”

He said in the account of the Messiah:

“And when I inspired the disciples, ‘Believe in Me and in My messenger,’ they said, ‘We believe, so bear witness that we are Muslims.’”

He said concerning the earlier prophets:

“The prophets who submitted to Allah judged by it for the Jews.”

He said concerning Balqīs that she said:

“My Lord, I have indeed wronged myself, and I have submitted, together with Solomon, to Allah, the Lord of the worlds.”

Islam entails submission to Allah alone. Whoever submits to Him and to another associates partners with Him, while whoever does not submit to Him is too proud to worship Him. Both the one who associates partners with Him and the one who is too proud to worship Him are disbelievers. Submission to Him alone entails worshipping Him alone and obeying Him alone.

This, then, is the religion of Islam, and Allah accepts no other. This can only be fulfilled by obeying Him at every time, doing what He has commanded at that time. Thus, if He initially commanded us to face

---

## Page 984

the Rock and subsequently commanded us to face the Kaaba, each of these two acts was part of Islam when He commanded it. Religion consists in obedience to Him and worship of Him in both acts; only an outward aspect of the act varies, namely, the direction faced in prayer. Likewise, the messengers’ religion is one, even though their laws, prescribed ways, prayer directions, and rites differ. Such differences do not prevent their religion from being one, just as they did not prevent this within the law of a single messenger.

Allah Most High made it part of the messengers’ religion that the first of them should announce the coming of the last and believe in him, and that the last should affirm the truth of the first and believe in him. Allah Most High said:

“And when Allah took the covenant of the prophets: ‘Whatever I give you of Scripture and wisdom, should a messenger then come to you confirming what you possess, you must believe in him and support him.’ He said, ‘Do you acknowledge this and accept My binding covenant?’ They said, ‘We acknowledge it.’ He said, ‘Then bear witness, and I am with you among the witnesses.’”

Ibn ‘Abbās said: Allah never sent a prophet without taking a covenant from him that, if Muhammad were sent while he was still alive, he would believe in him and support him. He also commanded him to take a covenant from his people that, if Muhammad were sent while they were still alive, they would believe in him and support him.

Allah Most High also said:

“And We have revealed to you the Scripture in truth, confirming the Scripture that came before it and standing as a guardian over it. So judge between them by what Allah has revealed, and do not follow their desires, turning away from the truth that has come to you. To each of you We have prescribed a law and a way.”

He made belief in any of the messengers inseparable from belief in the others, and declared anyone who claimed to believe in some and disbelieve in others to be a disbeliever.

---

## Page 985

Allah Most High said:

“Indeed, those who disbelieve in Allah and His messengers, wish to make a distinction between Allah and His messengers, say, ‘We believe in some and disbelieve in others,’ and wish to take a path in between—it is they who are truly disbelievers.”

He also said:

“Do you then believe in part of the Scripture and disbelieve in part? What recompense is there for those among you who do this except disgrace in the life of this world? On the Day of Resurrection, they will be returned to the severest punishment.”

Through to His words: “you do.”

He has also said to us:

“Say, ‘We believe in Allah, in what has been revealed to us, in what was revealed to Abraham, Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob, and the Tribes, in what Moses and Jesus were given, and in what the prophets were given by their Lord. We make no distinction between any of them, and to Him we submit.’

“If they believe as you have believed, they are rightly guided; but if they turn away, they are only in opposition. Allah will suffice you against them, and He is the All-Hearing, the All-Knowing.”

Thus, He commanded us to say: We believe in all of this, and to Him we submit. Whoever receives the message of Muhammad, peace and blessings be upon him, but does not acknowledge what he brought is neither a Muslim nor a believer. Rather, he is a disbeliever, even if he claims to be a Muslim or a believer.

As they relate, when Allah Most High revealed:

“Whoever seeks a religion other than Islam, it will never be accepted from him, and in the Hereafter he will be among the losers,”

the Jews and Christians said, “We too are Muslims.” Then Allah revealed:

“Pilgrimage to the House is a duty owed to Allah by people who are able to make their way there.”

They said, “We will not perform pilgrimage.” Then He, Most High, said:

“Whoever disbelieves—Allah has no need of any of His creation.”

For submission to Allah is not complete without acknowledging His right over His servants to require pilgrimage to the House, just as

---

## Page 986

he, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Islam is built upon five: testifying that there is no god but Allah and that Muhammad is the Messenger of Allah, establishing prayer, giving zakāh, fasting Ramadan, and performing pilgrimage to the House.”

Thus, when the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, stood at ʿArafah, Allah Most High revealed:

“Today I have perfected your religion for you, completed My favour upon you, and chosen Islam as your religion.”

People have disputed whether the earlier followers of Moses and Jesus were Muslims. “This is a dispute over terminology.” Islam in the specific sense—the religion with which Allah sent Muhammad, peace and blessings be upon him, encompassing the law of the Quran—is followed only by the community of Muhammad, peace and blessings be upon him. Today, when used without qualification, “Islam” refers to this. Islam in the general sense, however, encompasses every law with which Allah sent a prophet, and thus includes the submission of every community that followed one of the prophets.

The foundation of Islam in every sense is the testimony that there is no god but Allah. All the messengers were sent with this, as Allah Most High said:

“We certainly sent a messenger to every nation, saying, ‘Worship Allah and shun tāghūt.’”

Allah Most High also said:

“We never sent a messenger before you without revealing to him: ‘There is no god but Me, so worship Me.’”

He said of His intimate friend, Abraham:

“And when Abraham said to his father and his people, ‘I am free of all that you worship, except the One who created me; He will surely guide me.’ And he made this an enduring declaration among his descendants, so that they might return.”

Allah Most High also related his words:

“Have you considered what you have been worshipping—you and your forefathers? They are enemies to me, except the Lord of all worlds.”

Allah Most High said:

“You have a good example in Abraham and those with him, when they said to their people, ‘We are free of you and whatever you worship besides Allah. We reject you, and there has arisen between us and you

---

## Page 987

enmity and hatred forever, until you believe in Allah.’” He also said:

“And ask the messengers We sent before you: did We appoint gods besides the Most Merciful to be worshipped?”

He related that His messengers, such as Noah, Hūd, Sālih, and others, said to their people:

“Worship Allah; you have no god other than Him.”

He said of the People of the Cave:

“They were young men who believed in their Lord, and We increased them in guidance. We strengthened their hearts when they stood up and said, ‘Our Lord is the Lord of the heavens and the earth. We will never invoke any god besides Him, for then we would have said something far removed from the truth.’”

The passage continues to His words:

“Who, then, is more unjust than one who fabricates a lie against Allah?”

He, glorified be He, also said:

“Indeed, Allah does not forgive associating partners with Him, but He forgives anything less than that for whomever He wills.”

He stated this in two places in His Book. In His Book, He also explained the association of angels, prophets, celestial bodies, and idols with Allah—and the root of all such association is associating Satan with Him. He said of the Christians:

“They took their scholars and monks as lords besides Allah, and also the Messiah, son of Mary, although they were commanded only to worship one God. There is no god but Him. Glory be to Him, far above whatever they associate with Him.”

Allah Most High said:

“And when Allah said, ‘O Jesus, son of Mary, did you tell people, “Take me and my mother as two gods besides Allah”?’ He said, ‘Glory be to You! It is not for me to say what I have no right to say. Had I said it, You would surely have known it. You know what is within myself, whereas I do not know what is within Yourself. Indeed, You alone know all that is unseen. I told them nothing except what You commanded me: “Worship Allah, my Lord and your Lord.”’”

Allah Most High also said:

“It is not for any human being to be granted by

---

## Page 988

Allah the Scripture, wisdom, and prophethood, and then say to the people, ‘Be servants of mine rather than of Allah.’” The passage continues to His words:

“Nor would he command you to take the angels and prophets as lords. Would he command you to disbelief after you have become Muslims?”

He thus made clear that taking the angels and prophets as lords constitutes disbelief. It is well known that no created being has claimed that the prophets, scholars, monks, or the Messiah, son of Mary, shared with Allah in creating the heavens and the earth. Indeed, no one has even claimed that the world has two makers equal in their attributes and actions.

Nor has any descendant of Adam affirmed the existence of a god equal to Allah in all His attributes. Rather, most of those who associate partners with Allah acknowledge that the partner they associate with Him is not His equal. Indeed, most acknowledge that this partner belongs to Him, whether it is an angel, a prophet, a celestial body, or an idol. Thus, the Arab polytheists used to say in their pilgrimage invocation:

“Here I am at Your service. You have no partner except a partner who belongs to You: You own him and whatever he owns.”

The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, raised his voice proclaiming Allah’s oneness, saying:

“Here I am at Your service, O Allah, here I am. Here I am; You have no partner; here I am. Indeed, all praise and blessings are Yours, as is sovereignty. You have no partner.”

The writers on religious doctrines have recorded what they collected of the views of earlier and later peoples concerning religions, sects, opinions, and beliefs. Yet they have not reported anyone affirming a partner who shares with Him in creating all created things, or an equal to Him in all His attributes. Rather, among the most significant

---

## Page 989

views they have recorded on this subject is that of the dualists, who affirm two principles, “light” and “darkness,” and hold that light created good and darkness created evil. They then report two views held by them concerning darkness. The first is that it came into being, in which case it is among the things created by light. The second is that it is without beginning, but has produced only evil, and is therefore inferior to light in its essence, its attributes, and what it produces.

He, glorified be He, has told us in His Book of the polytheists’ acknowledgment that Allah is the Creator of all created things. He says:

“If you ask them who created the heavens and the earth, they will surely say, ‘Allah.’ Say, ‘Have you considered those you invoke besides Allah? If Allah intended harm for me, could they remove that harm? Or if He intended mercy for me, could they withhold His mercy?’ Say, ‘Allah is sufficient for me.
