He has recorded in the Preserved Tablet whatever He wills. When He creates the body of the fetus, before the soul is breathed into it, He sends an angel to it. The angel is instructed to record four matters and is told: “Write down his provision, his lifespan, his deeds, and whether he will be wretched or blessed.” There are other instances of this kind. This aspect of divine decree was denied in earlier times by the extremists among the Qadariyyah, but few deny it today.

The second level concerns Allah’s all-prevailing will and His all-encompassing power. It is to believe that whatever Allah wills happens, and whatever He does not will does not happen; that no movement or stillness occurs in the heavens or on earth except by the will of Allah, glorified be He; that nothing occurs in His dominion except what He wills; and that He, glorified and exalted, has power over all things, both those that exist and those that do not. There is no created being on earth or in heaven except that Allah, glorified be He, is its Creator. There is no creator other than Him and no Lord besides Him.

Nevertheless, He has commanded His servants to obey Him and His messengers and forbidden them to disobey Him. He, glorified be He, loves those who are mindful of Him, those who do good, and those who act justly. He is pleased with those who believe and do righteous deeds. He does not love the disbelievers, nor is He pleased with the defiantly disobedient. He does not command indecency, does not approve of disbelief for His servants, and does not love corruption.

---

## Page 1042

and His servants genuinely act, while Allah is the Creator of their actions. It is the servant who is a believer or a disbeliever, righteous or wicked, and who prays and fasts. His servants have the capacity to perform their deeds and possess a will, while Allah is their Creator and the Creator of their capacity and will, as He, exalted be He, says:

“For whoever wills among you to take a right course.”

“And you do not will except that Allah wills—Lord of the worlds.”

This level of divine decree is denied by most of the Qadariyyah, whom the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, called the Magians of this community. Some of those who affirm it go to such extremes that they have stripped the servant of his capacity and choice, and deny the wisdom and beneficial purposes of Allah’s actions and rulings.

---

## Page 1043

Section

Among the principles of Ahl al-Sunnah is that religion and faith consist of affirmation and action: affirmation of the heart and tongue, and action of the heart, tongue, and limbs. Faith increases through obedience and decreases through disobedience. Nevertheless, they do not declare those who pray toward the qiblah to be disbelievers merely for committing sins, including major sins, as the Khawārij do.

Rather, the brotherhood of faith remains despite sins, as Allah, glorified and exalted be He, says in the verse concerning legal retribution:

“But if someone is granted any pardon by his brother, then the claim should be pursued fairly.”

And He says:

“If two groups of believers fight, make peace between them. But if one of them transgresses against the other, fight the transgressing group until it returns to Allah’s command. If it returns, make peace between them with justice and act equitably. Indeed, Allah loves those who act equitably.”

“The believers are but brothers, so make peace between your two brothers.”

They do not entirely strip a sinful Muslim of the designation of believer, nor do they hold that he will remain in the Fire forever, as the Muʿtazilah maintain. Rather, a sinful Muslim is included under the designation of believer in passages such as the saying of Allah, exalted be He:

“Then a believing slave must be freed.”

Yet he may not be included under the unqualified designation of believer, as in the saying of Allah, exalted be He:

“The believers are only those whose hearts tremble when Allah is mentioned, and whose faith increases when His verses are recited to them.”

And the saying of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

---

## Page 1044

“A person who commits unlawful sexual intercourse is not a believer while committing it; a thief is not a believer while stealing; a person who drinks wine is not a believer while drinking it; and no one is a believer while plundering valuable property as people look up at him while he seizes it.”

They say: He is a believer whose faith is deficient, or a believer by virtue of his faith and sinful by virtue of his major sin. Thus, he is neither granted the unqualified designation of believer nor entirely stripped of that designation.

Section

Among the principles of Ahl al-Sunnah wa-l-Jamāʿah is keeping their hearts free of ill will and their tongues free of abuse toward the Companions of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, as Allah describes them in His saying, exalted be He:

“And those who came after them say, ‘Our Lord, forgive us and our brothers who preceded us in faith, and do not place in our hearts any resentment toward those who believe. Our Lord, indeed You are Kind and Merciful.’”

They also obey the Prophet in his saying:

“Do not revile my Companions. By Him in whose hand is my soul, if any one of you were to spend as much gold as Mount Uhud, it would not equal a mudd (a measure) given by one of them, nor even half of it.”

They accept what the Quran, the Sunnah, and scholarly consensus affirm concerning their virtues and ranks. They therefore consider those who spent and fought before the conquest—which refers to the Treaty of al-Hudaybiyah—superior to those who spent and fought afterward. They give precedence to the Muhājirūn (Emigrants) over the Ansār (Helpers), and they believe that “Allah said to the people of

---

## Page 1045

Badr—who numbered three hundred and ten-odd—‘Do whatever you wish, for I have forgiven you.’” And that no one who pledged allegiance beneath the tree will enter the Fire, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, stated. Indeed, Allah is pleased with them, and they are pleased with Him; they numbered more than fourteen hundred.

They testify that those whom the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, testified would enter Paradise will do so, including the ten, Thābit ibn Qays ibn Shammās, and other Companions.

They affirm what has been established through mass transmission from the Commander of the Faithful, ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib—may Allah be pleased with him—and others: that the best of this community after its Prophet is Abū Bakr, followed by ‘Umar. They place ‘Uthmān third and ‘Alī fourth—may Allah be pleased with them—as the transmitted reports indicate, and as the Companions—may Allah be pleased with them—unanimously agreed to give ‘Uthmān precedence when pledging allegiance.

Nevertheless, some among Ahl al-Sunnah previously differed over which of ‘Uthmān and ‘Alī—may Allah be pleased with them both—was superior, after agreeing on the precedence of Abū Bakr and ‘Umar. Some gave precedence to ‘Uthmān and said no more, or placed ‘Alī fourth; others gave precedence to ‘Alī, while others withheld judgment. However, Ahl al-Sunnah eventually settled on giving precedence to ‘Uthmān.

Although this question—the relative merit of ‘Uthmān and ‘Alī—is not, according to the majority of Ahl al-Sunnah, one of the foundational matters in which a person who disagrees is deemed misguided, the question in which disagreement does warrant that judgment is “the question of the caliphate.” They believe that the caliph after the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was Abū Bakr, followed by ‘Umar, then ‘Uthmān, then ‘Alī. Whoever impugns the caliphate of any of these leaders is more astray than his family’s donkey.

---

## Page 1046

They love the household of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and are loyal to them. They uphold the Messenger’s counsel concerning them, given on the day of Ghadīr Khumm:

“I remind you of your duty to Allah regarding my household. I remind you of your duty to Allah regarding my household.”

He also said to his uncle al-‘Abbās—who had complained to him that some of Quraysh treated Banū Hāshim coldly:

“By Him in whose hand is my soul, they will not believe until they love you for Allah’s sake and because of your kinship with me.”

He also said:

“Allah chose the descendants of Ishmael, and from the descendants of Ishmael He chose Kinānah; from Kinānah He chose Quraysh; from Quraysh He chose Banū Hāshim; and from Banū Hāshim He chose me.”

They hold the wives of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace—the Mothers of the Believers—in love and loyalty, and believe that they will be his wives in the Hereafter. This applies especially to Khadījah, may Allah be pleased with her, the mother of most of his children, the first to believe in him and support him in his mission, who held a lofty place in his esteem; and to the eminently truthful woman, daughter of the eminently truthful man—may Allah be pleased with them both—of whom the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“‘Ā’ishah’s superiority over other women is like the superiority of tharīd (bread soaked in broth) over all other food.”

They disavow the way of the Rāfidah, who hate the Companions and revile them, and the way of the Nawāsib, who harm the Prophet’s household through word or deed. They refrain from discussing the disputes that arose among the Companions.

---

## Page 1047

And they say:

Some of the reports transmitted about their misdeeds are false; others have been altered through additions, omissions, or distortion of their original meaning. As for the authentic reports, the Companions are excused in these matters: they exercised independent judgment, either correctly or mistakenly.

Nevertheless, they do not believe that every individual Companion is immune to major and minor sins. Rather, sins may occur among them. Yet their early service and virtues warrant forgiveness for whatever sins they may commit, if any should occur. Indeed, they are forgiven for misdeeds for which those who came after them would not be forgiven, because they possess good deeds that erase misdeeds to a degree unmatched by later generations. It has been established through the words of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“They are the best of generations.”

“A mudd (a small measure of food) given in charity by one of them is greater in merit than a mountain of gold the size of Uhud given by those who came after them.”

Furthermore, if one of them has committed a sin, he will either have repented of it, performed good deeds that erase it, been forgiven because of his early service, or been forgiven through the intercession of Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace—to whose intercession they are more entitled than anyone else—or suffered a worldly trial through which his sin was expiated.

If this holds true for established sins, what then of matters in which they exercised independent judgment: if they were correct, they receive two rewards; if they were mistaken, they receive one reward, and their error is forgiven?

Moreover, the objectionable actions of some of them are few and slight, overwhelmed by their virtues and merits: faith in Allah and His Messenger, striving in His cause, migration, giving support, beneficial knowledge, and righteous deeds.

---

## Page 1048

Whoever examines their lives with knowledge and insight, and considers the virtues Allah bestowed upon them, knows with certainty that they are the best of creation after the prophets: none like them have existed or will ever exist. They are the finest of the generations of this community, which is the best of all communities and the most honored by Allah Most High.

Among the fundamental principles of Ahl al-Sunnah wa-l-Jamā‘ah is belief in the miraculous gifts granted to Allah’s righteous friends and the extraordinary things Allah brings about through them: various forms of knowledge and disclosures of hidden matters, and various kinds of power and effects. Such things are related of earlier nations in Surah al-Kahf and elsewhere, and of the earliest members of this community—the Companions and the Successors—and all its subsequent generations. They will continue to occur within this community until the Day of Resurrection.

---

## Page 1049

Section

The way of Ahl al-Sunnah wa-l-Jamā‘ah also includes following the teachings and example of Allah’s Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, inwardly and outwardly; following the path of the first and foremost among the Emigrants and the Helpers; and following the counsel of Allah’s Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, who said:

“Adhere to my Sunnah and the Sunnah of the rightly guided caliphs after me. Hold fast to it and bite down upon it with your molar teeth. Beware of newly introduced matters, for every newly introduced matter is an innovation, and every innovation is misguidance.”

They know that the truest speech is Allah’s speech, and that the best guidance is the guidance of Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. They give Allah’s speech precedence over the speech of anyone else, whatever their station, and give Muhammad’s guidance, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, precedence over the guidance of everyone else. For this reason, they are called the People of the Book and the Sunnah.

They are also called Ahl al-Jamā‘ah (the people of unity), because jamā‘ah means coming together, and its opposite is division—although the word jamā‘ah has come to designate the assembled community itself. “Consensus” is the third foundation relied upon in matters of knowledge and religion. By these three foundations, they assess all people’s statements and deeds, inward or outward, that have a bearing on religion.

The consensus that can be reliably ascertained is that of the righteous early generations, since after their time disagreements multiplied and the community spread far and wide.

---

## Page 1050

Section

In addition to these principles, they enjoin what is right and forbid what is wrong as required by the Sharia. They hold that Hajj, jihad, Friday prayers, and Eid prayers should be conducted with the rulers, whether righteous or sinful, and they faithfully observe congregational prayers. They uphold sincere concern for the community as part of their religion and affirm the meaning of the Prophet’s saying, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“A believer is to another believer like a building, each part strengthening the other.”

He then interlaced his fingers, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. They also affirm his saying:

“The believers, in their mutual love, mercy, and compassion, are like a body: when one part suffers, the rest of the body responds with fever and sleeplessness.”

They enjoin patience in adversity, gratitude in prosperity, and contentment with the bitterness of divine decree. They call for noble character and good deeds, and affirm the meaning of his saying:

“The believers whose faith is most complete are those whose character is best.”

They encourage you to maintain ties with those who sever ties with you, give to those who withhold from you, and pardon those who wrong you. They enjoin dutifulness to parents, maintaining ties of kinship, being good neighbours, kindness to orphans, the needy, and wayfarers, and gentleness toward slaves; and they forbid boasting

---

## Page 1051

and arrogance, oppression, and asserting superiority over others, with or without justification. They enjoin noble character and forbid base conduct. In everything they say or do, in these matters or otherwise, they simply follow the Quran and the Sunnah.

“Their way” is the religion of Islam with which Allah sent Muhammad. However, since the Prophet foretold that his community would split into seventy-three sects, all of them in the Fire except one—the united community—and said in another hadith, “They are those who follow what I and my Companions follow today,” those who adhere to pure Islam, free of admixture, came to be known as the people of the Sunnah and the united community.

Among them are the steadfastly truthful, the martyrs, and the righteous. From their ranks come beacons of guidance and lamps in the darkness, whose merits have been handed down and whose virtues are recounted. Among them are the abdāl: the imams whose guidance and understanding the Muslims unanimously acknowledge. They are the victorious group of whom the Prophet said:

“A group of my community will continue to prevail upon the truth, unharmed by those who abandon them or oppose them, until the Hour comes.”

We therefore ask Allah, the Magnificent, to make us among them, not to let our hearts deviate after He has guided us, and to bestow mercy upon us from Himself. Indeed, He is the Bestower. Allah knows best. May Allah bless Muhammad, his family, and his Companions, and grant them abundant peace.

---

## Page 1052

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—said:

In the name of Allah, the Most Compassionate, the Most Merciful.

“All praise is for Allah, Lord of all worlds.”

“The Most Compassionate, the Most Merciful.”

“Master of the Day of Judgment.”

I bear witness that there is no deity but Allah alone, with no partner, supporter, or helper. And I bear witness that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger, whom He sent to all creation. May Allah bless him, his family, and his Companions, and grant them abundant peace, and likewise all of Allah’s righteous servants.

To proceed:

I have been asked more than once to write down what I could recall of the proceedings in the three sessions convened to debate matters of creed, pursuant to the Sultan’s letter sent from Egypt to his deputy, the governor of the region. This followed denunciations made to the Sultan by a group from among the Jahmiyyah, the Ittihādiyyah, the Rāfidah, and others who harboured grudges.

The governor therefore ordered the four judges—the judges of the four legal schools—to be assembled, together with others, including their deputies, muftis, and shaykhs who were respected and held in esteem. They did not know

---

## Page 1053

why they had been summoned for this appointed meeting. This was on Monday, the eighth of the blessed month of Rajab, 705 AH. He said to me:

“This session has been convened concerning you. The Sultan’s decree has arrived, instructing me to question you about your creed and the writings you have sent to Egypt, through which you call people to that creed.”

I believe he also said:

“And to assemble the judges and jurists so that you may discuss these matters together.”

I replied:

“As for creed, it is not to be taken from me, nor from anyone greater than I. Rather, it is to be taken from Allah and His Messenger, and from that upon which the early generations of the Muslim community agreed. Whatever is in the Quran must be believed, as must whatever is established in authentic hadith, such as those in the Sahīh collections of al-Bukhārī and Muslim.

“As for the writings, I have never, on my own initiative, written to anyone inviting him to any of these matters. Rather, I have written answers to questions put to me by people from Egypt and elsewhere.”

I had been told that a letter had been forged in my name to the emir Rukn al-Dīn al-Jāshankīr, the Sultan’s ustādh dār, containing a distorted creed. I did not know its exact nature, but I knew that it was fabricated. People would come to me from Egypt and elsewhere to ask about matters of creed and other subjects, and I would answer them on the basis of the Quran, the Sunnah, and what the early generations of the Muslim community followed.

---

## Page 1054

Then he said:

“We want you to write down your creed for us.”

I replied:

“Write it down.”

He then instructed Shaykh Kamāl al-Dīn to write, and he wrote down for him the main articles of belief concerning the divine attributes, divine decree, faith, the threat of punishment, leadership, and relative merit.

These were that the creed of Ahl al-Sunnah wa-l-Jamā‘ah entails believing in what Allah has said of Himself and what His Messenger has said of Him, without distortion or denial, without specifying how His attributes are, and without likening them to those of creation; and that the Quran is Allah’s speech, uncreated: from Him it began, and to Him it returns.

It also entails believing that Allah is the Creator of everything, including people’s actions and all else; that whatever Allah wills happens, and whatever He does not will does not happen; that He commands obedience, loves it, and is pleased with it, while He forbids disobedience and hates it. The servant truly acts, and Allah creates his action.

Faith and religion consist of words and deeds, and increase and decrease. We do not declare anyone who faces our direction of prayer an unbeliever on account of sins, nor do we hold that any believer will remain in the Fire forever. The caliphs after Allah’s Messenger were Abū Bakr, then ‘Umar, then ‘Uthmān, then ‘Alī. Their rank in merit follows their order in the caliphate, and whoever places ‘Alī ahead of ‘Uthmān has disparaged the Emigrants and the Helpers.

I stated this, or something to this effect. So much time has now passed that I do not remember the precise wording of what I dictated, but it was written down at the time. I then said to the emir and those present:

“I know that some people tell lies about me, just as they have done more than once before. If I dictate my creed from memory, they may say, ‘He concealed part of it;

---

## Page 1055

or compromised his convictions and sought to appease others.’ So I shall have a written creed brought, one written some seven years ago, before the Tatars came to Syria.”

Before it was brought, I said things that I can no longer recall precisely, and I became intensely angry. But I remember saying:

“I know that some people have lied about me and said things to the sultan.”

I said what I felt it necessary to say, including:

“Who other than me stood up for Islam in times of need? Who clarified its proofs and explained it, fought its enemies, and restored it when it faltered—when everyone abandoned it, no one presented its case, and no one fought in its defence? I stood up to make its case clear, fight in its defence, and encourage people to embrace it.

“If these people presume to malign me, how would they treat anyone else? Even if a Jew asked the sultan for justice, he would be obliged to grant him justice. I may waive my rights, or I may not. Indeed, I may ask him to grant me justice and have these people who lie brought forward, so that they may acknowledge their fabrications.”

I spoke at greater length along these lines, but so much time has passed that I no longer recall it precisely. The emir then motioned to Muhyī al-Dīn, the kātib al-daraj (scribe), to write that down.

I also said:

“Whoever disagrees with me on anything I have written, I know his own school of thought better than he does.”

I do not know whether I said this before the creed was brought or afterwards. But after it had been brought and read, I also said:

“There is not a single section in it on which someone among those who profess allegiance to our direction of prayer does not disagree, and every statement in it contradicts the position of one group or another.”

Then

---

## Page 1056

I sent someone to fetch it from my home, along with notebooks in my own handwriting, and “The Wāsitī Creed” was brought. I told them:

“The occasion for writing this was that a judge from the districts around Wāsit—a shaykh named Radī al-Dīn al-Wāsitī, of the Shāfi‘ī school—came to visit us while on pilgrimage. He was a man of goodness and religious devotion. He complained of the prevailing ignorance and injustice, and the fading away of religion and knowledge, in those lands and under Tatar rule. He asked me to write a creed that he and his household could rely upon. I asked to be excused and said, ‘People have already written numerous creeds; take one of the creeds of the imams of the Sunnah.’ But he persisted in his request, saying, ‘I want only a creed that you yourself write.’ So I wrote this creed for him while sitting after the afternoon prayer. Many copies of it have since circulated in Egypt, Iraq, and elsewhere.”

The emir indicated that I should not read it myself, so as to dispel any suspicion. He gave it to his scribe, Shaykh Kamāl al-Dīn, who read it aloud to those present, word for word. Everyone present listened, and any of them who wished could raise whatever objections he chose and challenge whatever he wished. The emir, too, asked about particular passages in it. People were aware of some of the opposition and partisan inclinations harboured by a group of those present. Some of this arose from matters of belief, and some from other causes.

It is impossible to recount the discussions and debates that took place in these sessions, for there was

---

## Page 1057

too much to recount fully. Nevertheless, I shall write a summary of what I can recall, despite the time that has elapsed and the raised voices and clamour that were difficult to follow.

One objection raised by someone present concerned the statement at the beginning of the creed:

“Faith in Allah includes believing in the attributes with which He has described Himself and with which His Messenger has described Him, without distorting or denying them, specifying their manner, or likening them to those of creation.”

He asked, “What is meant by distortion and denial?” His point was that this rules out the interpretation advocated by its proponents—namely, diverting an expression from its apparent meaning, whether this is held to be obligatory or permissible. I replied:

Distortion means altering words from their proper places, as Allah Most High condemns in His Book: diverting an expression from the meaning it conveys. An example is the interpretation given by some of the Jahmiyyah to His words, Most High:

“And Allah spoke to Moses directly.”

They take this to mean that He wounded him with the claws of wisdom. Other examples include the interpretations of the Qarmatians, the Bātiniyyah, and others among the Jahmiyyah, Rāfidah, Qadariyyah, and others.

He fell silent, while I had my own thoughts.

On another occasion, outside this session, I explained that I had chosen the term “distortion” rather than “interpretation” because distortion is expressly condemned in the Quran. In this creed, I endeavoured to follow the Book and the Sunnah, so I rejected the distortion that Allah condemns. I did not use the term “interpretation” either to deny or to affirm it, because it has several meanings, as I have explained in the relevant discussion of principles.

---

## Page 1058

for the meaning of the term “interpretation” in Allah’s Book differs from its meaning in the terminology of later scholars of legal theory and jurisprudence, and from its meaning in the terminology of many Quran commentators and scholars of the early generations. Some meanings described as interpretation are sound and have been transmitted from certain scholars of the early generations. I therefore did not deny anything whose soundness is established by proof. Thus, if something is established as sound by proof and is transmitted from the early generations, it is not distortion.

I also told him that, in stating what is denied, I mentioned likening Him to creation rather than resemblance, because Allah expressly denies the former in His Book, saying:

“There is nothing like Him.”

And:

“Do you know of any equal to Him?”

I preferred this to an expression found neither in Allah’s Book nor in the Sunnah of His Messenger, although denying resemblance may convey a sound meaning, just as it may convey a false one.

When I mentioned that they do not deny of Him the attributes with which He has described Himself, distort words from their proper places, or deviate concerning Allah’s names and signs, one of those present began to take offence, sensing the clear refutation of his position in those words. Yet he could find nothing to say. He wanted to steer the discussion towards questions familiar to me, but could not do so because he knew the answer.

When I mentioned the Verse of the Throne, I think he asked the emir about our statement, “No devil will approach him until morning.” I then mentioned Abū Hurayrah’s hadith concerning the one who was stealing the alms for Eid al-Fitr, and stated that al-Bukhārī had narrated it in his Sahīh. They began speaking at length about denying resemblance and corporeality, alluding to what some people attribute to us in these matters.

---

## Page 1059

so I said:

My statement, “without specifying how or likening Him to creation,” rules out every false conception. I chose these two expressions because the rejection of specifying how is transmitted from the early generations. Rabīʿah, Mālik, Ibn ʿUyaynah and others made the statement that scholars have received with acceptance:

“Rising over is known; its manner is unknown; belief in it is obligatory; and asking about it is an innovation.”

These scholars of the early generations thus agreed that the manner is unknown to us. I therefore rejected specifying it, following the early generations of this community.

It is also ruled out by scriptural evidence, for the interpretation of the verses concerning the attributes encompasses the true reality of the One described and the true reality of His attributes. This belongs to the interpretation known only to Allah, as I have explained in a separate treatise on interpretation, meaning, and the distinction between knowing the meaning of a statement and knowing its interpretation.

Likewise, likening Him to creation is ruled out by scriptural evidence and the consensus of the early generations. Reason also indicates that both likening Him to creation and specifying how must be rejected, since the Creator’s ultimate reality is unknown to human beings.

In this connection, I also mentioned al-Khattābī’s statement describing the position of the early generations: to take the verses and hadiths concerning the attributes according to their apparent meanings, while rejecting any specification of their manner or any resemblance to creation. For discussion of the attributes follows from discussion of the divine essence, proceeding along the same lines and following the same pattern. Thus, if affirming the divine essence means affirming its existence, not specifying how it is, then likewise affirming the attributes means affirming their existence, not specifying how they are.

---

## Page 1060

Then one of the leading opponents said: “In that case, it would be permissible to say: ‘He is a body unlike other bodies.’”

Some of the learned men present and I replied: “What was stated is only that Allah is described by what He has described Himself with and what His Messenger has described Him with. Neither the Quran nor the Sunnah says that Allah is a body, so this objection does not follow.”

One of the judges present, who was known for his religious devotion, sought to make it clear that we were innocent of the claims some people made and attributed to us. He therefore became increasingly emphatic in denying that we likened Allah to creation or attributed a body to Him.

I said: “In several places in it, I stated: ‘without distortion or denial of His attributes, and without specifying how or likening Him to creation.’ At its beginning I said: ‘Belief in Allah includes believing in the attributes with which He has described Himself in His Book and with which His Messenger Muhammad has described Him, without distortion or denial of His attributes, and without specifying how or likening Him to creation.’”

Then I said: “The descriptions that the Messenger gave of his Lord in the authentic hadiths accepted by those versed in this knowledge must likewise be believed.” I continued until I said: “And other similar authentic hadiths in which the Messenger of Allah gives such accounts. The saved group—the people of the Sunnah and the community—believe in these just as they believe in what Allah has related in His Book, without distortion or denial of His attributes, and without specifying how or likening Him to creation. Rather, they follow a middle course among the factions of this community, just as this community follows a middle course among the nations.

---

## Page 1061

and so, in matters of Allah’s attributes, they follow a middle course between the Jahmiyyah, who deny His attributes, and those who liken Him to creation.”

When this just judge saw their mutual support and partisanship, and how few knowledgeable supporters there were, he became afraid of them and said: “You have written an account of Imam Ahmad’s creed, so say: ‘This is Ahmad’s creed.’” He meant that a person may write according to his own school without being challenged, since this is an established school with its followers. His purpose was to put an end to the opponents’ dispute.

I replied: “I have compiled only the creed of all the righteous predecessors. This is not something exclusive to Imam Ahmad. Imam Ahmad merely conveys the knowledge brought by the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him. If Ahmad were to say something on his own initiative that the Messenger had not brought, we would not accept it. This is the creed of Muhammad.”

I said repeatedly: “I have given anyone who disagrees with anything in it three years. If he produces a single word contradicting what I have stated from anyone of the three generations—the generations praised by the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, when he said:

‘The best of generations is the generation in which I was sent, then those who follow them, then those who follow them’—

then I will retract that statement. For my part, I must produce reports from the three generations that agree with what I have stated, as transmitted by all the groups—the Hanafīs, Mālikīs, Shāfi‘īs, Hanbalīs, Ash‘arīs, scholars of hadith, Sufis, and others.”

---

## Page 1062

I also said on an occasion other than this gathering:

“Imam Ahmad—may Allah have mercy on him—received more of the Sunnah and the statements of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, than others did. He also endured more through the inquisition and the task of refuting proponents of innovation. His knowledge and teaching in this field were therefore more extensive, and he became more prominent than others as an imam of the Sunnah. Otherwise, the matter is as one of the righteous scholars of the Maghrib said: ‘The doctrine is that of Mālik and al-Shāfi‘ī; the prominence is Ahmad ibn Hanbal’s.’ This means that all the imams of Islam followed what Ahmad followed, although some surpassed others in knowledge, explanation, making the truth manifest, and refuting falsehood.”

When we reached the passage in the creed, “And the descriptions the Prophet gave of his Lord in the authentic hadiths that the scholars have accepted,” and then the hadith of Abū Sa‘īd, reported from the Prophet in both Sahīh collections:

“Allah will say on the Day of Resurrection, ‘O Adam!’ He will reply, ‘At Your service and ready to obey You.’ Then a call will be made in a voice: ‘Allah commands you to send forth a contingent to the Fire.’”

—and the remainder of the hadith—the emir asked them, “Is this hadith authentic?”

I replied, “Yes. It is in both Sahīh collections.” No one disputed this. The disputant had to acknowledge it, and those present agreed.

The emir requested a discussion of the question of letters and voice, since a request to that effect had been made.

I said: “This claim that many people attribute to Imam Ahmad and his followers—that the voices of those reciting the Quran and the ink in copies of the Quran are eternal and without beginning—as reported by Fakhr al-Dīn Ibn al-Khatīb (*)

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) The printed edition reads “Majd al-Dīn Ibn al-Khatīb.” The correction is based on Siyānat Majmū‘ al-Fatāwā, p. 255.

---

## Page 1063

and others—is a fabricated lie. Neither Ahmad nor any Muslim scholar ever said this, whether among Ahmad’s followers or anyone else.”

I brought out a booklet I had brought along with the creed. It contained Ahmad’s own words, as reported from Imam Ahmad by Shaykh Abū Bakr al-Khallāl in The Book of the Sunnah, together with what his companion Abū Bakr al-Marwadhī had collected of Imam Ahmad’s statements and those of the imams of his time and his other companions:

“Whoever says, ‘My utterance of the Quran is created,’ is a Jahmī; whoever says, ‘It is uncreated,’ is an innovator.”

I said: “This is precisely what al-Ash‘arī reported from Ahl al-Sunnah and the scholars of hadith in his Book of Doctrines, and he stated that he himself held this view.”

I said: “What, then, of someone who says, ‘My utterance is eternal’? What of someone who says, ‘My voice is uncreated’? What of someone who says, ‘My voice is eternal’?”

Imam Ahmad’s explicit statements distinguish between Allah speaking with a voice and the servant’s voice, as transmitted by al-Bukhārī, the compiler of the Sahīh, in The Creation of Human Actions, and by other imams of the Sunnah.

I also brought my answer to a question I had been asked long before, concerning someone who had sworn an oath of divorce regarding the question of “letters and voice” and the question of “the apparent meaning concerning the Throne.” I cited portions of that earlier answer and its detailed discussion of this question, including the point that to state without qualification that

---

## Page 1064

the Quran consists of letters and sound, or is neither letters nor sound, is—in either case—an innovation that arose after the third century. I said: “This is my answer.”

This question had been sent by a group of obstinate, Jahmī-leaning opponents, some of whom were present at the gathering. When the answer reached them, it silenced them. They had imagined that if I answered with what they supposed Ahl al-Sunnah held, they would achieve their aim of discrediting me; and if I answered with their own position, they would achieve their aim of securing my agreement. But when they received an answer setting out the distinction upheld by Ahl al-Sunnah—neither what they themselves maintained nor what they attributed to Ahl al-Sunnah, though some ignorant people might say it—they were confounded.

The answer also stated that the entire Quran, its letters and its meanings, is Allah’s speech. “Quran” does not designate the letters alone or the meanings alone.

During the discussion, I said to Sadr al-Dīn ibn al-Wakīl—to expose his many contradictions, his failure to adhere to a single position, and his pursuit of discord and division among Muslims—“I have a statement of creed by Shaykh Abū al-Bayān. It states: ‘Whoever says that a single letter of the Quran is created has disbelieved.’ You have written upon it in your own hand that this is the position of al-Shāfiʿī and the leading scholars among his followers, and that you hold it as your belief before Allah.”

He acknowledged this, whereupon Shaykh Kamāl al-Dīn ibn al-Zamlakānī challenged him on it. Ibn al-Wakīl said: “This is al-Shāfiʿī’s explicit statement.” He questioned him repeatedly about this. When we assembled at the second gathering, he mentioned to Ibn al-Wakīl that Ibn Dirbās had transmitted in the book

---

## Page 1065

al-Intisār (The Defence) a statement from al-Shāfiʿī like the one I had cited. Then, at the third gathering, Ibn al-Wakīl raised the matter again.

Shaykh Kamāl al-Dīn said to Sadr al-Dīn ibn al-Wakīl: “At that gathering, you told Shaykh Taqī al-Dīn that whoever says that a single letter of the Quran is created is a disbeliever.” He repeated this several times. At this point, Shaykh Kamāl al-Dīn became extremely angry, raised his voice, and said:

“This declares our fellow Ashʿarī theologians disbelievers—those who say that the letters of the Quran are created, such as Imām al-Haramayn and others. We will not tolerate our fellows being declared disbelievers.”

Ibn al-Wakīl denied having said that, saying: “I did not say that. I only said that whoever denies a single letter of the Quran has disbelieved.”

Those present rejected his denial and said: “What you said was precisely such-and-such.” They also said: “You should not make a statement and then retract it.”

One of them said: “He did not say this.” Then, when they distorted the account, he said: “We did not hear him say this.” Eventually, the sultan’s deputy said: “One man lies, and another bears witness!”

Shaykh Kamāl al-Dīn, still angry, turned to the Shāfiʿī chief judge, Najm al-Dīn, urgently appealing for his support against Ibn al-Wakīl for having declared his fellows disbelievers.

Judge Najm al-Dīn said: “I did not hear this.”

Shaykh Kamāl al-Dīn became angry and said something whose exact wording I did not retain, but whose meaning was that this was a slight upon al-Shāfiʿī and a disgrace to them: their leading scholars were being declared disbelievers, yet no one came to their defence.

I did not hear what Shaykh Kamāl al-Dīn said concerning Judge Najm al-Dīn. Other attendees were asked whether they had heard him say anything about the judge. They said: “No.” But the judge believed

---

## Page 1066

that the reproach was directed at him because he was the judge of the school yet had not defended his fellows, and that this was what Shaykh Kamāl al-Dīn intended. Chief Judge Najm al-Dīn became angry and said:

“Bear witness that I have resigned.”

He then began recounting the qualities that entitled him to precedence and justified his appointment, and his restraint from speaking ill of those present, calling upon the sultan’s deputy to bear witness to this. I addressed him in words affirming my esteem for him and his fitness to continue performing his duties in these circumstances.

When we reached the passage concerning the Quran:

“Faith in Him includes believing that the Quran is the speech of Allah, uncreated. From Him it originated, and to Him it returns,”

some of them disputed the statement, “From Him it originated, and to Him it returns,” and asked for an explanation.

I said:

This statement is authentically transmitted from the early generations. For example, ʿAmr ibn Dīnār reported:

“For seventy years, I have known people to say: ‘Allah is the Creator, and everything besides Him is created, except the Quran, for it is the speech of Allah, uncreated. From Him it originated, and to Him it returns.’”

Several scholars have compiled the reports concerning this from the Prophet, the Companions, and their successors, including the hadith scholars Abū al-Fadl ibn Nāsir and Abū ʿAbd Allāh al-Maqdisī.

As for its meaning, their statement, “From Him it originated,” means that He is the One who spoke it and sent it down from Himself. It is not as the Jahmiyyah claim: that it was created in al-hawā or elsewhere, or that it originated with someone other than Him.

As for “to Him it returns,” it will be taken away by night, at the end of time, from the written copies of the Quran and from people’s hearts, so that no

---

## Page 1067

word of it will remain in people’s hearts, nor a single letter in the written copies of the Quran. Most of those present agreed with this, and those who disputed it fell silent. On another occasion, outside this gathering, I spoke to one of them and showed him the creed compiled by Imam al-Qādirī, which states that the Quran is the speech of Allah and came forth from Him. He hesitated over this wording.

I said:

This is how the Prophet put it:

“The servants draw near to Allah through nothing like that which came forth from Him.”

He meant the Quran. Khabbāb ibn al-Aratt said:

“You there, draw near to Allah as best you can, for nothing through which one draws near to Him is dearer to Him than that which came forth from Him.”

Abū Bakr al-Siddīq said, upon reading the ‘Quran’ of Musaylimah the Liar:

“This speech did not come from an ill”—meaning a Lord.

The creed also states:

“Faith in Him includes believing that the Quran is the speech of Allah, sent down and uncreated; that from Him it originated, and to Him it returns; that Allah truly spoke it; and that this Quran, which Allah sent down to Muhammad, is truly the speech of Allah, not the speech of anyone else. It is not permissible to state without qualification that it is a reproduction of Allah’s speech or an expression of it. Rather, when people recite it or write it in copies of the Quran, it does not thereby cease to be the speech of Allah. For speech is truly attributed to the one who originally uttered it, not to the one who conveys and relays it.”

One of them took exception to affirming that it is truly the speech of Allah, despite having conceded that Allah Most High truly spoke it.

---

## Page 1068

Then he conceded this when it was explained to him that a figurative usage can validly be denied, whereas this attribution cannot. It was also explained to him that the sayings transmitted from earlier generations, and the poetry attributed to poets, are truly their words; the attribution of the Quran to Allah can be no less real than that. Everyone present therefore agreed with what was stated concerning the Quran: that Allah truly spoke, and that the Quran is truly the speech of Allah, not the speech of anyone else.

When the creed stated:

“Speech is truly attributed to the one who originally uttered it, not to the one who conveys and relays it,”

they approved of these words and held them in high regard. Even the foremost opponents, such as Ibn al-Wakīl and others, began expressing great admiration for them. They showed delight in this concise formulation, saying, “You have dispelled this doubt for us and set our hearts at ease,” and making other remarks of this kind.

When we reached the discussion of belief in the Last Day, with its detailed explanation and orderly presentation, they likewise approved of it and held it in high regard.

The same was true when we reached the discussion of belief in divine decree and its two levels, along with the other weighty principles contained in the creed. Likewise, they approved of the discussion concerning the sinner who remains within Islam, and concerning faith. Objections were, however, raised to this, which I shall mention.

---

## Page 1069

After the entire creed had been read and discussed, the objections raised by the obstinate disputants amounted to four questions:

— The first concerned our statement:

“Among the fundamental principles of the saved group is that faith and religion consist of speech and action, increasing and decreasing: the heart’s affirmation and the tongue’s utterance, and the actions of the heart, tongue, and limbs.”

They said:

“If this is said to be among the fundamental principles of the saved group, then anyone who does not affirm it falls outside that group—for example, our fellow speculative theologians who say that faith consists of assent, and those who say that faith consists of assent and verbal profession. If they are not among those who are saved, it follows that they are doomed.”

As for the other three questions, which were their principal objections, they directed them at our statement:

“Included in what we have mentioned concerning belief in Allah is belief in what Allah has stated in His Book, what has been mass-transmitted from the Messenger of Allah, and what the early generations of the Muslim community unanimously agreed upon: that He, glorified be He, is above His heavens, upon His Throne, exalted above His creation, and that He is with them wherever they are, knowing what they do. He combined both matters in His saying, exalted be He:

‘It is He who created the heavens and earth in six days and then established Himself above the Throne. He knows what penetrates into the earth and what emerges from it and what descends from the heaven and what ascends therein; and He is with you wherever you are. And Allah, of what you do, is Seeing.’

His saying, ‘And He is with you,’ does not mean that He is intermingled with creation. The language does not entail this, and it contradicts both the unanimous agreement of the early generations of the Muslim community and the innate disposition upon which Allah created His creatures.

---

## Page 1070

Rather, the moon is one of Allah’s signs, among the smallest of His creatures. It is situated in the sky, yet it is with the traveller wherever he may be, and with those who are not travelling. He, glorified be He, is above the Throne, watching over His creation, overseeing them and fully aware of them, along with the other meanings of His Lordship. All these statements that Allah Most High has made—that He is above the Throne and that He is with us—are true in their real sense and require no distortion. They must, however, be safeguarded against false assumptions.”

The second question:

Some of them said: “We affirm the transmitted wording, such as that in the hadith of al-‘Abbās—the hadith of the mountain goats—‘Allah is above the Throne.’ But we do not say ‘above the heavens,’ nor do we say ‘upon the Throne.’”

They also said: “We say:

‘The Most Merciful established Himself upon the Throne.’

But we do not say ‘Allah established Himself upon the Throne,’ nor do we describe Him as ‘established.’”

They repeated this point several times: namely, that the transmitted wording must be repeated exactly, without replacing it with synonymous wording, and that no meaning whatsoever is to be understood from it. Nor should it be said that it indicates any attribute of Allah at all. We shall discuss this at length in the second session, as we shall relate, if Allah Most High wills.

The third question:

They said: “The comparison with the moon likens Allah’s being in heaven to the moon’s being in the sky.”

The fourth question:

They said: “You say, ‘True in their real sense.’ The real sense is the literal linguistic meaning, and the only thing understood from that literal meaning is the establishment of physical bodies and their being above other things. The Arabs coined these expressions only for physical bodies. Thus, affirming the real sense is nothing but attributing corporeality to Allah, and denying corporeality while affirming this is either a contradiction or mere appeasement.”

---

## Page 1071

So I answered their questions by explaining that, in my expression “the creed of the saved group,” I meant the group that the Prophet described as saved when he said:

“My nation will divide into seventy-three groups: seventy-two in the Fire and one in Paradise. That one consists of those who follow what I and my Companions follow today.”

This creed is what has been transmitted from the Prophet and his Companions, may Allah be pleased with them. They and those who follow them are the saved group. It is reliably established that more than one Companion said, “Faith increases and decreases.” Everything I mentioned concerning this has been transmitted from the Companions through reliable chains of transmission, in both wording and meaning. If those who came after them disagreed with them, that would not detract from this.

Then I told them: “Not everyone who disagrees with any part of this creed must necessarily be doomed. The person who disputes it may have exercised scholarly judgment but reached an erroneous conclusion, and Allah may forgive his error. He may not have received sufficient knowledge concerning the matter for the proof to be established against him. He may also have good deeds through which Allah erases his sins.

“If statements threatening punishment that encompass such a person do not necessarily apply to someone acting on an interpretation, a devout worshipper, someone whose good deeds erase his sins, someone who has been forgiven, or others in similar circumstances, then this statement is even less grounds for such a conclusion. Rather, what these words entail is that whoever holds this creed is saved with respect to this belief, whereas whoever holds the opposite may or may not be saved. This is like the saying, ‘Whoever remains silent is saved.’”

As for the second question, I first answered them by explaining that every expression I had used was transmitted from the Prophet, such as “above the heavens,” “upon the Throne,” and “above

---

## Page 1072

the Throne.” I said, “Write down the answer,” and the scribe began writing it.

Then some of those present said, “Today’s session has gone on long enough. Let this be deferred to another session. You can write the answer yourselves and bring it to that session.” Some who agreed with me suggested completing the discussion by writing down the answer, lest their questions and objections circulate unanswered. The opponents had a purpose in delaying the written answer: they wanted to prepare themselves, consult their books, bring those of their companions who had been absent, and examine the creed together so that they could challenge it and raise objections. It was therefore agreed that the discussion would be concluded on Friday, and we adjourned on that understanding.

Allah had made the proof manifest and the right path clear in a manner that strengthened the Sunnah and the community and humbled the people of innovation and misguidance. Many people had various thoughts about what might happen at the second session. During those days, they began examining the creed and considering the answers I had given on matters of belief, such as the Hamawiyyah response concerning Allah’s rising over the Throne, the divine attributes known through revelation, and other matters.

---

## Page 1073

Section

When the second session was held on Friday, 12 Rajab, they had brought most of their shaykhs who had not attended the first session. They had also brought Safī al-Dīn al-Hindī, saying, “He is the foremost among them and their master in kalām (theology).” They had discussed matters among themselves, reached an agreement, and coordinated their position. They came with greater strength and preparation than before, for the first session had taken them by surprise—though it had likewise taken by surprise the person they were addressing, who was being questioned and was to answer and debate.

When we assembled, I had brought my written response to their earlier questions, which they had requested be deferred until that day. I praised Allah with the Sermon of Need, the sermon narrated by Ibn Mas‘ūd, may Allah be pleased with him. Then I said:

Allah Most High has commanded us to remain united and harmonious, and has forbidden us to become divided and disagree. He has said to us in the Quran:

“And hold firmly to the rope of Allah all together and do not become divided.”

And He has said:

“Indeed, those who have divided their religion and become sects—you have nothing to do with them.”

And He has said:

“And do not be like those who became divided and differed after clear proofs had come to them.”

---

## Page 1074

And our Lord is one, our Book is one, and our Prophet is one. The fundamentals of religion admit neither division nor disagreement. What I say brings Muslims together and is agreed upon by the early generations. If it accords with the community, then praise be to Allah. Otherwise, whoever opposes me after that—I shall reveal the secrets to him, tear away the veils, and expose the corrupt doctrines that have ruined religious communities and states. I shall travel by postal relay to the reigning sultan and inform him of matters that I will not mention in this assembly, for peace calls for one kind of speech and war another.

I also said:

There is no doubt that people dispute with one another. One says, “I am a Hanbalī,” and another says, “I am an Ash‘arī.” Division, strife, and disagreement arise between them over matters whose true nature they do not understand. I have brought material demonstrating that the schools agree on what I have stated. I have also brought (the book *Exposing the Slanderer’s Lies Concerning What Is Attributed to Shaykh Abū al-Hasan al-Ash‘arī*—may Allah have mercy on him—by the hadith scholar Abū al-Qāsim Ibn ‘Asākir—may Allah have mercy on him.

I said:

No book like this has been written concerning the praiseworthy accounts of al-Ash‘arī. It includes his own words from his book *al-Ibāna* (The Elucidation).

When I reached the mention of the Mu‘tazila, the emir asked what “Mu‘tazila” meant. I replied: In earlier times, people disagreed concerning a sinful member of the Muslim community: was he an unbeliever or a believer? This was the first disagreement to arise within the religion.

The Khawārij said:

“He is an unbeliever.”

And the community said:

---

## Page 1075

“He is a believer.”

Another group said:

“We say that he is sinful, neither a believer nor an unbeliever; we assign him an intermediate position between the two.”

They held that he would remain in the Fire forever, and withdrew from the study circle of al-Hasan al-Basrī—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—and his companions. They were therefore called the Mu‘tazila.

The senior shaykh, wearing his gown and cloak, said:

“It is not as you have said. Rather, the first question over which Muslims disagreed was the question of speech. The speculative theologians were called mutakallimūn because they discoursed on that subject. The first to advance this position was ‘Amr ibn ‘Ubayd; after his death, ‘Atā’ ibn Wāsil succeeded him.”

That is how he put it, and he said more along these lines.

I became angry with him and said:

“You are mistaken. This is a falsehood contrary to the consensus.”

I also told him:

“Neither manners nor merit! You have neither addressed me courteously nor answered correctly.”

Then I said:

“People disagreed over the question of speech during the caliphate of al-Ma’mūn and thereafter, toward the end of the second century AH. The Mu‘tazila, however, had existed long before that, in the time of ‘Amr ibn ‘Ubayd, after the death of al-Hasan al-Basrī, early in the second century AH. Those people had neither discussed the question of speech nor disputed it. Their first innovation was their discourse concerning religious designations, the rulings attached to them, and threats of punishment.”

He said:

“Al-Shahrastānī mentioned this in his book al-Milal wa-l-Nihal (Religions and Sects).”

I replied:

“Al-Shahrastānī mentioned that in connection with the designation mutakallimūn: why were they called mutakallimūn? He did not mention it in connection with the designation Mu‘tazila.”

---

## Page 1076

and the emir had asked only about the name Mu‘tazila. Those present objected to him, saying:

“You are mistaken.”

In the course of my remarks, I also said:

“I know every innovation that has arisen in Islam, who first introduced it, and what led to its introduction.”

Moreover, what al-Shahrastānī mentioned concerning the name mutakallimūn is incorrect. The speculative theologians were known by this name before they disputed the question of speech. They used to say of Wāsil ibn ‘Atā’, “He is a speculative theologian,” and describe him as versed in speculative theology, even though people had not yet disagreed over the question of speech.

Others and I said:

“It was Wāsil ibn ‘Atā’”—not ‘Atā’ ibn Wāsil, as the objector had stated.

I said:

“Wāsil did not come after the death of ‘Amr ibn ‘Ubayd; rather, he was his contemporary.”

It is reported that Wāsil once delivered an address, whereupon ‘Amr ibn ‘Ubayd said:

“If a prophet were sent, he would not speak any better than this.”

His eloquence is well known. It is even said that he had a speech impediment and carefully avoided the letter rā, to the point that, when he was told:

“The emir has ordered that a well be dug,”

he said:

“The chief has commanded that a well be dug on the highway.”

When the discussion reached what al-Ash‘arī had said, the leading shaykh among them said:

“There is no doubt that Imam Ahmad is an imam of great stature and one of the foremost imams of Islam. Yet some people who affiliated themselves with him introduced innovations.”

---

## Page 1077

I replied:

“That is true, but it is not peculiar to Ahmad. Every imam has had people claim allegiance to him whom he himself disavows. Some have claimed allegiance to Mālik whom Mālik disavows; others have claimed allegiance to al-Shāfi‘ī whom he disavows; and others have claimed allegiance to Abū Hanīfa whom he disavows. Some have claimed allegiance to Moses, peace be upon him, whom he disavows, and others to Jesus, peace be upon him, whom he disavows. Some have claimed allegiance to ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib whom he disavows. Our Prophet, too, has had people claim allegiance to him—Qarmatians, esotericists, and other kinds of heretics and hypocrites—whom he disavows.”

In his remarks, he mentioned that some of the Hashwiyya and those who liken Allah to His creation had claimed allegiance to Ahmad, and made similar statements.

I replied:

“Those who liken Allah to His creation and those who attribute a body to Him are more numerous outside Imam Ahmad’s following than within it. These various Kurdish groups are all Shāfi‘īs, and among them there is a degree of likening Allah to His creation and attributing a body to Him that is found in no other group. The people of Jīlān include both Shāfi‘īs and Hanbalis.”

I said:

“As for the Hanbalis proper, such views are not found among them to the extent that they are found among others.”

The full response also included the point that the Karrāmiyya, who attribute a body to Allah, are all Hanafis. I also discussed the term Hashwiyya—I do not recall whether this was in response to a question from the emir or someone else, or whether it was not in response to a question at all.

I said:

“The first to coin this term were the Mu‘tazila, for they call the community

---

## Page 1078

and the vast majority hashw (the common masses), just as the Rāfida call them ‘the general populace.’ The expression hashw al-nās refers to people at large, the general populace, rather than distinguished individuals. They say, ‘He is one of the common masses,’ just as one says, ‘He is one of the general populace.’ The first to use this expression was ‘Amr ibn ‘Ubayd, who said, ‘‘Abdullāh ibn ‘Umar, may Allah be pleased with him, was a Hashwī.’ Thus, the Mu‘tazila called the community hashw, just as the Rāfida call them ‘the general populace.’”

I said—I do not recall whether this was in the first session or the second—“The first to say that Allah is a body was Hishām ibn al-Hakam, the Rāfidī.”

I said to this shaykh:

“Which of Imam Ahmad’s followers—may Allah have mercy on him—is a Hashwī in the sense you intend? Al-Athram, Abū Dāwūd, al-Marwadhī, al-Khallāl, Abū Bakr ‘Abd al-‘Azīz, Abū al-Hasan al-Tamīmī, Ibn Hāmid, al-Qādī Abū Ya‘lā, Abū al-Khattāb, Ibn ‘Aqīl?”

I raised my voice and said:

“Name them! Tell me which of them! Who are they? Are Ibn al-Khatīb’s lies and fabrications about people’s beliefs to invalidate the Sharia and erase the defining features of the religion?

“For instance, he and others attributed to them the assertion that the eternal Quran consists of the voices of its reciters and the ink of its scribes, and that these voices and this ink are eternal, without beginning. Who said this? In which book is this found as a statement of theirs? Tell me! The same applies to the claim attributed to them that Allah will not be seen in the Hereafter, based on the implication he alleged and the premise he attributed to them.”

I then began to speak of the regard this shaykh deserved, noting that he was the elder of the group

---

## Page 1079

and their shaykh, and that he should be treated with the consideration warranted by his intelligence and piety. I instructed that the entire creed be read to him, for he had not attended the first session; they had only brought him to the second to enlist his support.

A trustworthy man told me that, after the shaykh had left the session, he met with him and said, “Tell me about this session.”

He replied, “Neither so-and-so nor I was at fault. The emir asked about one matter, and he answered him accordingly, whereas I thought he had asked about something else.”

He also said, “I told them: ‘You have no grounds for objecting to this man. He defended refraining from non-literal interpretation, while you defend adopting it. Both positions are attributed to al-Ash‘arī.’”

He said, “I prefer the position of refraining from non-literal interpretation.” He then produced his testament, which contained that position.

The man who related this to me said:

“I said to him, ‘I heard that at the end of the session—when the group were made to attest to their agreement—you said: “Do not record either a denial or an affirmation on my behalf.” Why was that?’

“He replied, ‘For two reasons. First, I was not present when the entire creed was read in the first session. Second, my associates had summoned me to enlist my support, so it would not have been fitting for me to express disagreement with them.’”

Thus, he remained silent regarding both parties.

---

## Page 1080

And more than once I instructed that the entire creed be read again to this shaykh. Some of those present, however, thought this would take too long and that only the passage about which they had questions should be read to him. Their principal question concerned the expression “in the literal sense,” so they read that passage to him.

He then offered a sound discussion concerning the meaning conveyed by the wording. I approved of it, praised him for it, and said:

“There is no doubt that Allah is truly living, truly knowing, truly hearing, and truly seeing. This is agreed upon by the people of the Sunnah and those who affirm the divine attributes, across all groups. Even if some adherents of innovation dispute part of this, there is no doubt that Allah exists and that the created being exists. Whether the term ‘existence’ is applied to both equivocally, with only the wording shared; univocally, with both wording and meaning shared; or by graded predication, which is a form of univocal predication—on any of these views, Allah truly exists and the created being truly exists. Applying the same designation to the Creator and the created being in the literal sense entails no inadmissible consequence.”

On that occasion, I did not give preference to any one of these three views over the others, for my purpose was fully achieved in any case. My aim was to establish what I had stated on the premises of all the groups, and to show that the early generations and those who followed them agreed upon what I had stated, and that the leading scholars of the four schools, al-Ashʿarī, and his foremost followers held the position I had set out.

For before the second session, I had been approached by some leading scholars of the Shāfiʿī school, scholars affiliated with the Ashʿarīs, Hanafīs, and others. They were deeply apprehensive about this session, feared that their opponents would prevail in it, and also feared for themselves

---

## Page 1081

because of the breakdown of unity. For if the proof supporting what I had stated were presented, or if none of the leading scholars among their own adherents agreed with it, division would result. It would then be difficult for them to declare publicly that they were departing from their groups’ positions, since this would enable their enemies to achieve their aims.

But if some leading scholars of their schools held that position, the evidence established it, and it became clear that it was the position of the early generations, they could openly affirm it, in keeping with their inward conviction that it was the truth. Indeed, one of the leading Hanafī scholars who met with me said, “If you said, ‘This is Ahmad’s position,’ and stood by that, the dispute would end.”

He meant that your opponents could be kept at bay on the grounds that this was an established position with recognized followers, while both the supporter and the disputant would be spared having to declare their agreement.

I replied:

“No, by Allah! This is not something peculiar to Ahmad ibn Hanbal. Rather, it is the creed of the early generations of this Ummah and the leading scholars of hadith.”

I also said, “This is the creed of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. For every expression I have used, I cite a Quranic verse, a hadith, or a consensus of the early generations. I also identify those who report that consensus from the early generations, drawn from all Muslim groups: the jurists of the four schools, the speculative theologians, the scholars of hadith, and the Sufis.”

I also spoke to the leading Shāfiʿī scholars who addressed me—in order to make clear that what I had stated was the position of the early generations and of the leading scholars among al-Shāfiʿī’s followers, and to cite the statements of al-Ashʿarī and his foremost followers that refute these opponents—saying that every Shāfiʿī would surely prevail, as would every adherent of al-Ashʿarī’s position

---

## Page 1082

that accords with the position of the early generations. I would also explain that the view attributed to him concerning the figurative interpretation of the attributes known through revelation has no basis in his own statements; it is merely the view of a group of his followers. Thus, the Ash'arīs have two positions; al-Ash'arī himself does not.

When, during the gathering, I mentioned that all the names of Allah also applied to created beings are like the term “existence”—which, according to all three views, is predicated literally of both the Necessary Being and contingent beings—two senior scholars disputed whether it is predicated equivocally or univocally.

One said, “It is univocal.” The other said, “It is equivocal, so as not to entail composition.”

The latter added, “Fakhr al-Dīn has stated that this dispute rests on whether a thing’s existence is identical with its essence. Those who say that the existence of every thing is identical with its essence hold that the term is predicated equivocally, whereas those who say that its existence is something additional to its essence hold that it is predicated univocally.”

The first then began arguing in favour of the view that existence is additional to essence, in order to defend its univocal predication.

The second said, “It is not the position of al-Ash'arī and Ahl al-Sunnah that a thing’s existence is identical with its essence.” The first rejected this.

I said, “As for the speculative theologians of Ahl al-Sunnah, they hold that the existence of every thing is identical with its essence;

---

## Page 1083

whereas the other view is that of the Mu'tazilah: that the existence of every thing is something additional to its essence. Each of the two was correct in one respect. The correct position is that these names are predicated univocally, as I have established elsewhere, and I have answered the objection concerning composition with the two well-known responses.

As for making this depend on whether a thing’s existence is identical with its essence, this is one of the errors attributed to Ibn al-Khatīb. Even if we say that a thing’s existence is identical with its essence, it does not follow that the term must be predicated of it and of another like it only equivocally, as is evident with all generic terms. The term ‘blackness’ is predicated univocally of this instance of blackness and that instance of blackness, yet the one instance is not identical with the other. The term denotes the feature common to both, namely the unrestricted universal. But it exists as unrestricted, on condition that it remain unrestricted, only in the mind. This does not entail denying the common feature shared by particular things existing in external reality. Otherwise, univocal terms would cease to exist, although they constitute the great majority of terms generally encountered.

(These are generic terms in the linguistic sense: a term applied to a thing and to everything resembling it, whether it names an entity or an attribute, whether it is non-derived or derived, and whether or not it denotes a genus in logic or jurisprudence. Indeed, generic terms in language encompass genera, classes, species, and the like. All are univocal terms, while the particular things they denote in external reality are distinct.)”

Some of them asked for the hadiths mentioned in the creed to be read again, in order to impugn

---

## Page 1084

some of them. I understood what he intended and said: “It seems you have prepared yourself to impugn the hadith of the mountain goats—the hadith narrated by al-'Abbās ibn 'Abd al-Muttalib.”

They had persisted in finding fault until they came upon Zakī al-Dīn 'Abd al-'Azīm’s discussion of al-Bukhārī’s statement in his History: “'Abd Allāh ibn 'Amīrah is not known to have heard directly from al-Ahnaf.”

I said: “Besides being narrated by the compilers of the Sunan collections, such as Abū Dāwūd, Ibn Mājah, al-Tirmidhī and others, this hadith is transmitted through two well-known routes. Discrediting one does not discredit the other.”

He said: “Does its transmission not hinge on Ibn 'Amīrah? Al-Bukhārī said that he is not known to have heard directly from al-Ahnaf.”

I replied: “The imam of imams, Ibn Khuzaymah, narrated it in his Book of Divine Oneness, with the stipulation that he would cite as evidence only what one upright narrator transmitted from another through an unbroken chain back to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.”

I added: “Affirmation takes precedence over negation. Al-Bukhārī only denied knowing that he had heard directly from al-Ahnaf; he did not deny that others knew this. Thus, if someone else—such as the imam of imams, Ibn Khuzaymah—knows what establishes the chain of transmission, his knowledge and affirmation take precedence over another person’s negation and lack of knowledge.”

Those present agreed with this. Some of them then began praising me in terms that it would be inappropriate for me to repeat. They began debating matters that were not in the creed, but were related to answers I had given on certain questions and to what they might understand from

---

## Page 1085

the creed. One of their leading figures then brought out al-Bayhaqī’s Book of Names and Attributes—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—and said: “This contains an interpretation of ‘the Face’ transmitted from the early generations.”

I replied: “Perhaps you mean the words of Allah Most High:

‘And to Allah belong the east and the west. So wherever you turn, there is the Face of Allah.’”

He said: “Yes. Mujāhid and al-Shāfi‘ī said that it means the direction of prayer prescribed by Allah.”

I replied: “Yes, this is authentically reported from Mujāhid, al-Shāfi‘ī and others, and it is correct. This verse is not one of the verses concerning the divine attributes. Anyone who counts it among those verses is mistaken, as some have done. The context indicates the intended meaning, for Allah says:

‘And to Allah belong the east and the west. So wherever you turn, there is the Face of Allah.’

“The east and the west are directions, and here wajh means ‘direction.’ One says, ‘Which way do you mean?’—that is, which direction—and, ‘I mean this way’—that is, this direction. As Allah Most High says:

‘For each is a direction toward which he turns.’

“This is why He says:

‘So wherever you turn, there is the Face of Allah.’

“That is, whichever direction you face and turn toward.”

And Allah knows best. May Allah bless Muhammad.

---

## Page 1086

[Shaykh ʿAlam al-Dīn reported that the Shaykh—may Allah sanctify his soul—said at the gathering of the sultan’s deputy, al-Afram, when asked about his creed, having brought his creed, “al-Wāsitiyyah”: “I wrote this some seven years ago, before the Tatars came to Syria.” It was then read aloud at the gathering.

ʿAlam al-Dīn further reported that the Shaykh said:

“The reason I wrote it was that one of the judges of Wāsit, a man of virtue and religious devotion, complained of the people’s plight in their lands under Tatar rule: the prevalence of ignorance and injustice] (*) and the fading away of religion and knowledge. He asked me to write a creed for him. I told him, ‘People have already written down the creeds of the imams of the Sunnah.’ But he persisted in his request, saying, ‘I want only a creed that you yourself write.’ So I wrote this creed for him while sitting after the ʿAsr prayer.

“The emir then signalled to his scribe, who read it aloud to those present, word for word. One of them objected to my statement in it:

‘Part of faith in Allah is to believe in what He has described Himself with and what His Messenger has described Him with, without distortion or negation, without specifying how His attributes are or likening them to those of creation.’

“What he meant was that this rules out figurative reinterpretation—that is, diverting an expression from its apparent meaning—whether obligatory or permissible.

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd states (p. 25):
> “‘Alam al-Dīn is al-Birzālī, Muhammad ibn al-Qāsim, the renowned hāfiz (hadith master), who died in 739. He has a well-known chronicle from which Ibn ‘Abd al-Hādī—may Allah have mercy on him—quotes in his biography of Shaykh al-Islām. Al-Dhahabī’s concluding remarks indicate that the original account was taken from one of his chronicles, but I have not found it among the materials available to me.”

---

## Page 1087

“So I said: ‘I chose the word “distortion” rather than ta’wīl (interpretation), because distortion is a term condemned in the Quran. In this creed, I sought carefully to follow the Book and the Sunnah, so I ruled out the distortion that Allah condemned. I did not use the term ta’wīl, because it has several meanings, as I explained in the relevant discussion of principles. The meaning of ta’wīl in Allah’s Book differs from its technical meaning among later scholars of legal theory and jurisprudence, and also from its usage among many Quranic commentators and early scholars.’

“I also told them: ‘Among the things ruled out, I mentioned likening Him to creation, but did not mention resemblance, because Allah explicitly ruled out the former in His Book, saying:

“There is nothing like unto Him.”’

“They began discussing the rejection of resemblance and of attributing a body to Allah, speaking at length and alluding to accusations of this kind that some people make against us. I said: ‘My words, “without specifying how His attributes are or likening them to those of creation,” rule out every false notion. I chose these two terms because the rejection of specifying how His attributes are has been transmitted from the early scholars. Rabīʿah, Mālik, Ibn ʿUyaynah and others made the statement that scholars have accepted:

“Istiwāʾ is known; its manner is unknown; believing in it is obligatory, and asking about it is an innovation.”

‘These early scholars thus agreed that its manner is unknown to us. I therefore ruled out specifying it, following the early scholars of this community. It is also ruled out by scripture. For the ta’wīl of the verses concerning the attributes encompasses the true nature of

---

## Page 1088

the One described, as well as the true nature of His attributes, neither of which is known. This belongs to the ta’wīl that only Allah knows, as I explained in a separate discussion on “Ta’wīl and Meaning” and the distinction between knowing the meaning of a statement and knowing its ta’wīl.
