Likewise, likening Him to creation is ruled out by scripture and the consensus of the early scholars. Reason also indicates that both likening Him to creation and specifying how His attributes are must be ruled out, since the Creator’s true nature is unknown to human beings.’

In this connection, I cited al-Khattābī’s statement, which he reported as the position of the early scholars:

“The verses and hadiths concerning the attributes are to be understood according to their apparent meanings, without specifying how those attributes are or likening them to those of creation. Discussion of the attributes follows from discussion of the Essence, proceeding on the same basis and following the same pattern. Thus, if affirming the Essence means affirming its existence, not specifying how it is, then affirming the attributes likewise means affirming their existence, not specifying how they are.”

One of the leading opponents then said: “In that case, it is permissible to say that He is a body unlike other bodies.”

Some of the learned men and I replied to him: “All that was said was that Allah is described as He described Himself and as His Messenger described Him. Neither the Book nor the Sunnah states that Allah is a body, so this conclusion does not follow. The first person to say that Allah is a body was Hishām ibn al-Hakam, the Rāfidī (Rejectionist).”

As for our statement:

“They occupy the middle position among the community’s sects, just as this community occupies the middle position among the nations. Regarding Allah’s attributes, they occupy the middle position between the Jahmiyyah, who deny the attributes, and the Mushabbihah, who liken Him to creation.”

I was told: “You have written an account of Imām Ahmad’s creed.” They intended thereby to end the dispute, since his was an established school with followers.

---

## Page 1089

I replied: “What I have set out is nothing but the creed of all the righteous early generations; it is not peculiar to Imām Ahmad.”

I also said: “I have allowed anyone who disagrees with any part of it three years. If he produces even a single statement from the first three generations that contradicts what I have stated, I will retract that point. For my part, I undertake to produce reports from the first three generations agreeing with what I have stated, as transmitted by all the groups: the Hanafīs, Mālikīs, Shāfiʿīs, Hanbalīs, Ashʿarīs, scholars of hadith, and others.”

Then the disputant asked to discuss (the question of letters and sound. I said: “The claim attributed to Ahmad and his followers—that the voices of those reciting the Quran and the ink in its written copies are eternal and without beginning—is a fabricated lie. Neither Ahmad nor any Muslim scholar said this.”

I brought out a booklet containing what Abū Bakr al-Khallāl reported from Imām Ahmad in “The Book of the Sunnah,” together with what Ahmad’s companion Abū Bakr al-Marwudhī had collected from his statements and those of the leading scholars of his time: whoever says, “My utterance of the Quran is created,” is a Jahmī, while whoever says, “It is uncreated,” is an innovator in religion.

I said: “What, then, of someone who says, ‘My utterance is without beginning’? What of someone who says, ‘My voice is eternal’?”

The disputant said: “Some of the Hashwiyyah and those who liken Allah to creation have claimed affiliation with Ahmad,” and made similar remarks.

I replied: “Those who liken Allah to creation and attribute a body to Him are more numerous among people outside Imām Ahmad’s following than among his followers. All these various groups of Kurds are Shāfiʿīs, yet among them there is a degree of likening Allah to creation and attributing a body to Him not found in any other group. Among the people of Jīlān are both Shāfiʿīs and Hanbalīs. As for those who are exclusively Hanbalī, these views are not found among them to the extent that they are among others. And the Karrāmiyyah, who attribute a body to Allah, are all Hanafīs.”

---

## Page 1090

And I said to him: “Who among our scholars is a Hashwī in the sense you intend? Al-Athram, Abū Dāwūd, al-Marwudhī, al-Khallāl, Abū Bakr ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz, Abū al-Hasan al-Tamīmī, Ibn Hāmid, Judge Abū Yaʿlā, Abū al-Khattāb, Ibn ʿAqīl?”

I raised my voice and said: “Name them! Tell me which of them! Is the Sharia to be invalidated and the defining features of religion effaced on the strength of Ibn al-Khatīb’s lies and fabricated accounts of people’s doctrines? He and others have attributed to them the claim that the eternal Quran consists of the reciters’ voices and the scribes’ ink, and that the voice and ink are eternal and without beginning. Who said this? In which book of theirs is this found? Tell me! Likewise, the claim that Allah will not be seen in the Hereafter was attributed to them on the basis of the necessary implication he alleged and the premise he ascribed to them.”

When “the question of the Quran” arose—namely, that it is Allah’s speech, uncreated, that it originated with Him and will return to Him—some of them disputed the statement that it originated with Him and will return to Him, and asked for an explanation. I said: “This statement is authentically transmitted from the early generations.

“For example, ʿAmr ibn Dīnār reported: ‘For seventy years I have known people to say: Allah is the Creator, and everything besides Him is created, except the Quran, for it is Allah’s speech, uncreated; it originated with Him and will return to Him.’

“The meaning of ‘it originated with Him’ is that He is the One who spoke it and sent it down from Himself. It is not as the Jahmiyyah claim: that it was created in the air or elsewhere and originated with someone other than Him.

“As for ‘it will return to Him,’ it will be taken away by night, at the end of time, from written copies and from people’s hearts,

---

## Page 1091

so that not a single word of it will remain in people’s hearts, nor a single letter in written copies.” Most of those present agreed with this.

I then said: This is likewise what the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Nothing brings servants closer to Allah than that which came from Him”—meaning the Quran.

Khabbāb ibn al-Aratt said: “You there, draw near to Allah by whatever means you can, for nothing brings one closer to Allah that is more beloved to Him than that which came from Him.”

I also said: “Allah truly spoke it, and this Quran, which Allah sent down to Muhammad, peace and blessings be upon him, is truly Allah’s speech, not the speech of anyone else. It is not permissible to say without qualification that it is a reproduction of Allah’s speech or an expression of it. Rather, when people recite the Quran or write it in written copies, it does not thereby cease to be truly the speech of Allah Most High. For speech is properly attributed to the one who originally uttered it, not to someone who conveys and transmits it.”

One of them took exception to affirming that it is truly Allah’s speech, despite having conceded that Allah truly spoke it. He then conceded this too, once it was explained to him that a figurative attribution may validly be negated, whereas this attribution cannot; and that the sayings transmitted from earlier generations, and the poetry attributed to its poets, are truly their speech.

When I stated in it that speech is properly attributed to the one who originally uttered it, not to someone who conveys it, they approved of this statement and held it in high regard.

I also mentioned that what the early generations of the Ummah unanimously affirmed—that He, glorified be He, is above the Throne and that He is with us—is true in its actual sense. It requires no distortion, but must be protected from false assumptions.

---

## Page 1092

And His statement:

“He is with you wherever you are”

does not mean that He is mingled with creation. The language does not entail this; moreover, it contradicts the consensus of the early generations of the Ummah and the innate disposition upon which Allah created His creatures. Indeed, the moon is one of Allah’s signs and among the smallest of His creations. It is situated in the sky, yet it is with the traveller wherever he may be.

When I discussed all the names of Allah that are also applied to created beings—such as the term “existence,” which is truly predicated of both the Necessary Being and contingent beings—two leading scholars disputed whether it is predicated equivocally, with distinct meanings, or univocally, with a shared meaning. One said, “It is univocal.” The other said, “It is equivocal, so as not to entail composition.”

The latter said: “Fakhr al-Dīn stated that this dispute rests on whether existence is identical with essence or not. Whoever holds that the existence of every thing is identical with its essence says that the term is predicated equivocally; whoever holds that its existence is something additional to its essence says that it is predicated univocally.”

The first then began arguing in favour of the position that existence is additional to essence, to support his claim that the term is predicated univocally. The second said, “The position of al-Ash‘arī and Ahl al-Sunnah is that existence is identical with essence.” The first denied this.

I said: As for the scholastic theologians of Ahl al-Sunnah, they hold that the existence of every thing is identical with its essence. The other position belongs to the Mu‘tazilah: that the existence of every thing is something additional to its essence. Each of the two was correct in one respect, for the correct position is that these names are predicated univocally, as I have established elsewhere.

---

## Page 1093

As for basing this on whether a thing’s existence is identical with its essence or is not identical with the existence of its essence, this is an error attributed to Ibn al-Khatīb. For even if we say that a thing’s existence is identical with its essence, it does not follow that the term must be predicated of it and of other things merely equivocally.

This holds for all generic terms. Thus, “blackness” is predicated univocally of this instance of blackness and that instance, although the two instances are not identical. The term signifies what they have in common, namely, the unrestricted universal. Yet this exists as unrestricted, subject to the condition of being unrestricted, only in the mind.

This does not entail denying what concrete entities in external reality have in common. Otherwise, there would be no “univocal terms,” which constitute the majority of terms found in languages. These are “generic terms in linguistic usage”: terms applied to a thing and to other things resembling it, whether they denote an entity or an attribute, whether they are underived or derived, and whether they designate a genus in logic or jurisprudence, or neither. Indeed, generic terms in linguistic usage encompass genera, classes, species, and the like. All these terms are univocal, while the individual things they denote are distinct in external reality.

Al-Dhahabī said:
“It was then agreed that this is a sound creed in accordance with that of the early generations (*).”

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) See the note at the bottom of page 194 of this volume.

---

## Page 1094

And ʿAbd Allāh ibn Taymiyyah wrote to his brother Zayn al-Dīn:

In the name of Allah, the Most Merciful, the Bestower of Mercy.

From his brother, “ʿAbd Allāh ibn Taymiyyah,” to the shaykh, imam, learned and virtuous scholar, and eminent dignitary, “Zayn al-Dīn.” May Allah Most High adorn him with the virtues of His friends, honor him in this world and the Hereafter with the honor He bestows upon His chosen servants, grant him glad tidings of the greatest victory over his enemies, and inspire him to give thanks for His blessings—especially the greatest of them: the mighty victory that Allah, glorified is He, has graciously granted to Islam, the Sunnah, and its followers over the party of Satan and his allies.

To proceed:

In writing to you, I praise Allah, besides whom there is no deity, and who is worthy of all praise. I invoke blessings upon His Prophet Muhammad—may the finest blessings and peace be upon him. I also inform you of the greatest victory and manifest triumph that Allah, glorified is He, has graciously bestowed upon us and upon all Muslims. Although minds cannot grasp the full measure of its excellence, nor tongues describe it fully, we shall briefly recount whatever Allah, glorified is He, enables us to convey, without undue length.

---

## Page 1095

namely, that on Monday, 8 Rajab, the sultan’s deputy assembled the four judges, their deputies, the muftis, and the shaykhs: Najm al-Dīn, Shams al-Dīn, Taqī al-Dīn, and Jamāl al-Dīn; Jalāl al-Dīn, the deputy of Najm al-Dīn; Shams al-Dīn ibn al-ʿIzz, the deputy of Shams al-Dīn; ʿIzz al-Dīn, the deputy of Taqī al-Dīn; Najm al-Dīn, the deputy of Jamāl al-Dīn; Shaykh Kamāl al-Dīn ibn al-Zamalkānī, Shaykh Kamāl al-Dīn ibn al-Sharshī, and Ibn al-Wakīl, of the Shāfiʿīs; Shaykh Burhān al-Dīn ibn ʿAbd al-Haqq, of the Hanafīs; Shaykh Shams al-Dīn al-Harīrī, of the Mālikīs; Shaykh Shihāb al-Dīn al-Majd, of the Shāfiʿīs; Shaykh Muhammad ibn Qawwām; and Shaykh Muhammad ibn Ibrāhīm al-Urmawī.

The sultan’s deputy then asked about the creed. He replied:

“Creed is not for me—or anyone greater than me—to determine. Rather, creed is taken from Allah, glorified and exalted is He, and His Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and from that upon which the early generations of the Muslim community reached consensus. It is taken from the Book of Allah Most High, from the hadiths recorded by al-Bukhārī and Muslim and other well-known hadiths, and from what is authentically established from the early generations of the Muslim community.”

The emir said, “We want you to write us a statement of the creed.”

The shaykh replied, “If I say something now from memory, the liars may say, ‘He concealed part of it or compromised.’ Instead, I shall bring what I wrote several years before this gathering, before the Tatars arrived.”

I then brought al-Wāsitiyyah. It was given this name because the man who requested it from the shaykh was one of the judges of Wāsit, a follower of al-Shāfiʿī, who had come to perform Hajj some ten years earlier. He was a man of great righteousness and devotion, and he asked the shaykh to write a creed for him. The shaykh said to him:

---

## Page 1096

“People have written extensively on this subject, so take one of the creeds of Ahl al-Sunnah.”

He replied, “I would like you yourself to write one for me.” So, while seated in his gathering after the afternoon prayer, the shaykh wrote this “creed” for him.

The shaykh related the substance of this exchange to the emir. The creed was then read aloud to those present, word for word, from beginning to end, and he discussed several passages in it. Among those present were people who harbored feelings towards the shaykh that Allah alone knew. They thought that, by debating this work with him, they would demonstrate that he differed from the beliefs of Ahl al-Sunnah wa-l-Jamāʿah.

They raised three questions concerning three passages: “calling it the creed of the saved sect,” the statement “He truly rose,” and the statement “above the heavens.” The shaykh said to the scribe whom the sultan’s deputy had appointed—Shaykh Kamāl al-Dīn ibn al-Zamalkānī—“Write down the answers to these questions.” The session had lasted from midmorning until shortly before the afternoon prayer, so they proposed postponing this until a second session, on Friday, 12 Rajab.

They assembled, and al-Safī al-Hindī attended with them. I attended this second session; I had not known about the first session when they gathered for it. During those days, they had discussed al-Fusūs and studied it, and had agreed to leave no possibility unexplored.

When I arrived after the Friday prayer and the gathering had settled, those present praised al-Safī al-Hindī. Several of them said, “He is the group’s leading shaykh and foremost authority in this subject; people have studied this discipline under him.” They agreed that he alone would speak with the shaykh; once he had finished, the others would speak one after another.

---

## Page 1097

Then the shaykh addressed the gathering, praising and extolling Allah with the sermon transmitted by Ibn Masʿūd, may Allah be pleased with him. He then said:

“Allah Most High has commanded us to remain united and harmonious, and has forbidden division and discord. Our Lord is one, our Messenger is one, our Book is one, and our religion is one. The early generations and the imams of Islam do not disagree over the fundamentals of religion, nor is division over them permissible, for Allah Most High says:

‘And hold firmly to the rope of Allah, all together, and do not become divided.’

“And He says:

‘Indeed, those who have divided their religion and become sects—you have nothing to do with them.’

“People have disputed this subject. One says, ‘I am a Hanbalī,’ and another says, ‘I am an Ashʿarī.’ I have brought the books of al-Ashʿarī and those of his foremost followers, such as the books of Abū Bakr ibn al-Bāqillānī. I have also brought accounts of the beliefs of the early scholars among the Mālikīs, Shāfiʿīs, Hanbalīs, hadith scholars, and Sufi shaykhs, showing that they all agree upon a single creed.”

He likewise brought accounts from the leading followers of Abū Hanīfah, such as Muhammad ibn al-Hasan and al-Tahāwī, including their statements concerning the divine attributes and other fundamentals of religion. He read a section of what the hadith scholar Ibn ʿAsākir had mentioned in his book, “al-Ibānah” (The Elucidation), and the statement that he upheld Imam Ahmad’s position.

He brought “Kitāb al-Tamhīd” (The Book of the Introduction) by Judge Abū Bakr ibn al-Bāqillānī. He also brought “transmitted accounts” from Mālik and his foremost followers, such as Ibn Abī Zayd, Judge ʿAbd al-Wahhāb, and other leading followers of Mālik, explicitly stating that Allah, in His very essence, is established over the Throne.

He said, “What I set forth is the doctrine of the early generations.” He brought their own statements and those of people who

---

## Page 1098

transmitted their doctrines—scholars of the four schools, hadith scholars, speculative theologians, and Sufis. “I set out how this accords with the Book and sunnah, and how nothing in it is contradicted by reason. If Allah Most High unites the hearts of those gathered upon this, then praise be to Allah, Lord of the worlds. But if anyone opposes it, I would have something to say concerning the other person’s words. I would reveal the secrets, tear away the veils, explain whatever requires explanation, meet with the Sultan, and speak to him in other terms.”

It was a momentous day, witnessed by many, on which a great deal was made clear to those present through discussion and transmitted evidence. He discussed matters outside “al-Aqīdah al-Wāsitiyyah” (The Wāsitī Creed) when he brought them his response concerning the Quran and Allah’s being established over the Throne—questions he had been asked some twelve years earlier—and read passages from that response to them. They asked him about certain expressions in the response known as “al-Hamawiyyah” (The Hamā Response), and presented him with all the replies they had in mind. They said, “This is our question, and nothing further remains on our minds.” When the shaykh answered their questions, they agreed with him, and the gathering concluded on that basis.

He had told them, “Anyone who disagrees with anything I have said should write out his disagreement in his own hand and cite statements from the early generations concerning the point on which he disagrees. Alternatively, let each person write a creed, and let these creeds be submitted to those in authority, so that it may be determined which accords with the Book and sunnah.”

He also said, “Whoever brings even a single word from the early generations contrary to what I have stated, I will adopt it. I will bring the accounts transmitted by all the groups, showing that they have described the doctrine of the early generations just as I have set it out. I agree with the early generations

---

## Page 1099

and defend that position in debate. All the leading authorities of the various groups—the Hanafīs, Mālikīs, Shāfiʿīs, Hanbalīs, Ashʿarīs, hadith scholars, and Sufis—agree with what I say.”

They asked him whether the apparent meaning was in agreement with this or not. He replied: “This is not in ‘The Creed,’ but I will volunteer an answer on behalf of most of those who have recounted the doctrine of the early generations—such as al-Khattābī, Abū Bakr al-Khatīb, al-Baghawī, Abū Bakr and Abū al-Qāsim al-Tamīmī, Abū al-Hasan al-Ashʿarī, Ibn al-Bāqillānī, Abū ʿUthmān al-Sābūnī, Abū ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-Barr, Judge Abū Yaʿlā, al-Sayf al-Āmidī, and others—concerning their rejection of modality and resemblance to created beings in relation to the attributes, and their statement that discussion of the attributes follows from discussion of the Essence: it follows the same approach and the same model. Thus, if affirming the Essence is an affirmation of existence for the purpose of affirming a modality, then affirming the attributes likewise is an affirmation of existence, not an affirmation of a modality.

“A group has reported . . . (1) (*) that the doctrine of the early generations is that the apparent meaning is not intended.”

He said: “These two reports can be reconciled by recognizing that ‘apparent meaning’ is an expression with more than one meaning. An apparent meaning befitting only a created being is not intended; whereas an apparent meaning befitting the majesty and greatness of Allah Most High is intended. This is the meaning intended in the names and attributes of Allah Most High, such as the Ever-Living, the All-Knowing, the All-Powerful, the All-Hearing, and the All-Seeing.”

Subtle discussions took place which only a few people could understand. It was explained that Allah Most High is above His Throne in a manner befitting His majesty; and I do not say

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank in the original.
> 
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd states (p. 25):
> The Shaykh, may Allah have mercy on him, attributed this statement in (33/177) to “some later scholars,” without naming them, saying: “Those among the later scholars who say that the position of the salaf (early generations) is that the apparent meaning is not intended.” In (6/355), he likewise identified those making this statement as “some people,” without naming them.
> It therefore appears that the blank should read “min al-muta’akhkhirīn (among the later scholars).” Allah Most High knows best.

---

## Page 1100

that He is above it as one created being is above another, as those who liken Allah to His creation maintain. Nor should it be said that there is no Lord above the heavens or upon the Throne, as the Jahmiyyah who deny the divine attributes maintain. Rather, it should be said that He is above His heavens, upon His Throne, distinct from His creation.

He discussed the term “direction,” explaining that it has more than one meaning, and also the term “reality.” He was asked about the Quran and voice, and gave a detailed answer, as he had previously done. He said:

“Whoever says that a person’s voice when reciting the Quran, or the ink of the written Quran, is eternal is mistaken and astray. No scholar among Imam Ahmad’s followers, or among others, has said this. The claim attributed to them—that the Quran is nothing but the voice heard from the reciter and the ink in the written Quran, and that it is nevertheless eternal—is a fabricated lie. Ahmad did not say this.”

He produced statements from Imam Ahmad and his followers, and from the followers of Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, al-Ashʿarī, and others, stating:

“Whoever says, ‘My utterance of the Quran is uncreated,’ is an innovator.”

How much more, then, someone who says, “My voice when reciting it is uncreated,” or, “My voice when reciting it is eternal”!

He clarified the issue, explaining that an unqualified denial of letters in Allah’s speech is an innovation: neither Imam Ahmad nor any of the other imams whose teachings are followed ever made such a statement. Rather, the position of the early generations is that the Quran—its letters and its meanings—is the speech of Allah. Speech is attributed in its literal sense to its original speaker, not to the person who conveys and transmits it. He also explained that Allah spoke with a voice, and cited the hadith of Abū Saʿīd, may Allah be pleased with him, recorded in the two Sahīh collections. Then the deputy began

---

## Page 1101

—the Mālikī’s deputy—to say:

“You say that Allah calls out with a voice.”

The shaykh replied, “That is what your Prophet said, if you believe in him; and that is what Muhammad ibn ʿAbd Allāh said, if you regard him as a Messenger.”

Whenever the shaykh cited a hadith and attributed it to the two Sahīh collections, the sultan’s deputy would ask them, “Is this what the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said?” They would answer, “Yes.” He would then say, “What, then, can be said against someone who affirms what the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said?”

He also asked the shaykh, “Did you say everything you have said on your own authority?”

He replied, “Rather, I transmit all of it from the Prophet of this community, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. I explain that the various groups within Islam transmit it from the early generations just as I have transmitted it, and that the imams of Islam uphold it. I debate in its defence, and I am familiar with the school of thought of everyone who opposes me.”

The shaykh became greatly indignant with the Mālikī’s deputy and al-Safī al-Hindī, silencing them both so completely that neither said anything worth mentioning thereafter. These sheets cannot accommodate all the details of what happened.

After the session, some of the Shāfiʿīs brought an excerpt from al-Qurtubī’s Quran commentary stating that none of the early generations denied that Allah Most High truly rose over the Throne; that they did not maintain that direction must be denied, and spoke only according to what His Messengers had conveyed; and that the Throne was singled out in this regard because it is the greatest of created things. What they did not know was the manner of His rising over the Throne, whose actual nature is unknown. As Mālik, may Allah have mercy on him, said:

“Rising over the Throne is known—meaning in the language—but its manner is unknown. Belief in it is obligatory, and asking about it is an innovation.”

The Mālikī said, “We did not know this.”

---

## Page 1102

After the session, Ibn al-Wakīl and others spread an indescribable amount of lies and fabrications that contradicted the actual state of affairs. Know, therefore, that anything reaching you that contradicts what I have described is lies and fabrication.

We still do not know how the matter unfolded in Egypt, except for what appears in the sultan’s letter:

“It has reached us that Shaykh So-and-so has written a statement of creed to which he calls people, and that some people have objected to it. Let a session therefore be convened for him concerning this matter. Review what transpires, investigate the matter yourself thoroughly, and inform us of it.”

May Allah’s peace, mercy, and blessings be upon you. The finest greetings of peace also to the eminent shaykh and imam, the learned and virtuous ‘Izz al-Dīn, a delight to the eyes; and likewise to every member of the family, every friend, and every acquaintance. Peace.

---

## Page 1103

Imam Abū al-Abbās Ahmad ibn Taymiyyah said in his “Reply” to a note sent to him in prison in Ramadan, 706 AH:

All praise is due to Allah. We praise Him, seek His help and forgiveness, and seek refuge in Allah from the evil within ourselves and from our evil deeds. Whoever Allah guides, none can lead astray; and whoever He leads astray, none can guide. I bear witness that none has the right to be worshipped except Allah alone, without partner. And I bear witness that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger:

He sent him with guidance and the religion of truth, to make it prevail over all religion; and sufficient is Allah as a witness.

May Allah send blessings upon him and his family, and grant him abundant peace.

To proceed: The “note” containing the message of the two eminent, learned, devout shaykhs, both exemplary figures, has arrived. May Allah strengthen them and all the brethren with a spirit from Him, inscribe faith in their hearts, grant them an honourable entrance and an honourable departure, and make them among those through whom authority is upheld: the authority of knowledge, argument, exposition, and proof; and the authority of power and victory through spears and supporters.

May He make them among His God-fearing allies and His troops who prevail over those of their peers who oppose them, and among the leaders of the God-fearing, who combined patience

---

## Page 1104

and certainty; Allah will bring this about and fulfil His promise, in private and in public, and exact vengeance upon Satan’s party on behalf of the servants of the Most Merciful. Yet this will be in accordance with His wisdom and His established way, through trials and tests by which Allah separates the people of sincerity and faith from the people of hypocrisy and slander. His Book indicates that trials are inevitable for everyone who calls to faith, as is punishment for those guilty of evil deeds and transgression. Allah Most High says:

“Alif, Lām, Mīm.”

“Do people think they will be left to say, ‘We believe,’ and will not be tested?”

“We certainly tested those before them, and Allah will surely make evident those who are truthful, and He will surely make evident the liars.”

“Or do those who do evil deeds think they can outrun Us? Evil is what they judge.”

Thus, He—glory be to Him—rejects the supposition that evildoers can elude the One who pursues them, or that those who profess faith will be left without a trial that distinguishes the truthful from the liar. He has declared in His Book that sincerity in faith requires striving in His cause. Allah Most High says:

“The Bedouins say, ‘We believe.’ Say, ‘You have not believed; rather, say, “We have submitted.”’”

Up to His words:

“The believers are only those who believe in Allah and His Messenger, then do not doubt, and strive with their wealth and their lives in the cause of Allah. It is they who are truthful.”

He has also declared in His Book the loss suffered by one who turns back on his face when tested, who worships Allah in such trials on an edge—that is, on a side or brink where one cannot stand securely. Rather, his faith remains firm only when he enjoys the worldly good he desires. Allah Most High says:

“And among the people is he who worships Allah

---

## Page 1105

on an edge”—to the end of the verse. Allah Most High also says:

“Do you think you will enter Paradise before Allah has made evident those of you who strive and those who are patient?”

Allah Most High says:

“We will surely test you until We make evident those of you who strive and those who are patient, and test your affairs.”

He—glory be to Him—has declared that when apostates appear, there must also be those who love Him and are loved by Him, and who strive in His cause. He says:

“Whoever among you turns away from his religion—Allah will bring forth a people whom He loves and who love Him”—to the end of the verse.

These are the ones who are grateful for the blessing of faith and patient under trial, as Allah Most High says:

“Muhammad is no more than a messenger; messengers have passed away before him. If he dies or is killed, will you turn back on your heels? Whoever turns back on his heels will not harm Allah at all. And Allah will reward the grateful.”

“No soul can die except by Allah’s permission, at a term decreed. Whoever desires the reward of this world, We will give him some of it; and whoever desires the reward of the Hereafter, We will give him some of it. And We will reward the grateful.”

“How many a prophet fought with many followers alongside him! They did not lose heart because of what befell them in the cause of Allah, nor did they weaken or yield. And Allah loves those who are patient.”

“Their only words were, ‘Our Lord, forgive us our sins and our excesses in our conduct, make our feet firm, and grant us victory over the disbelieving people.’”

“So Allah gave them the reward of this world and the excellent reward of the Hereafter. And Allah loves those who do good.”

When Allah blesses a person with patience and gratitude, everything Allah decrees for him is good for him, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah decrees nothing for the believer except that it is good for him: if prosperity comes to him and he is grateful, it is good for him; and if adversity comes to him

---

## Page 1106

and he is patient, it is good for him.”

The patient and grateful person is the believer whom Allah has described in several places in His Book. Whoever has not been blessed by Allah with patience and gratitude is in a wretched state: both prosperity and adversity lead him to an evil outcome. How much more so when these concern momentous matters of the kind in which prophets and the utterly truthful are tried—matters involving the establishment of the foundations of religion and the safeguarding of faith and the Quran against the schemes of those given to hypocrisy, heresy, and slander. All praise, then, belongs to Allah: abundant, pure, and blessed praise, as our Lord loves and is pleased with, and as befits His noble Face and majestic glory.

It is Allah whom we ask to keep you and all other believers steadfast through the firm word in this worldly life and in the Hereafter; to perfect His inward and outward blessings upon you; and to grant His religion, His Book, and His believing servants victory over the disbelievers and hypocrites—those against whom we have been commanded in His clear Book to strive and to be stern.

As for you, rejoice in the good tidings of blessings and joys beyond anything you have imagined. The significance of this “case” and what it entails is greater than those who consider only the particulars of matters suppose.

For this reason, among the things I said when addressing Amīn al-Rasūl, ‘Alā’ al-Dīn al-Taybarsī, were these words:

The right at stake in this “case” is not mine; rather, it belongs to Allah, His Messenger, and the believers from the east of the earth to its west. I cannot alter the religion, lower the banner of the Muslims, or renounce the religion of Islam for the sake of so-and-so.

---

## Page 1107

Yes, I can refrain from defending myself or retaliating against those who have wronged me and slandered me. I can forgo what is due to me and refrain from seeking to harm anyone by asserting my rights. All this I willingly offer, praise be to Allah, and I am entirely content to do so.

I had said to him: “The harm in this ‘case’ falls not upon me, but upon you. Those who stirred it up are enemies of Islam: they hate it, hate its devoted adherents and those who strive in its defence, and prefer that its enemies—the Tatars and others like them—should prevail. They have devised a scheme against you by which they seek to corrupt your religion and your state. Some of them have gone to the lands of the Tatars, while others remain in Syria and elsewhere.

“There are secrets behind this case that I cannot disclose, nor can I name those involved, until you consult the sultan’s deputy. If he gives permission, I will tell you; otherwise, he is not to be told. Investigate what I am telling you for yourselves.”

He was astonished at this and said: “My master, will you not name anyone for me yourself?”

I replied: “I will not do so, for that would not be proper. But you should understand, in general terms, that they intended to corrupt your religious and worldly affairs. They have put me forward as a cover, knowing that I am loyal to you and work for the good of your religious and worldly affairs. In time, Allah willing, the matter will become clear.”

I said to him: “Besides, what have I to fear? If I were killed, I would be among the finest martyrs, and mercy and divine pleasure would be upon me until the Day of Resurrection. Whoever killed me would incur an enduring curse in this world and punishment in the Hereafter. Let everyone who believes in Allah and His Messenger know that if I am killed

---

## Page 1108

it will be for the sake of Allah’s religion. If I am imprisoned, imprisonment is, for me, among the greatest blessings Allah has bestowed upon me. By Allah, I cannot adequately thank Him for His blessing upon me in this imprisonment. I have nothing over which to fear people—neither land grants, nor a school, nor wealth, nor authority or prestige. Rather, it is you who have reason to fear, should you lose the authority and wealth you now enjoy, and should your religion, through which you attain happiness in this world and the Hereafter, become corrupted. This was the aim of the enemy who stirred up this turmoil.”

I said:

“These emirs, judges and shaykhs in Egypt are my brothers and companions. I have never wronged any of them; I have always treated them well. What grievance, then, is there between me and them? But the hypocrites, the enemies of Islam, have misled them.”

I say to you—though I did not happen to say this to him—that among the believers are those who listen to the hypocrites and obey them, even though they are not themselves hypocrites. As Allah Most High said:

“And among you are those who listen to them.”

Allah also said to His Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Do not obey the disbelievers and the hypocrites, and disregard their harm.”

Hypocrisy has branches and pillars, just as faith has branches and pillars. In the two authentic collections, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is reported to have said:

“The signs of a hypocrite are three: when he speaks, he lies; when he promises, he breaks his promise; and when he is entrusted with something, he betrays that trust.”

It is also reported in both collections that he said:

“Whoever possesses four traits is a complete hypocrite, and whoever possesses one of them has a trait of hypocrisy until he abandons it: when he speaks, he lies; when he makes a covenant, he betrays it; when he disputes, he acts wickedly; and when he is entrusted with something, he betrays that trust.”

---

## Page 1109

And I said to him:

“This affair is more serious than you imagine, for a group of these enemies have gone to the lands of the Tatars.”

He said, “To the lands of the Tatars?”

I replied, “Yes. They are among those most eager to stir up trouble against you, besides other matters that I ought not mention to you.”

He had also said to me, “So you oppose the four schools of law,” and mentioned the ruling of the four judges. I replied:

“On the contrary, what I have said accords with the position of the four imams of those schools. In Syria, I produced more than fifty books by Hanafī, Mālikī and Shāfiʿī scholars, scholars of hadith, speculative theologians and Sufis. All agree with what I said, in its very wording, and include explicit statements from the early generations of this ummah and its imams. Despite their prolonged searches through the books and libraries of the city, my opponents could not produce anything from any of Islam’s imams or early scholars that contradicted this.”

When he gave me the document, I examined it and said to him:

“All of this is false except for one statement: that He truly rose over the Throne, but without specifying how or likening Him to creation.”

I said, “This appears in ‘The Creed’ in these very words: without specifying how, asserting any likeness, distorting the meaning, or negating the attributes.”

He said, “Then write this in your own hand.”

I replied, “This was already written in ‘The Creed,’ and I have said nothing that contradicts it. What benefit, then, is there in writing it out again?”

I also said:

“More than one scholar—among the Mālikīs, Shāfiʿīs, scholars of hadith and others—has reported the consensus of Ahl al-Sunnah wa-l-Jamāʿah upon this wording. Among the scholars of Islam, none rejects this except these opponents.”

---

## Page 1110

I said:

“These people say that there is no Lord above the Throne to be called upon, nor any God above heaven to be worshipped; there is nothing there but sheer nonexistence and absolute negation. They say that the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was not taken up to Allah Most High, but merely ascended to heaven and came back down, and that a person making supplication does not raise his hands to Allah.

“Some of them say, ‘Allah is existence itself; I am Allah; you are Allah—as are the dog, the pig, and excrement.’ They also say, ‘Allah dwells in these things.’”

He found this outrageous and was horrified that anyone would say such a thing. He asked, “These people?”—meaning Ibn Makhlūf and his associates.

I replied:

“I have not heard their statements, nor have they said anything to me, so it is not lawful for me to attribute to them anything I do not know. But this is the position of those who disputed with me in Syria and debated me. They explicitly stated this to me, and one of them explicitly declared that he would not accept anything the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said on this subject that contradicted their views.”

As the conversation continued, the man began listening attentively to what I said and taking it in, having seen my anger. Thus, I learned from several sources that he left happy and pleased with what he had heard from me. He said:

“This man is in the right, while these people have lost Allah. Otherwise, where is Allah?”

This is what everyone whose innate disposition is sound says. Jamāl al-Dīn al-Akhram likewise said this to al-Malik al-Kāmil when the latter spoke to him about these people. Al-Akhram told him:

“These people have lost your God, so find yourself a god to worship.”

It is known, by the unanimous agreement of Muslims, that Allah is truly Living, truly All-Knowing, truly All-Powerful, truly All-Hearing, and truly All-Seeing, and likewise with His other names and attributes. This is denied only by

---

## Page 1111

the esotericist philosophers. They say:

“We apply these names to Him, but do not say that they apply in a real sense.”

Their purpose is to make it permissible to deny these attributes, for they say:

“He is neither truly living nor truly dead, neither knowing nor ignorant, neither powerful nor powerless, neither hearing nor deaf.”

Once they say that these names are figurative, they can deny what they signify, because a defining feature of figurative usage is that its literal meaning can validly be denied. Thus, anyone who denies that an expression is literal must allow its negation. Whoever denies that Allah has truly risen over the Throne consequently says, “The Most Merciful has not risen over the Throne.” Likewise, anyone who says that calling a courageous man a lion or a dull-witted man a donkey is not literal must acknowledge that these descriptions can validly be denied. He therefore says, “This is neither a lion nor a donkey, but a human being.”

These people tell them, “Allah does not rise over the Throne,” just as their brethren say, “He is neither All-Hearing nor All-Seeing, nor does He speak,” because they regard these expressions as figurative. Thus, they confront the very substance of what the messengers conveyed about Allah, glorified is He, with denial and rejection, just as the polytheists confront it with charges of falsehood. These people, however, do not reject the wording outright.

Al-Talamankī, one of the leading Mālikī scholars—who preceded Ibn ʿAbd al-Barr, al-Bājī, and their contemporaries—said in Kitāb al-Wusūl ilā Maʿrifat al-Usūl (The Book of Attaining Knowledge of the Fundamentals):

“The Muslims of Ahl al-Sunnah unanimously agree that the meaning of ‘And He is with you wherever you are’ and similar passages in the Quran is that this refers to His knowledge, and that Allah Himself is above the heavens, established over the Throne in whatever manner He wills.”

He also said:

“Ahl al-Sunnah said, concerning the words of Allah Most High: ‘The Most Merciful, over the Throne

---

## Page 1112

has risen’: that Allah’s rising over His glorious Throne is real, not figurative.”

Ibn ‘Abd al-Barr said in al-Tamhīd, his commentary on al-Muwatta’—the finest book composed in its field—when discussing the hadith of the descent:

“This is an established hadith whose authenticity the hadith scholars do not dispute. It provides evidence that Allah is in heaven, upon the Throne, above the seven heavens, as the community has affirmed. It is among their proofs against the Mu‘tazilah, who say that He is everywhere and is not upon the Throne.”

He said:

“The evidence supporting what those who adhere to the truth have said is Allah Most High’s statement:

‘The Most Merciful has risen over the Throne.’

And He said:

‘To Him good words ascend, and righteous deeds raise them.’

And He said:

‘The angels and the Spirit ascend to Him.’

And He said:

‘O Jesus, I will take you and raise you to Myself.’”

He mentioned other verses, eventually saying:

“This is so well known among ordinary people and scholars that it needs no more than to be stated, for it is an unavoidable awareness which no one taught them, and no Muslim has disagreed with them about it.”

This resembles what Muhammad ibn Tāhir reported about Abū Ja‘far al-Hamdānī: he attended a gathering held by one of the speculative theologians, who said:

“Allah existed when there was no Throne.”

Abū Ja‘far said:

“Teacher, let us set aside discussion of the Throne. Tell us about these inescapable impulses we find in our hearts. No one who knows Allah has ever said, ‘O Allah,’ without finding in his heart an irresistible impulse seeking what is above, turning neither right nor left.”

The theologian struck his head with his hand and said:

“Al-Hamdānī has confounded me! Al-Hamdānī has confounded me!”

The shaykh meant that the acknowledgment

---

## Page 1113

by people’s innate dispositions that the One they worship and invoke is above is self-evident to reason and innate disposition, rather than something learned merely from transmitted revelation. This differs from His rising over the Throne—after creating the heavens and the earth in six days—which is known through transmitted revelation. Thus, the days of the week are known only through those who acknowledge prophethood. Those unfamiliar with this, such as the polytheist Turks, have no names for the days of the week in their language. This is part of the wisdom behind the followers of each religion gathering on one day of the week, as the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Today is ours, tomorrow is for the Jews, and the day after tomorrow is for the Christians.”

Ibn ʿAbd al-Barr discussed this at length, eventually saying:

“As for their appeal to the statement of Allah Most High:

‘There is no private conversation between three but He is their fourth, nor between five but He is their sixth,’

it offers them no proof. For the scholars among the Companions and their successors explained this verse by saying: ‘He is upon the Throne, and His knowledge is everywhere.’ No one whose statement carries authority disagreed with them on this.”

Abū ʿUmar said:

“Ahl al-Sunnah unanimously affirm all the attributes mentioned in the Quran and Sunnah, believe in them, and understand them in their real, not figurative, sense. Yet they do not specify how any of them are, nor define them through a circumscribed description.

“As for the people of innovation—the Jahmiyyah, the Muʿtazilah, and the Khawārij—they all deny these attributes and understand none of them in their real sense. They claim that whoever affirms them likens Allah to His creation, whereas those who affirm them regard these deniers as negating the One who is worshipped. The truth is what the Book of Allah and the Sunnah of His Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, declare; those who affirm these attributes are the leading scholars of the community.”

---

## Page 1114

He also said:

“The position of Ahl al-Sunnah and the leading scholars of jurisprudence and transmitted reports concerning this issue and similar matters is to believe in what has come from the Prophet, affirm its truth, and refrain from defining limits or specifying how any of it is.”

Al-Sijzī said in al-Ibānah:

“Our leading scholars—such as al-Thawrī, Mālik, Ibn ʿUyaynah, Hammād ibn Salamah, Hammād ibn Zayd, Ibn al-Mubārak, al-Fudayl, Ahmad, and Ishāq—agree that Allah, glorified be He, is above the Throne in His essence; that His knowledge is everywhere; that on the Day of Resurrection He will be seen with the eyes while He is above the Throne; that He descends to the lowest heaven; and that He becomes angry, is pleased, and speaks whatever He wills. Whoever opposes any of this is dissociated from them, and they are dissociated from him.”

Shaykh ʿAbd al-Qādir said in al-Ghunyah:

“As for knowing the Maker through signs and proofs, this consists, in brief, in knowing and being certain that Allah is One, Unique, the Eternal Refuge.”

He went on to say:

“He is in the upper direction, risen over the Throne, encompassing the dominion; His knowledge encompasses all things.”

He said:

“It is not permissible to describe Him as being everywhere. Rather, it should be said that He is in heaven, above the Throne.”

He went on to say:

“The attribute of rising over the Throne should be affirmed without reinterpretation, and this is the rising of His essence over the Throne.”

He said:

“His being above the Throne is stated in every Book revealed to every prophet sent, without specifying how.”

Shaykh Nasr al-Maqdisī related in Kitāb al-Hujjah that Ibn Abī Hātim said:

“I asked my father and Abū Zurʿah about the doctrines of Ahl al-Sunnah. They replied: ‘We encountered scholars in all

---

## Page 1115

regions: the Hijaz, Iraq, Egypt, Syria, and Yemen. Among their beliefs were that faith consists of speech and action, increases and decreases, and that the Quran is Allah’s speech, revealed and uncreated in every respect.’”

He went on to say:

“Allah is above His Throne, distinct from His creation, as He has described Himself in His Book and through His Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, without specifying how. His knowledge encompasses all things.”

Shaykh Nasr said elsewhere in the book:

“If someone says, ‘You have mentioned what Muslims must do: follow Allah’s Book, the Sunnah of Allah’s Messenger, and the consensus of the leading scholars. Now state their position and what they agreed upon,’ the answer is the position we found the scholars adhering to, together with the views of others that have reached me.”

He then outlined “the creed of Ahl al-Sunnah,” which includes:

“Allah is risen over His Throne, distinct from His creation, as He has stated in His Book.”

Abū al-Hasan al-Karajī al-Shāfi‘ī said in his “celebrated poem on the Sunnah”:

Their creed is that God, in His essence, … is above His Throne, while knowing all things unseen.

Al-Qurtubī, the author of the extensive Quran commentary, said concerning the words of the Most High, “Then He rose over the Throne—the Most Merciful”:

“This is the question of rising over the Throne, concerning which scholars have much to say.”

He mentioned the position of the speculative theologians, then said:

“The earliest generations did not hold that direction should be denied of Allah, nor did they express such a denial. Rather, they and everyone else explicitly affirmed it of Allah, as His Book declares and as reported by

---

## Page 1116

His messengers.”

He said:

“None of the righteous early generations denied that He truly rose over His Throne. They were ignorant only of the manner of His rising, for its true nature is not known.”

Then, after recounting fourteen positions, he said:

“The clearest position is the one jointly supported by the Quranic verses, the transmitted reports, and the virtuous and righteous: Allah is above His Throne, as He has stated in His Book and through His Prophet, without specifying how, distinct from all His creation. This is the position of the righteous early generations, as trustworthy narrators have transmitted from them.”

When we met in Damascus, the books brought in included works by Abū al-Hasan al-Ash‘arī, such as al-Maqālāt (Doctrinal Positions) and al-Ibāna (The Clarification), and works by the leading scholars among his followers, such as al-Qādī Abū Bakr, Ibn Fūrak, al-Bayhaqī, and others. Al-Ibāna was brought in, together with what Ibn ‘Asākir cited in his book Tabyīn Kadhib al-Muftarī fīmā Nusiba ilā al-Ash‘arī (Exposing the Slanderer’s Lies Concerning What Has Been Attributed to al-Ash‘arī). Abū Zakariyyā al-Nawawī had transcribed this in his own hand.

He said in it:

“If someone says, ‘You have rejected the positions of the Mu‘tazilah, the Qadariyyah, the Jahmiyyah, the Harūriyyah, the Rāfidah, and the Murji’ah. Tell us, then, the position you yourselves hold,’ he is told:

‘Our position is to adhere firmly to Allah’s Book, the Sunnah of His Messenger, and what has been transmitted from the Companions, the Successors, and the leading scholars of hadith. To these we hold fast. We profess what Ahmad ibn Hanbal professed—may Allah make his face radiant, raise his rank, and grant him an abundant reward—and shun whatever contradicts his position. For he is the eminent imam through whom Allah made the truth manifest when misguidance appeared, clarified the path, and quelled the innovations of the innovators, the deviations of those who strayed, and the doubts of those who doubted.’”

---

## Page 1117

He also set out the creed he had attributed to Ahl al-Sunnah in al-Maqālāt (Doctrinal Positions). He then presented arguments concerning the fundamental doctrines, including the questions of the Quran, the vision of Allah, and the divine attributes. He then said:

“Chapter on Rising over the Throne

If someone asks, ‘What do you say concerning rising over the Throne?’ the answer is that Allah has risen over His Throne, as He, glorified be He, said:

‘The Most Merciful rose over the Throne.’

And He said:

‘To Him ascends good speech, and righteous deeds raise it.’

And He, glorified be He, said:

‘Rather, Allah raised him to Himself.’

And Pharaoh said:

‘O Hāmān, build me a lofty tower so that I may reach the ways—the ways of the heavens—and look upon the God of Moses, though I certainly think him a liar.’

He accused Moses of lying when Moses said that Allah is above the heavens. And He said:

‘Do you feel secure that He who is in heaven will not cause the earth to swallow you?’

The Throne is above the heavens, and what He meant was the Throne that is above the heavens. Do you not see that Allah mentioned the heavens and said:

‘And made the moon a light therein’?

He did not mean that the moon fills all of them or that it is in all of them. We also see all Muslims raising their hands toward the Throne when they supplicate.”

He said:

“Some among the Mu‘tazilah, the Jahmiyyah, and the Harūriyyah have said that His words, ‘The Most Merciful rose over the Throne,’ mean that He took possession, ruled, and subdued; that Allah is everywhere; and they denied that Allah is over His Throne, as the people of truth affirm.”

He said:

“If it were as they claim, there would be no difference between the Throne and the seventh, lowest earth, for Allah has power over all things and has decreed it.”

---

## Page 1118

He continued his discussion until he said:

“Further evidence for you that Allah has risen over His Throne, and not over anything else, is what the transmitters have related from the Messenger of Allah, who said: ‘Allah descends to the lowest heaven every night and says: Is there anyone asking, that I may give to him? Is there anyone seeking forgiveness, that I may forgive him? This continues until dawn breaks.’”

He then mentioned the hadiths. Allah, Most High, said:

“O Jesus, I will take you and raise you to Myself and purify you from those who disbelieve.”

He said: “The Muslim community has unanimously agreed that Allah raised Jesus to heaven.” He cited further evidence, until he said:

“All of this indicates that Allah is not within His creation, nor is His creation within Him, and that He, Mighty and Majestic, has risen over His Throne. Majestic and Mighty is He, far exalted above what the wrongdoers say. Exalted is He above what is said by those who, in describing Him, have affirmed no reality for Him, nor established His oneness in speaking of Him. Their words ultimately amount to denial, and all their descriptions, when interpreted, amount to negation. By this they claim to intend to declare Him free of imperfections and deny any resemblance to creation. We therefore seek refuge in Allah from declaring Him free of imperfections in a way that entails negation and denial.”

This is a vast subject: the statements of scholars from all groups, the rational and transmitted evidence concerning it, the opposing arguments of those who deny, and the responses to those arguments cannot all be encompassed here. I have written several volumes on this subject, presenting the positions of all the groups and their scriptural and rational arguments. I have comprehensively addressed what al-Rāzī mentioned in his books Ta’sīs al-Taqdīs (Establishing Divine Sanctity), Nihāyat al-‘Uqūl (The Utmost Reach of Intellects), and other works, even covering the doctrines of

---

## Page 1119

the Peripatetic philosophers—the followers of Aristotle—and the non-Peripatetics, both earlier and later figures, including the foremost of their later thinkers, “Ibn Sīnā,” and their unrivalled figure in his own time, “Abū al-Barakāt.” I have also presented their arguments. I know that this subject has been beset by considerable confusion, and that groups of distinguished, intelligent scholars have been perplexed by what they perceived as conflicting evidence. I have established the sound textual proofs and distinguished them from fallacious objections, while also addressing important foundations and major principles that arise in the course of this discussion.

Among the first of these—and something many people regard as a matter of the utmost importance—is establishing the spherical form of the celestial spheres. I have demonstrated this and cited the statements of those who reported Muslim consensus on it, such as Ibn al-Munādī, Ibn Hazm, and Ibn al-Jawzī. I have also discussed the related mathematical matters and scriptural evidence from the Quran and Sunnah, along with other matters that would take too long to describe.

Furthermore, when I was in the tower, I was told that someone had written a criticism of the fatwa “al-Hamawiyya,” and it was sent to me. I have written on this matter enough to fill several volumes. There is no power or strength except through Allah.

People know that there was estrangement and antagonism between the Hanbalīs and the Ash‘arīs. I was among those who worked hardest to bring Muslims’ hearts together, to unite their voices, and to follow the command we have been given to hold fast to the rope of Allah. I removed most of the estrangement they felt and explained to them that al-Ash‘arī was among the most eminent speculative theologians affiliated

---

## Page 1120

with Imam Ahmad—may Allah have mercy on him—and others like him, upholding his approach, as al-Ash‘arī states in his books.

As Abū Ishāq al-Shīrāzī said:
“The Ash‘arīs gained acceptance among people only through their affiliation with the Hanbalīs.”

The early Hanbalī imams, such as Abū Bakr ‘Abd al-‘Azīz, Abū al-Hasan al-Tamīmī, and others like them, cited his statements in their books. Indeed, his standing among their earlier scholars was like that of Ibn ‘Aqīl among their later scholars. Ibn ‘Aqīl, however, had particular expertise in jurisprudence and its principles, whereas al-Ash‘arī was closer to Ahmad’s principles and followed them more faithfully. For the closer a person is in time to the early generations, the better acquainted he is with rational and transmitted knowledge.

I would explain this to the Hanbalīs, making clear that although al-Ash‘arī had been a pupil of the Mu‘tazilah and then repented, he had studied under al-Jubbā’ī, inclined toward the approach of Ibn Kullāb, and learned the foundations of hadith from Zakariyyā al-Sājī in Basra. Then, when he came to Baghdad, he learned other matters from the Hanbalīs there. This was his final position, as he and his followers mentioned in their books. Likewise, Ibn ‘Aqīl had been a pupil of the two Mu‘tazilīs, Ibn al-Walīd and Ibn al-Tabbān, but then repented of that. His repentance in the presence of al-Sharīf Abū Ja‘far is well known.

Just as some of Ahmad’s followers hate and disparage Ibn ‘Aqīl, those who disparage al-Ash‘arī are not confined to Ahmad’s followers; rather, there are such people in every group. When I presented al-Ash‘arī’s statements and the Hanbalīs saw them, they said:
“This is better than

---

## Page 1121

the words of Shaykh al-Muwaffaq.” The Muslims rejoiced at this unity. I also presented what Ibn ‘Asākir mentioned in his account of al-Ash‘arī’s merits: that the Hanbalīs and Ash‘arīs had remained united until the time of al-Qushayrī, when the discord that arose in Baghdad divided them. It is well known that every group includes both those who deviate and those who remain upright.

Yet throughout my life, up to this very moment, I have never called anyone to a Hanbalī or non-Hanbalī school in the fundamentals of religion. Nor have I championed any such school or mentioned it in my discourse. I mention only what the early generations of this community and its imams agreed upon.

I have told them more than once:
“I give anyone who disagrees with me three years. If he produces a single word from any imam of the first three generations that contradicts what I have said, I will acknowledge it. As for what I state, I transmit it from the imams of those three generations, in their own words and in the words of those from across the various groups who have reported their consensus.”

Moreover, I have always been among those who most strongly forbid declaring a particular person an unbeliever or an evildoer, or accusing him of disobedience—and those who have sat with me know this about me—unless it is known that the proof conveyed by the Messenger has been established against him: a proof whose violation constitutes unbelief in some cases, wickedness in others, and disobedience in others. I also affirm that Allah has forgiven this community its errors. This includes errors in matters of belief and doctrinal statements as well as practical matters.

The early generations continually disagreed over many such questions, yet none of them accused another of unbelief, wickedness, or disobedience. For example, Shurayh rejected the reading of those who recited:

“But you marvel, while they mock.”

He said, “Allah does not marvel.” This reached Ibrāhīm al-Nakha‘ī

---

## Page 1122

who said, “Shurayh is merely a poet who is impressed by his own knowledge. ‘Abdullāh was more knowledgeable than he was, and he used to recite: ‘But I marvel.’”

Likewise, ‘Ā’ishah and other Companions disagreed over whether Muhammad saw his Lord. She said, “Whoever claims that Muhammad saw his Lord has uttered a monstrous falsehood against Allah.” Nevertheless, we do not say that Ibn ‘Abbās and others who disagreed with her fabricated falsehoods against Allah. She also disputed whether the dead hear the speech of the living, whether the deceased is punished because of his family’s weeping, and other matters.

Discord among the early generations even culminated in fighting. Yet Ahl al-Sunnah agree that both parties were believers and that their fighting did not negate their established uprightness. For even if a combatant was a rebel, he was acting on an interpretation, and that interpretation precludes a judgment of wickedness.

I would explain to them that the statements reported to them from the early generations and the imams, declaring in general terms that whoever says such and such is an unbeliever, are also true. However, one must distinguish between a general declaration and its application to a particular individual. This is the first of the major questions of religious fundamentals over which the community disagreed: the question of “threats of punishment.” The Quranic texts concerning threats of punishment are unqualified, as in His words:

“Indeed, those who consume the property of orphans unjustly”—and the rest of the verse.

The same applies to all other statements of the form: “Whoever does such and such will receive such and such.” These are unqualified, general statements. They are comparable to the statements of some of the early generations: “Whoever says such and such is such and such.”

In the case of a particular individual, the threatened punishment is then averted by repentance, good deeds that erase sins, afflictions that expiate sins, or accepted intercession.

---

## Page 1123

And declaring someone an unbeliever is among these threats of punishment. Even if a statement constitutes a denial of what the Messenger said, the person may have only recently embraced Islam or grown up in a remote desert region. Such a person does not become an unbeliever through what he denies until the proof has been established against him. He may not have heard those texts, or he may have heard them without their authenticity being established to his satisfaction. Alternatively, something else may have conflicted with them in his understanding, compelling him to interpret them differently—even if he was mistaken.

I would regularly cite the hadith in the two Sahīh collections concerning the man who said:

“‘When I die, burn me, then grind me to powder, then scatter me in the sea. By Allah, if Allah has power over me, He will punish me as He has never punished anyone in all creation.’ They did that to him, and Allah said to him, ‘What prompted you to do what you did?’ He replied, ‘Fear of You.’ So He forgave him.”

This man doubted Allah’s power and His bringing him back to life after he had been scattered. Indeed, he believed that he would not be brought back to life. This is unbelief by the agreement of the Muslims. Yet he was ignorant and did not know this, and he was a believer who feared that Allah would punish him, so Allah forgave him on that account. A person qualified to exercise independent judgment who adopts an interpretation and is keen to follow the Messenger is even more deserving of forgiveness than such a man.

---

## Page 1124

Section

As for what you mentioned about gentle speech and addressing others in the best manner, you know that I am among those who practise this most often. Yet everything is good in its proper place. Where Allah and His Messenger have commanded sternness towards a speaker because of his transgression and aggression against the Book and the Sunnah, we are commanded to confront him accordingly; we have not been commanded to address him in the best manner.

It is well known that Allah Most High says:

“Do not lose heart or grieve; you will have the upper hand if you are believers.”

Thus, whoever is a believer has the upper hand, according to the explicit wording of the Quran. He also says:

“Honour belongs to Allah, His Messenger, and the believers.”

And He says:

“Indeed, those who oppose Allah and His Messenger are among the most humiliated.”

“Allah has decreed: ‘I and My messengers will surely prevail.’”

Allah will fulfil His promise to whoever fits this description, whoever he may be.

It must also be understood that seeking the approval of created beings is permissible neither according to reason nor according to religion, for two reasons.

The first is that this is impossible. As al-Shāfiʿī, may Allah be pleased with him, said: “People’s approval is a goal that cannot be attained. So hold fast to what sets you right, leave everything else aside, and do not trouble yourself with it.”

The second is that we are commanded to seek the approval of Allah and His Messenger. As the Most High says:

---

## Page 1125

“Allah and His Messenger have more right to be pleased.”

We must fear Allah and fear no one but Him, as the Most High says:

“Do not fear them, but fear Me, if you are believers.”

He also says:

“Do not fear people, but fear Me.”

And He says:

“Fear Me alone.”

“And be mindful of Me alone.”

We must therefore fear Allah and remain mindful of Him in our dealings with people. We must not wrong them, either in our hearts or through our actions, and we must fulfil their rights both in our hearts and through our actions. We must not let fear of them cause us to abandon what Allah and His Messenger have commanded.

Whoever adheres to this path will ultimately prevail, as ʿĀʾishah wrote to Muʿāwiyah:

“To proceed: Whoever seeks people’s approval at the cost of Allah’s displeasure, Allah will be displeased with him and will cause people to be displeased with him; those who once praised him will come to condemn him. Whoever seeks Allah’s approval at the cost of people’s displeasure, Allah will be pleased with him and will cause people to be pleased with him.”

The believer’s thoughts and intentions are therefore directed only towards pleasing his Lord and avoiding His displeasure, and the final outcome will be in his favour. There is no power nor strength except through Allah.

Moreover, the envoy was inwardly delighted with these matters. Whatever he outwardly displays is merely for show before his companion; in reality, the two are inwardly at odds. There are also matters known to their inner circle. Al-Taybarsī alone suffices: numerous reports establish his joy and delight at what happened, even though he was the adversary who had been treated sternly. Whether this was so or not, the principle that must be followed is the first one. As for the Prophet’s words:

“Do not initiate fighting against them; if they come close to you, shoot arrows at them.”

We accept them wholeheartedly. We did not shoot until they sought to harm us and had come close to us. That is why Allah brought benefit through it.

---

## Page 1126

Section

As for your statement that I seek to have judgment entrusted to a particular individual:

“This is not appropriate. Rather, it would harm both that individual and me, and cause widespread damage. You know, concerning Judge” Badr al-Dīn, “that I have been among those most loyal to him, supporting and assisting him and defending him against his enemies in numerous matters. Indeed, I know of no one more sincere in his friendship with him or more helpful to him. This has been for Allah alone, not out of any hope of gain or fear on my part.

“A substantial part of the harm I have suffered—in Damascus and in Egypt as well—has been solely because I defended him and his deputies, such as al-Zur‘ī, al-Tabrīzī and others in his circle, and spoke highly of his merits in Egypt as well. I have become known for this, and the party of perdition and others oppose me on that account.

“Allah knows that no one else comes close to the esteem in which I hold him or the place he has in my heart, let alone equals him. Allah forbid that Badr al-Dīn should be equated with someone from whom Allah has distinguished him in so many ways. In the Sunan of Abū Dāwūd, it is reported:

“‘Ā’ishah said: ‘The Messenger of Allah commanded us to accord people their proper standing.’”

---

## Page 1127

In my view, among the most unjust of people are those who place him on the same level as anyone else in Syria or Egypt. Badr al-Dīn has continually been wronged by such comparisons, and I regard supporting him, remaining loyal to him, and assisting him as among the greatest means by which I draw near to Allah. … (1)

You know about this, especially in these lands. He ought to receive greater assistance and support here than he did in Syria, for many of these people display an aversion to him, falsehood, and wickedness not found in others. I therefore favour and choose everything that elevates his standing in worldly and religious affairs. I do not wish to make him a target for his enemies’ arrows.

Rather, for whatever I have done for him and others, and whatever I continue to do for them, my reward rests with Allah, who says:

“So whoever does an atom’s weight of good will see it.”

“And whoever does an atom’s weight of evil will see it.”

Thus, when al-Taybarsī mentioned the judges collectively, I told him that only “Ibn Makhlūf” had become involved in this case, and that he was a lying, wicked man with little knowledge or religious commitment. He began smiling as I said this, as though he knew him and as though the man were notorious for his bad conduct.

I said: “What business does Ibn Makhlūf have involving himself in this? Has anyone brought a claim against me of a kind that falls within his jurisdiction? Or does what I have discussed belong to general scholarly matters, such as Quranic interpretation, the meanings of hadiths, and discussions of jurisprudence and the fundamentals of religion? In these matters, recourse is

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank space in the original.

---

## Page 1128

to those who possess knowledge of these matters and fear Allah in addressing them. If the sultan or judge has these qualifications, he may speak on such matters in that capacity. When a judge is removed from office, he does not lose his entitlement to undertake such activities as issuing fatwas and the like; speaking on these matters is not restricted to those holding office. If the sultan or judge possesses neither knowledge of these matters nor the requisite fear of Allah, he may not lawfully speak on them, let alone sit in judgment on them. Ibn Makhlūf possesses neither knowledge of these matters nor the requisite fear of Allah.”

I said: “As for Judge Badr al-Dīn—Allah forbid! His virtue and religious devotion would prevent him from participating in this ruling, which contravenes the consensus of the Muslims in twenty-odd respects.”

I said: “Whoever persists in maintaining that this ruling issued by Ibn Makhlūf is a ruling according to the law of Muhammad is an unbeliever once the proof has been established against him. Even Muslim children know, as a self-evident part of the religion of Islam, that neither Jews nor Christians would accept this ruling, let alone Muslims.”

I mentioned to him some of the grounds that demonstrate the invalidity of this ruling. They have been written down and are in the possession of “al-Sharaf Muhammad.” Likewise, I cleared Judge “Shams al-Dīn al-Sarūjī” of any involvement in such a ruling. I told him: “Your aim was not to issue a ruling according to Islamic law; rather, your aim was only to counter

---

## Page 1129

the suspicions concerning the king that you had heard. When the judges learned that the case involved the king, they held back and feared to speak—a fear for which Allah may or may not excuse them. But for this, they would have had things to say. Had this ruling been an aberration, or involved some interest of a man wielding the sword, extraordinary things would have happened.”

They said: “Our master, who would speak about the king’s affairs? We will not speak. Spare us any talk of the king.”

I replied: “You oblivious man! I will spare you talk of the king—but was this turmoil, with which you have filled the world, stirred up by anything other than that? We had already heard this in Damascus, but we did not believe that any rational person would credit it.”

Once these people have put the suspicions concerning the king out of their minds, whenever some of what my opponents say is mentioned to them, they regard it as extremely grave and believe that whoever says such things deserves the severest punishment. For Allah, glorified be He, says:

“He is the One Who sent His Messenger with guidance and the religion of truth to make it prevail over all religion. And sufficient is Allah as a Witness.”

He therefore knows that, were I to request this, tales about Badr al-Dīn and me would fly in every direction, and people would say that we had secret agreements between us. I deal with him in a manner for which I hope to receive Allah’s reward, and he acts according to the requirements of his religion.

Moreover, “Badr al-Dīn” cannot endure people’s talk and harassment—the sort inflicted by people like these—being a man who holds office and has enemies. As for me—and there is no power nor strength except through Allah—they have already done everything within their power. Nothing remains but the help of Allah, which He has promised His Messenger and those who believe, in the life of this world and on the Day when the witnesses stand forth.

---

## Page 1130

Moreover, he knows that this matter either falls within judicial jurisdiction or it does not. If it does, the defendant has no right to choose a particular judge to rule on it; rather, he must appear before someone who judges with knowledge and justice. If it does not concern the judge, then that is all the more untenable.

Furthermore, no claim has been brought against me concerning prescribed punishments or individual rights—matters within a judge’s jurisdiction—such as homicide, an accusation of unchastity, a financial claim, or the like. Rather, the dispute concerns general questions of knowledge, such as Quranic exegesis, hadith, jurisprudence, and other such fields. These include matters on which the Muslim community agrees and others over which it disagrees.

When the Muslim community disagrees over the meaning of a verse or a hadith, or over a declarative or prescriptive ruling, the correctness of one position and the invalidity of the other cannot be established merely by a judge’s ruling. His ruling is enforceable only in particular cases, not in general matters. Were this permissible, a judge could rule that Allah Most High’s words,

“They shall wait for three periods,”

refer to menstrual periods and intervals of purity, and make his interpretation a ruling binding on everyone. Or he could rule that the touching mentioned in Allah Most High’s words,

“Or you have touched women,”

means sexual intercourse and physical intimacy short of it; or that the one in whose hand is the marriage tie is the husband, or the father and the master. No one maintains this.

Likewise, when people disagree over His words,

“The Most Merciful rose over the Throne,”

one says: “It means that He Himself, in His very essence, rose above the Throne. The meaning of rising above is known, but its manner is unknown.”

Others say: “There is no Lord above the Throne, nor is there anything there at all. But

---

## Page 1131

the verse means that He has power over the Throne, or something of that kind.” A judge’s ruling that one of these two positions is correct and the other invalid would serve no useful purpose. If that were so, someone who supported the other position would rule that it was correct, since he says:
