The same applies to acts of worship: whether touching the penis invalidates ablution; whether it is recommended to offer the afternoon prayer early or delay it; whether supplication is always recited in the dawn prayer, not recited, or recited during calamities; and similar matters.

In matters disputed within the Muslim community, the ruler must take one of two courses. Either he must require everyone to follow what the Quran and Sunnah teach and what the early generations of the Muslim community agreed upon, in accordance with the words of Allah Most High:

“If you disagree over anything, refer it to Allah and the Messenger.”

When they dispute, what they say is understood if he is capable of understanding the truth. Once what the Quran and Sunnah teach becomes clear to him, he calls people to it. Or he is to allow people to remain as they are, just as he allows them to follow their respective schools in matters of practice.

If, however, the religious innovation is evident—such that ordinary people know it contravenes the Sharia—as with the innovations of the Khawārij, Rāfidah, Qadariyyah, and Jahmiyyah, then the ruler must condemn it, because its nature is common knowledge. Likewise, he must condemn those who deem indecencies, wine, abandoning prayer, and similar things lawful.

Nevertheless, the adherents of these deviant doctrines may become so numerous in certain places and times that

---

## Page 1132

their discourse, through its sheer volume, comes to be regarded—by the ignorant—as equal to that of the people of knowledge and the Sunnah. The matter may then become unclear to the ruler responsible for these people, who will need someone to present Allah’s proof and explain it, so that punishment follows the establishment of that proof. Punishment before the proof has been established is not lawful. Allah Most High says:

“And We never punish until We have sent a messenger.”

For this reason, the jurists have stated concerning rebels that the ruler should correspond with them: if they raise a doubt, he should clarify it; and if they complain of an injustice, he should remove it. Thus Alī sent Ibn Abbās to the Khawārij, and he debated with them until four thousand of them abandoned their position. Likewise, Umar ibn Abd al-Azīz summoned the advocates of the Qadariyyah and the Khawārij and debated with them until the truth became clear to them and they acknowledged it. After his death, however, Ghaylān al-Qadarī went back on his repentance and was crucified.

As for a ruler compelling adherence to a position in disputed matters without evidence from the Quran and Sunnah, this is impermissible by the agreement of the Muslims. A judge’s ruling that one position rather than another is correct in such matters carries no weight unless he has evidence that must be followed. In that case, his words carry the same weight before and after his appointment to office, just like the scholarly books he writes. Certainly, holding office may enable him to proclaim a truth and disseminate knowledge that he had previously been unable to spread. But the question of ability or inability is distinct from that of having or lacking the right to do something.

A judge may, however, establish what Zayd or Amr has said. If that statement relates specifically to the individual concerned, it falls within the matters over which judges have jurisdiction,

---

## Page 1133

but if it is a statement of general application, it belongs to the realm of people’s doctrinal positions.

As for establishing this statement before Zayd through evidence, an admission, or a written document, that is a matter for judges. There is no doubt that someone like Badr al-Dīn is among the most just of people and the most loving toward those who uphold truthfulness and justice, and among those who most detest false witnesses. Had he had power over them, he would have taken action. Thus, if someone like Badr al-Dīn were needed in this matter, he would be the judge who ought to handle it, rather than someone notorious for wickedness.

But these judicial records they possess are not worth the ink used to write them. They know that they are false and invalid. I have no objection to contesting them before him, so that the truth, rather than falsehood, may be established before him. If he agrees to this, that would be most welcome. Yet I fear that he might suffer harm on my account through the discrediting of certain people. He should therefore seek Allah’s guidance concerning what to do; may Allah choose what is best for him in all matters.

Rather, I and others prefer to contest this before one of his deputies, such as Judge Jamāl al-Dīn al-Zarʿī, for he is one of the upright judges. Otherwise, Badr al-Dīn is too eminent to be burdened with this, were I to need it.

But now that the matter has become clear to both the elite and the general public, there is no need for this. As I told al-Taybarsī: “The letter from the sultan, written in the sultan’s name, together with all that was reported concerning it of falsehood and violations of the Sharia, involves grave matters on some ten counts. The letter written in Ghāzān’s name was closer to the Sharia than this letter written in the sultan’s name.”

And whether

---

## Page 1134

he does so or not, I believe and affirm as part of my faith in Allah that supporting him and assisting him in righteousness and piety, helping his truthfulness and justice prevail rather than the lies and injustice of others, and raising his standing above theirs are among the greatest obligations. There is no power or strength except through Allah.

Shaykh Nasr sent me a message offering to have the judicial records brought to me, should I wish, so that I could challenge them.

I replied: “They are too contemptible and insignificant for their refutation to require that they be produced. I have already demonstrated, on more than twenty grounds, that this judge has departed from the Sharia of Islam, according to the consensus of Muslims—those belonging to the four schools of law and others.”

---

## Page 1135

Section

Among the things you should know is that these people—Ibn Makhlūf and the others—are utterly unequal to debating the merits of the dispute. They have deliberated among themselves and know that, in such a debate, they would be defeated and confounded. Al-Taybarsī has asked me more than once to abandon such debate.

I said to him:

“I have not oppressed anyone, nor have I said to anyone, ‘Agree with my beliefs, or I will take action against you.’ Nor have I compelled anyone by word or deed. Indeed, I have never written anything on this subject except in response to a request for a religious ruling, after the questioner’s insistence, intense concern, and repeated requests. It is not my practice to initiate discussions of this subject with people.

“It is these people who have called others to what they advocate and honored them for it. They must therefore explain to people what they have commanded them to do and what they have forbidden them to do.

“If they have commanded them to do what Allah and His Messenger have commanded, then obedience is due to Allah, His Messenger, and whoever commands what Allah and His Messenger have commanded. But if they have enjoined both truth and falsehood, forbidden both truth and falsehood, and issued commands and prohibitions concerning matters whose true nature they do not understand, then they would thereby be among the ignorant and unjust. The judge who rules on that basis would be among the two kinds of judges who are in the Fire. Obeying them in this would not be permissible; rather, it would be forbidden.

---

## Page 1136

And had I chosen to pursue the dispute, the consequences would have been momentous. But whoever objects to anything I have said should declare, ‘I object to such-and-such,’ write out his objection in his own hand, and specify how it applies to what I have said. I will write my reply in my own hand, and both statements should be submitted to all the Muslim scholars, East and West. I stand by this.”

I had previously said in Damascus:

“These sweeping objections achieve nothing. Rather, whoever objects to something should write out his objection and its supporting evidence in his own hand. I will write my reply in my own hand, and people of knowledge and faith can examine both statements. This is the proper procedure in matters of public concern. As for statements that are not committed to writing, they are frequently confused, added to, and diminished—as has indeed happened.”

Among the things I said to al-Taybarsī was:

“What you have done undermines your religion, your state, and your religious law. The ‘royal’ letter written in the Sultan’s name contains numerous falsehoods concerning you and violations of religious law, amounting to more than ten points. Ghāzān’s letter, which was read from the pulpit in Syria, accords more closely with Islamic law than this letter written in the name of the Sultan of the Muslims and read from the pulpits of Islam. If, in their presence, falsehoods about you and the judges are committed to writing and the religion of Islam is altered, what, then, of other matters that have escaped your notice?”

Likewise, I sent a message through al-Fattāh to the Sultan’s deputy, saying:

“You have this statement of belief in your possession; it is the one examined by the scholars of Syria. Whoever objects to anything in it should specify it.”

---

## Page 1137

It must also be understood that whoever wishes to censure others may do so only with proof and a clear explanation. No one may impose an obligation on another person or forbid him anything without specific proof, except the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, who conveys Allah’s message. Allah has required His creatures to obey him in matters their minds comprehend and in those they do not. What he reports is to be believed, whether it concerns matters we know or matters we do not know.

As for anyone else who says, “This is right” or “This is wrong,” if he does not substantiate this with grounds that make following him obligatory, then the first requirement for censure is that the person censuring know what he is censuring and what people are capable of. No creature of Allah, whoever he may be, has the right to invalidate a statement or declare an action forbidden except on the authority of proof. Otherwise, he is among those of whom Allah says:

“Indeed, those who dispute concerning the verses of Allah without any authority having come to them have nothing in their hearts but a desire for greatness they will never attain.”

He also says of them:

“Those who dispute concerning the verses of Allah without any authority having come to them are greatly hated by Allah and by those who believe. Thus does Allah seal the heart of every arrogant tyrant.”

Nevertheless, I remain forbearing towards those who disagree with me. Even if someone transgresses Allah’s limits in his treatment of me—by declaring me an unbeliever or a sinner, fabricating accusations against me, or displaying the partisan zeal of the days of ignorance—I do not transgress Allah’s limits in my treatment of him. Rather, I keep my words and actions under control, weigh them in the scales of justice, and make them follow the Book that Allah revealed as guidance for people and as a judge in their disagreements. Allah Most High says:

“Mankind was one community. Then Allah sent the prophets as bearers of glad tidings and warnings, and sent down with them the Book in truth to judge between people concerning their disagreements.”

He Most High also says:

“If you disagree over anything, refer it to Allah

---

## Page 1138

and the Messenger,” to the end of the verse. Allah Most High also says:

“We sent Our messengers with clear proofs and sent down with them the Book and the balance, so that people may uphold justice.”

For you cannot repay someone who disobeys Allah in his treatment of you better than by obeying Allah in your treatment of him:

“Indeed, Allah is with those who fear Him and those who do good.”

Allah Most High also says:

“If you are patient and fear Allah, their plotting will not harm you at all. Indeed, Allah encompasses all that they do.”

If they wish to challenge my position with whatever rational or transmitted proofs they choose, I am prepared to respond to them on every such basis. I will explain, in terms that specialists and ordinary people alike can understand, that what I say accords with the self-evident requirements of reason and innate human nature, and with the Book, the Sunnah, and the consensus of the early generations of this community. Whoever opposes it opposes clear reason and authentic transmitted evidence.

Even if I had been the first to censure others and speak in this manner, the argument would still stand against them. How much more so, then, when someone else initiated the censure?

“As for those who defend themselves after being wronged, there is no blame upon them”—this verse and the next.

“Our word has already gone forth to Our servants, the messengers: they will surely be helped, and Our forces will surely prevail.”

“Indeed, We will help Our messengers and those who believe in the life of this world and on the Day when the witnesses stand forth.”

Peace be upon you, and Allah’s mercy and blessings, and upon the rest of the group. Give “Badr al-Dīn” especially my warmest greetings, and show him these pages if you wish, for he used to say regarding certain matters: “No secret is concealed from the beloved.” And convey glad tidings of all

---

## Page 1139

that Allah has made a source of relief for His believing servants and through which He exacts retribution from the disbelievers and hypocrites. For I know something of the bitter suffering he and his associates—those who seek authority through falsehood, men of lies and absurdities—endure. Allah will support His religion and His believing servants against those who oppose them unjustly. But this is not the place to recount the heartening details.

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of the worlds. May Allah bless Muhammad, his family, and his Companions, and grant them peace.

---

## Page 1140

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—said:

In the name of Allah, the Most Merciful, the Bestower of Mercy.

All praise is due to Allah. We seek His help and forgiveness, and we seek refuge in Allah from the evil within ourselves and from our evil deeds. Whoever Allah guides, none can lead astray; and whoever He leads astray, none can guide. We bear witness that there is no deity worthy of worship except Allah, and we bear witness that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

To proceed:

Your letter has reached me. In it, you mentioned informing the shaykh of the envoy’s meeting with me and of the conversation you related to him. You also mentioned that the shaykh said: “Know that, by Allah, I am deeply troubled by how matters came to this”—and so forth.

You also wrote that he said you should meet with the shaykh and reach an agreement with him—on what he himself sees fit and chooses, whether it is as you said or otherwise—then greet him and speak to him:

As for this matter, I have no particular personal aim in it whatsoever. I am merely one of the Muslims: I have the same rights as they do and bear the same obligations,

---

## Page 1141

and, praise be to Allah, I have no particular need that I seek to have fulfilled by any created being, nor any affliction that I seek to have removed by any created being. Rather, I enjoy abundant blessing from Allah and immense mercy for which I cannot adequately give thanks. However, I must obey Allah and His Messenger, and obey those in authority when they command me to obey Allah. If they command me to disobey Allah, no created being may be obeyed in disobedience to the Creator. This is what the “Book” and the “Sunnah” establish, and what the “leading scholars of the Ummah” have agreed upon. Allah Most High says:

“O you who believe, obey Allah and obey the Messenger and those in authority among you. If you disagree over anything, refer it to Allah and the Messenger, if you believe in Allah and the Last Day. That is better and leads to the best outcome.”

It is authentically reported that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“No created being may be obeyed in disobedience to Allah.”

“Obedience is only in what is right.”

I must also patiently endure the injustice of rulers and not rise against them in civil strife, because of the authentic report from Ibn ‘Abbās, who said that the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Whoever sees something in his ruler that he dislikes should bear it patiently, for whoever departs from the community by even a handspan and then dies, dies a death of pre-Islamic ignorance.”

Alongside this, I am also commanded to speak the truth—or uphold it—wherever I may be, fearing no one’s reproach for Allah’s sake. This accords with what al-Bukhārī and Muslim both related in their Sahīh collections from ‘Ubādah ibn al-Sāmit, who said:

“We pledged allegiance to the Messenger of Allah, undertaking to listen and obey in ease and hardship, willingly and unwillingly, even when others are given preference over us; not to dispute authority with those who hold it; and that

---

## Page 1142

we would speak the truth—or uphold it—wherever we might be, fearing no one’s reproach for Allah’s sake.”

Thus, he took their pledge upon these “three comprehensive principles”: obedience in what constitutes obedience to Allah, even if the person giving the command is unjust; refraining from disputing authority with those who hold it; and upholding the truth without fear of created beings. Allah, glorified be He, has commanded in His Book that when the Ummah disputes, the matter be referred to Allah and His Messenger. He did not command recourse to any particular thing at all in the event of a dispute.

The leading scholars have said that those in authority are of two kinds: scholars and rulers. This includes religious shaykhs and Muslim kings. Each is to be obeyed within the sphere entrusted to him. The former are obeyed regarding the acts of worship they are commanded to observe, and are consulted concerning the meanings of the Quran and hadith and what they convey concerning Allah. The latter are obeyed in jihad, the enforcement of prescribed punishments, and other duties they carry out which Allah has commanded them to perform. When they agree on a matter, their consensus constitutes conclusive proof, for the Ummah of Muhammad—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—does not agree upon misguidance. If they dispute, recourse is to the Book and the Sunnah.

Various things have occurred in this affair which this is not the place to recount. Through your conversations and letters, you conveyed to me what you conveyed from the shaykh. You have seen and heard my agreement to everything that constitutes obedience to Allah and His Messenger, and my disregard for demanding my own entitlements or retaliating against those who harm me. You have become certain of this about me. What more, then, can be required of a Muslim? You advised me to set aside fear and adopt a gentle manner, and I am willing to comply with all of this.

Al-Fattāh came first and said, “The deputy sends you his greetings.” He also said, “How long will you remain

---

## Page 1143

in prison? Will you not come out? Do you still stand by that statement or not?” I knew that entrusting the message to al-Fattāh alone would serve no good purpose, for reasons that are clear.

I said to him, “Convey my greetings to the deputy and tell him that I do not know what this statement is. To this very hour, I do not know why I have been imprisoned or what my offence is. My reply to this message cannot be conveyed through you. Rather, he should send four emirs from among his trusted men—men who understand and acknowledge the truth—so that what is said in their presence is accurately preserved, without additions or omissions. I already know what lies have been told in this affair.”

After that, al-Fattāh came with someone whom I did not know, but who told me that he was called ʿAlāʾ al-Dīn al-Taybarsī. I subsequently found that those who knew him praised him and spoke well of him. However, he did not begin by saying anything that allowed for a courteous reply. He did not say, “The statement you repudiated was such and such,” nor did he ask, “Will you agree to such and such?”

Had he said what he said—including his lies about me, his unbelief, and his disputation—in a manner that called for a courteous reply, I would have replied accordingly. People know that I am among the most forbearing and patient in enduring bitter words, and among the fairest in addressing even the humblest of people, let alone (those in authority. But his approach was one of compulsion: I was to agree to what he was calling for. He brought out a scroll containing

---

## Page 1144

lies, injustice, calls to disobey Allah, and prohibitions against obeying Him, the extent of which Allah knows. Whenever I tried to answer him and entrust him with a message to convey, he refused to hear or convey any of it. He wanted nothing but an acknowledgment of what he had stated and a commitment not to return to it. Yet Allah Most High says:

“Do not argue with the People of the Scripture except in the best manner, except with those among them who commit injustice.”

When the person addressing us acts unjustly, we are not commanded to answer him in the best manner. Indeed, when ʿUrwa ibn Masʿūd said, “I see a rabble of men likely to flee and abandon you,” Abū Bakr al-Siddīq, may Allah be pleased with him, replied in the Prophet’s presence, may Allah bless him and grant him peace: “Suck al-Lāt’s clitoris! Would we flee from him and abandon him?”

It is well known that honour belongs to Allah, His Messenger, and the believers, whoever they may be. Allah Most High has said:

“Do not lose heart or grieve, for you will have the upper hand if you are believers.”

Whoever is a believer has the upper hand, whoever he may be. As for those who oppose Allah and His Messenger, Allah Most High has said:

“Indeed, those who oppose Allah and His Messenger will be among the most abased.”

Whichever of these two groups I or anyone else belongs to, Allah will deal with me and with others according to His promise, for His word is the truth:

“The promise of Allah—Allah never fails in His promise.”

Among the things I said to him was: “The right at stake in this affair is not mine; it belongs to Allah, His Messenger, and all the believers, from the east of the earth to its west. I have no intention of altering or changing the religion. Neither for your sake nor for anyone else’s will I apostatize from Islam and admit to unbelief, lies, and slander, whether retracting it or assenting to it.”

---

## Page 1145

When I saw him persisting in demanding this, I spoke sharply to him and said, “Stop this nonsense, get up, and go about your business. I never asked you to release me.” They had closed the upright door through which one passes to the trapdoor, so I said, “Open the door for me so that I can go down,” meaning that the discussion was over.

More than once he said to me, “Do you oppose the four schools of law?”

I replied, “I have said nothing except what agrees with the four schools of law. None of the judges has passed judgment against me except Ibn Makhlūf, and you were present that day. I said to him, ‘Are you judging alone, or together with these men?’

“He replied, ‘I alone.’

“I said to him, ‘You are my adversary. How, then, can you judge me?’

“He replied with such-and-such words, drawing out his voice, and withdrew into the corner. Then he said, ‘Get up! Get up!’

“They made me stand and ordered me taken to prison. My brothers and I then said repeatedly, ‘I will return and answer, even if you alone are the judge.’ But this was not accepted from me.

“Once they had taken me to prison, he issued the judgment he issued, placed on record what he placed on record, and gave the orders he gave in the sultan’s decree. Would any Jew or Christian—let alone a Muslim—say that this man was imprisoned according to religious law, much less according to the law of Muhammad ibn Abd Allāh? Even young children know, as a self-evident matter of the religion of Islam, that this contravenes the law of Muhammad ibn Abd Allāh. Yet this judge and his associates constantly say, ‘We did what we did according to the law of Muhammad ibn Abd Allāh.’”

---

## Page 1146

And this judgment contravenes Allah’s law—upon which the Muslims are agreed—in more than twenty respects.

Moreover, the Christians are held in a comfortable prison, where they associate partners with Allah and establish churches. Would that our confinement were like that of the Christians! Would that we had been treated on an equal footing with the polytheists and idol worshippers! Instead, they are honored while we are humiliated.

Can anyone who believes in Allah and the Last Day say that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, commanded this? And for what offense under the religion of Islam did he imprison my brothers? His grounds were nothing but lies and slander. Whoever says, “That was done in accordance with religious law,” has committed disbelief, by the consensus of the Muslims.

I also said to him in the course of our conversation, “If a man brought a claim against you for ten dirhams while you were present in the town and were not refusing to attend the judge’s court, the judge would have no right to rule against you in your absence. This is so in matters concerning rights; how much more so in matters involving punishments, where doing this is forbidden by the consensus of the Muslims!

“Furthermore, this man has been exposed as a liar more than once. That day, he lied about me in most of what he said. The document he ordered to be written consists mostly of lies, and the sultan’s decree written at his command contravenes the Sharia in some ten respects. It also contains grave falsehoods about the session that was held, which are known to the elite and the common people alike. So if the document

---

## Page 1147

that was written in the sultan’s name and read from the pulpits of Islam contains such blatant lies and slander about those who attended the session—the emirs and judges—what then of matters they did not witness?”

I said, “He constantly claims that I say Allah is in a corner and has begotten a child. All of this is false. His reputation for lying and wickedness is known to the elite and the common people alike. Is someone like this fit to adjudicate questions concerning the foundations of religion and the meanings of the Quran and Sunnah, when he knows nothing of them?” At this point, I saw him smile like someone who knew that what I had said was true. It seemed that this judge’s evil conduct was notorious among the Muslims.

He began saying to me, “Here are the records, and they found material in your handwriting.”

I replied, “You were present that day. Did anyone show me an error or a written record that day? Was I told, ‘Testimony has been given against you concerning such-and-such’? Or was anything I had to say heard? Rather, when I began to praise and extol Allah, in accordance with the saying of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace,

‘Every important undertaking that is not begun with praise of Allah is incomplete,’

they stopped me from praising Allah. They said, ‘Do not praise Allah; just answer.’

“I said to Ibn Makhlūf, ‘Am I to answer you or this claimant?’ Each of them had made statements that were mostly lies.

“He said, ‘Answer the claimant.’

“I said, ‘Will you alone give judgment, or will you and these judges do so?’

“He said, ‘I alone.’

“I said, ‘Then you are my adversary. How can your judgment against me be valid?’ Yet you did not ask me to explain the grounds of this dispute? For this man was

---

## Page 1148

my adversary on several grounds known to all Muslims.”

Then I said, “As for what was in my handwriting, I stand by it. As for the records, there are several grounds that discredit the witnesses named in them and preclude acceptance of their testimony by the consensus of the Muslims. Moreover, Muslims, both the elite and the common people, in Syria and elsewhere, know the opposite of what they testified to.

“Trustworthy people heard this judge, Sharaf al-Dīn Ibn al-Maqdisī, say, ‘I follow so-and-so’s creed.’ Indeed, three days before his death, I visited him, it seems, with a group of people, and he said in their presence, ‘I shall die following your creed, so-and-so. I do not follow the creed of these people’—meaning the adversaries. Likewise, Judge Shihāb al-Dīn al-Khawlī has said more than once, behind your back, ‘I follow his creed.’ Regarding Judge Imām al-Dīn, there is testimony involving trustworthy witnesses that he said, ‘Nothing objectionable has appeared in his statements, and anyone who speaks against him I shall punish.’”

During our discussion, he said to me, “Some of the judges have said that they removed you from the teaching chair.”

I replied, “This is among the most blatant lies, as everyone knows. I have never been removed from the teaching chair. No one has ever asked me to repent of anything or to retract anything. I also said: You have received the record bearing the signatures of the shaykhs of Syria and the leading figures of Islam, and the document containing the statements of the judges who are my adversaries, such as Jamāl al-Dīn al-Mālikī and Jalāl al-Dīn al-Hanafī, including what they stated in it that contradicts

---

## Page 1149

these records. There is also al-Mālikī’s statement: ‘I have never heard that he was asked to repent, barred from issuing fatwas, removed from his teaching chair, or subjected to this or that. Nor has anything that impugns his religious integrity ever been established against him before me.’ The other scholars and judges have likewise spoken in my absence.

As for the testimonies, they contain matters of great gravity, so consider them carefully. All the more so since there are grounds for disqualifying the witnesses named in the record—grounds that can be stated when necessary.”

---

## Page 1150

A Digression

You mentioned in your letter that you said to the shaykh:

“I would like you to obtain the records for me so that he may examine them. If he has a rebuttal, well and good; otherwise, the entire group is excused.”

There is absolutely no need for this. These records are too insignificant and contemptible for their refutation to require having them before me. I have already demonstrated, on twenty-odd grounds, that this judgment falls outside Islamic law according to the consensus of the Muslims—the four schools of law and all the other leading religious scholars.

I said to the messenger:

“What business do Ibn Makhlūf and others like him have intervening in matters of religious knowledge that others understand better than he does—such as the interpretation of the Quran, the hadiths of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, the statements of the early generations, and the fundamentals of religion, which he does not know? These matters must be referred only to those who understand them. If the sultan or his governing deputy understands them, he stands in this respect like anyone else who understands them; otherwise, they have no authority in these matters, just as requests for fatwas are addressed only to those competent to issue them.”

I also said to him:

“Nothing has ever come from me except answers to questions and fatwas for those seeking them. I have never written to anyone or spoken to him about any of this. Rather, a man comes to me seeking guidance and a fatwa based on what Allah revealed to His Messenger. He asks me, despite the distance, burning with longing for guidance

---

## Page 1151

—does my religion, then, permit me to withhold knowledge from him? The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

‘Whoever is asked about knowledge he possesses and conceals it, Allah will bridle him with a bridle of fire on the Day of Resurrection.’

Allah Most High also said:

‘Indeed, those who conceal what We sent down of clear proofs and guidance after We made it clear for the people in the Scripture—those are cursed by Allah and cursed by those who curse.’

Am I, at your command, to refrain from answering someone seeking guidance, only to become one of them? Would the sultan, or any other Muslim, command me to do this? Yet your only aim was to thwart the supposed bid for kingship, because of the lies that had reached you.”

He said: “My master, leave aside the question of kingship. No one is talking about kingship.”

I replied: “Well! Now no one is talking about kingship! Was this turmoil stirred up for any other reason? While we were in Syria, we heard that what had prompted it was the accusation of seeking kingship, but we did not think anyone would believe this.”

I also explained to him that I was not the one who would be harmed by this affair. What, after all, have I to fear? If I were killed, I would be among the finest martyrs, and that would bring me blessedness: Allah’s pleasure would be invoked upon me until the Day of Resurrection, while whoever worked to bring this about would be cursed until the Day of Resurrection. For the entire community of Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, knows that I would be killed for the truth with which Allah sent His Messenger.

If I were imprisoned, then, by Allah, my imprisonment would be one of Allah’s greatest blessings upon me. I have nothing over which to fear what people might do: no school, no land grant, no wealth, no position of authority—nothing whatsoever.

---

## Page 1152

But the harm caused by this affair will fall upon you. I know that those in Syria who pursued it intended to plot against you and undermine your religion and your state. Some of them have gone to the lands of the Tatars, and some reside there. They are the ones who sought to ruin your religious and worldly affairs and portrayed me as a covert leader, knowing that I am loyal to you, sincerely counsel you, and desire good for you in this world and the Hereafter. This affair has secrets that are gradually coming to light. Otherwise, there has never been any enmity or hatred between me and anyone in Egypt. I have always loved them and remained loyal to them—their emirs, their shaykhs, and their judges.

He said to me: “What, then, should I tell the sultan’s deputy?”

I replied: “Convey my greetings to him and tell him everything you have heard.”

He said: “That is a great deal.”

I replied: “In brief, most of what is in this scroll is false. As for the expression ‘He rose in a real sense,’ several scholars from the various schools—the Mālikīs and others—have stated that Ahl al-Sunnah wa-l-Jamāʿah agreed upon it unanimously. None of the early generations of this community or its leading scholars rejected it. Indeed, I know of no scholar who rejected it. How, then, could I abandon what Ahl al-Sunnah unanimously affirmed and no scholar rejected?”

By this I was referring to several statements, including that of Imam Abū ʿUmar al-Talamankī, one of the leading Mālikī scholars before al-Bājī, Ibn ʿAbd al-Barr, and their generation. He said:

“The Muslims of Ahl al-Sunnah unanimously agreed that the meaning of ‘And He is with you wherever you are,’ and similar passages in the Quran, is that this refers to His knowledge, and that Allah is above the heavens in His Essence, having risen over His Throne however He willed.”

And he said:

---

## Page 1153

also:

Ahl al-Sunnah said concerning Allah’s words,

“The Most Merciful rose over the Throne,”

that Allah’s rising over His glorious Throne is real, not metaphorical.

Abū ‘Abd Allāh al-Qurtubī, author of the well-known Quran commentary, said concerning the Most High’s words,

“Then He rose over the Throne”:

Scholars have discussed this “question of rising over the Throne” and devoted treatises to it. We have explained their views in our book “Al-Asnā: An Explanation of Allah’s Most Beautiful Names,” mentioning fourteen positions on the matter.

Later, he said:

The earliest generations—may Allah be pleased with them—did not maintain that direction should be denied, nor did they express such a denial. Rather, they and everyone else explicitly affirmed it for Allah Most High, as His Book declares and His messengers reported.

He said:

None of the righteous early generations denied that He truly rose over His Throne. The Throne was singled out because it is the greatest of His creations. What they did not know was only the manner of His rising over it, for its actual nature is unknown. As Mālik said, “Rising over the Throne is known”—meaning in the language—while its manner is unknown, and asking about this is an innovation. Umm Salamah—may Allah be pleased with her—said likewise.

This shaykh, renowned in Egypt and elsewhere, said in his book “An Explanation of the Names”:

Imam Abū Bakr Muhammad ibn al-Hasan al-Hadramī al-Qayrawānī, who wrote the epistle he entitled “The Epistle on the Names up to the Question of Rising over the Throne,” mentioned, when discussing the disagreement among later scholars concerning rising over the Throne, the view of “al-Tabarī—that is, Abū Ja‘far, author of the extensive Quran commentary—and Abū Muhammad ibn Abī Zayd, Qādī ‘Abd al-Wahhāb, and a number of leading scholars of hadith and jurisprudence. He said: This is the apparent sense of some of the books of Qādī Abū Bakr,” and of Abū al-Hasan—that is,

---

## Page 1154

al-Ash‘arī—and Qādī ‘Abd al-Wahhāb also reported this from him, meaning Qādī Abū Bakr:

He, glorified be He, has risen over the Throne in His very essence. In some passages, they used the expression “above His Throne.” Imam Abū Bakr said: “This is the correct position, which I affirm, without delimitation, settlement in a place, presence within it, or contact.”

Shaykh Abū ‘Abd Allāh said:

This is the position of Qādī Abū Bakr in his book Tamhīd al-Awā’il (Introduction to First Principles). The learned Abū Bakr ibn Fūrak also stated it in his Sharh Awā’il al-Adillah (Commentary on the First Proofs). It is likewise the position of Abū ‘Umar ibn ‘Abd al-Barr, al-Talamankī, and other Andalusian scholars, and of al-Khattābī in Shi‘ār al-Dīn (The Emblem of Religion).

Then, after relating fourteen positions, he said:

“The clearest position is that supported by the converging testimony of the verses, the reports, and the virtuous and righteous scholars: Allah is over His Throne, as He has informed us in His Book and through His Prophet, without specifying how, and is distinct from all His creation. This is the position of the righteous early generations, as trustworthy authorities have transmitted from them.”

All of this is his exact wording.

Shaykh Abū Nasr al-Sijzī said in his book al-Ibānah (The Elucidation):

“Our imams—such as Sufyān al-Thawrī, Mālik ibn Anas, Sufyān ibn ‘Uyaynah, Hammād ibn Salamah, Hammād ibn Zayd, ‘Abd Allāh ibn al-Mubārak, Fudayl ibn ‘Iyād, Ahmad ibn Hanbal, and Ishāq ibn Rāhawayh—agree that Allah, glorified be He, is above the Throne in His very essence; that His knowledge encompasses every place; that on the Day of Resurrection He will be seen with the eyes while He is above the Throne; that He descends to the lowest heaven; and that He becomes angry, is pleased, and speaks whatever He wills. Whoever opposes any of this is disassociated from them, and they are disassociated from him.”

---

## Page 1155

Abū Bakr ibn ‘Abd al-Barr said in al-Tamhīd, his commentary on al-Muwatta’—the finest work composed in its field—when discussing the hadith of the descent:

“This hadith is firmly established through transmission and has an authentic chain of narration. Hadith scholars do not disagree about its authenticity. It has also been transmitted through other routes, through reports from trustworthy narrators relating from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. It provides evidence that Allah is in heaven, over the Throne, above the seven heavens, as the community has affirmed. It is among their proofs against the Mu‘tazilah, who say that Allah is everywhere and is not over the Throne.”

He said: “Among the proofs establishing the correctness of what the people of truth affirm are Allah’s words:

‘The Most Merciful rose over the Throne.’

And His words:

‘To Him ascends good speech.’

And His words:

‘The angels and the Spirit ascend to Him.’

And His words to Jesus:

‘I will take you and raise you to Myself.’”

He cited other verses and went on to say:

“This is so well known among ordinary people and scholars alike that it requires no more than a statement of it, for it is known of necessity: no one taught it to them, and no Muslim disagreed with them about it.”

This was discussed at length, until he said:

“As for their appeal to the words of the Most High:

‘There is no private conversation among three but He is their fourth, nor among five but He is their sixth, nor among fewer or more than that but He is with them wherever they may be,’

the apparent meaning of the verse offers them no proof. For the scholars among the Companions and their successors—from whom its interpretation has been transmitted—said in explaining this verse: ‘He is over the Throne, and His knowledge is everywhere.’ No one whose opinion carries authority disagreed with them about this.”

---

## Page 1156

He also related that al-Dahhāk ibn Muzāhim said, concerning Allah’s words:

“There is no private conversation among three”:

“He is over His Throne, and His knowledge is with them wherever they may be.” A similar statement was related from Sufyān al-Thawrī.

It was also related from Ibn Mas‘ūd that he said: “Allah is above the Throne, and nothing of your deeds is hidden from Him.”

Abū ‘Umar ibn ‘Abd al-Barr said:

“Ahl al-Sunnah unanimously affirm all the attributes mentioned in the Quran and Sunnah, believe in them, and understand them in their real rather than figurative sense. However, they do not specify how any of them are, nor define them by a circumscribed description. As for the adherents of innovation—the Jahmiyyah, all the Mu‘tazilah, and the Khawārij—they all deny these attributes and understand none of them in their real sense. They claim that whoever affirms them likens Allah to His creation, whereas those who affirm them regard these people as denying the One who is worshipped. The truth concerning these attributes is what is affirmed by those who uphold what Allah’s Book and the Sunnah of His Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, declare. These are the leaders of the community.”

Abū ‘Umar also said:

“The position of Ahl al-Sunnah and the leading scholars of jurisprudence and transmitted reports concerning this matter and similar matters is to believe in and accept as true what has come from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, concerning them, without defining any of these attributes or specifying how they are.”

The shaykh versed in spiritual knowledge, Abū Muhammad ‘Abd al-Qādir ibn Abī Sālih al-Kīlānī, said in his book al-Ghunya:

“As for knowing the Creator through signs and proofs—to state it briefly—it consists in knowing with certainty that Allah is One, uniquely One.”

He continued until he said:

“He is in the direction above, risen over the Throne, possessing all dominion, and His knowledge encompasses all things.”

He said:

“It is not permissible to describe Him as being everywhere. Rather, it should be said that He is in heaven, over the Throne, as He said:

‘The Most Merciful rose over the Throne.’”

He cited verses and hadiths until he said: “One should affirm the attribute of rising over the Throne

---

## Page 1157

without reinterpretation, and affirm that it is His very Essence that has risen over the Throne.” He said: “His being over the Throne is mentioned in every book sent down to a prophet commissioned as a messenger, without specifying how.” He discussed this at length.

Imam Abū al-Hasan al-Karajī al-Shāfi‘ī said in his well-known introduction on “The Creed of Ahl al-Sunnah,” copied from the handwriting of Shaykh Abū ‘Amr ibn al-Salāh:

Their creed is that Allah Himself is over His Throne,
While His knowledge encompasses all unseen things.

I did not mention all these reports to the envoy. They are, however, among those to which I referred when I said: “I have said nothing of my own accord. I have only stated what the early generations of this community and its leading scholars agreed upon.” There is not enough space here to include the community’s statements on this matter.

He said to me: “Yes, He Himself is truly risen over the Throne, without specifying how and without likening Him to creation.”

I said: “Yes, and this is what the Creed states.”

He said: “Then write it down now,” or “Write this down,” or words to that effect.

I replied: “This is already written in these very words in the Creed you have—the one examined in Damascus and agreed upon by the Muslims. What, then, is it that I want?”

I also told him: “I have brought more than fifty books—works by hadith scholars, Sufis, theologians, and jurists of the four schools: the Hanafīs, Mālikīs, Shāfi‘īs, and Hanbalīs—all agreeing with what I have said.”

I said: “I allow anyone who disagrees with me three years to produce a single word from the leading scholars of Islam that contradicts what I have said. What am I to do?”

When al-Taybarsī and al-Fattāh left, al-Fattāh returned a little later and said: “Greetings to you from

---

## Page 1158

the Sultan’s deputy, who said: ‘Now write out a creed for us in your own hand.’”

I replied: “Convey my greetings to the Sultan’s deputy and tell him: ‘If I were to write something now, someone would say that I had added or omitted something, or changed the creed. Likewise, in Damascus, when they requested the creed, the accusations against me concerned only something already written.’”

I said: “This is the creed that was read in Syria at the three sessions. Your deputy sent it to you by courier, and you have all the documents. Then, when the second letter arrived, he again sent you, through al-‘Umarī, the statements of the judges and scholars, the official record, and the book by al-Bukhārī that al-Mizzī had read. The creed is not something I devise myself, such that I would have a different creed every day. It is that very same creed, and the very same copy. Examine it.”

He went away, then returned and asked me to write anything at all in my own hand. I asked: “What should I write?” He replied: “Something about pardoning people and not taking action against anyone.”

I said: “Yes, I am willing to do that. My aim is not to harm anyone, take revenge on him, or hold him to account. I forgive those who have wronged me.” I intended to write this, but then said: “It is not customary to put such a thing in writing, for a person’s relinquishing his own claim does not require this. You know that, when matters unfolded in this way, some people nearly turned against the shaykh. They thought that he had endorsed this document and that it contradicted what he had been saying and conveying in his messages. My brother and I therefore began countering this, saying: ‘This is Ibn Makhlūf’s doing.’ I have personally ascertained that it was indeed Ibn Makhlūf’s doing. The shaykh knows that a case like this, which has become widely known and publicized, cannot be resolved

---

## Page 1159

in this manner. I therefore do all I can to show kindness, refrain from retaliation, and bring hearts together. But he knows many people in Egypt, and knows that one can escape their evil and injustice only by following two paths: one is stable, while the other is subject to change.

(The first is to have support and authority from Allah, to seek refuge in Him and His help, to rely upon Him, seek His forgiveness, and obey Him. Through this, Allah wards off the evil of the devils among mankind and the jinn. This is the firm and enduring path.

The second path is when protection comes from someone of influence. They show consideration for an influential person as long as his influence lasts. But when his fortunes change, those very same people become some of his fiercest opponents. They may even beat the judge with rods and do similar things scarcely known among others. His enemies and those who hate him are numerous, and he has become involved in legal confirmations, property matters, and other affairs concerning both the state and private parties.

Thus, if someone influential had an interest in overturning his judgments and transferring ownership of properties, this would be exceedingly easy for him: he could put his apostasy on record, and an apostate’s judgments have no legal effect. For both the learned and the general public know that he has presented what he did in this case as the religious law of Muhammad ibn Abd Allāh, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Whenever a person declares lawful something unanimously agreed to be forbidden, declares forbidden something unanimously agreed to be lawful, or alters religious law on which there is consensus, he becomes an unbeliever and an apostate, by agreement of the jurists. And concerning a case such as this

---

## Page 1160

His words were revealed, according to one of the two interpretations:

“And whoever does not judge by what Allah has revealed—it is they who are the disbelievers.”

That is, the one who considers it lawful to judge by something other than what Allah has revealed.

In common usage, the term “religious law” has three meanings:

The first is “revealed religious law”: what the Messenger brought. This must be followed, and whoever contravenes it must be punished.

The second is “interpreted religious law”: the opinions of scholars qualified to exercise independent legal reasoning, such as the school of Mālik and others like it. Following this is permissible, but neither obligatory nor forbidden. No one may compel people at large to follow it or prohibit them from doing so.

The third is “altered religious law”: lies about Allah and His Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, or lies against people through false testimony and the like, as well as manifest injustice. Whoever says that this is part of Allah’s religious law has disbelieved, without dispute. This is like someone saying, “Blood and carrion are lawful”—even if he says, “This is my school of thought,” or something similar.

Even if the ruling issued by Ibn Makhlūf had been the position of Mālik or al-Ash'arī, he would have had no right to impose it upon everyone and punish anyone who did not agree with him on it, by the consensus of the Muslim community. How much more so, then, when the position he professes and imposes contradicts the explicit statements of Mālik and the leading scholars of his school, and those of al-Ash'arī and the leading scholars of his school, such as al-Qādī Abū Bakr and Abū al-Hasan al-Tabarī,

---

## Page 1161

and Abū Bakr ibn Fūrak, Abū al-Qāsim al-Qushayrī, Abū Bakr al-Bayhaqī, and others? They all explicitly affirm what we have said and the opposite of what he has said. This is why the Hanbalīs and the Ash'arīs reconciled, and everyone came to an agreement.

When the Hanbalīs read the words of Abū al-Hasan al-Ash'arī, they said, “This is better than the words of Shaykh al-Muwaffaq.” The resentments harboured in their hearts disappeared, and the Shāfi'ī jurists and others began saying, “Praise be to Allah for bringing the Muslims into agreement.”

Moreover, even supposing that the matter on which he ruled admitted of independent legal reasoning, he would have had no right to overturn another judge’s ruling. How much more objectionable, then, is overturning the rulings of all the judges of Syria without even a semblance of justification—indeed, on grounds that contradict the Muslims’ religion, by their unanimous agreement! Even if someone claimed that the judges of Syria had been coerced, more than one of them explicitly denies having been coerced. These judges in Egypt were more evidently under coercion, since it was widely known that he had acted to serve a state objective concerning political power, and that, were it not for this, the judges would have spoken about certain matters. This is established on the authority of the judges of Egypt.

How much more so when the ruling he issued contradicts Islamic religious law in twenty-odd respects, most of which are established by the unanimous agreement of the Muslims? These points have been set down in writing and are with al-Sharaf Muhammad. Shaykh “Nasr” should therefore be informed of the true state of affairs and the underlying circumstances of the case, so that he may remedy it through his judicious handling.

I seek nothing but obedience to Allah and His Messenger. The only danger feared for the Egyptians is what some of them may do to others, as has habitually happened. You have heard what happened in Damascus—even though

---

## Page 1162

those people were closer to agreement—how the aforementioned judge renewed his profession of Islam before another judge. When I was there, this attendant, “Yahyā al-Hanafī,” went to Judge Taqī al-Dīn al-Hanbalī and renewed his profession of Islam. The judge ruled that his life was inviolable after some of their associates brought accusations against him concerning certain matters.

Some time earlier, when Judge Husām al-Dīn al-Hanafī was serving as judge in Syria, he wanted to shave this al-Adhra'ī’s beard. He had a razor and a donkey brought so that al-Adhra'ī could be made to ride it and be paraded around. His brother came and informed me of this, so I went to the judge and persisted with him until he desisted. Other incidents occurred in which I continued to treat them kindly. Such actions are neither mine nor those of people like me. We involve ourselves only in what Allah, His Messenger, and the believers love. We pursue no personal ends in our dealings with anyone; rather, we repay evil with good, pardon, and forgive. This case has become widely known; what he did in it has come to light, and both the elite and the general public know of it.

If circumstances were to change, and an emir or minister were to come with an interest in transferring ownership that he had established or ruled upon, this would be among the easiest things for the Egyptians to accomplish. They would establish his apostasy—and an apostate’s judicial rulings are invalid by agreement of the scholars. The harm would then fall upon those who had assisted him and supported him in falsehood, whether state officials or others. This is a serious matter that should not be neglected. The shaykh is experienced and understands the consequences of affairs.

---

## Page 1163

By Allah, I am among those most eager to help extinguish every evil in this matter and elsewhere, and to establish every good. Whatever Ibn Makhlūf might do, by Allah, I will do him whatever good I can, and I will never help his enemy against him. There is no power or strength except through Allah. This is my intention and resolve, despite my knowledge of all these affairs. I know that Satan sows discord among the believers, and I will not help Satan against my Muslim brothers. If I were outside, I would know better how to help him. But they have acted falsely in this matter, and Allah chooses for all Muslims what is best for their religion and their worldly affairs. This cycle will not end, nor will the confusion disappear, except through returning to Allah, seeking His forgiveness, repenting, and sincerely seeking refuge in Him. Indeed, there is no refuge from Him except with Him. There is no power or strength except through Allah.

As for what you mentioned about Shaykh Nasr, that he said, “I would have preferred that they not become aware of him until he had already left, for fear that so-and-so might find out, and they would learn of it and begin talking, causing much uproar and discussion”—tell him that whenever anyone speaks the truth, I, more than anyone, ought to listen to it, abide by it, and accept it, whether it is sweet or bitter. I, more than anyone, ought to repent of the sins I have committed; indeed, I would be most deserving of punishment if I were leading Muslims astray from their religion.

I have said before that no one should allow his affection for someone and his loyalty to him to lead him to take that person’s side in falsehood or to suspend Allah Most High’s prescribed punishments for his sake. Indeed, these are the words of

---

## Page 1164

the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Whoever intercedes to prevent the enforcement of one of Allah’s prescribed punishments has opposed Allah in His command.”

As for what he fears—the “enemy” and others like him taking action concerning the written statement he brought from Syria to Ibn Makhlūf about seeking aid from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace—if they make it public, its evil consequences will fall upon them, and it will demonstrate that they are polytheists who do not distinguish between the religion of the Muslims and that of the Christians. For Muslims agree on what is necessarily known to be part of Islam: a servant may worship, invoke, seek aid from, and place his trust in none but Allah. Whoever worships an angel brought near to Allah or a prophet sent as a messenger, invokes him, or seeks his aid is a polytheist.

Thus, no Muslim considers it permissible for anyone to say, “O Gabriel,” “O Michael,” “O Abraham,” “O Moses,” or “O Messenger of Allah,” followed by “Forgive me,” “Have mercy on me,” “Provide for me,” “Grant me victory,” “Come to my aid,” “Protect me from my enemy,” or anything similar. Rather, all these are exclusive prerogatives of divinity.

These are weighty, well-known matters that scholars have explained, distinguishing between Allah’s rights, which belong exclusively to Him [rather than to] (1) the messengers, and the rights due to both Him and His messengers. He, glorified be He, distinguishes between these in such statements as:

“And support him and honour him, and glorify Him morning and evening.”

Supporting and honouring are due to the Messenger, whereas glorification morning and evening is due to Allah.

## Page 1165

And as He says:

“Whoever obeys Allah and His Messenger, fears Allah and is mindful of Him—it is they who will succeed.”

Obedience is due to Allah and His Messenger, whereas reverent fear and mindfulness are for Allah alone. Likewise, the messengers say:

“Worship Allah, be mindful of Him, and obey me.”

They thus direct worship and mindfulness to Allah alone, while requiring obedience to themselves. Allah Most High says:

“The mosques belong to Allah, so do not invoke anyone alongside Allah.”

“When the servant of Allah stood up to invoke Him, they almost crowded upon him.”

“Say: ‘I invoke only my Lord, and associate no one with Him.’”

“Say: ‘I have no power to harm you or guide you rightly.’”

“Say: ‘No one can protect me from Allah, nor will I find any refuge besides Him.’”

Allah Most High also says:

“Do not invoke another god alongside Allah, lest you be among those who are punished.”

Allah Most High says:

“Say: ‘Invoke those whom you claim to be gods besides Allah. They possess not an atom’s weight in the heavens or on earth, nor do they have any share in either, nor does He have any helper among them.’”

“Intercession does not avail with Him except for one whom He permits.”

Allah Most High says:

“Who can intercede with Him except by His permission?”

Allah Most High says:

“Say: ‘Invoke those whom you claim to be gods besides Him. They have no power to remove your affliction or shift it elsewhere.’”

“Those whom they invoke themselves seek a means of drawing near to their Lord, striving to be nearest to Him. They hope for His mercy and fear His punishment. Indeed, your Lord’s punishment is something to beware of.”

Allah Most High says:

“They have taken their rabbis and monks as lords besides Allah, and also the Messiah, son of Mary, although they were commanded only to worship one God. There is no god but Him. Glory be to Him, far above whatever they associate with Him.”

Allah Most High says:

“It is not for a human being whom Allah has given the Scripture, judgment and prophethood to then say to people, ‘Be my worshippers rather than Allah’s.’ Rather, ‘Be devoted scholars of the Lord because you have taught the Scripture and because you have been

---

## Page 1166

studying.’”

“Nor would he command you to take the angels and prophets as lords. Would he command you to disbelieve after you have submitted to Allah?”

Thus, whoever takes the angels and prophets as lords has disbelieved after embracing Islam, by the consensus of the Muslims. For this reason, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade building mosques over graves and setting up any rival to Allah in matters exclusive to His lordship.

It is reported from him in the two Sahīh collections that he said:

“May Allah curse the Jews and Christians: they took the graves of their prophets as places of worship.”

He was warning against what they had done. It is also reported from him in the Sahīh collection that he said:

“Those who came before you used to take graves as places of worship. Beware! Do not take graves as places of worship, for I forbid you to do so.”

It is reported from him in the Sunan collections that he said:

“Do not make my grave a place of recurring gatherings.”

It has also been reported from him that he said:

“O Allah, do not make my grave an idol that is worshipped.”

A man said to him, “Whatever Allah wills and you will.” He replied, “Have you made me a rival to Allah? Say: ‘Whatever Allah alone wills.’”

For this reason, the scholars have said that whoever visits the grave of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, should neither touch it reverentially nor kiss it, nor liken the dwelling of a created being to the House of the Creator, whose Black Stone corner is touched and kissed, while its Yemeni Corner is touched. Accordingly, the scholars agree that kissing or reverentially touching any stone is not prescribed—with the exception of the two Yemeni corners. Even the “Station of Abraham” in Mecca is neither kissed nor rubbed. How much more, then, does this apply to other stations and shrines?

---

## Page 1167

And when you mentioned this that day, I told you, “This is one of the fundamentals of Islam.” If the judge cannot distinguish between the religion of Islam and the religion of the Christians who invoke the Messiah and his mother, then what am I to do?

But someone who takes Nafīsah as a lord and says, “She grants refuge to the fearful and comes to the aid of the distressed, and I am in her care,” prostrates to her, pleads with her in supplication as one pleads with the Lord of the earth and the heavens, and relies upon someone once alive who has died rather than upon the True One who never dies—such a person would undoubtedly be all the more ready to associate someone superior to her with Allah.

Allah Most High says:

“Say, ‘In whose hand is the dominion of all things—and He grants protection, while none can grant protection against Him—if you know?’”

“They will say, ‘Allah’s.’ Say, ‘Then how are you deluded?’”

There is also the hadith about Mu‘ādh: when he returned from Syria, he prostrated to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. The Prophet asked, “What is this, Mu‘ādh?” He replied, “I saw them in Syria prostrating to their bishops, and they say this practice was handed down from their prophets.” He said, “Mu‘ādh, tell me: if you passed by my grave, would you prostrate to it?” He replied, “No.” He said, “Then do not prostrate to me. If I were to command anyone to prostrate to another, I would command a woman to prostrate to her husband.”

How can someone who does not forbid those who have gone astray from engaging in such association of partners with Allah—forbidden by the consensus of the Muslims—forbid something less serious? Whoever invokes a man or woman besides Allah resembles those who take the Messiah and his mother as two gods besides Allah. And in the Sahīh collection, there is a report from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace,

---

## Page 1168

that he said:

“Do not praise me excessively as the Christians praised Jesus, son of Mary. I am only a servant, so say: ‘The servant of Allah and His Messenger.’”

Indeed, whoever permits invoking a created being while forbidding supplication to the Creator—which affirms His being the One upon whom all depend and His divinity—has contradicted “Islam” in both its negation and its affirmation: the testimony that there is no god but Allah.

As for the rights of Allah’s Messenger—may my father and mother be sacrificed for him—such as loving him more than oneself, one’s family and one’s wealth; supporting, revering and venerating him; obeying him and following his sunnah, and other such duties, they are very great indeed. [Likewise, seeking a means of approach to Allah through him in supplication is prescribed, as in the hadith reported by al-Tirmidhī and graded authentic by him, in which the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, taught someone to say:

“O Allah, I ask You and seek a means of approach to You through Your Prophet Muhammad, the Prophet of mercy. O Muhammad, O Messenger of Allah, I seek a means of approach through you to my Lord concerning my need, that He may fulfill it. O Allah, accept his intercession for me.”

Such seeking a means of approach through him is good] (*).

As for invoking him and seeking his aid, that is forbidden. The distinction between these two is agreed upon among the Muslims. Someone seeking a means of approach invokes Allah alone, addresses Him and asks of Him. He does not call upon anyone else except by way of evoking his presence, not by way of requesting anything from him. By contrast, someone who invokes another and seeks his aid asks the one invoked, makes requests of him, seeks his aid and places his reliance upon him. And Allah is the Lord of the worlds

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd states (p. 24):
> Here the shaykh, may Allah have mercy on him, discusses tawassul (seeking a means of approach to Allah) in general terms. He treats it in detail at the end of volume one, in al-Tawassul wa-l-Wasīla (Seeking a Means of Approach and the Means of Approach).

---

## Page 1169

and the Sovereign of all dominion and Creator of all things. He answers the distressed when they call upon Him; He is near and answers the supplicant when he calls upon Him; and He hears all supplication. Glorified and exalted is He, far above what the wrongdoers say.

I have written a substantial book entitled “The Unsheathed Sword against One Who Insults the Messenger,” in which I discussed points concerning this issue that, to my knowledge, no one had addressed before. Likewise, I have written chapters on these “principles of faith” that are among the most beneficial writings on matters of religion.

The shaykh should also be informed that I fear the affair may pass entirely beyond his control and involve things harmful to him, Ibn Makhlūf, and others like them. For he has asked me for something that could be used to bring that about, but I have not agreed to it. I am always the same. By Allah, I have never deceived either of them; had I deceived them, I would have concealed it. I am ready to assist them both in every act of righteousness and piety.

There is no doubt that the foundation for setting matters right is for each person to return to Allah and repent to Him during these blessed ten days. When inward intentions are sound, Allah sets outward affairs right. Indeed, Allah is with those who fear Him and those who do good. This is a major affair: the more it comes to light, the more widely it spreads.

Peace be upon you, and Allah’s mercy and blessings. All praise belongs to Allah alone. May Allah bless Muhammad and his family and grant them abundant peace.

## Page 1170

Shaykh al-Islām Taqī al-Dīn Ahmad ibn Taymiyyah—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

In the name of Allah, the Most Compassionate, the Most Merciful.

Allah, exalted and holy, said:

“O you who believe, fear Allah as He should be feared, and do not die except as Muslims.”

“And hold firmly to the rope of Allah all together, and do not become divided. Remember Allah’s favor upon you: you were enemies, then He brought your hearts together, and by His favor you became brothers. You were on the brink of a pit of Fire, and He saved you from it. Thus Allah makes His signs clear to you, so that you may be guided.”

“Let there be among you a group who call to goodness, enjoin what is right, and forbid what is wrong. It is they who will prosper.”

“And do not be like those who became divided and differed after clear proofs had come to them. For them there will be a tremendous punishment.”

“On the Day when some faces will turn white and some faces will turn black.”

Ibn ‘Abbās and others said: The faces of the people of the Sunnah and the community will turn white, while the faces of the people of innovation and division will turn black.

“As for those whose faces turn black: ‘Did you disbelieve after having believed? Then taste the punishment for your disbelief.’”

“But as for those whose faces turn white, they will be within Allah’s mercy, remaining there forever.”

---

## Page 1171

Al-Tirmidhī also relates from Abū Umāmah al-Bāhilī that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said concerning the Khawārij:

“They are the dogs of the inhabitants of the Fire.”

Abū Umāmah recited this verse:

“On the Day when some faces will turn white and some faces will turn black.”

Imam Ahmad ibn Hanbal said: “The hadith concerning the Khawārij has been authentically transmitted through ten routes.” Muslim included these reports in his Sahīh, and al-Bukhārī included some of them. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Any one of you would consider his prayer insignificant compared with theirs, his fasting insignificant compared with theirs, and his recitation insignificant compared with theirs. They recite the Quran, but it does not go beyond their throats. They pass out of Islam as an arrow passes through its quarry.”

In another narration:

“They kill the people of Islam and leave the idolaters alone.”

The Khawārij were the first to declare Muslims disbelievers. They declare people disbelievers on account of sins, and they declare anyone who disagrees with their innovation a disbeliever, considering it lawful to shed his blood and seize his property. Such is the way of the people of innovation: they introduce an innovation and declare anyone who disagrees with it a disbeliever. The people of the Sunnah and the community, however, follow the Book and the Sunnah and obey Allah and His Messenger. Thus, they follow the truth and show mercy to creation.

The first innovations to arise in Islam were those of the Khawārij and the Shī‘ah. Both arose during the caliphate of the Commander of the Faithful, ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib, and he punished both groups. As for the Khawārij, they fought against him, and he killed them. As for the Shī‘ah, he burned their extremists with fire and sought to have ‘Abd Allāh ibn Saba’ put to death, but he fled from him. He also ordered that anyone who considered him superior to Abū Bakr and ‘Umar be flogged.

It has been related from him through numerous routes that he said:

“The best of this community after its Prophet is Abū Bakr, then ‘Umar.”

Al-Bukhārī also related this from him in his Sahīh.

---

## Page 1172

Section

Among the principles of the people of the Sunnah and the community is that they perform the Friday prayers, the Eid prayers, and the congregational prayers. They do not abandon the Friday and congregational prayers as the people of innovation, such as the Rāfidah and others, have done.

If an imam’s inward condition is unknown, but no innovation or sinful conduct has become apparent in him, one may perform the Friday and congregational prayers behind him, by agreement of the four imams and the other imams of the Muslims. None of the imams has said that prayer is permissible only behind someone whose inward condition is known. Rather, ever since the time of their Prophet, Muslims have continued to pray behind Muslims whose inward condition is unknown.

But if innovation or sinful conduct becomes apparent in the person leading the prayer, and it is possible to pray behind someone known to be an innovator or a sinful person, despite being able to pray behind someone else, most scholars regard the follower’s prayer as valid. This is the position of al-Shāfiʿī and Abū Hanīfah, and one of the two positions within the schools of Mālik and Ahmad.

If, however, it is only possible to pray behind an innovator or a sinful person—as in the case of a Friday prayer whose imam is an innovator or a sinful person, when no other Friday prayer is available—then this prayer is performed behind that innovator or sinful person according to the people of the Sunnah and the community generally. This is the position of al-Shāfiʿī, Abū Hanīfah, Ahmad ibn Hanbal, and the other imams of the people of the Sunnah, with no disagreement among them.

When deviant views became widespread, some people preferred to pray only behind someone they knew, regarding this as recommended. This has been reported from Ahmad, who mentioned it to someone who asked him. Ahmad did not say: “Prayer is valid only behind someone whose condition I know.”

---

## Page 1173

When Abū ʿAmr ʿUthmān ibn Marzūq arrived in Egypt, its rulers at that time openly professed Shiism and were heretical Bātinīs (esotericists). As a result, religious innovations had proliferated and become widespread in Egypt. He therefore instructed his followers to pray only behind those they knew. After his death, Sunni rulers such as Saladin conquered Egypt, and the teachings of the Sunnah, opposed to those of the Rāfidah, gained prominence there. Knowledge and the Sunnah then became increasingly widespread and manifest.

Praying behind someone whose inward condition is unknown is therefore permissible by agreement of the Muslim scholars. Whoever says that prayer behind someone whose condition is unknown is forbidden or invalid has opposed the consensus of the people of the Sunnah and the community.

The Companions, may Allah be pleased with them, used to pray behind people whose sinful conduct they knew. Thus, ʿAbd Allāh ibn Masʿūd and other Companions prayed behind al-Walīd ibn ʿUqbah ibn Abī Muʿayt, who drank wine and once prayed four units for the dawn prayer. ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān had him flogged for this. ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar and other Companions also prayed behind al-Hajjāj ibn Yūsuf. The Companions and the Successors prayed behind Ibn Abī ʿUbayd, who was suspected of heresy and called people to misguidance.

---

## Page 1174

Section

It is not permissible to declare a Muslim an unbeliever because of a sin he has committed or an error he has made, as in matters disputed within the Muslim community. Allah Most High says:

“The Messenger believes in what has been revealed to him from his Lord, and so do the believers. All believe in Allah, His angels, His Books, and His messengers: ‘We make no distinction between any of His messengers.’ And they say, ‘We hear and we obey. We seek Your forgiveness, our Lord, and to You is the final return.’”

It is established in an authentic hadith that Allah Most High answered this supplication and forgave the believers their errors.

The renegade Khārijites, whom the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, commanded Muslims to fight, were fought by the Commander of the Faithful, ʿAlī ibn Abī Tālib, one of the Rightly Guided Caliphs. The leading religious scholars among the Companions, the Successors, and those who came after them agreed that they should be fought. Yet ʿAlī ibn Abī Tālib, Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqās, and other Companions did not declare them unbelievers. Rather, they regarded them as Muslims despite fighting them. ʿAlī did not fight them until they shed blood unlawfully and raided the Muslims’ property. He fought them to repel their oppression and aggression, not because they were unbelievers. For this reason, he neither took their families captive nor seized their property as spoils.

If these people, whose misguidance is established by explicit scriptural texts and scholarly consensus, were not declared unbelievers despite Allah and His Messenger commanding that they be fought, then what of the differing groups who are uncertain about the truth in matters in which even those more learned than they have erred? Thus, it is not permissible for any of these

---

## Page 1175

groups to declare another an unbeliever or to regard the shedding of its members’ blood and the seizure of their property as lawful, even if that group unquestionably holds an innovation. How much more so if the group declaring it an unbeliever also holds innovations? The latter’s innovation may even be more grievous. In most cases, they are all ignorant of the true nature of the matters over which they differ.

The fundamental rule is that Muslims’ blood, property, and honour are inviolable among them, and may not be violated except by the permission of Allah and His Messenger. When the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, addressed them during the Farewell Pilgrimage, he said:

“Your blood, your property, and your honour are inviolable among you, just as this day of yours is sacred, in this city of yours, in this month of yours.”

He also said:

“Every aspect of a Muslim is inviolable to another Muslim: his blood, his property, and his honour.”

And he said:

“Whoever prays as we pray, faces our direction of prayer, and eats the meat we slaughter is a Muslim under the protection of Allah and His Messenger.”

He also said:

“When two Muslims confront one another with their swords, both the killer and the one killed will be in the Fire.” He was asked, “O Messenger of Allah, we understand about the killer, but why the one killed?” He replied, “He intended to kill his companion.”

And he said:

“Do not become unbelievers after me, striking one another’s necks.”

He also said:

“If a Muslim says to his brother, ‘You unbeliever,’ one of the two incurs that designation.”

All these hadiths are found in the authentic collections.

If a Muslim acts on an interpretation when fighting or declaring someone an unbeliever, he is not thereby declared an unbeliever. Thus, Umar ibn al-Khattāb said concerning Hātib ibn Abī Balta‘ah, “O Messenger of Allah, let me strike this hypocrite’s neck.” The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, replied, “He participated in the Battle of Badr. And how do you know? Perhaps Allah has looked upon

---

## Page 1176

the people of Badr and said, ‘Do whatever you wish, for I have forgiven you’?” This is reported in both Sahīh collections.

Both collections also relate, in the account of the slander:

“Usayd ibn al-Hudayr said to Sa'd ibn Ubādah, ‘You are a hypocrite, defending the hypocrites.’ The two groups quarrelled, and the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, reconciled them.”
