Thus, among these participants in the Battle of Badr were those who said to another of their number, “You are a hypocrite.” Yet the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, declared neither an unbeliever; rather, he testified that they would all enter Paradise.

Likewise, both Sahīh collections report:

“Usāmah ibn Zayd killed a man after he had said, ‘There is no god but Allah.’ When Usāmah told him, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, regarded this as a grave matter and said, ‘Usāmah, did you kill him after he had said, “There is no god but Allah”?’ He repeated this to him until Usāmah said, ‘I wished that I had not embraced Islam until that day.’”

Nevertheless, he did not require him to be executed in retaliation, to pay blood money, or to perform an expiation, because he had acted on an interpretation. He had thought it permissible to kill the man who uttered those words, believing that he had said them to protect himself.

Similarly, members of the early generations fought one another—those who participated in the Battle of the Camel and at Siffīn, and others like them—yet they were all Muslims and believers. As Allah Most High says:

“If two groups of believers fight one another, make peace between them. But if one of them transgresses against the other, fight the transgressing group until it returns to Allah’s command. Then, if it returns, make peace between them with justice and act equitably. Indeed, Allah loves those who act equitably.”

Allah Most High has thus made clear that, despite their fighting one another and some of them transgressing against others, they are brothers in faith. He commanded that peace be made between them with justice.

---

## Page 1177

For this reason, even while fighting one another, the early generations maintained their bonds of religious allegiance. They did not treat one another with the hostility shown to unbelievers. They accepted one another’s testimony, learned from one another, inherited from one another, intermarried, and dealt with one another as Muslims ordinarily do, despite the fighting, mutual cursing, and other disputes between them.

It is established in the Sahīh collection that:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, asked his Lord not to destroy his community through a widespread famine, and He granted him that. He asked Him not to give an enemy from outside their community power over them, and He granted him that. He asked Him not to let them fight one another, but that request was not granted.”

He also stated that Allah would not give an enemy from outside their community power to overcome them all until some of them began killing others and taking others captive. It is also established in both Sahīh collections that:

“When the words of Allah Most High were revealed, ‘Say: He has the power to send punishment upon you from above you,’ he said, ‘I seek refuge in Your Face.’ At the words, ‘or from beneath your feet,’ he said, ‘I seek refuge in Your Face.’ At the words, ‘or to divide you into factions and make you taste one another’s violence,’ he said, ‘These two are less severe.’”

This is so even though Allah commanded unity and concord and forbade religious innovation and division. He said:

“Indeed, those who have divided their religion and become sects—you have nothing to do with them.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Hold fast to the community, for Allah’s Hand is over the community.”

And he said: “Satan

---

## Page 1178

is with the lone individual, but is farther away from two.” He also said:

“Satan is a wolf to man, just as the wolf is to sheep; the wolf takes only those sheep that stray and wander far away.”

When a Muslim finds himself in a Muslim city, he must attend Friday and congregational prayers with its people, maintain allegiance to the believers, and not treat them as enemies. If he sees any of them misguided or wayward and can guide them and set them on the right course, he should do so. Otherwise, Allah does not burden any soul beyond its capacity. If he can appoint the best person to lead the Muslims in prayer, he should appoint him. If he can prevent someone who openly practises religious innovation and wickedness from doing so, he should prevent him.

If he cannot do that, it is preferable to pray behind the person most knowledgeable about Allah’s Book and His Prophet’s Sunnah and foremost in obedience to Allah and His Messenger. As the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said in an authentic hadith:

“The people should be led in prayer by the one best versed in Allah’s Book. If they are equal in recitation, then by the one most knowledgeable about the Sunnah. If they are equal in knowledge of the Sunnah, then by the one who emigrated earliest. If they emigrated at the same time, then by the oldest.”

If shunning someone who openly practises religious innovation and wickedness brings a preponderant benefit, he should shun him, just as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, shunned the three who were left behind until Allah accepted their repentance. But if someone else is appointed without his consent, and there is no religiously legitimate benefit in refraining from praying behind him, then missing these Friday and congregational prayers is ignorance and misguidance: he has countered one religious innovation with another.

Indeed, scholars have differed over whether someone who performs the Friday prayer behind a wicked person should repeat the prayer, and most have considered repeating it reprehensible. Ahmad ibn Hanbal even said, in the narration transmitted by Abdūs: “Whoever repeats it is an innovator.” This is the better-supported of the two views, because the Companions did not repeat their prayers when they prayed behind

---

## Page 1179

people of wickedness and religious innovation. Allah Most High has never commanded anyone to repeat a prayer performed as He commanded, to the best of that person’s ability. Thus, the sounder of the two scholarly views is that anyone who prays to the best of their ability need not repeat the prayer. This includes someone who performs dry ablution for fear of the cold, someone who has neither water nor earth and prays as their circumstances allow, a prisoner, and those with exceptional or common, persistent or intermittent excuses. None of them is required to repeat a prayer initially performed to the best of their ability.

It is established in an authentic report that the Companions prayed without either water or dry ablution when Ā’ishah lost her necklace, and the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not command them to repeat their prayers. More striking still, he did not command those who had omitted prayer out of ignorance of its obligation to make it up. When Amr and Ammār entered a state of major ritual impurity, Amr did not pray, while Ammār rolled in the dust as an animal does; he did not command either of them to make up the prayer. Nor did he command Abū Dharr to make up his prayers when he would enter a state of major ritual impurity and refrain from praying. Likewise, when a woman suffered severe, abnormal uterine bleeding that prevented her from praying and fasting, he did not command her to make them up.

Nor did he command those who ate during Ramadan until they could distinguish a white cord from a black cord to make up their fasts. They had misunderstood the verse, thinking that the words of Allah Most High,

“Until the white thread of dawn becomes distinct to you from the black thread,”

referred to an actual cord. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, therefore said:

“It means the blackness of night and the whiteness of day.”

He did not command them to make up their fasts. Nor did he command the man who prayed incorrectly to repeat his earlier prayers. And those who prayed towards Jerusalem in Mecca, Abyssinia, and elsewhere after that direction of prayer had been abrogated (by the command to pray towards the Kaaba, and continued praying towards the Rock until they learned of

---

## Page 1180

the abrogation, he did not command them to repeat the prayers they had performed, although they had greater grounds for excuse than others because they were adhering to an abrogated religious law.

Scholars have differed over whether the rulings conveyed by Allah and His Messenger apply to His servants before those rulings reach them. There are three views within the school of Ahmad and elsewhere: some hold that they apply; others hold that they do not; and others hold that newly instituted rulings apply, but abrogating rulings do not. The correct view is that indicated by the Quran in the words of Allah Most High:

“And We never punish until We have sent a messenger.”

And His words:

“So that people will have no argument against Allah after the messengers.”

The two Sahīh collections also report that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“No one loves giving people due warning more than Allah; for that reason He sent the messengers as bearers of good tidings and warners.”

Thus, someone acting on a mistaken interpretation, or someone whose ignorance is excusable, is not judged in the same way as someone who knowingly defies the truth or acts wickedly. Rather, Allah has appointed a measure for everything.

---

## Page 1181

Section:

Muslims unanimously affirm the testimony that there is no deity worthy of worship except Allah and that Muhammad is the Messenger of Allah. They affirm that this is true, holding it with firm conviction and certainty, without any doubt. Whatever a Muslim knows and firmly believes, he holds with certainty, even though Allah is able to change it. A Muslim is certain of what he sees and hears, and certain that Allah is able to do whatever He wills. When a Muslim says, “I am certain of that,” he does not mean that Allah is unable to change it. Rather, anyone who says that Allah is unable to do such things as cause His creatures to die and raise them alive from their graves, move the mountains, or replace the earth with another earth must be called upon to repent; if he does not repent, he is put to death.

Those among the followers of Abū Amr ibn Marzūq who dislike the expression “certainty” introduced this objection themselves. The shaykh himself did not object to this expression. The origin of the matter, however, was their practice of qualifying their profession of faith, as has been reported from the early generations. One of them would say, “I am a believer, if Allah wills.” They would likewise qualify statements concerning righteous deeds, saying, “I have prayed, if Allah wills.”

The early generations intended this qualification for one of several reasons. A person might not be certain that he had fulfilled the obligation as Allah and His Messenger had commanded, and thus be unsure whether Allah would accept it, so he would add the qualification. Alternatively, he might be uncertain about his ultimate outcome. Or he might add it because all things occur only by Allah’s will, as in the words of Allah Most High:

“You will surely enter

---

## Page 1182

the Sacred Mosque, if Allah wills,” even though Allah knew that they would enter it, without any doubt. Another reason for this qualification was to avoid declaring oneself righteous.

Those earlier people refrained from expressing certainty in matters of this kind. Then ignorant people came after them and disapproved of the word “certainty” in every matter, narrating fabricated hadiths to support this. Anyone who reports that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, his Companions, or any Muslim scholar disapproved of the word “certainty” in matters known with certainty has made a false attribution. These people came to imagine that accepting this expression meant accepting something momentous in religion. This is ignorance and misguidance on their part; no group of Muslims had preceded them in this. Neither their shaykh, Abū Amr ibn Marzūq, nor his followers during his lifetime, nor the best of his followers after his death rejected this expression outright. Only a group of their ignorant followers did so.

Likewise, another group claimed that Allah does not accept the repentance of anyone who insults the Companions, even if he repents. They attributed to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, the saying:

“Insulting my Companions is a sin that will not be forgiven.”

This hadith is falsely attributed to the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. No scholar has narrated it, nor is it found in any of the authoritative books of the Muslims. It also contradicts the Quran, for Allah said:

“Indeed, Allah does not forgive associating partners with Him, but He forgives what is less than that for whom He wills.”

This concerns those who have not repented. Concerning those who repent, He said:

“Say: O My servants who have transgressed against themselves, do not despair

---

## Page 1183

of Allah’s mercy. Indeed, Allah forgives all sins. Indeed, He is the All-Forgiving, the Most Merciful.”

Thus, Allah’s Book and the Sunnah of His Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, establish that Allah accepts the repentance of everyone who repents. It is well known that if an unbeliever at war with the Messenger insults him, saying that he is a magician, a poet, a madman, someone taught by another, or a fabricator, and then repents, Allah accepts his repentance.

Some of those at war with the Prophet used to insult him, then embraced Islam and became good Muslims, and the Prophet accepted their conversion. Among them were Abū Sufyān ibn al-Hārith ibn Abd al-Muttalib, the Prophet’s cousin, and Abd Allah ibn Saʿd ibn Abī Sarh. The latter had apostatized and used to make false claims about the Prophet, saying, “I used to teach him the Quran.” He then repented and embraced Islam, and the Prophet accepted his pledge of allegiance on that basis.

If it is said, “Insulting the Companions is a matter of rights owed to individuals,” the reply is: Someone who regards insulting them as permissible, such as a Rāfidī, considers it part of his religion, just as an unbeliever considers insulting the Prophet part of his religion. If he repents and comes to love them, praise them, and pray for them, Allah erases his evil deeds through his good deeds. Likewise, if someone wrongs another person by slandering, backbiting, or insulting him, and then repents, Allah accepts his repentance.

However, if the wronged person knows about it, he must allow him to claim his due. If he slandered or backbit him and the matter has not reached him, the scholars hold two views, both reported from Ahmad. The stronger of the two is that he should not tell him, “I backbit you.” It has also been said that he should instead do good to him in his absence, just as he wronged him in his absence. As al-Hasan al-Basrī said:

“The expiation for backbiting is to ask forgiveness for the person you have backbitten.”

Thus, if a man has insulted the Companions or anyone else and then repents, he should do good to them by praying for them and praising them in proportion to

---

## Page 1184

the harm he has done them. Good deeds erase evil deeds.

Likewise, if an unbeliever who used to insult the Prophet and call him a liar repents, testifies that Muhammad is Allah’s Messenger, who speaks the truth and is told the truth, and comes to love him, praise him, and invoke blessings upon him, his good deeds erase his evil deeds. Allah Most High

“accepts repentance from His servants and pardons evil deeds, and He knows what you do.”

He Most High has also said:

“Hā Mīm. The revelation of the Book is from Allah, the Almighty, the All-Knowing, the Forgiver of sins and Accepter of repentance, severe in punishment, abounding in bounty. There is no deity except Him; to Him is the final return.”

May Allah bless Muhammad and his Companions and grant them peace.

---

## Page 1185

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah sanctify his soul—was asked:

(*)

Is it permissible to discuss questions concerning the fundamentals of religion that people have debated, but about which no statement has been transmitted from our master Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, or not?

If this is deemed permissible, on what basis? For we have understood from him, peace be upon him, that he prohibited discussion of certain questions.

If it is deemed permissible, is it also obligatory? Has anything been transmitted from him, peace be upon him, that entails its being obligatory? Is a strongly probable conclusion reached by a scholar exercising independent reasoning sufficient in this regard, or must he attain certainty? If he cannot attain certainty, is he excused, or does he remain obligated to attain it? Under these circumstances, does this amount to imposing an obligation beyond a person’s capacity, or not?

If it is deemed obligatory, what is the wisdom behind the absence of an explicit text from the Lawgiver on this matter that would safeguard people from falling into ruinous pitfalls, when he, peace be upon him, was so eager to guide his community? And Allah knows best.

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd stated (p. 26):
> This fatwa appears in al-Fatāwā al-Kubrā, 1:373; the marginal notes to Mukhtasar al-Misriyya, 1:213–241; and Dar’ al-Ta‘ārud, 1:25–78.
> 
> Before this fatwa, the Shaykh—may Allah have mercy on him—stated in al-Dar’:
> “When I was in Egypt, some of its learned men asked me about this matter. They said in their question”—he then quoted the question and the fatwa.
> 
> In al-Majmū‘, the fatwa ends at 3:326 with his words:
> “We have discussed this matter at length elsewhere and addressed the false premise assumed by the divided groups: that affirming the true meaning they call jabr (compulsion) is incompatible with commands and prohibitions. The Qadariyya thus deemed it incompatible with commands and prohibitions altogether. A group among the Jabriyya deemed it incompatible with the goodness and badness of acts, relying on this to deny the goodness and badness inherent in an act and discernible by reason. It is well known that it is no more incompatible with this than with an act’s being congenial and beneficial to its agent, and its being adverse and harmful to him.”
> 
> Some questions raised here by the questioner remain unanswered in al-Fatāwā. Their answers appear in al-Dar’, from 1:72, line 5, through p. 78, line 7.

---

## Page 1186

He replied:

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of the worlds.

As for the first question:

The questioner asks: “Is it permissible to discuss questions concerning the fundamentals of religion that people have debated, but about which no statement has been transmitted from our master Muhammad, or not?” This question reflects the false, innovated formulations that have become familiar.

As for questions that truly belong to the fundamentals of religion—those that deserve to be called its fundamentals, meaning the religion with which Allah sent His Messenger and revealed His Book—it is not permissible to say that no statement concerning them has been transmitted from the Prophet. Indeed, such a claim is inherently contradictory. Their being fundamentals of religion necessarily means that they are among its most important matters and that religion requires them. To deny that any statement concerning them has been transmitted from the Messenger therefore entails one of two things: either the Messenger neglected important matters essential to religion and did not explain them, or he explained them but the community did not transmit them. Both possibilities are categorically false. This is among the gravest attacks that hypocrites make against religion. Only someone ignorant of the realities of what the Messenger brought, or ignorant of what people grasp with their hearts, or ignorant of both, would entertain this and similar notions.

His ignorance of the first leaves him unaware of the fundamentals and subsidiary matters of religion contained in what the Messenger brought.

His ignorance of the second leads him to include among truths apprehended by reason what he himself calls

---

## Page 1187

and others like him call rational truths, whereas they are nothing but ignorant notions.

His ignorance of both leads him to regard certain unsound questions and means as fundamentals of religion when they are not, and to suppose that the Messenger did not explain what ought to be believed concerning these matters. This is indeed the case among various groups of people—even their most proficient members, let alone ordinary people. For the fundamentals of religion consist either of matters that must be believed and affirmed in word, or in both word and deed—such as questions concerning Allah’s oneness, His attributes, divine decree, prophethood, and the life to come—or of the evidence for these matters.

As for the first category:

Everything that people need to know, believe, and accept as true concerning these matters has been explained by Allah and His Messenger in a thorough manner that leaves no excuse. These are among the most important matters that the Messenger conveyed clearly and explained to people, and among the most important matters concerning which Allah established proof against His servants through the messengers who explained and conveyed them. Allah’s Book, whose wording and meanings the Companions and then their Successors transmitted from the Messenger, together with the wisdom—that is, the Sunnah of Allah’s Messenger—which they likewise transmitted from him, encompasses these matters to the fullest extent intended, including all that is obligatory and recommended.

All praise is due to Allah, who sent us a Messenger from among ourselves, reciting His verses to us, purifying us, and teaching us the Book and wisdom; who perfected our religion for us, completed His favour upon us, and approved Islam as our religion; who sent down the Book as a detailed explanation of all things, and as guidance, mercy, and glad tidings

---

## Page 1188

for the Muslims.

“It is not a fabricated account, but a confirmation of what came before it, a detailed explanation of all things, and guidance and mercy for people who believe.”

Only those deficient in their reasoning and listening suppose that the Book and wisdom do not explain these matters—those to whom the words of the people of the Fire apply in some measure:

“If only we had listened or reasoned, we would not be among the inhabitants of the Blaze.”

This deficiency is nevertheless widespread among many who engage in philosophy and speculative theology, and among the ignorant adherents of hadith, jurisprudence, and Sufism.

As for the second category—the evidence for these fundamental questions—some groups of speculative theologians and philosophers suppose that revelation provides evidence only through truthful reports. Its evidentiary force, they argue, therefore depends on knowing that the one conveying it is truthful, and they regard the grounds for establishing his truthfulness as purely rational propositions. They have made a grave error in this; indeed, they have plainly gone astray in supposing that the Book and Sunnah provide evidence only through reports alone.

Rather, the truth is what the early generations of this community and its leading scholars—people of knowledge and faith—held: Allah, glorified and exalted is He, has explained the rational evidence needed to understand these matters to an extent that none of these people properly appreciates. The utmost that they present is distilled in the Quran in the finest manner. This includes the illustrative examples that Allah Most High sets forth in His Book, concerning which He says:

“We have certainly set forth for people in this Quran every kind of example.”

These illustrative examples are “rational inferences,” whether syllogistic or analogical. They include what they call demonstrative proofs, namely

---

## Page 1189

syllogistic inference constructed from premises known with certainty, although the word “proof” has a broader linguistic meaning, as when Allah called the two signs of Moses two proofs.

This is further clarified by the fact that, in inquiry concerning Allah, one may not reason by an analogy that treats the original case and the case inferred from it as equal, nor by a syllogism that treats its instances alike. For Allah, glorified and exalted is He, has nothing like Him. He therefore cannot be likened to anything else, nor can He and anything else be subsumed under a universal proposition that treats all its instances alike. Thus, when groups of philosophers and speculative theologians employed such inferences in theological questions, they did not attain certainty through them. Rather, their arguments contradicted one another, and ultimately bewilderment and confusion prevailed among them, because they perceived their arguments to be unsound or to counterbalance one another.

Instead, a fortiori reasoning is employed in these matters, whether analogical or syllogistic, as Allah Most High says:

“To Allah belongs the highest description.”

For example, we know that every perfection established for a contingent being or one that has come into existence, provided it involves no deficiency whatsoever—that is, a perfection of an existent being that does not entail nonexistence—belongs all the more fittingly to the One whose existence is necessary and who has no beginning. Every perfection involving no deficiency whatsoever, whose kind is found in a created being—subject to lordship, caused, and governed—has been received solely from its Creator, Lord, and Governor. He is therefore more deserving of that perfection than the creature.

We also know that every intrinsic deficiency or defect—that is, anything involving the absence of such perfection—must, if it is necessarily denied of any kind of created, originated, or contingent being, be denied all the more of the Lord, blessed and exalted is He. He is more deserving of qualities of existence than any existent being, whereas qualities involving absence or nonexistence are more fittingly ascribed to contingent beings, and so forth.

---

## Page 1190

Such methods were used by the early Muslim generations and the leading scholars in addressing questions of this kind, as Imam Ahmad and the leading scholars of Islam before and after him employed similar methods. The Quran likewise uses such reasoning to establish the “fundamentals of religion,” including questions of Allah’s oneness, His attributes, the resurrection, and related matters.

An example is that when He, glorified is He, announced the resurrection—knowledge of which depends on knowing that it is possible, since what is impossible cannot occur—He demonstrated its possibility with the utmost clarity. He did not follow the approach of “groups of speculative theologians,” who establish possibility in reality merely on the basis of possibility in thought. They say: “This is possible because, if its existence were assumed, that assumption would entail nothing impossible.” Yet this very premise is at issue: how is it known that assuming its existence would entail nothing impossible? What is impossible here includes both what is impossible in itself and what is impossible owing to something else.

Possibility in thought is, in fact, the absence of knowledge that something is impossible. Not knowing that something is impossible does not entail knowing that it is possible in reality. Rather, it remains in the mind as something neither known to be impossible nor known to be possible in reality. This is possibility in thought.

Allah, glorified and exalted is He, did not content Himself with this when demonstrating the possibility of resurrection. Something may be impossible—even if owing to something else—although the mind does not know it to be impossible. Possibility in reality is different: once it is known, the thing cannot be impossible.

A person knows that something is possible in reality sometimes by knowing that it exists, sometimes by knowing that something comparable to it exists, and sometimes by knowing that something greater than it exists. For the existence of something is evidence that what is less than it is all the more possible.

---

## Page 1191

Then, once He has shown that something is possible, He must also make clear the Lord’s power to bring it about. Otherwise, merely knowing that it is possible does not suffice to establish that it can occur unless the Lord’s power to bring it about is also known.

He, glorified is He, explained all this in statements such as:

“Do they not see that Allah, who created the heavens and the earth, is able to create the like of them? He has appointed for them a term about which there is no doubt, yet the wrongdoers refuse anything but disbelief.”

And His statement:

“Is not He who created the heavens and the earth able to create the like of them? Yes indeed! He is the Creator, the All-Knowing.”

And His statement:

“Do they not see that Allah, who created the heavens and the earth and was not wearied by their creation, is able to give life to the dead? Yes indeed! He has power over all things.”

And His statement:

“The creation of the heavens and the earth is greater than the creation of mankind.”

For it is self-evident to reason that creating the heavens and the earth is greater than creating beings like the children of Adam, and involves greater power. Thus, this easier act is all the more possible and within His power.

Likewise, He demonstrates this by referring to the first creation, in statements such as:

“He is the One who originates creation, then repeats it, and that is easier for Him.”

This is why He then says:

“To Him belongs the highest description in the heavens and the earth.”

He also says:

“If you are in doubt about the resurrection, then indeed We created you from dust”—to the end of the verse.

Likewise, He says:

“He presents an example to Us and forgets his own creation. He says, ‘Who will give life to bones when they have crumbled away?’”

“Say, ‘He who created them the first time will give them life’”—and the following verses.

For His statement, exalted is He, “Who will give life to bones when they have crumbled away?” constitutes a syllogism in which one of the two premises has been omitted because it is obvious. The other is a universal negative, accompanied by its supporting argument. This is the example presented, which He mentions in His statement:

---

## Page 1192

“He presents an example to Us and forgets his own creation. He says, ‘Who will give life to bones when they have crumbled away?’”

This is a rhetorical question expressing denial: that is, no one can give life to bones when they have crumbled away. In his view, their crumbled state prevents their being brought to life because they have become dry and cold—conditions incompatible with life, which rests upon heat and moisture—and because their parts have scattered and mingled with other things, along with similar objections.

The argument, stated in full, is: these bones have crumbled away; no one can give life to bones when they have crumbled away; therefore, no one can give life to them. But this negative proposition is false: it asserts that bringing them to life is impossible. He, glorified is He, demonstrated its possibility in several ways, showing that what is even harder to conceive is possible and within His power. Thus, He said:

“He who created them the first time will give them life.”

He had created them from dust. Then He said:

“And He has knowledge of every creation.”

This makes clear His knowledge of the parts that have scattered and undergone transformation. Then He said:

“He who made fire for you from the green tree.”

He thus explained that He brought forth hot, dry fire from something cold and moist. This involves an even greater opposition, for heat and moisture combine more readily than heat and dryness: moisture can be affected in ways that dryness cannot.

Then He said:

“Is not He who created the heavens and the earth able to create the like of them?”

This is a self-evident premise. Hence, He expressed it as a question calling for acknowledgment, indicating that this is already firmly established and known to the person addressed. As He, glorified is He, said:

“They bring you no example without Our bringing you the truth and the best explanation.”

Then He explained His all-encompassing power by saying:

“His command, when He wills something, is only to say to it, ‘Be,’ and it is.”

---

## Page 1193

This passage and others in the Quran contain profound insights and demonstrations of conclusive proofs concerning matters of religion that cannot be discussed here. The purpose is merely to draw attention to them.

The same applies to the arguments He, glorified is He, employs to declare Himself free and exalted above the begetting they attribute to Him, whether they call it physical or intellectual. Thus, the Christians claim that the Word—which they make the substance of the Son—was begotten of Him. Likewise, the Sabian philosophers claim that the ten intellects and the nine celestial souls were begotten of Him, although they are inconsistent concerning these: are they substances or accidents? They may treat the intellects as males and the souls as females, regarding them as their fathers and mothers, their gods and their proximate lords.

Their knowledge of the souls is more clearly established because circular motion exists, indicating voluntary motion, which in turn indicates a soul that imparts motion. Most of them, however, consider the celestial soul an accident, not a self-subsisting substance. This resembles the assertion of the Arab polytheists and others who attributed sons and daughters to Him. He, exalted is He, said:

“They have made the jinn partners with Allah, although He created them, and have falsely attributed to Him sons and daughters without knowledge. Glorified and exalted is He above what they ascribe to Him.”

He, exalted is He, also said:

“Indeed, out of their falsehood they say, ‘Allah has begotten offspring.’ They are surely liars.”

They used to say that the angels were Allah’s daughters, just as these men claim that the intellects—or the intellects and souls—“are the angels” and are begotten of Allah. Allah, exalted is He, therefore said:

“They attribute daughters to Allah—glorified is He!—while for themselves they have what they desire.

“When one of them is given news of a female child, his face darkens, and he is filled with suppressed grief.

“He hides from his people because of the bad news he has received. Should he keep her in humiliation or bury her in the dust? How evil is their judgment!

“Those who do not believe in the Hereafter have an evil description, while Allah’s description is

---

## Page 1194

the highest, and He is the Almighty, the Wise.” He continues to His words:

“They attribute to Allah what they themselves dislike, while their tongues falsely claim that the best reward will be theirs. Without doubt, the Fire will be theirs, and they will be abandoned therein.”

He, exalted is He, also said:

“Or has He taken daughters from among His creation and chosen sons for you? When one of them is given news of what he has attributed to the Most Merciful, his face darkens, and he is filled with suppressed grief. Is one brought up amid ornaments and unable to argue clearly in a dispute [to be attributed to Allah]? They have made the angels, who are servants of the Most Merciful, females. Did they witness their creation? Their testimony will be recorded, and they will be questioned.”

He, exalted is He, also said:

“Have you considered al-Lāt and al-ʿUzzā?”

He continues to His words:

“Is the male for you and the female for Him? That, then, is an unfair division.”

That is, an unjust division. There are other such passages in the Quran.

He, glorified is He, thus makes clear that the Lord, the Creator, has a greater right than you to be declared free of imperfections. How, then, can you attribute to Him what you dislike for yourselves and are unsettled to have attributed to you—even though it inevitably occurs among you—yet fail to declare Him free of it and deny it of Him? He has a greater right than you to be declared free of objectionable attributes that imply imperfection.

Likewise, He says concerning His oneness:

“He sets forth for you an example drawn from yourselves: do you have, among those whom your right hands possess, any partners who share equally with you in what We have provided for you, so that you fear them as you fear one another?”

That is, as some of you fear others. The same usage occurs in His words:

“Yet here you are, killing one another,”

and in His words:

“Why, when you heard it, did believing men and women not think well of one another?”

and in His words:

“Do not defame one another,”

and in His words:

“So repent to your Maker and kill one another,”

and in His words:

“Do not drive one another out of your homes,”

continuing to His words:

“Yet here you are, killing one another.”

The intended meaning is of the same kind in all these passages. He, glorified is He, thus makes clear that a created being does not have his slave as a partner in what he possesses, such that he fears his slave as he fears his equal

---

## Page 1195

—rather, you refuse to allow a slave to be your equal. How, then, can you be content to make what I have created and own a partner to Me, invoked and worshipped as I am invoked and worshipped? This is like what they used to say in their talbiyah: “Here I am at Your service. You have no partner except a partner who belongs to You: You own him and whatever he owns.” This is an exceedingly vast and important subject, but this is not the place to discuss it.

The purpose here is simply to point out that the Quran and Prophetic wisdom contain the main foundations of religion, both the doctrinal questions and the proofs that rightly deserve to be considered its foundations. The falsehoods that some people include under this designation are not foundations of religion, even if they place them under that heading. These include false doctrinal positions and invalid proofs: positions such as denying the divine attributes and divine decree, and proofs such as arguing that the world came into existence because the “accidents”—the attributes that subsist in bodies—came into existence, whether these are the akwān or other accidents.

This includes establishing the premises required by that argument: first, establishing the existence of “accidents,” which are attributes, or of “some of them,” such as the akwān—motion, rest, conjunction, and separation; second, “establishing that they came into existence” by refuting the claim that they become manifest after being latent, and refuting their transfer from one locus to another; third, establishing “the impossibility of a body being devoid” either of any class of accidents—by proving that a body is capable of possessing them and that whatever is capable of possessing something cannot lack both it and its opposite—or of the akwān; and fourth, establishing “the impossibility of events having no beginning.” This rests on two premises:

---

## Page 1196

the first is that a body cannot be devoid of “accidents,” which are attributes; and the second is that whatever cannot be devoid of “attributes,” which are accidents, must have come into existence, because attributes—which are accidents—can only be things that have come into existence. They may posit this concerning certain attributes, or accidents, such as the akwān. Whatever cannot be devoid of events as a class must itself have come into existence, since an infinite series of events is impossible.

It is self-evident that Muhammad did not use this method to call people to acknowledge the Creator and the prophethood of His prophets. For this reason, even accomplished speculative theologians, such as al-Ashʿarī and others, have acknowledged that it is not the method of the messengers and their followers, nor of the early generations of the Muslim community and its leading scholars. They have stated that those authorities regarded it as forbidden. Indeed, scholars who have critically examined it hold that it is an invalid method, and that its premises require distinctions and qualifications that prevent it from establishing the claim unconditionally.

Thus, anyone who relies on it for the foundations of his religion inevitably faces one of two outcomes. Either he discovers its weakness and compares it with the arguments of those who maintain that the world has always existed, so that he finds the arguments evenly balanced, or favours one side at one time and the other at another—as is the case with various groups among them—or he accepts, for its sake, implications known to be unsound according to both revelation and reason. Thus, for its sake, Jahm accepted the annihilation of Paradise and Hell, and Abū al-Hudhayl accepted the cessation of the movements of the inhabitants of Paradise. Others, such as al-Ashʿarī and those like him, accepted for its sake that water, air, and fire have taste, colour, smell, and similar attributes.

For the sake of this method and other considerations, some accepted that all “accidents,” such as taste, colour, and others, cannot persist at all, because they needed to answer the objection

---

## Page 1197

raised against them when they affirmed Allah’s attributes while arguing from the attributes of bodies that those bodies must have come into existence.

They said: The attributes of “bodies” are accidents—that is, they occur and disappear, and therefore cannot persist at all—unlike Allah’s attributes, which endure. The majority of rational people, however, said: This contradicts what is known through the senses.

For the sake of this method, groups of speculative theologians among the Muʿtazilah and others accepted the denial of all the Lord’s attributes, or of some of them. For, in their view, what proves that these things came into existence is that attributes inhere in them, and an argument must be applied consistently. They therefore accepted that anything possessing an attribute that inheres in it must have come into existence. This too is utterly unsound and misguided. Consequently, they accepted the doctrine that the Quran is created, and denied that Allah will be seen in the Hereafter and that He is above His Throne, along with other such implications accepted by those who consistently applied the premises of this argument, which the Muʿtazilah and their followers made the foundation of their religion.

These matters fall within what they called the foundations of religion, but in reality they are not among the foundations of the religion Allah prescribed for His servants. As for the religion concerning which Allah said:

“Or do they have partners who have prescribed for them a religion that Allah has not authorized?”

That religion has foundations and subsidiary matters of its own.

Once it is understood that, in the usage of those who employ this term, “the foundations of religion” is an imprecise and ambiguous designation—because it bears different meanings according to different definitions and conventions—it becomes clear that what Allah, His Messenger, and His believing servants regard as the foundations of religion has been handed down from

---

## Page 1198

the Messenger. As for someone who prescribes a religion that Allah has not authorized, it is clear that the foundations which necessarily entail it cannot have been transmitted from the Prophet. Such a religion is false, and whatever necessarily entails falsehood is itself false, just as whatever necessarily follows from truth is true.

This distinction also clarifies what the early generations and the imams meant when they condemned speculative theology and its practitioners: their condemnation applies to those who reason with unsound proofs or argue in support of false doctrines. But whoever speaks the truth that Allah has authorized, both in his conclusions and in his evidence, belongs among the people of knowledge and faith.

“Allah speaks the truth, and He guides to the right way.”

Addressing people who use a particular terminology in their own terminology and language is not objectionable—provided there is a need for it and the meanings conveyed are sound. This is like addressing non-Arabs, such as the Romans, Persians, and Turks, in their own languages and customary usage: doing so is permissible and commendable when needed. The imams disapproved of it only when there was no need for it.

Thus the Prophet said to Umm Khālid, daughter of Khālid ibn Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀs—a young girl born in Abyssinia because her father was among those who had emigrated there:

“O Umm Khālid, this is sanā.”

Sanā means “beautiful” in the language of Abyssinia; he used it because she spoke that language. Likewise, the Quran and hadith are translated for those who need translation in order to understand them. A Muslim may also read whatever he needs from the books and writings of other nations in their own languages and translate them into Arabic. Similarly, the Prophet instructed Zayd ibn Thābit to learn the Jews’ written language so that he could read and write for him in it, since he did not trust the Jews in this matter.

---

## Page 1199

Thus the early generations and the imams did not disapprove of speculative theology merely because it employed newly coined terms such as “substance,” “accident,” “body,” and the like. Rather, the meanings expressed through these terms contain reprehensible falsehoods in their arguments and conclusions that must be prohibited, for these terms encompass ambiguous meanings in both negation and affirmation.

As Imam Ahmad said when describing the people of innovation:

“They differ over the Book, oppose the Book, and agree in opposing the Book. They speak in equivocal terms and mislead the ignorant with the equivocal language they use.”

If the meanings they intend by such expressions are understood and weighed against the Quran and Sunnah—so that the truth affirmed by the Quran and Sunnah is affirmed, and the falsehood rejected by the Quran and Sunnah is rejected—then this is the truth. This differs from the approach of those who follow their desires: they use these terms to negate and affirm, both in their methods of argument and in the questions they discuss, without explaining the distinctions and classifications that constitute the straight path.

This is one of the sources of confusion. Nothing in the words of the Prophet, any of the Companions or their successors, or any of the imams whose teachings are followed makes any aspect of the foundations of religion—whether its proofs or its doctrinal questions—depend upon what is designated by terms such as “substance,” “body,” “spatial occupancy,” “accident,” and the like.

Those who use these expressions differ in what they intend by them, sometimes because they assign different meanings to the terms, and sometimes because they disagree about the meaning a term denotes. For example, some say that a “body” is something composite, then dispute whether it is a single substance, subject to the condition of composition?

---

## Page 1200

or two substances or more? Or six? Or eight? Or something else? Some say that a body is that in which three dimensions can be conceived, and that it is composed of matter and form. Others say that it is whatever exists, or whatever exists in its own right, and that nothing can exist otherwise.

The early generations and the imams who condemned discussion of substance, body, and accident and declared it an innovation also condemned those who introduce the meanings these people intend by such terms into the foundations of religion—both its proofs and its doctrinal questions, whether by negation or affirmation. If, however, someone understands the sound meanings established by the Quran and Sunnah and expresses them in these terms to those who understand them, so as to clarify which of these people’s meanings accord with the truth and which contradict it, this is immensely beneficial. It is a form of judging by the Book between people concerning their disagreements, as Allah Most High says:

“Mankind was one community. Then Allah sent the prophets as bearers of glad tidings and warnings, and sent down with them the Book in truth, to judge between people concerning that over which they differed.”

This is like judging by the Book between other nations concerning their disagreements over meanings that they express according to their own terminology and customary usage. This requires understanding the meanings of the Quran and Sunnah, understanding the meanings these people express through their terms, and then weighing the latter against the former to reveal what accords with them and what contradicts them.

As for the questioner’s statement, “If this is deemed permissible, what is the justification, when we have understood him, peace be upon him, to have forbidden discussion of certain questions?” the reply is that the necessary clarification and distinctions have already been given in answering the question, and that what truly constitutes the foundations of the religion with which Allah sent His Messenger

---

## Page 1201

may under no circumstances be prohibited, unlike what is called the foundations of religion but is not truly so, whether in its proofs or its doctrinal questions. Or it may constitute the foundations of a religion that Allah has not prescribed, but which was prescribed by someone who introduced into religion what Allah has not authorized.

As for the Prophet’s prohibition mentioned by the questioner, the Book and Sunnah prohibit a number of things. These include speaking about Allah without knowledge, as in His saying:

“Say, ‘My Lord has forbidden only shameful deeds, whether openly or in secret, sin, unjust aggression, associating with Allah that for which He has sent down no authority, and saying about Allah what you do not know.’”

And His saying:

“Do not pursue that of which you have no knowledge.”

They also include saying about Him anything other than the truth, as in His saying:

“Was not the covenant of the Book taken from them, that they should say nothing about Allah except the truth?”

And His saying:

“Do not go to extremes in your religion, and say nothing about Allah except the truth.”

They also include disputing without knowledge, as in His saying:

“Here you are: you have argued about matters of which you have knowledge. Why, then, do you argue about matters of which you have no knowledge?”

They also include disputing the truth after it has become clear, as in His saying:

“They dispute with you concerning the truth after it has become clear.”

They also include arguing with falsehood, as in His saying:

“They argued with falsehood in order to overthrow the truth thereby.”

They also include disputing concerning His signs, as in His saying:

“None dispute concerning the signs of Allah except those who disbelieve.”

And His saying:

“Those who dispute concerning the signs of Allah without any authority having come to them—this is greatly hateful to Allah and to those who believe.”

And His saying:

“There is nothing in their hearts but pride, which they will never attain.”

And His saying:

“And that those who dispute concerning Our signs may know that they have no escape.”

A similar statement is His saying: “And those who dispute concerning Allah after a response has been given

---

## Page 1202

to Him, their argument is invalid before their Lord.” And His saying:

“They dispute concerning Allah, while He is mighty in power.”

And His saying:

“Among people are those who dispute concerning Allah without knowledge, guidance, or an enlightening Book.”

Among the things Allah has forbidden in His Book are division and disagreement, as in His saying:

“Hold firmly to the rope of Allah all together, and do not become divided,”

through to His saying:

“Do not be like those who became divided and differed after clear proofs had come to them. For them there is a tremendous punishment on the Day when some faces will become white and some faces will become black.”

Ibn ‘Abbās said: “The faces of the people of the Sunnah and the community will become white, and the faces of the people of innovation and division will become black.”

Allah Most High also says:

“Indeed, those who have divided their religion and become sects—you have nothing to do with them.”

And He says:

“So set your face toward the religion, inclining to truth, in accordance with the natural disposition upon which Allah created mankind. There is no changing Allah’s creation,”

through to His saying:

“And do not be among those who associate partners with Allah—those who have divided their religion and become sects.”

He has condemned those given to division and disagreement in passages such as His saying:

“Those who were given the Scripture did not differ until after knowledge had come to them, out of mutual transgression.”

And His saying:

“They will continue to differ, except those upon whom your Lord has mercy; and for that He created them.”

And His saying:

“Indeed, those who differ concerning the Book are in profound discord.”

Likewise, the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah accords with the Book of Allah. One example is the well-known hadith from him, part of which Muslim narrated from ‘Abdullāh ibn ‘Amr. The remainder is well known in the Musnad of Ahmad and elsewhere, through the narration of ‘Amr ibn Shu‘ayb, from his father, from his grandfather: “The Messenger of

---

## Page 1203

Allah came out to his Companions while they were debating divine decree. One man was saying, ‘Did Allah not say such-and-such?’ and another was saying, ‘Did Allah not say such-and-such?’ His face looked as though pomegranate seeds had been crushed upon it. He said, ‘Is this what you were commanded to do? Those before you perished precisely because of this: they set parts of Allah’s Book against one another. Allah’s Book was revealed so that its parts would confirm one another, not contradict one another. Consider what you have been commanded to do and do it, and what you have been forbidden to do and avoid it.’” This is the hadith, or words to that effect.

Likewise, he said:

“Disputation concerning the Quran is disbelief.”

Likewise, al-Bukhārī and Muslim narrated in their two Sahīh collections from ‘Ā’ishah:

“The Prophet recited His words: ‘It is He who has sent down to you the Book. In it are verses that are clear—they are the foundation of the Book—and others that are ambiguous. As for those in whose hearts is deviation, they follow what is ambiguous in it, seeking discord and seeking its interpretation.’ The Prophet then said, ‘When you see those who follow what is ambiguous in it, they are the ones Allah has named, so beware of them.’”

As for the Book or the Sunnah forbidding knowledge of matters that rightly belong to the fundamentals of Allah’s religion, this cannot be the case—unless, of course, we prohibit some of this in certain circumstances, such as addressing someone with something beyond his understanding, thereby causing him to go astray. This accords with the saying of ‘Abdullāh ibn Mas‘ūd:

“No man relates to people something beyond their understanding without it becoming a trial for some of them.”

And with the saying of ‘Alī, may Allah be pleased with him:

“Speak to people about what they understand, and leave aside what they find unfamiliar. Do you wish Allah and His Messenger to be disbelieved?”

Or, for example, uttering a truth that entails harm

---

## Page 1204

greater than that resulting from leaving it unsaid. This falls under his saying:

“Whoever among you sees an evil, let him change it with his hand. If he cannot, then with his tongue. If he cannot, then with his heart—and that is the weakest level of faith.”

Narrated by Muslim.

As for the questioner’s words, “If it is held to be permissible, is it obligatory? Has anything been transmitted from him, peace be upon him, indicating that it is obligatory?” the answer is:

There is no doubt that everyone must believe, in general terms, in what the Messenger brought. Nor is there any doubt that knowing in detail what the Messenger brought is a collective obligation. This forms part of conveying that with which Allah sent His Messenger; reflecting upon the Quran, comprehending it and understanding it; knowing the Book and wisdom; preserving the Reminder; calling to the way of the Lord with wisdom and good counsel; and debating in the best manner, along with other duties Allah has imposed upon the believers. It is therefore a collective obligation upon them.

As for what is obligatory upon them individually, this varies according to their capacities, knowledge and needs, and the commands addressed to them individually. Someone unable to hear certain teachings or understand their subtleties is not required to do what someone capable of this must do. Someone who has heard and understood the scriptural texts is required to have detailed knowledge that is not required of someone who has not heard them. Likewise, someone who issues fatwas, a hadith scholar, or someone who engages in debate has obligations that others do not.

As for his question, “Is the preponderant belief reached by a scholar exercising independent judgment sufficient in this regard, or is it necessary

---

## Page 1205

to attain certainty?”

The answer is: the correct approach is to distinguish between cases.

Some groups of speculative theologians claim that certainty is required in all matters of belief—which they may call fundamental questions—and that no evidence may be used in them unless it yields certainty. They may even require everyone to attain certainty in every such matter. Taken without qualification and applied universally, this claim is erroneous and contrary to the Book, the Sunnah, and the consensus of the early generations of the Muslim community and its leading scholars.

Moreover, they themselves are among the furthest from fulfilling what they require. They frequently advance arguments in these matters which they claim are conclusive, but which are actually fallacies, not even amounting to probable evidence. Indeed, the same person often asserts with certainty that an argument is valid in one place and invalid in another. For some, even the ultimate conclusions of their reasoning follow this pattern. Each of two disputants may even claim immediate certainty about the opposite of what the other claims.

The distinction is this: wherever Allah has required knowledge and certainty, the knowledge and certainty He has required are obligatory, as in His saying:

“Know that Allah is severe in punishment and that Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.”

And His saying:

“So know that there is no deity except Allah, and ask forgiveness for your sin.”

Likewise, one must believe in whatever Allah has required belief in. It is established in Islamic law that obligation depends upon a person’s ability, as in His saying:

“So fear Allah as much as you are able.”

And the saying of the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him:

“When I command you to do something, do as much of it as you can.”

Both al-Bukhārī and Muslim narrated it in their Sahīh collections.

---

## Page 1206

Thus, if many of the subtle questions disputed by the Muslim community may be unclear to many people, who cannot find evidence—whether from revelation or otherwise—that would give them certainty, a person in this position is not required to achieve what is beyond his ability. Nor must he abandon the strongly held belief that he considers most probable merely because he cannot attain complete certainty. Rather, this is what he is capable of, especially if it accords with the truth. A belief that accords with the truth benefits the person who holds it; he is rewarded for it, and it fulfils his obligation if he is incapable of more.

It should be understood, however, that most of those who go astray in this matter, or fail to discover the truth concerning it, do so because they neglect to follow what the Messenger brought and abandon the reflection and reasoning that lead to that knowledge. When they turned away from the Book of Allah, they went astray, as Allah Most High says:

“When guidance comes to you from Me, whoever follows My guidance will neither go astray nor suffer.”

“But whoever turns away from My remembrance will have a life of hardship, and We will raise him blind on the Day of Resurrection.”

Ibn ‘Abbās said: “Allah has guaranteed that whoever reads the Quran and acts upon what it contains will neither go astray in this world nor suffer in the Hereafter.” He then recited this verse.

Likewise, in the hadith narrated by al-Tirmidhī and others from ‘Alī, the Prophet said: “There will be trials.” I said, “What is the way out of them, O Messenger of Allah?” He replied:

“The Book of Allah. It contains accounts of what came before you, news of what will come after you, and rulings concerning what arises among you. It is decisive, not frivolous. Any tyrant who abandons it, Allah will crush; whoever seeks guidance elsewhere, Allah will lead astray. It is Allah’s firm rope, the wise reminder, and the straight path. It is that by which desires do not go astray, and by which no confusion befalls

---

## Page 1207

tongues. Its wonders never cease; it does not grow worn through repeated recitation, and scholars never have their fill of it. In another narration: ‘Nor do opinions differ through it.’ It is that which, when the jinn heard it, they could not help but say:

‘Indeed, we have heard an amazing Quran, which guides to the right course.’

Whoever speaks according to it speaks the truth; whoever acts upon it is rewarded; whoever judges by it is just; and whoever calls to it is guided to a straight path.”

Allah Most High says:

“And this is My path, which is straight, so follow it, and do not follow other paths, for they will separate you from His path.”

And He says:

“Alif, Lām, Mīm, Sād. This is a Book sent down to you, so let there be no distress in your heart because of it,”

up to His words:

“Follow what has been sent down to you from your Lord, and do not follow other protectors besides Him.”

And He says:

“And this is a blessed Book which We have sent down, so follow it and fear Allah, that you may receive mercy. Lest you say, ‘The Scripture was sent down only to two groups before us, and we were indeed unaware of what they studied,’ or say, ‘If the Scripture had been sent down to us, we would have been better guided than they.’ Now clear evidence has come to you from your Lord, along with guidance and mercy. Who, then, is more unjust than one who denies Allah’s signs and turns away from them? We will repay those who turn away from Our signs with a terrible punishment for having turned away.”

Thus, He—glory be to Him—states that He will repay anyone who turns away from His signs, without qualification—whether he denies them or not—with a terrible punishment for having turned away.

This is clarified by the fact that everyone who does not acknowledge what the Messenger brought is a disbeliever, whether he believes the Messenger to be lying, is too proud to believe in him, turns away from him in pursuit of his own desires, or doubts what he brought. Everyone who denies what he brought is therefore a disbeliever. Yet someone who does not accuse him of lying may also be a disbeliever if he does not believe in him. For this reason, Allah declares in several places in His Book that those who abandon following what He has sent down are astray and subject to punishment, even if they engage in inquiry, debate, and diligent investigation into rational questions and other matters; and He deemed

---

## Page 1208

this to be among the characteristics of the disbelievers and hypocrites. Allah Most High says:

“We gave them hearing, sight, and hearts, but neither their hearing nor their sight nor their hearts benefited them at all, since they denied Allah’s signs; and they were engulfed by what they used to ridicule.”

And He says:

“When their messengers came to them with clear proofs, they rejoiced in the knowledge they possessed; and they were engulfed by what they used to ridicule.”

“When they saw Our punishment, they said, ‘We believe in Allah alone, and we reject whatever we used to associate with Him.’”

“But their faith did not benefit them once they had seen Our punishment. Such is Allah’s established way with His servants; and there the disbelievers lost.”

And He says:

“Those who dispute Allah’s signs without any authority that has come to them—this is deeply hateful to Allah and to those who believe.”

And He says:

“There is nothing in their hearts but a desire for greatness which they will never attain. So seek refuge in Allah.”

The authority mentioned here is proof revealed by Allah, as He says:

“Or have We sent down to them an authority that speaks in support of what they associate with Him?”

And He says:

“Or do you have clear authority?”

“Then bring your scripture, if you are truthful.”

And He says:

“They are nothing but names you and your forefathers have named, for which Allah has sent down no authority.”

He—glory be to Him—has challenged those who adopt a religion, saying:

“Bring me a scripture prior to this, or a remnant of knowledge.”

The “scripture” means a revealed book. As for the “remnant of knowledge,” some of the early generations said that it means narration and chains of transmission. They also said that it means writing, since narrations and their chains of transmission are recorded in writing. This is because athāra (a remnant) derives from athar (a trace): knowledge stated by someone whose word is accepted is transmitted through a chain of transmission and preserved in writing. All of this thus forms part of the legacy he leaves behind.

---

## Page 1209

Allah Most High also says, describing the hypocrites:

“Have you not seen those who claim to believe in what has been revealed to you and what was revealed before you? They wish to seek judgment from false authorities, although they were commanded to reject them. Satan wishes to lead them far astray.”

“When they are told, ‘Come to what Allah has revealed and to the Messenger,’ you see the hypocrites turn away from you in aversion.”

“How will it be when a calamity befalls them because of what their own hands have done, and then they come to you, swearing by Allah, ‘We intended nothing but goodness and reconciliation’?”

“Those are the ones whose hearts Allah knows. So turn away from them, admonish them, and speak to them in words that reach their very souls.”

These verses contain many lessons. They show the misguidance and hypocrisy of anyone who seeks judgment from something other than the Book and the Sunnah, even if he claims that he wishes to reconcile scriptural proofs with what he himself calls “rational arguments”—notions taken from certain false authorities among the polytheists and the People of the Book. Other lessons may also be drawn from them.

Anyone whose error arises from neglecting his obligations—for example, following the Quran and having faith—or from transgressing Allah’s limits by taking paths He has forbidden, or from following his own desires without guidance from Allah, has wronged himself and is among those subject to the threat of punishment. This is unlike someone who strives earnestly to obey Allah and His Messenger, inwardly and outwardly, seeking the truth through his efforts as Allah and His Messenger have commanded. Such a person’s error is forgiven.

As Allah Most High says:

“The Messenger believes in what has been revealed to him from his Lord, and so do the believers. All believe in Allah, His angels, His books, and His messengers. ‘We make no distinction between any of His messengers.’”

And continuing to His words:

“Allah does not burden any soul beyond its capacity. It will have the good it has earned and bear the evil it has earned

---

## Page 1210

Our Lord, do not take us to task if we forget or make a mistake.”

It is established in Sahīh Muslim that Allah said, “I have done so.” It is likewise established therein, in a hadith narrated by Ibn ʿAbbās, that “the Prophet never recited any passage from these two verses or from Sūrat al-Fātihah without being granted what it asks for.” This makes clear that this supplication has been answered for the Prophet and the believers, and that Allah does not take them to task if they forget or make a mistake.

As for the questioner’s words, “Under these circumstances, does this constitute imposing an obligation beyond one’s capacity?” the answer is:

Although people have disputed this expression extensively, some denying it and others affirming it, it should be understood that the actual disagreement concerns two categories.

The first concerns matters whose permissibility and actual occurrence everyone accepts; they dispute only whether these may be described as beyond one’s capacity.

The second concerns matters that everyone agrees are beyond one’s capacity. They dispute whether commanding them is permissible, but agree that such commands have not actually occurred. There is, however, no case in which the people of knowledge and faith agree that something is beyond one’s capacity yet dispute whether it has actually been commanded.

An example of the first category is the disagreement among dialectical theologians who affirm divine decree and those who deny it concerning “a person’s capacity”—that is, his ability and power. Must it accompany the act without preceding it? Must it precede the act? Or must it accompany the act, even if it also precedes it? Whoever adopts the first view must consequently hold that every person who fails to do what he has been commanded has been charged with something beyond his capacity

---

## Page 1211

if he has no ability except when the act occurs. Thus, the correct view, held by discerning scholars among the dialectical theologians, jurists, hadith scholars, Sufis, and others, is what the Quran indicates: the “capacity” upon which commands and prohibitions depend, and which makes the act possible, need not accompany the act. By contrast, the “capacity” whose presence necessarily entails the occurrence of the act accompanies it.

“The first” is exemplified by His words:

“Pilgrimage to the House is a duty owed to Allah by those who are able to undertake it.”

And by the words of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, to ‘Imrān ibn Husayn:

“Pray standing; if you are unable, then sitting; and if you are unable, then on your side.”

It is well known that pilgrimage and prayer are obligatory for someone who is able to perform them, whether or not he actually does so. This shows that this capacity need not accompany the act.

“The second” is exemplified by His words, exalted is He:

“They were unable to hear, nor could they see.”

And His words, exalted is He:

“And on that Day We shall display Hell before the disbelievers—those whose eyes were veiled from My remembrance and who were unable to hear.”

This is according to those who interpret capacity in this sense. According to the interpretation of the early generations and the majority, however, their inability means that this was burdensome for them and difficult for their souls. Their souls cannot will it, even though they would be capable of doing it if they willed it. This is the condition of someone whose desires and unsound views prevent him from listening to Allah’s revealed Books and following them. Allah has stated that such a person is unable to do so. This “capacity” is the one that accompanies the act and necessarily entails it.

---

## Page 1212

As for “the first,” without it no obligation would be established, in accordance with His words:

“So fear Allah as much as you are able.”

And His words, exalted is He:

“And those who believe and do righteous deeds—We do not burden any soul beyond its capacity.”

And similar passages. Thus, those who fall short or overstep the bounds in the fundamentals of religion, when unable to hear what was revealed to the Messenger, belong to this category.

Likewise, they disagree about “the commanded act” which Allah knows will not occur, or which He has also declared will not occur. Some people say that such an act is beyond one’s power, just as the extreme Qadariyyah deny that Allah could have known, declared, or recorded beforehand that it would not occur. This is because both groups agree that anything contrary to what is known is neither possible nor within one’s power. Most people, however, disagree with them on this.

They say that Allah’s own power refutes this position. They also say that Allah knows the act as it really is: He knows it to be possible and within the servant’s power, yet not occurring or coming into being because the servant does not will it, or dislikes it, or for some similar reason—not because he is incapable of it. This disagreement is resolved by distinguishing the kinds of power to perform it, as explained above. The act is beyond the power that accompanies the act, even though it is within “the power that makes the act possible,” upon which commands and prohibitions depend.

(As for the second type, an example is their agreement that someone incapable of an act cannot perform it, just as the blind, amputees, and the physically infirm cannot add dots to a Quran manuscript, write it out, or fly. They agree that requirements of this kind do not occur in the Shariah.

---

## Page 1213

They differed only over whether it is rationally permissible to command such an act. Some even disputed whether something intrinsically impossible—such as combining contraries or contradictories—may rationally be commanded, although no such command occurs in the Shariah. Whoever goes to extremes and claims that obligations of this kind occur in the Shariah—for example, by claiming that Abū Lahab was required to believe that he would not believe—is mistaken in the view of the general body of Muslims across all groups. Rather, if it is supposed that he was told he would suffer in the Fire—which entails his dying in unbelief—and that this declaration was made known to him, then his religious obligation would cease in that circumstance, and faith would no longer benefit him. This would be like the faith of someone who believes only after witnessing the punishment. Allah Most High says:

“But their faith did not benefit them when they saw Our punishment.”

And He says:

“Now? Yet you had disobeyed before and were among the corrupters!”

The purpose here is to point out that disagreement over this principle concerns, at times, the act commanded and, at other times, the permissibility of issuing the command.

This is how some speculative theologians have confused people: they treated the two categories as one and asserted without qualification that obligations beyond one’s capacity are imposed, because certain kinds of obligation occur which the general body of Muslims does not classify as beyond one’s capacity. Disagreement concerning these kinds does not relate to questions of commands and prohibitions, but rather to questions of divine decree and predestination. Such a theologian then treats the permissibility of this category as entailing the permissibility of the category that Muslims agree lies beyond one’s power, drawing an analogy between the two. This is among the “analogies” whose invalidity is acknowledged by all Muslims—indeed, by the adherents of all religions, and even by all rational people. For whoever draws an analogy between a healthy person commanded to perform acts—arguing, for example, that power accompanies the act, or that Allah

---

## Page 1214

knew that he would not perform them—and an incapable person who could not perform the act even if he wished to do so, has equated two things whose difference is self-evident, both rationally and religiously. This is one of the sources of doctrinal deviation among the Qadariyyah and their brethren, the Jabriyyah. Once this is understood, making the unqualified assertion that obligations beyond one’s capacity are imposed is recognized as one of the innovations introduced into Islam.

The same applies to asserting without qualification that people are compelled to perform their acts. The early generations of the Muslim community and its imams unanimously rejected this and censured anyone who used such language, even if he intended thereby to refute the Qadariyyah, who do not acknowledge that Allah creates people’s acts or that He has willed all that occurs. They said that this amounts to refuting one innovation with another, countering error with error and falsehood with falsehood. Were this response not too brief to permit an extended discussion, I would quote their explicit statements demonstrating their rejection of this.

If, however, one explains precisely what the speaker means and sets forth the truth in language that does not confuse truth with falsehood, clearly distinguishing between the two, this constitutes sound discernment. Such clarification then falls outside the scope of the censure directed at people of this kind, whom the imams described as disagreeing over Allah’s Book, opposing Allah’s Book, and agreeing to abandon Allah’s Book. They also described them as using ambiguous language, distorting words from their proper meanings, and deceiving ignorant people through the confusion they create for them.

For this reason, they included the advocates of compulsion among the censured Qadariyyah, because they engaged in false discourse concerning divine decree. This is the common feature for which the Qadariyyah were censured. Thus Imām Abū Bakr al-Khallāl introduced a chapter in his Book of the Sunnah with the heading: (Refutation of the Qadariyyah and their claim that Allah

---

## Page 1215

compelled His servants to commit sins).

It was then narrated from ‘Amr ibn ‘Uthmān, from Baqiyyah ibn al-Walīd, who said:

“I asked al-Zubaydī and al-Awzā‘ī about compulsion. Al-Zubaydī replied: ‘Allah’s authority is too great, and His power too great, for Him to compel or obstruct. Rather, He decrees, ordains, creates, and fashions His servant’s disposition toward what he loves.’

“Al-Awzā‘ī said: ‘I know of no basis for compulsion in the Quran or the Sunnah, so I am reluctant to speak of it. But decree, ordainment, creation, and the fashioning of dispositions are recognized in the Quran and in the hadith narrated from Allah’s Messenger. I have set this forth only for fear that a man among the Followers, adhering to the community and affirming the truth, might fall into doubt.’”

These two answers, given by these two imams in the generation following the Followers, are among the finest answers.

Al-Zubaydī is Muhammad ibn al-Walīd, a companion of al-Zuhrī. He said: “Allah’s authority is too great, and His power too great, for Him to compel or obstruct.” Thus he denied compulsion, because the familiar linguistic meaning of compulsion is to force someone to do something against his consent. Thus, in their discussions of marriage, the jurists ask: “May a woman be compelled to marry, or not? And if her guardian prevents her from marrying, what should she do?” By compelling her, they mean marrying her off without her consent or choice; by obstructing her, they mean preventing her from what she consents to and chooses.
