He therefore said: “Allah is too great to compel or obstruct,” because Allah, glorified be He, is able to make the servant love and willingly accept what he does, and hate and dislike what he refrains from doing—as is indeed the case. Thus the servant is not compelled to do what he chooses, willingly accepts, and desires, namely, “his acts

---

## Page 1216

performed by choice.” Nor is he barred from what he leaves undone because he hates it, dislikes it, and does not desire it—namely, “his voluntary abstentions.”

As for al-Awzā‘ī, he prohibited using this term even when this meaning was intended, since it had no basis in the Quran or the Sunnah. This would lead to using an innovated expression whose apparent meaning is false, which is impermissible even if it is said that a sound meaning was intended.

Al-Khallāl said: “Al-Marwadhī informed us, saying: ‘I heard one of the elders say that he heard ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn Mahdī say: Sufyān al-Thawrī rejected compulsion and said, “Allah Most High fashioned His servants’ dispositions.”’”

Al-Marwadhī said: “I think he meant the Prophet’s words, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, to al-Ashajj of ‘Abd al-Qays”—meaning his words recorded in Sahīh Muslim:

“You possess two traits that Allah loves: forbearance and deliberation.”

He asked: “Are these two traits that I cultivated, or two traits with which I was endowed by nature?”

He replied: “Rather, they are two traits with which you were endowed by nature.”

He said: “Praise be to Allah, who endowed me by nature with two traits that Allah Most High loves.”

For this reason, al-Bukhārī and others cited Allah Most High’s words as evidence that human acts are created:

“Indeed, mankind was created anxious.”
“When evil touches him, impatient.”
“And when good touches him, withholding.”

Thus, Allah Most High states that He created man with this disposition. Al-Awzā‘ī’s answer is sounder than al-Zubaydī’s, because al-Zubaydī denied compulsion, whereas al-Awzā‘ī prohibited using the term. Since this expression can bear a sound meaning, denying it may entail denying both truth and falsehood. Thus al-Khallāl related what ‘Abd Allāh ibn Ahmad mentioned in his book al-Sunnah (The Sunnah), saying: “We were told by

---

## Page 1217

Muhammad ibn Bakkār, who said: Abū Ma‘shar narrated to us; Ya‘lā narrated to us, from Muhammad ibn Ka‘b, who said: ‘He is called al-Jabbār (the Compeller) only because He compels creation to do whatever He wills.’”

If one refrains from using an ambiguous expression that admits different meanings and may cause confusion, the danger is avoided. This is better than denying it—even if the unsound meaning is its apparent sense—lest one be thought to deny both meanings together.

The same applies to denying the capacity to perform what is commanded: affirming compulsion in committing what is forbidden is analogous to denying the capacity to perform what is commanded.

This was also the position of Imam Ahmad and other imams of the Sunnah.

Al-Khallāl said: Al-Maymūnī informed us, saying:

“I heard Abū ‘Abd Allāh—meaning Ahmad ibn Hanbal—debating Khālid ibn Khidāsh about divine decree. They mentioned a man, and Abū ‘Abd Allāh said: ‘What I object to here is his saying, “Allah compelled.”’”

He also said: Al-Marwadhī informed us:

“I said to Abū ‘Abd Allāh: ‘A man says that Allah compelled His servants.’

“He replied: ‘Do not speak like this.’ He rejected this wording and said: ‘He leads astray whom He wills and guides whom He wills.’”

He also said: Al-Marwadhī informed us, saying:

“A letter was written to ‘Abd al-Wahhāb concerning Hasan ibn Khalaf al-‘Ukbarī, stating that he had refrained, out of scrupulousness, from taking his father’s inheritance. A Qadarī man said: ‘Allah did not compel His servants to commit sins.’ Ahmad ibn Rajā’ replied to him: ‘Allah compelled His servants to do whatever He willed,’ intending thereby to affirm divine decree.

“Ahmad ibn ‘Alī then wrote a book arguing his case. I brought it to Abū ‘Abd Allāh and told him what had happened. He said: ‘And he writes a book!’ He censured them both: Ibn Rajā’ for saying, ‘He compelled His servants,’ and the Qadarī who said, ‘He did not compel them.’ He also censured Ahmad ibn ‘Alī for writing the book and presenting his arguments, and ordered that he be shunned for writing it. He said

---

## Page 1218

to me: ‘Ibn Rajā’ must ask his Lord’s forgiveness for saying, “He compelled His servants.”’ I then asked Abū ‘Abd Allāh: ‘What, then, is the answer to this question?’ He replied: ‘Allah leads astray whom He wills and guides whom He wills.’”

Concerning this issue, al-Marwadhī reported that he heard Abū ‘Abd Allāh, when he censured the man who said, “He did not compel,” and the man who answered him, “He compelled,” say:

“Whenever a man introduces an innovation, people broaden the scope of their response to it.”

He also said: “The man who answered them with his own newly introduced expression should ask his Lord’s forgiveness.” He censured anyone who responded with something of the nature of speculative theological discourse if he had no earlier imam to follow on that issue.

Al-Marwadhī said:

“Very soon afterwards, Ahmad ibn ‘Alī arrived from ‘Ukbar, accompanied by a group of elders and carrying a letter from the people of ‘Ukbar. I brought Ahmad ibn ‘Alī before Abū ‘Abd Allāh, and he said: ‘Abū ‘Abd Allāh, here is the book. Give it to Abū Bakr so that he may tear it up. I shall stand on the pulpit of ‘Ukbar and ask Allah—Mighty and Majestic—for forgiveness.’ Abū ‘Abd Allāh said to me: ‘You should accept this from him.’ So they returned to him.”

We have discussed this matter at length elsewhere and addressed the unsound premise assumed by the divergent factions: that affirming the true meaning which they call “compulsion” is incompatible with commands and prohibitions. The Qadariyyah went so far as to regard it as wholly incompatible with commands and prohibitions. A group among the Jabriyyah regarded it as incompatible with the goodness and badness of acts, and relied on this to deny that acts possess inherent goodness and badness discernible by reason.

It is well known that this is no more incompatible with such goodness and badness than it is with goodness and badness understood as an act’s being agreeable and beneficial to the person performing it, or disagreeable and harmful to him.

---

## Page 1219

Shaykh al-Islām Abū al-ʿAbbās Ibn Taymiyyah—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

“What must a religiously accountable person believe? What must he know? What knowledge is encouraged? What is certainty, and how is it attained? What is knowledge of Allah?”

He replied:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds.

As for the question, “What must a religiously accountable person believe?” this has both a general and a detailed answer. In general, he must believe in Allah and His Messenger and affirm everything the Messenger brought: belief in Allah, His angels, His Books, His messengers, and the Last Day, as well as the Messenger’s commands and prohibitions. Thus, he must affirm everything the Messenger reported and commanded. He must believe him in what he reported and submit to him in what he commanded.

As for the details, every religiously accountable person must affirm whatever has been established for him as something the Messenger reported or commanded. As for something the Messenger reported, if a person has not learned that the Messenger reported it and has had no means of knowing this, he is not punished for failing to affirm it specifically, and it is included in his affirmation

---

## Page 1220

in general terms. If he then says something contrary to it on the basis of an interpretation, he is mistaken, but his error is forgiven provided he has been neither negligent nor transgressive. Thus, scholars are required to hold beliefs that ordinary people are not required to hold, and someone raised in a land of knowledge and faith is required to hold beliefs that someone raised in a land of ignorance is not. As for what is established by rational inference alone, independently of the prophetic message, a person is not punished for failing to believe it.

As for the claim made by a group of speculative theologians that the attributes established by reason are those that must be affirmed, and that failing to affirm them constitutes disbelief, unlike those established by revelation: sometimes they deny the latter, sometimes they reinterpret them or entrust their meaning to Allah, and sometimes they affirm them but make faith and disbelief depend on the attributes established by reason. This has no basis in the teachings of the early generations of the Muslim community or its leading scholars. Faith and disbelief are classifications established through the prophetic message, and it is through scriptural evidence—not rational evidence alone—that a believer is distinguished from an unbeliever.

As for his question, “What must he know?” this, too, varies. Every religiously accountable person must know what Allah has commanded: what He has commanded him to believe and what He has commanded him to know. Thus, if he possesses wealth on which zakat is due, he must learn the rulings of zakat; if he has the means to perform Hajj, he must learn the rulings of Hajj. The same applies to similar matters. The Muslim community as a whole must know everything the Messenger brought, so that none of the knowledge the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, conveyed to his community is lost. This is what is indicated by

---

## Page 1221

the Book and Sunnah. However, knowledge beyond what a particular individual needs is a collective obligation: if a group fulfils it, the rest are relieved of it.

As for “knowledge whose pursuit is encouraged in general,” this is the knowledge that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, taught his community. Each person, however, is encouraged to pursue the knowledge he needs most and that benefits him most, and this varies. For people in general, seeking to learn about obligatory and recommended deeds, promises of reward, and warnings of punishment is more beneficial. Each person seeks whatever he needs of these matters. If a doubt arises in someone’s heart, pursuing an action that counters it may be more beneficial to him than anything else.

As for “certainty,” it is the tranquillity of the heart and the firm establishment of knowledge within it. This is the sense conveyed by the expression mā’ yaqan (“still water”), used when water has ceased moving. The opposite of certainty is doubt, which is a kind of movement and agitation. Thus one says rābanī, yarībunī (“it disturbed me; it disturbs me”). The same usage occurs in the hadith:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, passed by a gazelle curled up asleep and said, ‘Let no one disturb it.’”

Certainty thus encompasses two things: the heart’s knowledge and the heart’s actions. A servant may know something with absolute conviction, yet his heart may still experience unrest and agitation in connection with the action that this knowledge entails. For example, a servant knows that Allah is the Lord and Sovereign of everything; that there is no creator besides Him; and that whatever He wills happens, and whatever He does not will does not happen. This knowledge may be accompanied by tranquillity in turning to Allah and reliance upon Him, or it may not be accompanied by acting accordingly. This may be because the heart is heedless of this knowledge—heedlessness is the opposite of complete knowledge, though not of knowledge at its most basic level—or because thoughts arise in the heart that turn its attention to causes, or for some other reason.

---

## Page 1222

In the well-known hadith narrated by Abū Bakr from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, he said:

“Ask Allah for certainty and well-being, for after certainty no one has been given anything better than well-being. So ask Allah for both.”

Those who possess certainty remain steadfast when tested, unlike others, whose faith may be lost or diminished through trials. Allah Most High says:

“And We made from among them leaders guiding by Our command, when they were patient and had certainty in Our signs.”

Do you not see His words:

“Those to whom people said, ‘People have gathered against you, so fear them.’ But this increased them in faith, and they said, ‘Allah is sufficient for us, and He is the best Disposer of affairs.’”

Such was their state. Allah Most High also says:

“O you who believe, remember Allah’s favour upon you when armies came against you, and We sent against them a wind and armies you did not see. And Allah sees all that you do.”

The passage continues to His words:

“There the believers were tested and shaken severely.”

“And when the hypocrites and those in whose hearts is disease said, ‘Allah and His Messenger promised us nothing but delusion.’”

Allah Most High also says, in the two verses:

“We have appointed none but angels as guardians of the Fire, and We have made their number only a trial for those who disbelieve, so that those given the Scripture may attain certainty.”

As for how certainty is attained, it is through three things:

The first is reflecting upon the Quran.

The second is reflecting upon the signs Allah brings about within human beings and across the horizons, which show that it is the truth.

---

## Page 1223

And the third is acting in accordance with what one knows. Allah Most High says:

“We will show them Our signs on the horizons and within themselves until it becomes clear to them that it is the truth. Is it not enough that your Lord is a witness over all things?”

The pronoun refers to the Quran, as Allah Most High says:

“Say, ‘Have you considered: if it is from Allah and you then disbelieve in it, who is more astray than one who is in extreme opposition?’”

“We will show them Our signs on the horizons and within themselves until it becomes clear to them that it is the truth,” and the remainder of the verse.

As for the claim made by a group of philosophers and their followers among speculative theologians and Sufis—that the pronoun refers to Allah and that the verse describes the way to know Him through rational inference—this interpretation is mistaken in many respects and contradicts the consensus of the early generations of this community and its leading scholars.

He, glory be to Him, thus explains that He shows observable signs to demonstrate the truth of the verses that are heard, although His testimony through those verses is itself sufficient. For He, glory be to Him, has not guided His servants through the Quran merely by conveying reports, as some groups among the speculative theologians suppose. They believe that the Quran provides evidence only through reports; that accepting a report depends upon knowing the truthfulness of its speaker, namely the Messenger; and that knowing his truthfulness depends upon establishing the existence of the Creator, knowing what is necessary, possible, and impossible with respect to Him, knowing that sending messengers is possible, and knowing the signs that demonstrate their truthfulness. They call these foundations “rational principles,” because, in their view, revelation depends upon them.

This is a grave error and one of the greatest ways in which certain groups among the speculative theologians and proponents of religious innovation have gone astray. For Allah, glory be to Him, has explained in His Book everything needed concerning the foundations of the religion. In it He established

---

## Page 1224

Allah’s oneness, prophethood, and the resurrection through proofs whose thoroughness no reasoning can attain, unlike the Muslim speculative theologians, the philosophers, and their followers. In it He also argued through samadiyya analogies, which are rational comparisons that yield certainty. We have discussed this at length elsewhere.

As for observable signs, what is witnessed or known through mass transmission—the punishments inflicted upon those who denied the messengers or disobeyed them, and the victories granted to the messengers and their followers in the manner in which they occurred—together with what is known of how Allah Most High honors whoever obeys Him and grants him the final triumph, and how He exacts retribution from those who disobey Him and brings defeat upon them: all this contains lessons that make clear His commands and prohibitions, His promises and warnings, and other matters that accord with the Quran.

For this reason, Allah Most High says:

“He is the One who expelled the disbelievers among the People of the Book from their homes at the first gathering. You did not think they would leave,”

continuing to His words:

“So take heed, O people of insight.”

This clearly illustrates drawing inferences concerning the foundations of religion, although it also encompasses drawing inferences concerning its subsidiary matters. Likewise, He says:

“There was certainly a sign for you in two groups that met: one fighting in the cause of Allah and the other disbelieving,”

continuing to His words:

“Indeed, in that is a lesson for people of insight.”

As for action, acting in accordance with knowledge makes that knowledge firm and reinforces it, whereas acting contrary to it weakens it and may even cause it to disappear. Allah Most High says:

“When they deviated, Allah caused their hearts to deviate.”

And He, Most High, says:

“We turn their hearts and eyes away, just as they did not believe in it the first time.”

And He, Most High, says:

“And if they

---

## Page 1225

had done what they were admonished to do, it would have been better for them and would have given them greater firmness,” and the following verses.

He also says:

“There has come to you from Allah a light and a clear Book.”

“Through it Allah guides those who seek His pleasure to the ways of peace,” continuing to the end of the verse.

And He, Most High, says:

“O you who believe, fear Allah and believe in His Messenger. He will grant you a double share of His mercy, provide you with a light by which you may walk, and forgive you,” continuing to the end of the verse.

As for knowledge, two kinds are principally intended by it.

The first is knowledge of Allah Himself, of His attributes of majesty and honor, and of what His most beautiful names signify. When this knowledge becomes firmly rooted in the heart, it necessarily gives rise to fear of Allah. For one must know that Allah rewards obedience to Him and punishes disobedience to Him, as both the Quran and direct observation attest. This is the meaning of the statement of Abū Hayyān al-Taymī—one of the generation following the Successors:

“Scholars are of three kinds: one who knows Allah but does not know His commands; one who knows His commands but does not know Allah; and one who knows both Allah and His commands. The scholar who knows Allah is the one who fears Him, while the scholar who knows His commands is the one who knows what is lawful and unlawful.”

A man addressed al-Shaʿbī, saying, “O scholar!” He replied, “The scholar is only the one who fears Allah.”

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Masʿūd said:

“Fear of Allah is sufficient knowledge, and presumption concerning Allah is sufficient ignorance.”

The second kind intended by knowledge of Allah is knowledge of the rulings of the revealed law, as related in the authentic collection concerning the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“He availed himself of a concession in a certain matter, and news reached him that some people had scrupulously abstained from it

---

## Page 1226

and said: ‘Why do some people scrupulously abstain from things in which I avail myself of a concession? By Allah, I know Allah better than any of you and fear Him more than any of you.’” Another version reads:

“By Allah, I fear Allah more than any of you and know His limits better than any of you.”

He thus identified knowledge of Allah with knowledge of His limits. Similar to this is what one of the Successors said in describing the Commander of the Faithful, ʿAlī ibn Abī Tālib—may Allah be pleased with him:

“Allah was indeed great in my heart, and I was indeed knowledgeable concerning dhāt Allāh (that which pertains to Allah).”

By this he meant Allah’s rulings. In their usage, the word dhāt did not have the meaning it acquired in the terminology of later scholars; rather, it referred to what pertains to Allah. Thus Khubayb—may Allah be pleased with him—said:

“This is for God’s sake; and if He wills,
He will bless the limbs of a body torn apart.”

Another example is the hadith:

“Abraham told only three lies, all of them for Allah’s sake.”

Examples also include the words of the Most High:

“So fear Allah and set things right between you.”

“And He knows well what is within the hearts.”

And there are other similar expressions. Dhāt is the feminine form of (dhū). This word is used with a following noun in a genitive construction, enabling something to be described through nouns denoting kinds of things. If what is being described is masculine, one says dhū such-and-such; if feminine, dhāt such-and-such—as in dhāt siwār (a woman wearing a bracelet).

Thus, if one says, “So-and-so suffered harm fī dhāt Allāh,” the meaning is in His cause and for His sake: that is, in pursuing what He has commanded and loves, and on His account.

Then, because attributes are ascribed to the self, the self too is said to be dhāt ʿilm wa-qudrah wa-kalām—possessing knowledge, power, speech, and so forth. They omitted the following genitive complement and made the word definite, saying: “the essence characterized by attributes.”

---

## Page 1227

that is, the self characterized by attributes. Thus, when these people speak emphatically of “the essence,” they mean only the actual self, which has a description and possesses attributes.

The terms sifah (attribute) and wasf (description) sometimes refer to the words used to describe their subject, as when the Companion said concerning “Say: He is Allah, the One”:

“I love it because it describes the Most Merciful.”

At other times, they refer to the meanings conveyed by those words, such as knowledge and power. The Jahmiyyah, the Muʿtazilah, and others deny these meanings, saying that attributes are merely the expressions used to describe their subject. The Kullābiyyah and their followers among those who affirm the attributes may distinguish between sifah and wasf, taking wasf to mean the statement and sifah to mean the attribute subsisting in the subject described. Most people, however, know that both sifah and wasf are originally verbal nouns, like waʿd and ʿidah (promising), or wazn and zinah (weighing), and that each may be used in either of these two senses.

Since those Jahmiyyah deny that any attribute subsists in Allah—knowledge, power, will, or speech, all of which Muslims affirm—they began saying:

“These people have affirmed attributes additional to the essence.”

Some of their opponents who affirm the attributes have come to accept this formulation, saying:

“The attributes are additional to the essence they have described—an essence possessing attributes and a description.”

They thereby give people the impression that there is an essence distinct from the attributes, and that it possesses attributes distinct from the essence. Those who deny the attributes raise various allegations to vilify this position, which this is not the place to discuss. We have already demonstrated their invalidity elsewhere.

---

## Page 1228

The truth is that the essence characterized by attributes is never separable from those attributes, and an essence devoid of attributes cannot exist. To claim that there is a living, knowing, powerful, seeing being without life, knowledge, or power is like claiming that there are power, knowledge, and life whose possessor is not living, knowing, and powerful. Indeed, the existence of anything self-subsisting—whether beginningless or brought into existence—that is devoid of all attributes is impossible according to sound reason.

However, when the Jahmiyyah, the Muʿtazilah, and others affirmed an essence stripped of attributes, those debating them began to say:

“I affirm attributes in addition to the essence you have affirmed”; that is, “I do not confine myself to merely affirming an essence without attributes.”

By this, the speaker did not mean that, in external reality, there is an essence subsisting independently, nor that alongside it there are attributes additional to that essence and distinct from it.

Thus, some people say:

“The attributes are other than the essence.”

This is what the Muʿtazilah and the Karrāmiyyah say; the Muʿtazilah then deny the attributes, whereas the Karrāmiyyah affirm them.

Others say:

“The attribute is neither the subject described nor other than it.”

This is what certain groups who affirm the attributes say, including Abū al-Hasan al-Ashʿarī and others.

Still others say, as the imams said:

“We do not say that the attribute is the subject described, nor do we say that it is other than it; for neither do we say, ‘It is neither the subject itself nor other than it.’”

The word “other” is ambiguous. It may mean something separate from a thing, or something of which one accompanies the other, and something close to it in existence, time, or place. “Other” may also mean that one of two things can be known without the other being known.

---

## Page 1229

And according to the first meaning, the attribute is not other than the subject it describes, nor is part of a whole other than that whole. According to the second meaning, the attribute is other than the subject, and part of a whole is other than that whole. The early generations and the imams therefore refrained from unqualifiedly affirming or denying that an attribute is “other,” because of the ambiguity and confusion this entails.

Thus, a Jahmī would ask, “Is the Quran Allah Himself, or other than Allah?” Sometimes they counter his argument by referring to Allah’s knowledge, asking, “Is Allah’s knowledge Allah Himself, or other than Him?”—if he affirms knowledge or cannot deny it.

At other times, they resolve the objection and demonstrate the error in both unqualified formulations—negation and affirmation—because of the confusion they entail. Rather, the questioner seeks clarification and is told: “If by ‘other’ you mean something separate from the subject, then the attribute is not separate from it and is therefore not other than it. But if by ‘other’ you mean something such that the subject can be understood in general terms, even if it is not the subject itself, then it is ‘other’ in this respect.”

Allah Most High knows best. May Allah bless Muhammad.

---

## Page 1230

Section:

Since many of those devoted to discourse and letters, and those devoted to practice and sound, have turned away from the Quran and faith, you find them following the approach of many speculative theologians in their treatment of reason: they make reason alone the foundation of their knowledge, give it an independent role, and make faith and the Quran subordinate to it. For them, rational concepts are the primary, universal principles, sufficient in themselves without faith or the Quran.

Many Sufis, by contrast, disparage reason and find fault with it. They hold that exalted spiritual states and lofty stations can only be attained in its absence, and they affirm things that sound reason rejects. They praise intoxication, madness, emotional derangement, and forms of insight and spiritual states that occur only when reason and discernment are lost. They also believe claims whose falsity is evident to sound reason, accepting them from someone whose truthfulness has not been established.

Both extremes are blameworthy. Rather, reason is a prerequisite for acquiring knowledge and for the perfection and soundness of deeds, and it brings knowledge to perfection

---

## Page 1231

and deeds; yet it cannot do this independently. Rather, reason is an innate faculty and a power within the soul, comparable to the faculty of sight in the eye. If the light of faith and the Quran joins it, it is like the light of the eye when joined by the light of the sun and fire.

If left on its own, it cannot perceive matters beyond its unaided grasp. If it is set aside altogether, words and deeds performed without it are animal-like: they may involve love, emotional fervor, and felt experience, just as these may occur in an animal. Thus, states experienced without reason are deficient, and statements contrary to reason are false.

The messengers brought what reason cannot apprehend; they did not bring what reason knows to be impossible. Yet those who place excessive reliance on reason have declared things necessary, possible, or impossible on the strength of supposedly rational arguments which they believed to be true, though they were false. They used these arguments to oppose the prophetic messages and what they brought. Those who turn away from reason, meanwhile, have believed false things, entered into corrupt states and practices, and abandoned the discernment by which Allah has favoured the children of Adam over others.

Some of the people of hadith may incline towards either of these two groups: at times removing reason from its proper sphere of authority, and at other times using it to oppose the prophetic traditions.

This deviation concerning discriminating reason among those devoted to letters and those devoted to sound is comparable to their deviation concerning the heart’s emotional fervor. For those devoted to sound have affirmed it, exalted it, and gone to excess

---

## Page 1232

in it, to the point that they made it both the criterion and the ultimate goal, just as the others do with reason. Those devoted to letters, by contrast, have turned away from it, disparaged it, and have not counted it among the qualities of perfection.

The reason for this is that those devoted to letters seek knowledge, whose gateway is reason, whereas those devoted to sound seek action, whose gateway is love. Each group therefore exalts what pertains to its own pursuit and disparages the other, even though both knowledge and action are indispensable: reason directed towards knowledge, mental activity and love; discernment and movement; speech and inward state; letters and sound.

When both are measured against the Quran and Sunnah, they constitute the straight path. All praise belongs to Allah, Lord of the worlds. May Allah bless Muhammad and his family and grant them peace.

---

## Page 1233

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah sanctify his soul—said:

Section

If the two testimonies of faith constitute the foundation of the religion and its branches, with all its other pillars and subdivisions encompassed within them, then worship is bound up with obedience to Allah and His Messenger, as the Most High says:

“Whoever obeys Allah and the Messenger will be with those whom Allah has blessed: the prophets, those steadfast in truth, the martyrs, and the righteous.”

He also says in the verse prescribed for recitation in the Sermon of Need:

“O you who believe, fear Allah and speak words that are right.”

“He will set right your deeds for you and forgive you your sins. Whoever obeys Allah and His Messenger has indeed achieved a great triumph.”

The sermon also contains these words:

“Whoever obeys Allah and His Messenger has followed right guidance. Whoever disobeys them harms only himself and does not harm Allah at all.”

He says:

“Whoever obeys Allah and His Messenger, fears Allah, and is mindful of Him—it is they who will triumph.”

He also says:

“Whoever obeys Allah and His Messenger, He will admit them to gardens beneath which rivers flow, to abide there forever. That is the great triumph.”

“Whoever disobeys Allah and His Messenger and transgresses His limits, He will admit him to a Fire in which he will abide forever, and he will have a humiliating punishment.”

Likewise, He made matters dependent upon love for Allah and His Messenger, as in His words:

“more beloved to you than Allah

---

## Page 1234

and His Messenger.” Likewise, He made matters dependent upon pleasing Allah and His Messenger, as He says:

“Allah and His Messenger are more deserving that they should please Him.”

And upon accepting the judgment of Allah and His Messenger, as He says:

“When they are called to Allah and His Messenger so that he may judge between them.”

And His words:

“When it is said to them, ‘Come to what Allah has revealed and to the Messenger.’”

He commanded that disputes be referred to Allah and the Messenger, saying:

“Obey Allah and obey the Messenger and those in authority among you. If you disagree over anything, refer it to Allah and the Messenger.”

He also assigned the spoils of war to Allah and the Messenger, saying:

“They ask you about the spoils of war. Say, ‘The spoils of war belong to Allah and the Messenger.’”

There are numerous similar examples. As for making matters of love and hatred, loyalty and enmity, support and abandonment, agreement and disagreement, pleasure and anger, giving and withholding dependent upon something that contradicts these principles revealed by Allah—something “narrower than them,” “broader than them,” or “broader in one respect and narrower in another”—the broader category is exemplified by the position of those who engage in philosophy and their followers—among the misguided speculative theologians and Sufis, and the kingdoms founded upon that position, such as that of the king of the Turks and others—in permitting adherence to a religion other than that brought by Muhammad, the Messenger of Allah, even if one reveres Muhammad and regards his religion as the best of religions. The same applies to anyone who considers salvation and felicity possible, after he was sent as a messenger, through something other than his revealed law.

The category that is “broader in one respect and narrower in another” includes, for example, lineages, tribes, Arab, Persian, Roman, and Turkish peoples, or cities and lands.

---

## Page 1235

And the category that is “narrower in every respect” consists of affiliation with a particular branch of religious practice, such as military service among those who wage jihad, jurisprudence among scholars, or asceticism and Sufism among devoted worshippers. It also includes affiliation with particular factions within these groups: attachment to a particular imam, shaykh, king, or leading speculative theologian; to a doctrine or practice that distinguishes a group; or to these factions’ distinctive clothing, whether turbans or other garments. Thus, some people display partisan attachment to a garment or style of dress—meaning clothing shared by jurists and ascetics, clothing peculiar to either group or to particular factions within it, military attire, or the like.

All of this belongs to the practices of pre-Islamic ignorance that divide the Muslim community. Those who engage in it depart from the Sunnah and the united community and enter into religious innovation and division. Rather, the religion of Allah Most High requires that His Messenger Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, be the one whose commands and prohibitions are obeyed, and who is followed in his love and disobedience, his approval and anger, his giving and withholding, his loyalty and enmity, and his support and abandonment.

One must accord every individual or category of people the rights that the Messenger accorded them. Those he brought near are to be brought near; those he kept at a distance are to be kept at a distance; and those he placed between the two are to retain that intermediate position. Among these matters, both in their particular instances and their characteristics, one loves whatever Allah and His Messenger love, dislikes whatever Allah and His Messenger dislike, and leaves neither loved nor disliked whatever Allah and His Messenger likewise left neither loved nor disliked. One enjoins whatever Allah and His Messenger have commanded concerning these matters and forbids whatever Allah and His Messenger have forbidden

---

## Page 1236

and treats as permissible whatever Allah and His Messenger have permitted, overlooks whatever Allah and His Messenger have overlooked, gives preference to whatever Allah and His Messenger have preferred, gives precedence to whatever Allah and His Messenger have given precedence, gives lower priority to whatever Allah and His Messenger have given lower priority, and refers disputes concerning these matters to Allah and His Messenger. Whatever is clear is followed, and whatever is unclear is clarified.

Among these are disputed matters of independent reasoning that Allah and His Messenger have sanctioned. An example is the Companions’ differing judgments about delaying the afternoon prayer beyond its prescribed time on the day of the expedition against Banū Qurayzah or performing it within its time: the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, rebuked neither group. Likewise, some of them cut down the date palms of Banū al-Nadīr, while others had not done so, and Allah approved both courses of action.

Similarly, Allah mentioned that David and Solomon passed judgment concerning the cultivated field: one of them understood the judgment, yet He praised both for their knowledge and for judging by it. Likewise, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“When a judge exercises independent reasoning and reaches the correct judgment, he receives two rewards; when he exercises independent reasoning and errs, he receives one reward.”

Thus, latitude must be allowed wherever Allah and His Messenger have allowed it, and whatever Allah and His Messenger have overlooked must likewise be overlooked. Whatever the Muslims have agreed upon—whether declaring something obligatory, prohibited, recommended, or permissible, or forgiving one another for their errors and accepting one another’s exercise of independent reasoning—is among the things Allah and His Messenger have commanded. For Allah and His Messenger have commanded unity and forbidden division, and have shown that the Muslim community does not agree upon misguidance, as set out in the relevant discussions.

---

## Page 1237

Shaykh al-Islām Ahmad ibn Taymiyyah—may Allah sanctify his soul—was asked about the Prophet’s saying, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“My community will divide into seventy-three groups.”

What are these groups, and what does each of them believe?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. The hadith is authentic and well known in the Sunan and Musnad collections, such as the Sunan of Abū Dāwūd, al-Tirmidhī, al-Nasā’ī, and others. Its wording is:

“The Jews divided into seventy-one groups, all of which will be in the Fire except one. The Christians divided into seventy-two groups, all of which will be in the Fire except one. This community will divide into seventy-three groups, all of which will be in the Fire except one.”

Another wording reads:

“Into seventy-three religious communities.”

In one narration:

“They asked, ‘O Messenger of Allah, which is the saved group?’ He replied, ‘Those who follow the same way that I and my Companions follow today.’”

In another narration, he said:

“It is the community. Allah’s Hand is over the community.”

For this reason, he described the saved group as the people of the Sunnah and the community. They constitute the largest body and the overwhelming majority.

---

## Page 1238

As for the remaining groups, they are characterized by deviation, division, religious innovations, and whims. None of these groups comes close in size to the saved group, let alone equals it; indeed, a group among them may be exceedingly small.

The distinguishing feature of these groups is their departure from the Book, the Sunnah, and consensus. Whoever upholds the Book, the Sunnah, and consensus belongs to the people of the Sunnah and the community.

As for identifying these groups, people have written works about them and discussed them in books on sects and doctrines. However, to assert definitively that this group, described . . . (1), is one of the seventy-two requires evidence. Allah has forbidden speaking without knowledge in general, and speaking about Him without knowledge in particular. He, Most High, says:

“Say, ‘My Lord has only forbidden immoral deeds, whether committed openly or secretly; sin and unjust aggression; associating with Allah that for which He has sent down no authority; and saying about Allah what you do not know.’”

And He, Most High, says:

“O mankind, eat of whatever is lawful and wholesome on earth, and do not follow the footsteps of Satan. Indeed, he is a clear enemy to you.”

“He only commands you to commit evil and indecency, and to say about Allah what you do not know.”

And He, Most High, says:

“Do not pursue that of which you have no knowledge.”

Moreover, many people speak about these groups on the basis of conjecture and whim. They regard their own faction, and those affiliated with and loyal to the leader they follow, as the people of the Sunnah and the community, while regarding those who oppose that faction as people of religious innovation. This is manifest misguidance.

For the people of truth and the Sunnah follow none other than the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, who does not speak from his own desire: it is nothing but revelation revealed. He is the one who must be believed in everything he reports and obeyed in everything he commands, and it is not

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A word is not visible at this point in the original.

---

## Page 1239

a rank that belongs to any other imam. Rather, anyone’s statements may be accepted or rejected, except those of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Whoever makes any person other than the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, the standard by which those who love and agree with him are counted among the people of the Sunnah and the community, while those who oppose him are counted among the people of religious innovation and division—as occurs among groups that follow leading figures in religious discourse and other matters—is himself among the people of religious innovation, misguidance, and division. This makes clear that those most entitled to be considered the saved group are the people of hadith and the Sunnah.

They have no one whom they follow with unwavering allegiance except the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. They are the most knowledgeable about his sayings and his life, and the most discerning in distinguishing sound reports about him from unsound ones. Their imams possess deep understanding of these reports, knowledge of their meanings, and commitment to following them: believing them, acting upon them, loving them, showing loyalty to those who support them, and enmity towards those who oppose them.

They relate broadly worded doctrinal statements to the Book and wisdom that he brought. They do not establish a proposition as a foundation of their religion or a central tenet of their teaching unless it is established in what the Messenger brought. Rather, they make the Book and wisdom with which the Messenger was sent the foundation they believe in and rely upon.

Whatever people dispute concerning the divine attributes, divine decree, threats of punishment, religious designations, enjoining right and forbidding wrong, and other matters, they refer to Allah and His Messenger. They clarify the ambiguous expressions disputed by the people of division and disagreement. Whatever meanings accord with the Book and the Sunnah, they affirm; and whatever meanings conflict with the Book and the Sunnah

---

## Page 1240

they reject. They do not follow conjecture or what their souls desire, for following conjecture is ignorance, and following one’s desires without guidance from Allah is wrongdoing.

Ignorance and wrongdoing are the sum of all evil. Allah Most High says:

“and man undertook to bear it. Indeed, he was unjust and ignorant”

to the end of the surah. He mentions repentance because He, glorified and exalted, knows that every human being inevitably has some ignorance and wrongdoing in him. Then Allah accepts the repentance of whomever He wills. Thus, the believing servant continually comes to recognize truths of which he was previously ignorant and turns away from deeds in which he acted wrongfully.

The least of such wrongdoing is his wronging himself, as the Exalted says:

“Allah is the Protector of those who believe. He brings them out of darkness into the light.”

And the Exalted says:

“It is He who sends down clear signs upon His servant to bring you out of darkness into the light.”

And the Exalted says:

“Alif Lām Rā.”

“A Book which We have revealed to you so that you may bring people out of darkness into the light.”

It should also be understood that the groups affiliated with particular leaders in the fundamentals of religion and speculative theology vary in degree. Some have departed from the Sunnah in major foundational matters, while others have departed from it only in subtle matters. Some have refuted other groups that are further removed from the Sunnah than they are. They deserve praise for the falsehood they have refuted and the truth they have stated. Yet they may have overstepped the bounds of fairness in their refutation, denying some truth and asserting some falsehood. They have thus countered a major religious innovation with a lesser one, and refuted falsehood by using a lesser falsehood. This is the condition of most practitioners of speculative theology who affiliate themselves with the people of the Sunnah and the community.

---

## Page 1241

If people such as these do not turn what they have innovated into a doctrine by which they separate themselves from the Muslim community, making it the basis of their allegiance and enmity, then it falls within the category of error. Allah, glorified and exalted, forgives the believers their errors in such matters.

Thus, many of the early generations of this Ummah and its leading scholars fell into similar errors: through independent scholarly judgment, they expressed views that contradicted what is established in the Quran and Sunnah. This is unlike someone who gives allegiance to those who agree with him, shows enmity towards those who disagree with him, divides the Muslim community, and declares his opponents—but not his supporters—to be disbelievers and evildoers over matters of opinion and scholarly judgment. He also deems it lawful to fight those who disagree with him, but not those who agree with him. Such people are among those who cause division and discord.

For this reason, the first people of religious innovation to separate themselves from the Muslim community were the renegade “Khārijites.” The hadith concerning the Khārijites has been authentically transmitted from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, through ten routes of transmission, which Muslim included in his Sahīh. Al-Bukhārī also included more than one of these routes.

The Companions of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, fought them alongside the Commander of the Faithful, ʿAlī ibn Abī Tālib. They did not disagree about fighting them as they had disagreed about fighting during the civil strife at the battles of the Camel and Siffīn. In those conflicts, they fell into three groups: one fought on one side, another fought on the other, and a third refrained from fighting and stayed out of the conflict. The scriptural texts indicate that this last course was preferable.

Thus, when the Khārijites separated themselves from the Muslim community, declared its members disbelievers, and deemed it lawful to fight them, the Sunnah brought

---

## Page 1242

the pronouncements concerning them, such as these words of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Any one of you would consider his prayer insignificant compared with theirs, his fasting insignificant compared with theirs, and his recitation insignificant compared with theirs. They recite the Quran, but it does not pass beyond their throats. They pass out of Islam as an arrow passes through its quarry. Wherever you encounter them, kill them, for whoever kills them will have a reward with Allah on the Day of Resurrection.”

The first of them had already appeared during the lifetime of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. When he saw the Prophet’s distribution, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“He said, ‘O Muhammad, be just, for you have not been just!’ The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, replied, ‘You would indeed be ruined and lost if I were not just.’ One of his Companions said to him, ‘Let me strike off this hypocrite’s head, O Messenger of Allah.’ He replied, ‘From this man’s stock will emerge people whose prayer any one of you would consider his own insignificant beside, whose fasting he would consider his own insignificant beside, and whose recitation he would consider his own insignificant beside.’”

The hadith continues.

Thus, religious innovation began with casting aspersions on the Sunnah on the basis of conjecture and desire, just as Iblīs challenged his Lord’s command on the basis of his own opinion and desire.

As for identifying the sects destined for perdition, the earliest person known to us to have spoken of their being astray was Yūsuf ibn Asbāt, followed by ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Mubārak. Both were eminent imams, among the most distinguished leaders of the Muslims. They said:

“The four foundational groups of religious innovation are the Rāfidites, the Khārijites, the Qadariyyah, and the Murjiʾah.”

Ibn al-Mubārak was asked, “And the Jahmiyyah?”

He answered that those people were not part of Muhammad’s Ummah. He used to say:

“We can recount the statements of the Jews and Christians, but we cannot recount the statements of the Jahmiyyah.”

A group of scholars among Ahmad’s followers and others followed him in this view. They said:

---

## Page 1243

“The Jahmiyyah are disbelievers and therefore are not included among the seventy-two sects, just as the hypocrites who conceal disbelief while outwardly professing Islam are not included among them. These are the covert unbelievers.”

Others among Ahmad’s followers and other scholars said:

“Rather, the Jahmiyyah are included among the seventy-two sects.”

They identified five foundational groups of religious innovation. According to this view, each of the “five groups of innovators” comprises twelve sects, whereas according to the first view, each of the “four groups of innovators” comprises eighteen sects.

This rests upon another fundamental issue: “declaring proponents of religious innovation disbelievers.” Whoever excludes the Jahmiyyah from these sects does not declare them disbelievers, for he does not declare the other proponents of religious innovation disbelievers. Rather, he regards them as subject to the threatened punishment, like the wicked and the disobedient. The statement, “They are in the Fire,” is understood like the warnings concerning other sins, such as consuming an orphan’s property. As Allah Most High says:

“Indeed, those who devour the property of orphans unjustly are only consuming fire into their bellies.”

Those who include them among these sects hold two views. Some declare them all disbelievers. This was asserted only by certain later scholars affiliated with the imams or the speculative theologians. The early generations and the imams, however, did not dispute that the “Murjiʾah,” the “Shīʿah” who regard ʿAlī as superior to Abū Bakr and ʿUmar, and similar groups were not to be declared disbelievers. Ahmad’s recorded statements consistently affirm that these people are not to be declared disbelievers, although some of his followers reported disagreement concerning the declaration of all proponents of religious innovation—these groups and others—as disbelievers

---

## Page 1244

as reported from him or within his school, to the point that some unqualifiedly asserted that these people and others would remain eternally in the Fire. This misrepresents both his school and the Sharia.

Others did not declare any of these groups disbelievers, treating proponents of religious innovation like those who commit sins. They said:

“Just as a fundamental principle of Ahl al-Sunnah wa-l-Jamāʿah is that they do not declare anyone a disbeliever on account of a sin, likewise they do not declare anyone a disbeliever on account of a religious innovation.”

What is transmitted from the early generations and the imams consists of general statements declaring the “pure Jahmiyyah” disbelievers—those who deny the divine attributes. Their doctrine amounts to saying that Allah does not speak, is not seen, and is not separate from creation; that He has neither knowledge, power, hearing, sight, nor life; rather, that the Quran is created and the people of Paradise do not see Him, just as the people of the Fire do not see Him, along with other such assertions.

As for the Khawārij and the Rāfidah, disagreement and hesitation concerning declaring them disbelievers are reported from Ahmad and others. As for the Qadariyyah who deny the recording of the decree and Allah’s knowledge, they declared them disbelievers, whereas they had not declared disbelievers those who affirmed His knowledge but did not affirm His creation of human acts.

The decisive clarification of this issue lies in explaining two fundamental principles.

The first is to recognize that anyone among those who profess Islam who is in reality a disbeliever can only be a hypocrite. For since Allah sent Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, revealed the Quran to him, and he emigrated to Medina, people have fallen into three categories: one who believes in him; one who disbelieves in him and openly professes disbelief

---

## Page 1245

and a hypocrite who conceals disbelief. This is why Allah mentions these three categories at the beginning of Sūrat al-Baqarah: four verses describing the believers, two concerning the disbelievers, and over ten concerning the hypocrites.

Allah mentions the disbelievers and the hypocrites in several places in the Quran, as in His words:

“And do not obey the disbelievers and the hypocrites.”

And His words:

“Indeed, Allah will gather the hypocrites and the disbelievers all together in Hell.”

And His words:

“So today no ransom will be accepted from you or from those who disbelieved.”

He mentions them alongside the disbelievers as a distinct group because they outwardly profess Islam. Inwardly, however, they are worse than the disbelievers, as Allah Most High says:

“Indeed, the hypocrites will be in the lowest depths of the Fire.”

And as He says:

“And never pray over any of them who dies, nor stand at his grave. Indeed, they disbelieved in Allah and His Messenger.”

And as He says:

“Say: Spend willingly or unwillingly; it will never be accepted from you. Indeed, you have been a defiantly disobedient people.”

“Nothing prevents their contributions from being accepted except that they disbelieve in Allah and His Messenger, come to prayer only reluctantly, and spend only unwillingly.”

Since this is so, among the proponents of religious innovation are hypocrites who conceal disbelief; such a person is a disbeliever. People of this kind are numerous among the Rāfidah and the Jahmiyyah, for their leaders were hypocrites who concealed disbelief. The first person to introduce the Rāfidī doctrine was a hypocrite. The same applies to the Jahmī doctrine, whose origins lie in concealed disbelief and hypocrisy. This is why the hypocrites who conceal disbelief among the Qarāmitah—the esotericists who espouse philosophy—and their like incline towards the Rāfidah and the Jahmiyyah, because of their affinity with them.

Among the proponents of religious innovation are also those who have faith both inwardly and outwardly, but are marked by ignorance and wrongdoing

---

## Page 1246

such that he has erred in some matters of the Sunnah. Such a person is neither a disbeliever nor a hypocrite. He may nevertheless commit acts of aggression and wrongdoing that make him defiantly disobedient or sinful; or he may be mistaken in his interpretation and have his error forgiven. Alongside this, he may possess faith and piety that give him a degree of friendship with Allah commensurate with his faith and piety. This is the first of the two principles.

The second principle:

A statement may constitute disbelief, such as denying the obligation of prayer, zakah, fasting, and pilgrimage, or declaring lawful fornication, wine, gambling, and marriage to women whom one is permanently forbidden to marry. Yet the person who holds such a view may not have received the revealed instruction; in such a case, someone who denies it is not declared a disbeliever on that account. Examples include someone who has only recently embraced Islam or who grew up in a remote desert region where the laws of Islam had not reached him. Such a person is not judged to be a disbeliever for denying something revealed to the Messenger if he does not know that it was revealed to the Messenger. The doctrines of the Jahmiyyah are of this kind, for they deny what is true of the Lord, Most High, and what Allah revealed to His Messenger.

Their doctrines are judged erroneous on three grounds:

First: The texts of the Quran and Sunnah, together with consensus, that contradict their position are exceedingly numerous and well known. They reject them only by distorting their meaning.

Second: The reality of their position is the negation of the Creator, even though some of them do not know that their position necessarily entails negating the Creator. Just as the foundation of faith is acknowledging Allah, the foundation of disbelief is denying Allah.

---

## Page 1247

Third:

They contradict what all religions and all people of sound innate disposition agree upon. Nevertheless, many of their doctrines may be obscure to many believers, who therefore suppose that the truth is with them because of the specious arguments they present. These believers believe in Allah and His Messenger inwardly and outwardly; this matter has merely become confused and unclear to them, just as it has to others among the various groups of religious innovators. Such people are certainly not disbelievers. Rather, some may be defiantly disobedient or sinful, while others may be mistaken and have their errors forgiven. A person among them may possess faith and piety that give him a degree of friendship with Allah commensurate with his faith and piety.

The fundamental position of Ahl al-Sunnah, which distinguishes them from the Khawārij, the Jahmiyyah, the Mu‘tazilah, and the Murji’ah, is that faith varies in degree and can be present in part. As the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever has a speck’s weight of faith in his heart will come out of the Fire.”

Accordingly, friendship with Allah likewise varies in degree and can be possessed in part.

Once the roots of religious innovation are understood, one sees that the fundamental doctrine of the Khawārij is that they declare people disbelievers on account of sin, regard things that are not sins as sinful, and hold that the Quran should be followed to the exclusion of any Sunnah that conflicts with its apparent meaning—even when that Sunnah is established by mass transmission. They declare those who disagree with them disbelievers and, because they consider them apostates, deem permissible against them what they do not deem permissible against someone who was never a Muslim. As the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said of them:

“They kill the people of Islam and leave the idolaters alone.”

For this reason, they declared ‘Uthmān, ‘Alī, and their respective followers disbelievers, and declared both sides at Siffīn disbelievers, along with other vile doctrines of this kind.

---

## Page 1248

The fundamental doctrine of the Rāfidah is that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, explicitly designated ‘Alī in terms that left no room for excuse; that he is an infallible imam and whoever opposes him becomes a disbeliever; and that the Emigrants and the Helpers concealed this designation, rejected the infallible imam, followed their desires, altered the religion, changed the religious law, acted unjustly, and transgressed. Indeed, they hold that all became disbelievers except a small number—ten-odd people, or more. They then say that Abū Bakr, ‘Umar, and others like them had always been hypocrites. Sometimes, however, they say that these men first believed and then became disbelievers.

Most of them declare anyone who disagrees with their doctrine a disbeliever. They call themselves the believers and those who oppose them disbelievers. They regard Muslim cities in which their doctrines do not prevail as a domain of apostasy, worse than the cities of the polytheists and Christians. For this reason, they ally themselves with Jews, Christians, and polytheists against sections of the Muslim majority, supporting hostility and warfare against them. Thus, their alliances with disbelieving polytheists against the Muslim majority are well known, as are their alliances with the Christian Franks and with the Jews against the Muslim majority.

From among them emerged the principal forms of heresy and hypocrisy, such as the heresy of the esoteric Qarmatians and others like them. There is no doubt that, of all the groups of religious innovators, they are the furthest removed from the Quran and Sunnah. Hence, they became widely known among ordinary people for their opposition to the Sunnah. Most ordinary people recognize no opposite of a Sunni other than a Rāfidī. Thus, when one of them says, “I am a Sunni,” he means only, “I am not a Rāfidī.”

There is no doubt that they are worse than the Khawārij. Yet the Khawārij wielded the sword against the Muslim community in the early days of Islam, while the Rāfidah’s alliances with the disbelievers are more grievous than the swords of

---

## Page 1249

the Khawārij, for the Qarmatians, the Ismāʿīliyyah, and others like them wage war against the Muslim majority and affiliate themselves with the Rāfidah. The Khawārij are known for truthfulness, whereas the Rāfidah are known for lying. The Khawārij passed out of Islam, whereas these people openly repudiated Islam.

As for the Qadariyyah proper, they are far better than these groups and closer to the Quran and Sunnah. However, the Muʿtazilah and other Qadariyyah are also Jahmiyyah, and they may declare those who disagree with them disbelievers and deem it lawful to shed Muslim blood, thus approaching those other groups. The Murjiʾah, by contrast, are not among the proponents of these grave innovations. Indeed, groups of jurists and devout worshippers adopted their view and were regarded only as belonging to Ahl al-Sunnah, until their position became more serious through the grave doctrines they added.

Since prominent figures with followers had been associated with the Murjiʾī doctrine and the view that ʿAlī was superior, the renowned imams of the Sunnah condemned the Murjiʾah and those who preferred ʿAlī, to deter people from their teachings. One example is the statement of Sufyān al-Thawrī:

“Whoever ranks ʿAlī above Abū Bakr and the two elders has disparaged the Emigrants and the Helpers. I do not think any deed of his ascends to Allah while he holds that view.”

Or words to that effect. He said this when the preference for ʿAlī was attributed to some of the Kufan imams.

Likewise, Ayyūb al-Sakhtiyānī said:

“Whoever ranks ʿAlī above ʿUthmān has disparaged the Emigrants and the Helpers.”

He said this when such a view reached him concerning some of the Kufan imams. It has been reported that he retracted that view. Similarly, al-Thawrī, Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, and others condemned the Murjiʾah when some prominent figures were associated with the Murjiʾī doctrine.

---

## Page 1250

And Imam Ahmad’s statements on this subject accord with those of the rightly guided imams who preceded him. He introduced no doctrine of his own; rather, he made the Sunnah known, explained and defended it, clarified the standing of those who opposed it, strove in its cause, and patiently endured harm for its sake when deviant doctrines and innovations were openly promoted.

Allah Most High said:

“And We made from among them leaders guiding by Our command when they were patient and were certain of Our signs.”

It is through patience and certainty that leadership in religion is attained. When he embodied these qualities, his name became associated with the leadership in the Sunnah for which he became renowned. He became a model for those who came after him, just as he had followed those who came before him. The Sunnah itself, however, is what the Companions received from the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and what the Successors received from them, followed by their successors, down to the Day of Resurrection—even though some imams possess greater knowledge of it and greater patience in adhering to it.

Allah, glorified and exalted, knows best and is most wise. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 1251

Section:
A General Principle:

Most people frequently depart from the middle course in most matters. An example is their opposing attitudes towards certain practices: some adopt them as religious observances that are obligatory, recommended, or generally enjoined, while others consider them unlawful and disliked, or forbidden, or generally prohibited.

An example is “listening to singing.” A group among the Sufis and those who profess poverty adopt it as a religious practice, even if they neither declare with their tongues nor believe in their hearts that it is a means of drawing near to Allah. Their religion consists in an inner state, not a doctrinal conviction: their inner state and conduct consist in approving of it in their hearts and loving it as a religious observance and a means of drawing near to Allah. Some of them, however, may indeed believe this and state it verbally.

Others among them believe and say, “It is not a means of drawing near to Allah,” yet their inner state treats it as precisely that, as beneficial to religion and conducive to reforming the heart. Some go to such extremes in this regard that they regard everyone who abstains from it as excluded from friendship with Allah and from its fruits—the lofty spiritual stations.

---

## Page 1252

At the opposite extreme are those who condemn and prohibit every kind of singing, making no distinction between the singing of children and women on festive occasions, the singing of others, and women’s singing on other occasions. Some go to such extremes in condemning those who engage in it that they regard them all as defiantly sinful or as disbelievers.

These two extremes—turning what has not been prescribed into a religious practice, or prohibiting what has not been forbidden—constitute the religion of the Age of Ignorance and of the Christians, for which Allah censured them. As He, the Exalted, says:

“The polytheists said, ‘Had Allah willed, neither we nor our forefathers would have worshipped anything besides Him, nor would we have forbidden anything without His authority.’”

He, the Exalted, also says in the hadith of ‘Iyād ibn Himār narrated by Muslim in his Sahīh:

“I created My servants as upright monotheists, but the devils led them astray, forbade them what I had made lawful for them, and commanded them to associate with Me that for which I had sent down no authority.”

He also says concerning the Christians:

“They do not forbid what Allah and His Messenger have forbidden, nor do they follow the religion of truth.”

An example of this is when some people are guilty of “neglecting what is commanded” or “transgressing by doing what is forbidden,” whether through doubts or desires. Others respond by overstepping the bounds in enjoining what is right and forbidding what is wrong, or by falling short in doing so.

Such neglect and transgression—whether concerning what Islamic law commands and forbids, or the very act of commanding and forbidding others—are what caused the People of the Book to deserve punishment. Allah says:

“Humiliation has been laid upon them wherever they are found, except under a covenant from Allah and a covenant from people, and they have incurred Allah’s wrath

---

## Page 1253

—that was because they rejected Allah’s signs and killed the prophets unjustly. That was because they disobeyed and used to transgress.” Thus, He attributed this to disobedience and transgression.

Disobedience means failing to comply with a command, which constitutes falling short; transgression means exceeding a limit. Likewise, anyone “entrusted with property” is liable: if he falls short and neglects what he has been instructed to do—which constitutes disobedience; if he transgresses through betrayal or otherwise. Hence His words:

“Do not cooperate in sin and transgression.”

Sin here means disobedience, and Allah knows best. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah has prescribed obligations, so do not neglect them; He has forbidden certain things, so do not violate those prohibitions; He has set limits, so do not overstep them; and He has remained silent about certain things out of mercy for you, not forgetfulness, so do not ask about them.”

Disobedience consists in neglecting obligations and violating prohibitions—that is, failing to comply with commands and prohibitions. Transgression consists in exceeding the limits of what is permissible.

He, the Exalted, says:

“He enjoins upon them what is right and forbids them what is wrong, makes lawful for them good things, and prohibits for them evil things.”

Disobedience means failing to comply with His commands and prohibitions, while transgression means going beyond what He has permitted into what He has forbidden. The same distinction applies—and Allah knows best—to His words:

“Our Lord, forgive us our sins and our excesses in our affairs.”

Sins refer to disobedience, while excess refers to transgression and exceeding the limit. Know that “exceeding the limit” is a form of violating a prohibition, because overstepping the limit is unlawful and forbidden. It therefore falls within the category of prohibited acts. Prohibited acts, however, are of two kinds: those prohibited absolutely, such as disbelief—committing such an act is a sin and is forbidden.

---

## Page 1254

and another category in which certain kinds and quantities are permitted, but exceeding those kinds and quantities is forbidden; doing so constitutes transgression.

Likewise, transgression may occur in commanded acts just as it occurs in permissible acts. Going beyond what is commanded may constitute forbidden transgression, may be permissible without restriction, or may be permissible up to a certain point, beyond which it constitutes transgression.

In light of this distinction, the “Sharia” has been described as comprising commands and prohibitions, the lawful and the unlawful, obligations and limits, prescribed practices and rulings. “Obligations” are the prescribed measures for commanded acts, while “limits” are the outer bounds of what is permissible, whether commanded or not.

---

## Page 1255

Shaykh al-Islām also said—may Allah sanctify his soul:

In the name of Allah, the Most Merciful, the Especially Merciful.

From Ahmad ibn Taymiyyah to those Muslims whom this letter reaches who profess adherence to the Sunnah and the community, belong to the fellowship of the knowledgeable and exemplary shaykh, Abū al-Barakāt ʿAdī ibn Musāfir al-Umawī—may Allah have mercy on him—and those who follow their path.

May Allah guide them to follow His way, help them obey Him and His Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and make them hold firmly to His strong rope, guided along the path of those whom Allah has blessed: the prophets, the steadfastly truthful, the martyrs and the righteous. May He keep them away from the path of those given to misguidance and deviation, who depart from the law and the way with which Allah sent His Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—so that they may be among those upon whom Allah has bestowed His greatest favour through following the Book and the Sunnah.

Peace be upon you, and Allah’s mercy and blessings.

To proceed:

We praise Allah before you: there is no god but He, and He is worthy of all praise; and He is

---

## Page 1256

able to do all things. We ask Him to bless the Seal of the Prophets and the master of Adam’s descendants—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—the most honoured of all creation in his Lord’s sight, the nearest to Him and the highest in rank before Him: Muhammad, His servant and Messenger. May Allah bless him, his family and his Companions, and grant them abundant peace.

To proceed:

Allah sent Muhammad—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—with guidance and the religion of truth, to make it prevail over all other religions; and sufficient is Allah as a witness. He sent down to him the Book in truth, confirming the Scriptures that preceded it and as a guardian over them. He perfected the religion for him and his community, completed His favour upon them, and made them the best community brought forth for mankind. They bring the number of communities to seventy, and are the best and most honoured of them in Allah’s sight. He made them a community of the middle way—that is, just and excellent—and therefore made them witnesses over mankind. He guided them to the religion with which He sent all His messengers and which He ordained for all His creation. Then He further distinguished and favoured them through the law and the way He appointed for them.

(The former includes the “foundations of faith,” the highest and greatest of which is “monotheism”: the testimony that none has the right to be worshipped except Allah. As He, Most High, says:

“We never sent a messenger before you without revealing to him: ‘There is no deity except Me, so worship Me.’”

And He, Most High, says:

“We certainly sent a messenger to every community, saying: ‘Worship Allah and shun false gods.’”

And He, Most High, says:

“Ask those of Our messengers whom We sent before you: Have We appointed any gods besides the Most Merciful to be worshipped?”

And He, Most High, says:

“He has ordained for you the religion He enjoined upon Noah, and that which We have revealed to you, and what We enjoined upon Abraham

---

## Page 1257

and Moses and Jesus.” And He, Most High, says:

“O messengers, eat of the good things and do righteous deeds. Indeed, I know all that you do.”

“And indeed, this community of yours is one community, and I am your Lord, so fear Me.”

Other examples include belief in all of Allah’s Books and all of His messengers, as He, Most High, says:

“Say: ‘We believe in Allah and in what has been revealed to us, and in what was revealed to Abraham, Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob and the Tribes, and in what Moses and Jesus were given, and in what the prophets were given by their Lord. We make no distinction between any of them, and to Him we submit.’”

Likewise, He, Most High, says:

“And say: ‘I believe in every Book Allah has sent down, and I have been commanded to judge justly between you.’”

And He, Most High, says:

“The Messenger believes in what has been sent down to him from his Lord, and so do the believers. All believe in Allah, His angels, His Books and His messengers: ‘We make no distinction between any of His messengers.’ And they say: ‘We hear and obey. We seek Your forgiveness, our Lord, and to You is the final return.’”

And so on to the end.

Other examples include belief in the Last Day and the reward and punishment it holds. Allah tells us that the believers of earlier communities believed in it, saying:

“Indeed, those who believe, the Jews, the Christians and the Sabians—whoever believes in Allah and the Last Day and does righteous deeds—will have their reward with their Lord. No fear will be upon them, nor will they grieve.”

Other examples include the fundamental precepts of the revealed laws, as He mentions in the surahs al-An‘ām (“The Cattle”), al-A‘rāf (“The Heights”), Subhān (“Glory Be [to Him]”) and other Meccan surahs: His command to worship Him alone, with no partner; His command to be dutiful to parents, maintain ties of kinship, fulfil covenants, speak justly, give full weight and measure, and give to those who ask and those who are deprived; and His prohibition of taking a life without

---

## Page 1258

just cause; His prohibition of indecencies, whether open or hidden; His prohibition of sin and unjust aggression; and His prohibition of speaking about religion without knowledge. This also includes what affirming Allah’s oneness entails: devoting one’s religion sincerely to Allah, relying upon Him, hoping for His mercy, fearing Him, patiently accepting His judgment, and carrying out His commands; and that Allah and His Messenger be dearer to the servant than his family, his wealth, and all people. These and other fundamentals of faith are mentioned in various passages of the Quran, including the Meccan surahs and some of the Medinan surahs.

(As for the second, it consists of the laws of His religion that Allah revealed in the Medinan surahs, and what the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prescribed for his community. For Allah, glorified be He, revealed to him the Book and wisdom, bestowed this favour upon the believers, and commanded the wives of His Prophet to remember it. He said:

“Allah has sent down to you the Book and wisdom and taught you what you did not know.”

And He said:

“Allah certainly bestowed a favour upon the believers when He sent among them a Messenger from themselves, reciting His verses to them, purifying them, and teaching them the Book and wisdom.”

And He said:

“And remember what is recited in your houses of the verses of Allah and wisdom.”

Several of the early generations said: “Wisdom is the Sunnah.” For what was recited in the houses of his wives, may Allah be pleased with them, besides the Quran was his Sunnah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Hence he said:

“Indeed, I have been given the Book and something like it along with it.”

Hassān ibn ‘Atiyya said: “Gabriel, peace be upon him, would descend to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, with the Sunnah just as he would descend with the Quran, and would teach it to him just as he taught him the Quran.”

---

## Page 1259

These “laws,” through which Allah guided this Prophet and his community, include the direction of prayer, rites of worship, and the prescribed way of practice. Examples include the five daily prayers at their appointed times, with their specified number, recitation, bowing, prostration, and facing the Kaaba. They also include the obligations of zakat and its minimum thresholds, which He prescribed for the Muslims’ wealth—livestock, grain, fruit, merchandise, gold, and silver—and those designated to receive it. He says:

“Alms are only for the poor and the needy, those employed to administer them, those whose hearts are to be reconciled, for freeing slaves, for those in debt, for Allah’s cause, and for the traveller—an obligation ordained by Allah. And Allah is All-Knowing, All-Wise.”
